### MY LORD,

That I once more presume to give your Lordship the trouble of such an address is not because of any confidence I have in the value of these papers. It is partly because I well know that your Lordship's kindness <note>original: "candor"</note> and charity will be ready to pardon the faults and to cover the weaknesses of this undertaking. --- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication to Nathanael Crewe, Bishop of Durham. The author is describing the holy figures (the Early Church Fathers and Bishops) who are the subjects of this book, arguing that their lives prove the ancient and apostolic origin of the office of the bishop.</meta>

whose piety and patience, zeal and charity, sobriety and detachment from the world <note>original: "Contempt of the World"</note>, rendered them the honor of their times and recommend them as incomparable examples to posterity. We may see here, in more instances than one, the order of bishops <note>original: "Episcopal Order"</note> immediately deriving itself from *Apostolic* hands. Regarding this, if some men were not strangely biased by passion and prejudice, there could be no shadow of dispute. For he that can read the lives of *Timothy* and *Titus*, of *Ignatius*, *Polycarp*, etc., and yet imagine them to have been no more than mere parish priests who only supervised a little congregation, must certainly reveal either extraordinary ignorance or unreasonable partiality.

Here also we may find what a mighty reverence these first and better ages had for the governors of the Church and the guides of souls; no respect was then thought great enough. In this, they acted agreeably not only to the rules of Christianity, but to the common sense of mankind. And indeed, I could abundantly show—were it as appropriate to this place as it is necessary for the age we live in—with what honors and dignities, what rights and revenues, and what privileges and immunities the *sacred ministry* <note>original: "Sacred Function"</note> has been invested in all ages and nations, including both the primitive and barbarous as well as the more polite and civilized countries. 

For we have fallen into the worst of times, in which men have been taught, by bad principles and worse practices, to despise the holy order and to level it with the lowest <note>original: "meanest"</note> of the people. And this is done not only by professed enemies (for then...

# The Epistle Dedicatory

[...and that not by open enemies (if it had been so,] we could have endured it) but by insincere friends who seem to have a high zeal for religion and themselves. By these means, the hands of evil men have been strengthened, and the designs of those have been sufficiently satisfied who, it seems, would rejoice at the ruin of us both. I confess that it is less surprising that some men should treat the persons and reputation <note>original: "Credit"</note> of the ordained clergy <note>original: "Regular Clergy"</note> with contempt and scorn, when religion itself is not safe from the rude and bold mockery <note>original: "railleries"</note> of some, and the serious attempts of others who gravely intend to banish the reverence for religion and the impressions of whatever is divine and sacred from the minds of men.

But, my Lord, it is not my intention to entertain your Lordship with a scathing attack <note>original: "invective"</note> against the wickedness of the era <note>original: "Iniquity of the Times"</note>. I would rather silently mourn them and heartily pray for their reformation, so that the best of Churches may prosper and flourish under the best of Princes. May her peace and order be preserved inviolable, her prayer book <note>original: "Liturgy"</note> and divine services <note>original: "Offices"</note> be universally followed, her solemn assemblies be regularly attended, and her church laws <note>original: "Canons"</note> and governing rules <note>original: "Constitutions"</note> be observed and practiced! *May her priests be clothed with righteousness, and be able by sound doctrine both to encourage and to convince those who oppose it! May they be hardworking in their ministries and be very highly esteemed in love, at least for their relationship to God and for their work's sake! May her governors diligently oversee the flock of God, and may those who rule well be considered worthy of double honor!* In which number may your Lordship...

<summary>The author concludes his critique of the era's religious skepticism and internal hypocrisy, transitioning into a prayer for the prosperity of the Church of England and the dignity of its leadership.</summary>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page begins the "To the Reader" preface of Cave's work. It establishes the philosophical and intellectual justification for studying history and antiquity, transitioning from the specific dedication to the general reader.</meta>

# TO THE
# READER.

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'I' featuring two figures (cherubs or putti) intertwined with foliage and flowers within a square border.</image-desc>

It is no small argument for the spiritual and incorporeal nature of human souls—and evidence that they are driven by a higher principle than mere *matter* and *motion*—that they engage in such boundless and inquisitive research for knowledge. Our minds naturally grasp at a kind of omniscience; not content with the study of this or that particular science, they explore the entire course of nature. Nor are they satisfied with the present state of things, but pursue the records <note>original: "notices"</note> of former ages and desire to comprehend whatever events have occurred since time itself began. We endeavor to compensate for the brevity of our lives through the breadth of our knowledge; and because we cannot look forward to see what lies concealed in the womb of the future, we look back and eagerly trace the footsteps of the times that came before us. Indeed, to be ignorant of what happened before <margin>*In the Orator, page 268.* <note>original: "In Oratore pag. 268."</note></margin> we ourselves were born is (as Cicero truly observes) to be forever like children, and to deprive ourselves of what would simultaneously provide our minds with the highest pleasure and grant us the greatest authority and advantage. The knowledge of antiquity, besides the fact that it satisfies one of our noblest curiosities, improves our minds with the wisdom of preceding ages, acquaints us with the most remarkable occurrences of Divine Providence, and presents us with the most suitable and appropriate rules and examples that may shape us for a life of true philosophy and virtue. History (says <margin>*According to Dionysius of Halicarnassus, On the Examination of Orators, p. 65, Vol. 2.* <note>original: "Ap. Dion Halic. Περὶ λόγων ἐξετ. p. 65. Tom. 2."</note></margin> Thucydides) is nothing other than *philosophy drawn from examples* <note>original: "Φιλοσοφία ἐκ παραδειγμάτων"</note>; the one is a more coarse <note>original: "gross"</note> and popular philosophy, the other a more subtle and refined history.

These considerations, together with a desire to preserve the memory of courageous and great actions, gave birth to history and compelled mankind to record the more noteworthy events of both their own and preceding times for the benefit of posterity. The first of this kind was Moses, the great prince

<summary>William Cave argues that the human soul's innate desire for historical knowledge proves its spiritual nature, noting that studying antiquity provides the wisdom and examples necessary for a virtuous life.</summary>
<keywords>human soul, omniscience, Cicero, Thucydides, philosophy of history, Divine Providence, Moses, antiquity</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from the previous page, completing the word "virtue" and identifying Moses as the primary historian and legislator of the Jews. It surveys the historical traditions of ancient civilizations before pivoting to the supreme importance of Church history.</meta>

### To the READER.

...tue. And <term>Legislator</term> <note>Referring to Moses</note> of the Jewish nation, who from the creation of the world passed down the records of more than 2,550 <note>original: "MMDL"</note> years; this same practice was more or less continued through all the periods of the Jewish state. Among the Babylonians, they had their public archives, which were transcribed by Berosus, the priest of Belus, who composed the *Chaldean History*.

The Egyptians used to record their memorable acts on pillars in hieroglyphic notes and sacred characters, first begun (as they claim) by Thoth, or the first of their "Mercuries" <note>The Egyptians associated the god Thoth with the Greek god Hermes, whom the Romans called Mercury.</note>. From these, Manetho, their chief priest, collected his *Three Books of Egyptian Dynasties*, which he dedicated to Ptolemy Philadelphus, the second king of that line. The Phoenician history was first attempted by Sanchuniathon; it was compiled partly from the annals of cities and partly from the books kept in the temple, which were shared with him by Jerombaal, priest of the god Jao <note>This likely refers to a Phoenician version of the Hebrew name for God, Yahweh.</note>. He dedicated this work to Abibalus, King of Berytus, which Philo of Byblos translated into Greek around the time of the Emperor Hadrian.

The Greeks boast of the antiquity of Cadmus, Archilochus, and many others, though the most ancient of their historians still surviving are *Herodotus*, *Thucydides*, and *Xenophon*. Among the Romans, the foundations of history were laid in annals. The public acts of every year were compiled by the *Pontifex Maximus* <term>Chief Priest</term>, who kept them at his own house so the people could consult them for information in any emergency. These were the *Annales Maximi* <note>original: "Annales Maximi"; literally "Greatest Annals"</note>, and they provided excellent materials for those who later wrote the history of that great and powerful commonwealth.

But the history that demands the greatest respect—both because it concerns our present investigation more directly and because it contains accounts of things related to our greatest interests—is the history of the Church. For in this history, as if in a mirror <note>original: "Glass"</note>, we see the true face of the Church represented throughout its various ages. Here we find the infinite care with which those divine records—the great instruments of our eternal happiness—have been passed down to us through several periods of time. We see with what mighty success religion has triumphed over the greatest opposition and spread its banners to the furthest corners of the world. We see the incomparable zeal with which good men have fought earnestly for that faith which was once delivered to the saints. We see the bitter and relentless fury with which the enemies of religion have attacked it, and how clearly Divine Providence has appeared in...

<summary>Cave surveys the historiographical traditions of the ancient world—including the Jews, Babylonians, Egyptians, and Romans—before arguing that the history of the Christian Church is the most significant of all for its role in preserving divine truth and demonstrating God's providence.</summary>
<keywords>historiography, Moses, Berosus, Manetho, Sanchuniathon, Philo of Byblos, Roman Annals, Church History, Divine Providence, antiquity</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the preservation of historical records, transitioning from the secular annals of Rome to the divine history of the Christian Church and its first chroniclers, specifically Hegesippus.</meta>

->To the READER.<-

<margin>*Ecclesiastical History*, book 3, chapter 24, page 94.</margin>
<margin>Codex 232, column 893 <note>This refers to the *Bibliotheca* of Photius.</note>.</margin>
...its preservation, and turned the harm back upon their own heads. Here we see the constant succession of bishops and the ministers of religion in their several ranks: the *glorious company of the Apostles*, the *goodly fellowship of the Prophets*, and the *noble army of Martyrs*, who went to heaven through the most painful tortures with the most cheerful and calm minds. In short, we have here the most admirable examples of a divine and religious life, of a real and sincere piety, a sincere and universal charity, and a strict temperance and sobriety—all clearly represented to us as an unconquerable patience and submission. 

The further back we go in time, the more illustrious are the instances of piety and virtue. For however much later ages may have improved in knowledge—as experience daily adds to the arts and sciences—former times were most eminent for the practice and virtues of a holy life. The *Divine Laws*, while newly published, had a stronger influence upon the minds of men, and the spirit of religion was more active and vigorous. This continued until men gradually began to be corrupted by the impiety and profaneness that has overrun the world in these last times.

It would be altogether unnecessary and improper for me to consider what records exist of the state of the Church before our Savior's Incarnation; it is sufficient for my purpose to inquire by what hands the first affairs of the Christian Church have been passed down to us. As for the life and death, the actions and miracles of our Savior, and some of the first acts of his Apostles, they are fully described by the Gospel historians <note>original: "Evangelical Historians," referring to the authors of the four Gospels.</note>. 

Indeed, immediately after them we find nothing of this nature, for the Apostles and their immediate successors—as Eusebius observes—did not have the leisure to write many books, as they were employed in ministries that were greater and more immediately useful to the world. The first person to engage in this type of writing was Hegesippus, an ancient and apostolic man (as he is styled in Photius <note>original: "Pbotinus." This is a typo in the original text for the 9th-century scholar Photius.</note>). He was Hebrew by descent and likely born in Palestine. He flourished principally during the reign of Marcus Aurelius and came to Rome in the time of Anicetus, where he resided until the time of Eleutherius. He wrote five books of *Ecclesiastical History*, which he titled *Commentaries of the Acts of the Church*. In these, written in a plain and familiar style, he described the Apostles' travels and preachings, the remarkable events of the Church, and the various schisms, heresies, and persecutions that troubled it from the time of our Lord's death until his own day.

<summary>This page discusses the moral and historical importance of Church history, highlighting the virtues of early martyrs and introducing Hegesippus as the first historian to document the Church after the Apostles.</summary>
<keywords>Church History, Hegesippus, Eusebius, Martyrs, Apostles, Marcus Aurelius, Photius, Early Christian Virtues, Historiography</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of early Christian historians, moving from the lost works of Hegesippus to the life and monumental work of Eusebius of Caesarea, the "Father of Church History."</meta>

## To the READER.

<margin>Book 1, chapter 3, page 3.</margin>
time. But these, alas, have long since been lost. The next to succeed in this field <note>original: "Province"; here meaning a branch of study or responsibility</note>, though the first who reduced it to any degree of exactness and perfection, was *Eusebius*. He was born in *Palestine* during the later years of the Emperor *Gallienus* and was ordained as a priest by *Agapius*, the Bishop of *Caesarea*. When Agapius suffered martyrdom near the end of the *Diocletian Persecution*, *Eusebius* succeeded him in his see <note>The jurisdiction or office of a bishop</note>. He was a man of incomparable talent and learning, and of no less industry and diligence in searching out the records and antiquities of the Church. After writing several other volumes in defense of the Christian cause against the assaults of both *Jews* and *Gentiles*, he set himself to write an *Ecclesiastical History*. 

In this work, he intended (as he himself tells us) to recount the most memorable transactions of the Church from the birth of our Lord until his own time. This included the apostolic successions, the first preachers and planters of the Gospel, the bishops who presided in the most eminent sees, the most notable errors and heresies, the calamities that befell the *Jewish State*, the attempts and persecutions made against the Christians by the powers of the world, the torments and sufferings of the martyrs, and the blessed and happy period that was brought about by the conversion of *Constantine the Great*. Accordingly, he organized all of this into *Ten Books*, which he composed in the later part of his life. 

<margin>Preface on the Life and Writings of Eusebius <note>original Latin: "Præfat. de Vit. & Script. Euſeb."</note></margin>
As *Valesius* <note>Henri Valois (1603–1676), a French scholar who produced a famous edition of Eusebius’s work</note> conjectures, this was some years after the Council of *Nicea*. However, since Eusebius explicitly affirms not long before that this history was written before the *Nicene Synod*, I cannot imagine how he can be excused from a clear contradiction in this matter. It is true that *Eusebius* takes no notice of that Council, but that might be partly because he intended to end with that joyful and prosperous scene of things which *Constantine* restored to the Church (as he himself clearly suggests at the beginning of his history). He was perhaps unwilling to disturb that mood with the controversies and contentions of that Synod, following the habit of all historians who delight in ending their histories with some happy and successful period. Additionally, he intended to give an account of the affairs of that Council in his book on the *Life of Constantine the Great*.

The materials with which he was furnished for this great undertaking (which he complained were very small and insignificant) were, in addition to *Hegesippus’s* commentaries then still in existence, *Africanus’s* *Chronology*, the books and writings of several Fathers, the records of particular cities, and ecclesiastical letters...

<summary>This page introduces Eusebius of Caesarea, the author of the first comprehensive "Ecclesiastical History," detailing his background, the scope of his work, and the historical sources he used.</summary>
<keywords>Eusebius of Caesarea, Ecclesiastical History, Constantine the Great, Council of Nicea, Church records, Hegesippus, Africanus, historiography</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Eusebius's sources, focusing on the "Acts of the Martyrs" and later efforts by Simeon Metaphrastes to compile the lives of the saints.</meta>

->To the READER.<-

Epistles <note>original: "Epistles"; formal letters, often containing theological instruction or official records</note> written by the Bishops of those times and kept in the archives of their several churches—especially that famous library at Jerusalem established by Alexander, Bishop of that place—but chiefly the *Acts of the Martyrs*, which in those times were recorded in detail with great care and accuracy. Eusebius collected these, or at least a great many of them, into one volume under the title *A Collection of the Ancient Martyrdoms* <note>original Greek: 'Αρχαίων Μαρτυρίων Συναγωγὴ, *Archaion Martyrion Synagoge*</note>. He refers to this work frequently. He also wrote a particular narrative (still extant as an appendix to the *Eighth Book of his Ecclesiastical History*) concerning the martyrs who suffered in Palestine.

A great part of these *Acts*, through the negligence and unfaithfulness of succeeding times, were altered <note>original: "interpolated"; to insert or change text, often misleadingly</note> and corrupted. This happened especially in the darker and more uncritical ages when superstition had overspread the Church, and when ignorance and self-interest conspired to fill the world with idle and improbable stories. During those times, men took whatever liberty they pleased in publishing the inventions of their own minds. Consequently, some of the more wise and moderate even of the Roman Catholic Church <note>original: "Roman Communion"</note> have complained with a just sense of indignation that Diogenes Laertius wrote his *Lives of the Philosophers* with more truth and honesty <note>original: "chaſtneſs"</note> than many have written the *Lives of the Saints*.

Because of this, a great and general outcry has been made against Simeon Metaphrastes <note>A 10th-century Byzantine official and author famous for his "Menologion," a collection of saints' lives</note> as the "father of incredible legends" and as one who has notoriously deceived the world with the most mythical reports. Some, to disgrace him even more, have represented him as an insignificant schoolmaster. This is a charge which is, in my opinion, rash, inconsiderate, and in large measure groundless and uncharitable. He was a person of very significant birth and fortune, advanced to the highest honors and offices—one of the prime ministers of state and, in all probability, the Great Chancellor to the Emperor of Constantinople. He was learned and eloquent far beyond the common standard of his day. 

Influenced not only by the persuasion of some great figures of that time (he was active under Leo the Wise around the year 900, but wrote principally under the reign of his successor), but by the Emperor himself, he was convinced to organize the *Lives of the Saints*. To this end, through his own immense labor and the equal expense of the Emperor, he searched the libraries of the Empire until he had gathered a massive collection of volumes. He passed over the more ancient *Acts* without any significant alteration, other than correcting...

<summary>The author discusses the primary sources Eusebius used for his history, such as the "Acts of the Martyrs," and defends the 10th-century compiler Simeon Metaphrastes against claims that he fabricated legendary stories about saints.</summary>
<keywords>Eusebius, Acts of the Martyrs, Simeon Metaphrastes, Church History, Leo the Wise, hagiography, Jerusalem Library, historical accuracy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the defense of Simeon Metaphrastes, a 10th-century Byzantine author, before shifting focus to the importance of Eusebius’s Ecclesiastical History and the later work of the Magdeburg Centuriators.</meta>

# To the READER.

...correcting them by comparing several copies and expanding on some details to make them more plain and easy to understand, as is evident by comparing his versions with some that are still available today. Where the *Lives of the Saints* were confused and lacked method, or were written in a style that was unrefined and crude, he organized the history into order and expressed it in more refined and elegant language. Others that were lacking in neither area, he left as they were and included them among his own works. 

So, I see no reason for such a harsh criticism <note>original: "censure"</note>, unless it were proven that he did not take his accounts from the writings of those who came before him, but instead forged them from his own head. This is not even to mention that things have been made much worse by translations—which rarely appear in any form but the style of the Latin Church <note>original: "dress of the Latine Church"; referring to the Roman Catholic Church's influence on translations</note>—and that many *Lives* are attributed to him of which he was never the author. It has become common for some, when they encounter a *Life* of a Saint whose author they do not know, to immediately credit it to Metaphrastes. But to return to Eusebius, from whom we have wandered.

His *Ecclesiastical History*—almost the only remaining records of the ancient Church—deserves a just esteem and respect. Without it, those very fragments of antiquity would have been lost, having escaped the general shipwreck of history by this means. Indeed, St. Jerome <note>original: "S. Hierom"</note>, Nicephorus, and the rest not only build upon his foundation but derive almost all of their material from him. As for Socrates, Sozomen, Theodoret, and the later historians, they relate to times outside the scope of my current work; they generally pass down little more than the history of their own times, the Church history of those earlier ages being either completely neglected or very carelessly handled. 

The first who effectively broke the ice after the Reformation were the *Magdeburg Centuriators* <term>A group of Lutheran scholars in the 16th century who wrote the first comprehensive history of the Christian Church from a Protestant perspective, organized by century.</term>, a group of learned and hardworking men. The most prominent among them were Johannes Wigandus, Matthäus Judex, Basilius Faber, Andreas Corvinus, and especially Matthias Flaccius Illyricus, who was the very soul of the project. They committed themselves to thoroughly examining the writings of the Church Fathers and all the ancient monuments of the Church, collecting whatever suited their purpose. With tireless effort, they organized this material into an *Ecclesiastical History*. They divided this work into *Centuries*, and each *Century* into fifteen chapters. Into each of these chapters, as if into its proper category <note>original: "Classis"</note> and storage place, they placed everything concerning the spread of religion...

<summary>The author defends the editorial work of Simeon Metaphrastes and emphasizes that Eusebius's history is the essential foundation for all ancient church records, noting that the Magdeburg Centuriators were the first to systematically compile such history after the Reformation.</summary>

<keywords>Simeon Metaphrastes, Eusebius, Ecclesiastical History, St. Jerome, Magdeburg Centuriators, Matthias Flaccius Illyricus, Church Fathers, historiography</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of church historiography, moving from Eusebius to the work of the Magdeburg Centuriators and the subsequent Roman Catholic response by Caesar Baronius.</meta>

# To the READER.

...religion, the peace or persecutions of the Christians, the doctrines of the Church, and the heresies that arose within it. It also covers the rites and ceremonies, the government, schisms, councils, bishops, and individuals noted for either religion or learning, as well as heretics, martyrs, miracles, the state of the Jews, the religion of those who were "without" <note>Referring to non-Christians or those outside the Church.</note>, and the political revolutions of that age. This method is accurate and useful, providing a very clear and detailed understanding of the affairs of the Church. The first four centuries of this history were finished in the city of Magdeburg, and the rest elsewhere. It was a work of immense diligence and singular usefulness. It is true that it suffers from some faults and imperfections and is responsible for considerable errors and mistakes. This is no wonder: for besides the fact that the authors themselves may have been occasionally betrayed into an immoderate reaction <note>original Greek: *ἀμετρία τῆς ἀνθολκῆς* (amétria tēs antholkēs); meaning an excessive or unbalanced opposition in an argument</note> by the heated contentions of those times, it was the first attempt of its kind. Furthermore, it never underwent the corrections of a second review; it was a vast and sprawling undertaking, started while books were still scarce and less accurate.

<margin>Preface to the *Ecclesiastical History* prefixed to Century 1.</margin> 
Accordingly, the authors modestly enough confess that they attempted a sketch of Church history rather than one that was complete and absolute. They desired only to provide an opportunity for those who were able and willing to produce one more entire and perfect. And yet, even with all the faults and disadvantages that can be charged against it, these bear no proportion to the usefulness and excellency of the work itself.

As soon as this work was published, it caused a great stir in Rome, as it sufficiently exposed the corruptions and innovations of that Church to the world. Consequently, it was necessary that an antidote should be provided against it. For this purpose, Philip Neri <note>original: "Philip Nereus"</note>—who had lately founded the Oratorian Order at Rome—commanded Caesar Baronius to undertake the task. Baronius was then a very young man who had newly entered the congregation. In order to prepare him, Neri ordered him to give daily lectures on nothing but ecclesiastical history in the Oratory. He maintained this course for thirty years, going over the history of the Church seven different times. Thus trained and abundantly furnished with fit materials, he set upon the work itself, which he organized in the form of *Annals* <note>A chronological record of events year by year.</note>, comprising the affairs of the entire Christian world in the orderly series and succession of every year. This method was much more natural

<summary>This page describes the "Magdeburg Centuries," the first major Protestant church history, noting its ambitious scope despite its errors, and introduces Caesar Baronius's Catholic response, the *Annales Ecclesiastici*.</summary>

<keywords>Magdeburg Centuriators, Caesar Baronius, Philip Neri, Church History, Oratorian Order, Annals, Protestant Reformation, Counter-Reformation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the critique of church historiography. After discussing the Protestant "Magdeburg Centuries" on the previous page, the author now introduces Caesar Baronius’s "Ecclesiastical Annals," the monumental Catholic response to the Magdeburg work. He praises Baronius's immense labor but critiques his strong pro-Roman bias.</meta>

->To the READER.<-

...natural and historical than that of the Centuries <note>The "Centuries" refers to the *Magdeburg Centuries*, the first comprehensive Protestant history of the Church, organized by century.</note>. It was a noble design, and it would be an injustice to deny it the praise and commendation it deserves. In this work, besides recording the events concerning the state of the Church—organized under their proper time periods as far as his skill in chronology allowed—he brought to light many passages from ancient writers that were previously unknown. He was uniquely advantaged in this by access to the many noble libraries in Rome. The work is a monument of incredible effort and labor; despite the inherent difficulties of the project, he carried it out entirely by his own efforts, writing everything by hand. This was done in the midst of infinite distractions: the duties of a parish priest, the private affairs of his own Oratory <note>The Congregation of the Oratory, a religious society founded by St. Philip Neri, which Baronius joined.</note>, preaching, hearing confessions, and writing other books—not to mention the many troublesome, though honorable, offices and positions that were heaped upon him during the course of the work.

In short, it is a work for which he would have done the world a much greater service than can be expressed, had he handled it with as much faithfulness and impartiality as he did with learning and industry. But alas, it is all too evident that he intended to advance the honor and interests of a cause rather than the truth itself. Therefore, he portrayed the ancient Church not as antiquity truly represents it, but according to the current form and character of the Church of Rome. He forced everything to fit that perspective in order to justify the traditions and practices of Rome and to exalt the supreme power and grandeur of that Church, making both the Scepter <note>symbol of secular/royal power</note> and the Crosier <note>symbol of a bishop's authority</note> bow to the Triple Crown <note>The Papal Tiara, representing the Pope's authority.</note>. This bias runs through almost every page.

<margin>* Dedicatory Epistle to Pope Sixtus V, prefixed to Volume 1 of the *Annals*.
† Girolamo Barnabeo, *Life of Baronius*, Book 1, Chapter 18, pages 40 and 43.</margin>

Indeed, both he `*`himself and the `†` writer of his life expressly affirm more than once that his design was to defend the traditions and preserve the dignity of the Roman Church against the "late Innovators" <note>A common 17th-century term for Protestant reformers.</note> and the labors of the *Magdeburg Centuriators*. In fact, opposing them was the primary occasion for his work. It is a fatal reality that partiality and the interests of a cause so often spoil the most brave and generous undertakings.

I hope the reader will not judge what I have said so far as an unprofitable digression, nor think it altogether unsuitable to the present work, of which he likely expects some short account. Having been engaged some time ago—I know not how—in searching after the antiquities of the Apostolic Age <note>The period during the lives of the Twelve Apostles, roughly 33–100 AD.</note>, I was then strongly urged to carry on the design for some of the succeeding ages. This I then wholly...

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the author’s critique of other historians, such as Baronius, to a personal reflection on his own work. He explains his initial hesitation to write about the first three hundred years of the Church due to the scarcity of records, before ultimately deciding to proceed out of a desire to honor the early martyrs and scholars.</meta>

->To the READER.<-

I had completely set [this project] aside, without any further thoughts of resuming it. For experience had made me sufficiently aware of the difficulty of the task, and I well foresaw how almost impossible it was to manage it with any tolerable satisfaction; the accounts left to us of those early times are so small and insignificant, so broken and imperfect. Despite this, I have once more allowed myself to be engaged in it, and have endeavored to hunt out and gather together those ruins of the <term>Primitive Story</term> <note>The "Primitive Story" refers to the history of the early or original Christian Church.</note> that still remain. I have done this so that I might honor, as best as I was able, the memory of those brave and worthy men who were so instrumental in establishing <term>Christianity</term> in the world, sealing it with their blood, and benefiting future generations by those excellent <term>Monuments of Learning and Piety</term> <note>original: "Monuments of Learning and Piety"; here meaning works of scholarship and devotion.</note> which they left behind.

I have limited my account to the first three hundred years, despite the barrenness and obscurity of those ages of the Church. Had I consulted my own ease or reputation, I would have begun my project from the time that marks the end of my current undertaking—namely, the following century <term>Sæculum</term> <note>Latin: *saeculum*, meaning a century or age.</note>—when Christianity became the religion of the Empire, and the records of the Church furnished us with large and plentiful materials for such a work. But I confess my temperament and interest led me to the first and best ages of religion, the records <term>Memoires</term> <note>original: *Memoires*; historical records or accounts.</note> of which I have collected. By doing so, I have enabled myself to sketch the portraits of as many of those Apostolic figures <term>Apostolical persons</term> as I could recover any significant information or accounts of.

With what success I have done this, the reader must judge. I do not know what kind of reception it will find, nor am I very anxious about it. I have done what I could, and I am not aware that I have been lacking in any point of either faithfulness or care. If there are fewer people described here than a span of almost three hundred years might seem to promise, and less said concerning some of them than the reader expects, I assume he will be fair and charitable enough not to blame me. Instead, he should attribute it to the unhappy fate of so many ancient records that have been lost through the carelessness and unfaithfulness of succeeding times. As far as my modest abilities reach, and the nature of the subject allows, I have endeavored to satisfy the reader. Although I do not pretend to present an exact Church History <term>Church-History</term> of those times, I think I may say without vanity that there is scarcely any significant event in Church Antiquity <term>Church-Antiquity</term> of which, in some of these...

<summary>The author explains his decision to write a history of the first three centuries of the Church, acknowledging the difficulty caused by lost records but expressing a desire to honor the early martyrs and Apostolic figures.</summary>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page concludes the author's preface. He discusses the specific challenges of establishing an accurate chronology for the first three centuries of the Church, critiquing the sources available (primarily Eusebius) while introducing his own chronological tables. He ends with a list of corrections for the printed text.</meta>

->To the READER.<-

In these lives, the reader will not find a sufficient or reasonable account. Nor is the history of those ages only crippled and damaged in its main parts, but—what is greatly to be lamented—it is also shortsighted and defective in its "eyes"; by which I mean it is confused and uncertain regarding chronology. The greatest part of what we have comes from *Eusebius*, in whose account of time some things are false, more are uncertain, and the whole has suffered from passing through the hands of others after him. 

Indeed, next to recovering the lost portions of antiquity, I know of nothing that would be more welcome than setting right the disjointed structure of those times—a cure we hope for shortly from a very capable person <note>The author likely refers to a contemporary scholar working on chronological studies, such as Henry Dodwell or William Lloyd.</note>. In the meantime, for my own part, and so far as it may be useful for the purposes of these following papers, I have—using the best methods I could in some haste—drawn up a chronology of these first three centuries. Though it does not claim the absolute exactness and accuracy required for a matter of this nature, it will nonetheless serve to provide a quick and immediate overview of things, showing the connection and overlap of church affairs with the history of the *Roman Empire*. 

Where I follow *Eusebius*, I rely primarily on the accounts given in his *History*. Since this was written after his *Chronicle* <note>original: *Chronicon*</note>, it may be assumed to be the result of his more precise research and to have undergone the judgment of his more mature and thoughtful reflections. Perhaps the reader will say (and I confess I am somewhat of the same mind) that if I had applied the same standard of careful reflection to these papers, he would never have been troubled with them. But it is too late now to retract them, and it is foolish to lament what is impossible to fix.

---

These books as the source of "Enthusiasm" <note>in this context, a claim of direct, private divine inspiration</note> and mystical theology. A passage from them cited as an example. Page 65.*

## THE INTRODUCTION.

The various periods of the first three ages. Our Lord’s coming and the timeliness of it for the spreading of the Gospel. His entry into his prophetic office and the summary of his ministry. The success of his doctrine and the various places where he preached. The numbers and status of its converts. Its speedy and incredible success in all countries, as noted by the writers of those times. The early conversion of Britain to Christianity. The general decline of paganism. The silence and ending of their oracles. This was acknowledged by **Porphyry** <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher and prominent opponent of early Christianity</note> to be the effect of the Christian religion appearing in the world. This is a great argument for its truth and divinity. The reasons contributing to the success of Christianity. The miraculous powers then present in the Church. The most eminent of the Christian **Apologists** <note>Writers who composed formal defenses of the Christian faith</note>. The admirable lives of the ancient Christians. The singular effectiveness of the Christian doctrine upon the minds of men. A holy life is the most acceptable sacrifice. Their incomparable patience and constancy under sufferings.

His *burning of Rome* and the dreadfulness of that Great Fire. This was blamed upon the Christians, and their various kinds of punishment are noted by **Tacitus**. The excellent temperament and learning of **Marcus Aurelius**. The fifth persecution was raised by him. Its fierceness in the East, at Rome, and especially in France; the most eminent who suffered there. *The Emperor’s victory in his German wars was gained by the Christians' prayers.* **Severus’s** temperament: his cruelty toward the Christians. # The INTRODUCTION.

*Persecution. Maximinus's immoderate ambition and barbarous cruelty. The author of the seventh persecution. This was not universal. The common evils and calamities blamed upon the Christians. Decius, the eighth persecutor; otherwise an excellent Prince. The violence of this persecution and the most noted sufferers. The foundations of monasticism <term>Monachism</term> when they were laid. The ninth persecution, and its rage under Valerian. The most eminent martyrs. The severe punishment of Valerian: his miserable treatment by the Persian King. The tenth persecution begun under Diocletian, and why. The fierceness and cruelty of that time. The admirable conduct <term>carriage</term> and determination of the Christians under all these sufferings. The proper influence of this argument to convince the world. The whole concluded with Lactantius's excellent reasoning for this purpose.*

### I.

The state of the Christian Church in its first three ages may be considered in three phases: as it was first planted and established by our Lord himself during his residence in the world; as it was enlarged and propagated by the Apostles and first missionaries of the Christian faith; and as it grew up and prospered from the Apostolic Age until the times of Constantine <note>original: "Const-tianity"; likely a typographical error in the OCR for "Constantine" or "Christianity's triumph"</note>. God, who in former times was pleased by various methods of revelation to convey his will to mankind, *has in these last days spoken to us by his Son*. For the great blessing of the promised Seed, after a long succession of several ages, having come to its full maturity and perfection, God was resolved to perform the mercy promised to the Fathers and to remember his holy Covenant—the oath which he swore to our *Father Abraham*.

Accordingly, *in the fullness of time, God sent his Son*. It was in the declining part of *Augustus's* reign when this great ambassador arrived
<margin>² Origen, *Against Celsus*, book 2, p. 79.</margin>
from heaven to publish to the world the glad tidings of salvation. The Roman Empire had now reached its peak, with all its parts united under a monarchical government and a universal peace spread over all the provinces of the empire. Because of this, a smoother and speedier path was prepared for publishing the doctrine of the Gospel, which the Apostles and first preachers of religion might carry to all quarters of the world with greater ease and security.

As for the *Jews*, their minds were awakened around this time with busy expectations of their *Messiah's* coming. No sooner was the birth of the holy *Jesus* proclaimed by the arrival of the Eastern *Magi*, who came to pay homage to him, but *Jerusalem* was filled with noise and turmoil. The *Sanhedrin* <term>the supreme judicial and religious council of the Jews</term> was convened and consulted by *Herod*, who, jealous of his recently acquired sovereignty, was resolved to remove this new competitor. Deluded in his hopes of discovery by the *Magi*, he resorted to acts of open force and cruelty, commanding all infants under two years old to be put to death—and among them, it seems, even his own son. This made *Augustus* say jokingly (alluding to the Jewish custom of abstaining from swine's flesh), "*It is better to be Herod's pig than his son.*" <note>original: "Melius est Herodis porcum esse quam filium"; Macrobius, Saturnalia, 2.4</note>
<margin>³ Macrobius, *Saturnalia*, book 2, chapter 4, p. 279.</margin>
But the providence of God secured the holy infant by timely warning his parents to re-

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Christ's early life, specifically his return from Egypt, his private years as a carpenter, and the commencement of his public ministry through his baptism and the choosing of the Apostles.</meta>

...[re]tire into Egypt, where they remained until the death of Herod. Since this happened not long after, they returned.

II. For nearly thirty years, our Lord remained obscure in the privacy of a quiet life, committing himself (as the ancient writers tell us, and as the Gospel history plainly suggests) to Joseph’s employment, the trade of a **carpenter**. In doing so, he gave no support to an idle or unproductive way of life. But eventually, he was called out of his obscurity and solitude and publicly recognized as the person whom God had sent to be the great Prophet of his Church. This took place at his baptism, when the Holy Spirit <note>original: "Holy Ghost"</note> descended upon him in a visible shape, and God testified of him with an audible voice, saying, *This is my beloved Son, in whom I am well-pleased.* 

Accordingly, he began to declare the plans of God, *going throughout all Galilee, teaching in their synagogues, and preaching the Gospel of the Kingdom.* He specifically explained the Moral Law and restored it to its rightful authority and dominion over the minds of men, rescuing it from the corrupt and perverse interpretations that the masters of the Jewish Church had placed upon it. He next introduced the end <note>original: "abrogation"</note> of the Mosaic system <note>original: "Oeconomy"</note>, which he was sent to bring to a close in order to expand the boundaries of salvation and admit both Jew and Gentile to the terms of mercy. He showed that he came as a mediator between God and man to reconcile the world to the favor of Heaven through his death and sufferings, and to offer the pardon of sin and eternal life to all who were willing to accept it through a sincere belief, heartfelt repentance, and a holy life. 

This was the core of the doctrine which he preached everywhere as opportunity and occasion allowed. He did not impose this upon the world merely on the basis of his own authority and power, nor did he seek an uncertain reception for it. He did not tell men they must believe him simply because he said he came from God and had his warrant and commission to instruct and reform the world. Instead, he gave them the most satisfying and convincing evidence by performing miracles that were beyond all the powers and designs of either art or nature. By these, he unanswerably proved that *he was a teacher come from God, for no man could do the miracles he did unless God were with him.* 

And because he was soon to return to Heaven, he appointed **twelve** men, whom he called **Apostles**, as his immediate delegates and representatives <note>original: "Vicegerents"</note>. He granted them his authority and power, provided them with miraculous gifts, and left them to carry on that excellent religion which he himself had begun. To assist them, he added seventy disciples as regular helpers <note>original: "coadjutors"</note> and companions. For the time being, their commission was limited to Palestine, and they were sent out only to seek and to save the lost sheep of the house of Israel.

<margin>John 12:19</margin>
<margin>* Eusebius, *The Demonstration of the Gospel* <note>original: "Euseb. Demonstrat. Evang."</note>, book 9, page 439.</margin>
III. Just how successful our Savior's ministry was can be guessed from the complaint of the Pharisees: *Look, the world has gone after him!* People from all parts flocked after him in such vast multitudes that they did not even give him time for necessary solitude and rest. Indeed, *he went about doing good, preaching the word throughout all Judea, and healing all who were possessed by the Devil.* 

His usual place of residence was Galilee, living for the most part (as one of the ancient writers* says) in "Galilee of the Gentiles," so that he might sow and reap the first fruits of the calling of the Gentiles there. We usually find him preaching at Nazareth, Cana, Chorazin, and Bethsaida, and the cities around the Sea of Tiberias, but especially at Capernaum, the capital <note>original: "Metropolis"</note> of the province, a

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Christ’s public ministry, detailing his travels throughout the regions surrounding Judea and Samaria, his interaction with various people groups, and the concluding events of his life including the institution of the sacraments, his crucifixion, resurrection, and ascension.</meta>

# The INTRODUCTION.

place of great commerce and traffic. He often visited *Judea*, and the parts about *Jerusalem*, where he was accustomed to go during the Passover celebrations and some of the larger festivals. He did this so that the general gathering of people at those times might provide a better opportunity to "spread the net" and to communicate his doctrine to them. Nor did he—who was to be a Savior for all and came to break down the wall of separation <note>original: "Partition-wall"; a reference to the barrier between Jews and Gentiles</note>—disdain to converse with the *Samaritans*, who were so despised and hated by the *Jews*. In *Sychar*, not far from *Samaria*, he preached freely and won most of the inhabitants of that city to be followers of his doctrine. He traveled up and down the towns and villages of *Caesarea Philippi*, and went into the borders of *Tyre* and *Sidon*, and through the middle of the coasts of *Decapolis*. Where he could not go in person, his fame spread, bringing him disciples and followers from all quarters. Indeed, his fame went throughout all *Syria*, and great multitudes of people followed him from *Galilee, Judea, Decapolis, Idumaea, from beyond the Jordan*, and from *Tyre* and *Sidon*. Indeed, if we 
<margin>a *Ecclesiastical History, book 1, chapter 13, page 31* <note>original: "H. Eccl. l.1.c. 13. P.31"</note></margin>
<insert>a</insert> believe the story so solemnly reported by *Eusebius* and the ancient writers (and except for the silence of the Gospel historians, who recorded only some of the actions and passages concerning our Savior, I know of no logical argument against it), *Abgarus* <note>original: "Abarus"</note>, Prince of *Edessa* beyond the *Euphrates*, having heard of the fame of our Savior’s miracles, humbly pleaded with him by letter to come to him. This letter, together with our Lord’s answer, still exists in the writings of *Eusebius*. There is nothing in the letters themselves that would justly shake their credit or authority. Much more to this purpose was transcribed (as he tells us) out of the records of that city and translated by him from *Syriac* into *Greek*. This may explain why none of the ancient writers before him mention this affair, as they were generally strangers to the language, customs, and antiquities of those Eastern countries.

IV. Our Lord, having spent a little more than three years in the public exercise of his ministry, kept his last Passover with his Apostles. When this was done, he instituted the Sacramental Supper, entrusting it to his Church as the standing memorial of his death and the seal of the Gospel Covenant—just as he appointed Baptism to be the covenant rite <note>original: "Foederal Rite"</note> of initiation and the public token <term>Teſſera</term> <note>original: "Teſſera"; a Latin term for a small tablet used as a token, password, or sign of membership</note> or badge of those who would profess his religion. And now the fatal hour was at hand: being betrayed by the treachery of one of his own Apostles, he was apprehended by the officers and brought before the public tribunals. Heavy crimes were charged against him, but they were as false as they were spiteful. The two main articles of the charge were blasphemy against God and treason against the Emperor. Although they were not able to prove these by any tolerable pretense of evidence, they nevertheless condemned and executed him upon the Cross. Several of the executioners themselves vindicated his innocence, saying that he was a *righteous man and the Son of God.* The third day after his burial, he rose again, appeared to, and conversed with his disciples and followers. Having taken care of the affairs of his *Church* and given a larger commission and fuller instructions to his Apostles, he took his leave of them and visibly ascended into Heaven. There he sat down at the right hand of God as head over all things to the *Church*, with *angels, authorities*, and *powers* being made subject to him.

<meta>This page continues the argument for the historical truth of the Christian religion, moving from internal ecclesiastical evidence to the testimony of pagan historians and Roman records regarding the life and death of Jesus Christ.</meta>

# The INTRODUCTION.

This is justified not only by eyewitnesses, the evidence of miracles, and the consistent and unchallenged agreement of all ages of the Church, but also (regarding the core facts) by the clear confession of pagan <note>original: "Heathen"</note> writers and the enemies of Christianity.

<margin>a Tacitus, *Annals*, book 15, chapter 44, page 319.</margin>
a *Tacitus* tells us that the founder of this religion was *Christ*, who, under the reign of *Tiberius*, was put to death by *Pontius Pilate*, the governor <note>original: "Procurator"</note> of *Judea*. Although this "detestable superstition" was suppressed for a time, it broke out again, spreading itself not only through *Judea*—the source of the mischief—but even into the city of *Rome* itself, where everything wicked and shameful gathers and is eagerly promoted into popularity.

<margin>b Eusebius, *Ecclesiastical History*, book 2, chapter 2, page 40. See also Orosius, *Against the Pagans*, book 7, chapter 4, folio 293.</margin>
<margin>c Tertullian, *Apology*, chapter 5, page 6, and chapter 21, page 20.</margin>
b *Eusebius* assures us that after our Lord’s ascension, *Pilate*, according to custom, sent an account of him to the Emperor. *Tiberius* brought this before the Senate, but they rejected it under the pretext that they had not officially recognized the matter before it reached them. It was a fundamental law of the *Roman* state that no new god could be accepted without a decree from the Senate. Nevertheless, *Tiberius* continued to think well of *Christ* and showed kindness to the *Christians*. For this, he cites the testimony of *Tertullian*, who, in his c *Apology* presented to the *Roman authorities*, affirms that *Tiberius*—during whose reign the *Christian* religion entered the world—having received an account from *Pilate* in *Palestine* (Syria) concerning the truth of the divinity that was there, brought it to the Senate with the weight of his own vote. However, the Senate would not admit it because they had not previously approved it. Even so, the Emperor remained of the same mind and threatened to punish anyone who accused the *Christians*.

<margin>d Justin Martyr, *Second Apology*, page 76.</margin>
And before *Tertullian*, *Justin Martyr* d, speaking concerning the death and sufferings of our Savior, tells the Emperors that they could satisfy themselves regarding the truth of these things from the *Acts* <note>Official government records</note> written under *Pontius Pilate*. It was customary not only at *Rome* to keep the *Acts* of the Senate and the people, but also for the governors of provinces to keep accounts of what memorable things happened in their jurisdictions, which they then transmitted to the Emperor. And this is what *Pilate* did during his governorship of his province.

<margin>e In Epiphanius, *Against Heresies*, L., page 182.</margin>
<margin>f Eusebius, *Ecclesiastical History*, book 9, chapter 5, page 350.</margin>
How long these *Acts* remained in existence, I do not know. But in the controversy about the date of *Easter*, we find the *Quartodecimans* <note>A group of early Christians who celebrated Easter on the 14th day of the Hebrew month of Nisan</note> justifying the day on which they observed it by citing the *Acts of Pilate*, in which they boasted they had found the truth. Whether these were the same *Acts of Pilate* to which *Justin* appealed, or rather those *Acts of Pilate* drawn up and published by the command of f *Maximinus* (*Diocletian’s* successor) to discredit our Lord and his religion, is uncertain—though the latter is far more probable.

<margin>g At the end of the book *On the Destruction of the City of Jerusalem*, page 683.</margin>
However, *Pilate’s* letter to *Tiberius* (or, as he is called there, *Claudius*) which is still in existence today in the *Summary* <note>original: "Anacephalæosis"</note> g of the younger *Hegesippus*, is not very credible. Although that author claims to be older than some allow, he was probably a contemporary of *St. Ambrose*. Indeed, many think—based on the great similarity of style and phrasing—that it was *St. Ambrose* himself who compiled it from *Josephus* with some few additions. But then it must be considered whether that *Summary* was written by the same person or (which is most likely) by a much later hand. Some other specific passages concerning our Savior are noted by pagan <note>original: "Gentile"</note> writers: the appearance of the star by *Calcidius*, the murder of the infants by *Macrobius*, and the eclipse at our Savior's Passion by *Phlegon of Tralles*. I will not dwell on his miracles, which were frequently acknowledged by *Celsus*, *Julian*, and *Porphyry*.

<meta>This page continues the introduction to the history of the early Church, moving from the historical evidence of Christ's life to the immediate actions of the Apostles following his Ascension and the subsequent spread of Christianity throughout the Roman Empire.</meta>

# The INTRODUCTION.

VI. Immediately after our Lord’s Ascension (from which we date the next period of the Church), the Apostles began to execute the powers entrusted to them. They quickly filled the vacancy left by *Judas* through the election of a new Apostle; the lot fell upon *Matthias*, and he was numbered with the eleven Apostles. Soon after, being endowed with power from on high (as our Lord had promised them) and furnished with the miraculous gifts of the Holy Spirit, they set themselves to preach in places of the greatest public gathering and in the faces of their greatest enemies. 

Those who only a short while before had fled at the first approach of danger now boldly pleaded the cause of their crucified Master at the immediate risk of their lives. To ensure that nothing might interrupt them in this work, they instituted the office of Deacons, who could attend to the administrative <note>original: "inferior"</note> services of the Church while the Apostles devoted themselves to what was more immediately necessary for the good of souls. By this prudent course, religion gained ground quickly, and innumerable converts were daily added to the faith. This continued until a persecution arose following the martyrdom of *Saint Stephen*, which banished the Church out of *Jerusalem*. However, this also proved to be an advantage in the end, as Christianity spread all the more quickly through the neighboring countries because of it. 

Despite the rage of the persecution, the Apostles remained in *Jerusalem*, only occasionally sending out a few of their number to confirm and settle new settlements <note>original: "plantations"</note> and to propagate the faith as the needs of the Church required. They continued this for nearly twelve years, as our Lord himself had commanded them not to depart from *Jerusalem* and the surrounding areas until twelve years after his Ascension. This is mentioned by ancient tradition, as both 

<margin>a Cited in *Eusebius*, *Ecclesiastical History*, book 5, chapter 18, page 186.</margin>
*Apollonius* and 

<margin>b *Miscellanies* <note>original: "Stromat[eis]"</note>, book 6, page 636. See also *Life of Saint Peter*, section 2, number 5.</margin>
*Clement of Alexandria* inform us. After this, they thought it was high time to apply themselves to the full execution of the commission Christ had given them: *to go, teach, and baptize all nations*. Accordingly, having settled the general affairs and concerns of the Church, they traveled to the various provinces of the Gentile world, preaching the Gospel to every nation under heaven. In a literal sense, *their sound went into all the earth and their words unto the ends of the world.*

<margin>c *Ecclesiastical History*, book 2, chapter 3, page 41.</margin>
> "Vast multitudes of people in all cities and countries," says c *Eusebius*, "like grain poured into a well-filled storehouse <note>original: "granary"</note>, were brought in by that grace of God that brings salvation. Those whose minds were previously diseased and overrun with the errors and idolatry of their ancestors were cured by the sermons and miracles of our Lord’s disciples. Shaking off those chains of darkness and slavery which the merciless demons had placed upon them, they freely embraced and welcomed the knowledge and service of the only true God, the great Creator of the world. They worshiped Him according to the holy rites and rules of that divine and wisely designed religion which our Savior had introduced into the world."

Regarding the travels of the Apostles, the success of their ministry, the places and countries they visited, the churches they planted, and their acts and martyrdoms for the faith, we have provided an account in a work specifically dedicated to that subject—at least as far as the records of those times have preserved any significant information for us. For now, it is enough to observe that God was pleased to allow *Saint John* to live to a very great age, outliving all the rest, so that he might oversee and cultivate, confirm and establish what they had planted, and serve as a standing...

<meta>This page continues the introduction, describing the development of the early Church as a "living oracle" for settling doubts and confirming the truth against early heresies.</meta>

...a living and lively Oracle, to which they might from all parts have recourse in any significant doubts and emergencies <note>original: "exigences"</note> of the Church, and so that he might seal and attest to the truth of those things which men of corrupt and perverse minds even then began to call into question.

VII. From here we pass on to survey the state of the Church from the Apostolic Age until the times of *Constantine*, a space of at least two hundred years. During this period, we shall principally remark on two things: first, what progress the *Christian Religion* made in the world; and second, what contributed to such a vast growth and increase of it. It must be obvious to any impartial observer that *Christianity*—given the nature of its precepts, the sublimity of its principles, and its opposition to the established rites and religions of the world—was likely to find a poor reception <note>original: "bad entertainment"</note> and the fiercest opposition. This is exactly what happened. It met with every discouragement: secret undermining and open assaults from all the malice, prejudice, intelligence, talent <note>original: "wit and parts"</note>, learning, and power that the world could muster against it. Notwithstanding all of this, it held its head high and prospered under the greatest opposition. 

The triumph of the *Christian Faith* will appear even more significant whether we consider the number and quality of its converts, or the vast distance to which it extended and spread itself. Though it appeared with every possible disadvantage, yet no sooner did it set up its standard than people from all parts, and of every kind of principle and education, began to flock to it. As *Origen* 

<margin>
a *Against Celsus, book 1, pages 21, 22.*

<margin>

*Tertullian*, addressing the *Roman* governors on behalf of the *Christians*, assures them that although they were not an old group, they had already filled all the places of their dominion: their cities, islands, castles, corporations, councils, armies, tribes, companies, the palace, senate, and courts of justice. He argued that if they had a mind to take revenge, they would not need to resort to secret <note>original: "clancular"</note> and hidden tactics; their numbers were large enough to appear in open arms, having a party not just in this or that province, but in all quarters of the world. Indeed, he claimed that even unarmed as they were, they could be sufficiently revenged upon the Empire; for should they all simply agree to retire out of the *Roman* Empire, the world would stand amazed at the solitude and desolation that would follow, and the governors would find they had more enemies than friends or citizens left among them.

...and women, of all ranks and ages, who would readily offer themselves, what fires and swords he must have had to dispatch them. Nor is this anything more than what *Pliny* himself confesses to the Emperor <margin>To Trajan, book 10, Epistle 97.</margin>, that the case of the Christians was a matter worthy of deliberation, especially because of the multitudes involved; for many of each sex, of every age and rank, were and must be called into question. This "superstition" <note>original: "Superstition"; Pliny used the term *superstitio* to describe Christianity disparagingly from a Roman perspective.</note> had infected and overrun not only the city, but towns and the countryside, leaving the temples and sacrifices generally desolate and forsaken.

### VIII. Nor was it only this way in some parts and provinces of the *Roman Empire*, but in most nations and countries.

<margin>Dialogue with Trypho, page 345.</margin>*Justin Martyr* tells the *Jew*, that whatever they might boast of the universality of their religion, there were many places of the world where neither they nor it ever came; whereas there was no part of mankind—whether *Greeks* or *Barbarians*, or by whatever name they were called, even the most rude and unpolished nations—where prayers and thanksgivings were not made to the great Creator of the world through the name of the crucified *Jesus*.

<margin>Book on Fate, in Eusebius’s *Preparation for the Gospel*, book 6, chapter 10, page 279.</margin>The same *Bardesanes* the *Syrian*, *Justin’s* contemporary, affirms that the followers of the Christian institution, though living in different parts of the world and being very numerous in every climate and country, were yet all called by the name of Christians.

<margin>On Justice, book 5, chapter 13, page 194.</margin>So *Lactantius* says: the Christian Law is welcomed from the rising of the sun to its setting, where every sex, age, nation, and country worships God with one heart and soul. If we descend from generalities to particular places and countries, <margin>Against Heresies, book 1, chapter 3, section 52.</margin>*Irenaeus*, who entered upon the See of *Lyons* in the year of Christ 179 <note>original: "Ann.Chr. CLXXIX"</note>, affirms that though there were different languages in the world, the force of tradition (or that doctrine that had been delivered to the Church) was one and the same. He notes that there were churches settled in Germany, Spain, France, in the East, in *Egypt* and *Libya*, as well as in the center of the world. 

<margin>Against the Jews, chapter 7, section 189.</margin>*Tertullian*, who probably wrote no more than twenty years after *Irenaeus*, gives us a larger account. "Their sound," he says, "went through all the earth, and their words to the ends of the world. For in whom but Christ did all nations believe? *Parthians*, *Medes*, *Elamites*, the inhabitants of *Mesopotamia*, *Armenia*, *Phrygia*, and *Cappadocia*, of *Pontus*, *Asia*, and *Pamphylia*, those who dwell in *Egypt*, *Africa*, and beyond *Cyrene*, strangers at Rome, *Jews* at *Jerusalem*, and other nations; as also now the *Getuli* and the *Mauri*, the *Spaniards* and the *Gauls*, yes, and those places of *Britain* which were unapproachable by the *Roman Armies* are yet subdued to Christ. The *Sarmatians* also and the *Dacians*, the *Germans* and the *Scythians*, together with many undiscovered countries, many islands and provinces unknown to us, which he confesses himself unable to count. In all these places," he says, "the name of Christ reigns, as before whom the gates of all cities are set open, and to whom none are shut; before whom gates of brass fly open, and bars of iron are snapped asunder." To which <margin>Book 2, page 123.</margin>*Arnobius* adds the *Indians*, the *Persians*, the *Seres* <note>original: "Sere"; likely referring to the people of Northern China known for silk production.</note>, and all the islands and provinces visited by the rising or setting sun—yes, and *Rome* itself, the empress of all.

### IX. From *Tertullian’s* account, we have a most authentic testimony of how early Christianity stretched itself over this other world, having before his time conquered the most rough and inaccessible parts of Britain...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the spread of Christianity, moving from general accounts of its expansion to the specific history of the faith reaching Britain and the subsequent decline of paganism.</meta>

## The INTRODUCTION.

<margin>a *Letter to the Corinthians*, page 28.</margin>
<margin>b *In the same place*, page 8.</margin>
<margin>c *Commentary on Psalm 116*.</margin>
<margin>d *On the Treatment of Greek Maladies, Discourse 9, page 125*.</margin>
...pertain to the banner of the Cross. This likely refers to the conversion of King Lucius, the first Christian king in history and a powerful prince in this island. He embraced the Christian religion around the year 186 AD and sent a formal embassy to Eleutherius, the Bishop of Rome, requesting teachers who could further instruct him and his people in the faith. In response, Eleutherius dispatched Faganus and Derwianus to Britain for that purpose. 

This was not, however, the first time the Gospel had made its way across the *unpassable ocean* <note>original: "ὠκεανὸς ἀπέραντος" (okeanos aperantos)</note>—as St. Clement calls the British Ocean, a term the ancients used constantly—and reached those worlds that lie beyond it, namely the British Islands. The faith had been present here many years earlier, though it was likely suppressed and overshadowed by ancient paganism and idolatry. St. Clement tells us that St. Paul preached in both the East and the West. Having instructed the whole world in righteousness, Paul traveled to the furthest reaches of the West; by this, Clement must mean either Spain or, more probably, Britain—and perhaps both. 

Accordingly, when Theodoret speaks of Paul coming into Spain, he says that the apostle also brought great benefits to the "Isles of the Sea." He counts the Cimbri <note>An ancient Germanic or Celtic people.</note> and the Britons among the nations converted to the Christian faith by the Apostles (specifically mentioning the "tent-maker" <note>original: "Tent-maker"; a reference to the Apostle Paul, who practiced this trade to support his ministry.</note>). If it were necessary to go into even more detail, I could investigate not only which countries, but which specific towns and cities Christianity established itself in, where <term>Episcopal Sees</term> <note>The official seats or jurisdictions of bishops.</note> were founded, and which successions of bishops are mentioned in church records. However, such a study would not fit the intended brevity of this introduction and would perhaps test the reader's patience.

### X. 
The shadows of night vanish no more naturally at the rising of the sun than the darkness of pagan idolatry and superstition fled before the light of the Gospel. The more the Gospel prevailed, the more clearly it revealed the folly and wickedness of pagan worship. Their solemn rites appeared increasingly trivial and ridiculous, and their sacrifices appeared more barbaric and inhumane. Their demons were driven out by even the lowliest Christian, their oracles became silent, and their very priests began to be ashamed of their magic charms and incantations. Even the most clever and subtle defenders of paganism, who tried to protect the rites and ceremonies of their religion, were forced to reinterpret them as having mystical or symbolic meanings—meanings far removed from what the common people actually understood or intended. 

The truth is that the Devil, who for so many ages had claimed an empire and tyranny over human souls, became more aware every day that his kingdom was shaking. He sought in vain to support and prop it up by any means possible. Indeed, even some time before the birth of our Savior, the most famous oracle at Delphi had lost its reputation, and after his appearance in the world, such oracles declined further every day. Their own best writers complained universally that their gods had forsaken their temples and oracular shrines, leaving the world in darkness and obscurity, and that their worshippers sought their counsel and answers in vain. Plutarch, who lived during the reign of the Emperor Trajan, wrote a specific treatise (which still exists) concerning the "Cessation of Oracles." He attempted to explain this decline through natural, moral, and political causes, though all his philosophy was insufficient to give a truly satisfactory account of it.

<summary>This page discusses the early arrival of Christianity in Britain, specifically the conversion of King Lucius and the possible travels of St. Paul, while noting how the Gospel's rise led to the collapse of pagan oracles and rituals.</summary>

# The INTRODUCTION.

<margin>
*On the Obsolescence of Oracles*, page 419. <note>original: "Περὶ τῶν ἐκλελοιπ. χρηστηρ." (Peri tōn ekleloip. chrēstēr.) by Plutarch.</note>
</margin>
One cause he <note>Plutarch, the Greek historian.</note> assigns for this decline is the death and departure of those *demons* <note>original: "Dæmons"; in this context, these are the spirits or lesser deities believed to inhabit oracles.</note> that previously presided over these oracles. To this purpose, he relates a memorable story concerning a voice that called three times aloud to a man named *Thamus*, an Egyptian shipmaster, and his crew as they sailed by the *Echinades* Islands. The voice commanded him that when they came near *Palodes*, he should make a proclamation *that the great Pan was dead*. He did so, and the news was received not with the regret of just one or two people, but by *many*, who responded with great mourning and consternation. Plutarch reports the circumstances of this story at length and adds that when the event was publicized at *Rome*, *Thamus* was sent for by the Emperor *Tiberius*, to whom he gave an account and satisfied him of its truth.

<margin>
*Preparation for the Gospel*, Book 5, Chapter 17, page 207. <note>original: "Prepar. Evang." by Eusebius.</note>
</margin>
*Eusebius* observes that this specific time corresponds with our Lord’s time on earth, when he began openly to strip *demons* of the power and tyranny they had gained over mankind. And (if the calculations some people make are correct) it occurred around the time of our Savior's Passion, who *led captivity captive, spoiled principalities and powers, and made a show of them openly, triumphing over them on his Cross, and by his death destroyed him who had the power of death—that is, the Devil.* <note>Referencing Colossians 2:15 and Hebrews 2:14.</note>

<margin>
Regarding the fact that the wicked demons are no longer able to do or effect anything after the arrival of our Savior among men, even the enemy of demons himself <note>Porphyry, a famous pagan critic of Christianity.</note> testifies to this in his own writing, saying: "But now they marvel if the disease has gripped the city for so many years, Asclepius and the other gods no longer being present. For since Jesus has been honored, no one has perceived any public benefit from the gods." These are the very words of Porphyry. Eusebius, as cited above, Chapter 1, page 179. <note>original Greek: "Περὶ δὲ τοῦ μηκέτι δύνασθαί τι καὶ ἰσχύειν τοὺς φαύλους δαίμονας..."</note>
</margin>
XI. HOWEVER, we need no more evidence that the silence of the oracles and the weakening of the power of *demons* was the effect of the Christian religion in the world than the plain confession of *Porphyry* himself. Truth will sometimes extort a confession even out of the mouth of its greatest enemy. He says *that now it is no wonder if the city has been overrun with sickness for so many years, Asclepius and the rest of the gods having withdrawn their conversation with men; for since Jesus began to be worshipped, no man has received any public help or benefit from the gods.* This is a great argument, as *Eusebius* rightly points out, for our Savior’s divine authority and the truth of his doctrine. 

For when (as he says a little earlier) such numbers of fictional deities fled at our Lord’s appearance, who would not view it with admiration as an uncontrollable demonstration of his truly saving and excellent religion? Through this faith, so many churches and oratories have been erected and consecrated to the great Creator and the only Sovereign of the world throughout the whole earth—in cities, villages, and even in the deserts and wildernesses of the most barbaric nations. Consider the vast numbers of books that have been written, containing the most incomparable rules and institutions to shape mankind toward a life of the most perfect virtue and religion—precepts suitable not only for men, but for women and children as well. One can see that the oracles and divinations of the *demons* have ceased and gone; as soon as the divine and Gospel-driven virtue of our Savior visited mankind, people began to abandon their wild and frantic ways of worship. They began to abhor those human sacrifices (often of their dearest relatives) which they had used to appease and satisfy their bloody and merciless *demons*, and into which their wisest and greatest men had been bewitched and seduced. I will add nothing more than *St. Chrysostom’s* challenge: "Judge
