XII. We have seen the mighty success with which Christianity displayed its banners across the world; let us next consider what contributed to such a vast increase and spread of the faith. And here, without dwelling on the blessing of Divine Providence—which directly oversaw its prosperity and welfare—nor on the intrinsic excellence of the religion itself (which bore the essential marks of divinity, enough to recommend it to every wise and good person), there were five things in particular that helped pave the way for it: the miraculous powers then present in the Church, the great learning and skills of its champions and defenders, the tireless effort used in spreading it, the peerless lives of its followers, and their patience and steadfastness under suffering. The miraculous testimonies given to the Christian religion were by no means the least significant way it gained respect from the world. I will not concern myself here with showing that miracles—performed truly and publicly—are the highest external evidence that can be given for the truth of the religion they confirm; the force of this argument has been sufficiently defended by Christian apologists. We have the consistent testimony of all the earliest writers that such miraculous powers were common in the Church at that time.

<margin>a *First Apology*, page 45.</margin>

"But I am not able," he says, "to count the number of those gifts which the Church throughout the world, receiving them from God, exercises every day freely in the name of *Jesus Christ*, crucified under *Pontius Pilate*, for the benefit of the world."

Yes, even to this day the footsteps of that Holy Spirit, which appeared in the shape of a **Dove**, are preserved among the **Christians**; they exorcise **demons**, perform many cures, and according to the will of God foresee and foretell things to come. At which, though **Celsus** and the **Jew** he portrays <note>original: "personated Jew"—Celsus wrote a critique of Christianity from the perspective of a Jewish character</note> may laugh, yet I affirm further, that many even against their inclinations have been brought over to the Christian religion—their former opposition to it being suddenly changed into a resolute maintaining of it until death—after they have had visions communicated to them; several of which nature we ourselves have seen. And should we only reckon up those at which we ourselves have been present and beheld, it might only make the **infidels** merry, supposing that we, like themselves, did forge and feign them. But God bears witness with my conscience that I do not endeavor by falsely-contrived stories, but by various powerful instances, to recommend the divine religion of the holy **Jesus**.”

More testimonies of this kind I could easily produce from *Minucius Felix, Cyprian, Arnobius*, and *Lactantius*; but these are enough for my purpose.

## XIII.

ANOTHER advantage that exceedingly contributed to the triumph of Christianity was the exceptional learning of many who became champions to defend it. For it could not but be a mighty satisfaction—especially to men of ordinary capacities and humble professions <note>original: "mean employments"—this refers to the working class and those with lower social standing</note>, who make up the far greatest part of mankind—to see persons of the most sharp and sophisticated logic <note>original: "smart and subtil reasonings"</note>, of the most acute and refined understandings, and consequently not easily capable of being imposed upon by arts of deception <note>original: "sophistry"</note> and plausible stories, trampling upon their former sentiments and opinions. They were not only entertaining the Christian faith but defending it against its most venomous <note>original: "virulent"</note> opposers. 

It is true indeed that the **Gospel** at its first setting out was left to its own naked strength, and men of the most unpolished background were chosen to convey it to the world so that it might not seem to be a human artifice, or its success be ascribed to the skills and powers of man. But after it had proven itself to the world for a hundred years, and a sharper edge was set upon the malice and keenness of its adversaries, it was only proper to bring in external help to assist it.

<margin>c *Theodoret, Ecclesiastical History, Book 3, chapter 8, page 131.* <note>original: "Theod. H. Eccl. l. 3. c. 8. p. 131."</note></margin>
And herein the care of Divine Providence was very remarkable: that as miracles became less common and frequent in the Church, God was pleased to raise up even from among the **Gentiles** themselves men of profound abilities and excellent learning. These men could <gloss>be beaten with their own feathers,</gloss> <note>original: "τοῖς οἰκείοις πλεροῖς ἑάλω"—this refers to an old Greek proverb about an eagle shot by an arrow fletched with its own plumes.</note> as **Julian** <note>The Emperor Julian "the Apostate," who attempted to revive Roman paganism</note> said of the **Christians** of his time—beating them at their own weapons and wounding them with arrows drawn out of their own quiver. And it was high time to do so, for the **Gentiles** did not only attack the **Christians** and their religion by methods...

<summary>This page discusses how early Christianity was bolstered both by documented miracles, such as healings and exorcisms, and by the conversion of highly educated intellectuals who used their philosophical training to defend the faith.</summary>
<keywords>miracles, Origen, Celsus, Apostles, exorcism, visions, Christian Apologists, Theodoret, Julian the Apostate, conversion of intellectuals</keywords>

--- Page 55 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of how early Christians faced intense persecution and intellectual attacks, leading to the rise of the first great defenders of the faith.</meta>

<page-num>xiii</page-num>

<header>The INTRODUCTION.</header>

...methods of cruelty and through arts of insinuation. They did not only raise every objection that wit and subtlety could invent to create a shadow or pretense of reason, but they also burdened Christians with the blackest crimes—charges that nothing but the utmost malice and prejudice could ever suspect to be true. 

This provided the occasion for the Christian Apologists and the first writers against the Gentiles <note>original: "Gentiles"—in this context, the non-Jewish pagans of the Roman Empire</note>. Through their learned and rational discourses, they cleared <note>original: "assoil'd"—to absolve or clear from a charge</note> the Christians of the crimes charged against them. They justified the reasonableness, excellence, and divinity of their religion, while exposing the folly, falsehood, brutality, and impiety of pagan worship, with its absurd and trivial rites. By these means, prejudices were removed, and thousands were brought over to the Faith. 

Among those who made themselves most renowned and did the greatest service to the Christian cause were: **Quadratus**, Bishop of Athens, and **Aristides**, formerly a famous philosopher of that city. Both were wise and eloquent men who each dedicated an *Apology* to the Emperor **Hadrian** <note>original: "Adrian"</note>. **Justin the Martyr**, besides several tracts against the Gentiles, wrote two *Apologies*. The first was presented to **Antoninus Pius**, and the second to **Marcus Aurelius** and the Senate. Around that time, **Athenagoras** also presented his *Apology* to **Marcus Aurelius** and **Commodus**, not to mention his excellent discourse concerning the resurrection. 

To the same **Marcus Aurelius**, **Melito**, Bishop of Sardis, presented his *Apologetic Oration* for the Christians. Under this emperor, **Apollinaris**, Bishop of Hierapolis in Asia, also flourished and dedicated to him an incomparable discourse in defense of the Christian faith, along with five books he wrote against the Gentiles and two concerning the truth. Not long after, **Theophilus**, Bishop of Antioch, composed his three excellent books for the conviction of **Autolycus**. **Miltiades** also presented an *Apology*, probably to the Emperor **Commodus**. 

**Tatian the Syrian**, a student of **Justin Martyr** and a learned, eloquent man, wrote a book against the Gentiles, which clearly demonstrates his great abilities. **Tertullian**, a man of admirable learning and the first of the Latin writers to join this cause, published his *Apology* during the reign of **Severus**. It was addressed to the magistrates of the Roman Empire. He also wrote *To the Nations* <note>original: "Ad Nationes"</note>, *On Idolatry* <note>original: "De Idololatria"</note>, *To Scapula* <note>original: "Ad Scapulam"</note>, and many more. 

After him came **Origen**, whose eight books against **Celsus** did no greater service to the Christian cause than they did honor to himself. **Minucius Felix**, an eminent lawyer in Rome, wrote a short but very elegant dialogue between **Octavius** and **Caecilius**. As **Lactantius** 
<margin>a *On Divine Institutes, Book 5, chapter 1, page 459.* <note>original: "De Instit. l. 5. c. 1. p. 459." Referring to Lactantius's *Divinae Institutiones*.</note></margin>
observed long ago, this work shows how fit and able an advocate he would have been to defend the truth if he had fully applied himself to it. 

Around the time of **Gallus** and **Volusianus**, **Cyprian** addressed a discourse to **Demetrian**, the Proconsul of Africa, on behalf of the Christians and their religion. He also published his tract *On the Vanity of Idols* <note>original: "De Idolorum vanitate"</note>, which is essentially a summary of Minucius’s dialogue. Toward the end of that century, under **Diocletian**, **Arnobius** taught rhetoric with great success at Sicca in Africa. Once convinced of the truth of Christianity, he could hardly get the Christians to believe he was sincere at first. To prove his sincerity, he wrote seven books against the Gentiles, in which he smartly and rationally pleads the Christian cause. Not long after, his student **Lactantius**, who taught rhetoric at Nicomedia under Diocletian, set himself to composing several discourses in defense of the Christian religion and the subversion of paganism. He was a witty and eloquent man, though more successful at attacking his adversaries,

->then<-

It tracks the intellectual lineage of the faith from the 2nd century through the reign of Diocletian.</summary>

<page-num>xiv</page-num>

<header>The INTRODUCTION.</header>

...rather than in establishing the principles of his own religion, many of which he seems not to have understood very clearly. To all these I may add **Apollonius**, a man versed in all kinds of learning and philosophy; and (if **St. Jerome** <note>original: "St. Hierom"</note> is correct) a senator of **Rome**, who in a formal oration <note>original: "set Oration"</note> with such brave and generous confidence, eloquently pleaded his own cause and the cause of Christianity before the Senate itself; for this, he suffered as a martyr during the reign of **Commodus**.

---

XIV. And just as they defended Christianity on one hand from the open attacks and insults <note>original: "calumnies"</note> of the pagans <note>original: "Gentiles"</note>, they were no less careful on the other hand to clear it from the errors and heresies with which men of perverse and evil minds sought to corrupt and poison it. The leaders among those who engaged in this work were: **Agrippa Castor**, a man of great learning during the time of **Hadrian** <note>original: "Adrian"</note>, who wrote an accurate refutation of **Basilides** and his principles in twenty-four books; **Theophilus of Antioch** against **Hermogenes** and **Marcion**; **Apollinaris**; **Philip**, Bishop of **Gortyna** in **Crete**; **Musanus**; **Modestus**; **Rhodon**, the student of **Tatian**; **Miltiades**; **Apollonius**; **Serapion**, Bishop of **Antioch**; and hundreds more who fought against the **Marcionites**, **Montanists**, and other heretics of those times. 

But the most significant of all was ***Irenaeus***, who challenged the most well-known heresies of those ages. With incomparable industry and sharp reasoning, he unraveled their principles, exposed their practices, and refuted their errors. Through this (as he often mentions), many were brought back <note>original: "reduced"</note> and recovered to the Church. I might also mention several others who, though not known for specifically working in either of these ways, are nevertheless famous for their excellent skill in all arts and sciences, through which they became immensely useful to the Church. 

Such men (besides those described in the following work) were **Dionysius**, Bishop of **Corinth**; **Bardesanes the Syrian**, whose learning and eloquence were far above the common standard, although he also wrote against almost all the heresies of the age in which he lived; **Ammonius**, the celebrated philosopher of **Alexandria**; **Julius Africanus**, a man especially distinguished for history and chronology; **Dorotheus**, a priest <note>original: "Presbyter"</note> of **Antioch**, famous for his skill in **Hebrew** as well as other branches of learning; **Anatolius the Alexandrian**, whom **Eusebius** praises greatly as the most learned man and sharpest philosopher of his age—skilled in arithmetic, geometry, astronomy, logic, physics, rhetoric, and indeed, what not? **Pierius**, a priest of **Alexandria**, an eloquent preacher and so great a scholar that he was commonly called "**the Younger Origen**" <note>original: "Origen Junior"</note>. But this field is too large to proceed any further, and therefore I will stop here.

<margin>Therefore, let **Celsus**, **Porphyry**, and **Julian**, those rabid dogs against Christ, learn—and let their followers learn, who think the Church had no philosophers, eloquent men, or teachers—how great and what kind of men founded, built up, and adorned it. Let them stop accusing our faith of being only "rustic simplicity" and instead acknowledge their own ignorance. **St. Jerome**, Preface to the *Catalog of Church Writers*. <note>original Latin: "Discant ergo Celsus, Porphyrius, Julianus, rabidi adversus Christum canes..."</note></margin>

From all this, it is evident what **St. Jerome** remarks: how little reason **Celsus**, **Porphyry**, and **Julian** <note>historical note: these were three of the most famous pagan intellectual critics of early Christianity</note> had to complain <note>original: "clamour"</note> against the Christians as a crude and uneducated <note>original: "illiterate"</note> generation who had no learning, eloquence, or philosophy to recommend them.

---

XV. A third advantage that helped the progress of Christianity was the tireless <note>original: "indefatigable"</note> zeal and industry used in spreading <note>original: "propagation"</note> it. No stone was left unturned, no method unattempted, by which they might rescue men from error and bring them to acknowledge the truth.

<margin>From Gruter’s *Inscriptions*, page 238, number 9. <note>original: "Ap. Gruter. Inscript. p. 238. N. IX."</note></margin>

<page-num>XV</page-num>
<header>The INTRODUCTION.</header>

In Spain, to the honor of **Nero**, they are described with this character: <term>Those who instilled and forced a new superstition upon the human race</term> <note>original: "QUI NOVAM GENERI HUM. SUPERSTITION. INCULCAB."</note>. Indeed, they were infinitely zealous to gain **converts** <note>original: "Proselytes"</note> to the best **religion** in the world. They preached it boldly and prayed heartily for the conversion and reformation of mankind. They reached out to their neighbors who were yet strangers to the **faith**, instructed and informed new converts, and built them up in the most holy faith. Those who possessed greater talents and prominence established and instituted **schools**, where they publicly taught those who came to them, grounding them in the basics of the **faith** and protecting <note>original: "antidoting"</note> them against both **heathens** on one side and **heretics** on the other. "Among us," says **Tatian** <margin>*a Address to the Greeks* <note>original: "Ora. contr. Grac."</note>, p. 167.</margin>, "not only the rich and the wealthy learn our **philosophy**, but the poor are freely disciplined and instructed; we admit all who are willing to learn, whether they are old or young." And he tells us a little later what the success was <margin>b Ibid. p. 168.</margin>: that all their **virgins** were sober and modest, and were accustomed to discourse concerning divine things even while they were sitting at their **distaffs** <note>A tool used in spinning wool or flax by hand.</note>. Nor did they content themselves only to do this at home; many of them freely exposed themselves to all manner of hazards and hardships. No pains were thought too great, no dangers significant, and no difficulties insurmountable <note>original: "insuperable"</note> in their effort to enlarge the borders of the **Gospel**, traveling into the most barbarous **nations** and to the remotest corners of the world.

"The divine and admirable **disciples** of the **Apostles**," says **Eusebius** <margin>c *Church History* <note>original: "H. Eccles."</note>, book 3, chapter 37, p. 109.</margin>, "built up the additions to those **churches** whose foundations the **Apostles** had laid in all the places they visited. Everywhere they promoted the publication of the **Gospel**, sowing the seeds of that heavenly **doctrine** throughout the whole world. For their minds being inflamed with the love of a more divine **philosophy**, they distributed their estates to the poor and, leaving their own **countries**, took upon themselves the office of **evangelists**. They preached **Christ** and delivered the **Gospel** <note>original: "Evangelical"</note> writings to those who had not yet so much as heard of the **Christian faith**. And no sooner had they established the **faith** in any foreign **country** and ordained guides and **pastors**—to whom they committed the care of those new **communities** <note>original: "Plantations"</note>—but they immediately left for other **nations**. They confirmed their **doctrine** with the miraculous powers of that **Divine Spirit** that attended them, so that as soon as they began to preach, the people universally flocked to them and cheerfully and heartily embraced the worship of the true **God**, the great Creator of the world." Among the number of these **Gospel missionaries** who were of the first **Apostolic** succession were **Silas**, **Silvanus**, **Crescens**, **Andronicus**, **Trophimus**, **Mark**, **Aristarchus**, etc. Later came **Pantaenus**, who went into **India**; **Didymus**; and **Irenaeus**, who went from **Smyrna** into **France**, each successively becoming **Bishop of Lyons**. There were infinite others mentioned in the **histories** and **records of martyrs** <note>original: "Martyrologies"</note> of the **Church** who did not value their lives <note>original: "counted not their lives to be dear unto them"</note> so that they might finish their course with joy and make known the mystery of the **Gospel** to the ends of the earth.

### XVI. FOURTHLY,
Christianity recommended itself to the world by the admirable lives of its followers, which were so truly consistent with all the laws of virtue and goodness that they could not help but reconcile the wiser and more fair-minded part of the **pagan** <note>original: "Gentile"</note> world to a better opinion of it. This also defended it from those absurd and senseless insults <note>original: "cavils"</note> that—

...that were made against it. For when people saw Christians everywhere who were so seriously devout and pious, so incomparably chaste and sober, of such humble and restrained <note>original: "mortified"</note> tempers, so strictly just and righteous, so kind and charitable—not only to themselves, but to all mankind—they concluded there must be something more than human in it. Indeed, no argument is so convincing as a demonstration from experience. Their singular piety and the discipline of their behavior <note>original: "manners"</note> outweighed all the disadvantages they faced.

<margin>
a) *As above, chapter 24, p. 94.* <note>original: "Ubi supr. c. 24. p. 94."</note>

The divine and most admirable Apostles of Christ (says **Eusebius** a)), however unrefined <note>original: "rude"</note> they were in speech, were yet <term>highly purified in their lives and had their souls adorned with every virtue</term> <note>original: "τὸν βίον ἄκρως κεκαθαρμένοι, & ἀρετῇ πάσῃ τὰς ψυχὰς κεκοσμημένοι"</note>. And such, generally, were the Christians of the succeeding ages; they did not entertain the world with a collection of fine words and a plausible story, but showed their faith by their works and proved the divinity of their religion by the heavenly nature of their lives.

<margin>
b) *Minucius Felix, Dialogue, not far from the end, p. 31.* <note>original: "M. Fel. Dial. non longe a fin. p. 31."</note>
</margin>
"We," (says the Christian in **Minucius Felix** b)) "despise the pride and arrogance of philosophers, whom we know to be immoral <note>original: "debauched"</note> persons who are always eloquent against those vices of which they themselves are most guilty. For we do not measure wisdom by men’s clothing <note>original: "garbs and habits"</note> but by their minds and behavior; nor do we speak great things so much as live them, glorying that we have attained what they earnestly sought but could never find." Christians were then the only people who really were what they pretended to be: individuals heartily reformed from vice to virtue.

<margin>

Indeed, the power <note>original: "efficacy"</note> of the Christian doctrine over the minds of men was remarkable. The Christian **Apologists** <note>Those who wrote formal defenses of the faith.</note> pleaded this at every turn as an undeniable evidence of their religion: that it made all sorts of people who complied with it chaste and temperate, quiet and peaceable, meek and modest, and afraid of even the slightest appearance or hint of what was evil. When the heathens mocked them for the simple and unpretentious <note>original: "unpompous"</note> ceremonies of their religion, they universally declared that God respected no man for any external excellence or advantage; it was the pure and holy soul He delighted in. He stood in no need of blood or smoke, perfumes or incense; the greatest and best sacrifice was to offer up a mind truly devoted to Him. Meekness and kindness, a humble heart, and an innocent...

<page-num>xvii</page-num>
<header>The INTRODUCTION.</header>

...an innocent life was the sacrifice with which God was well pleased, and was infinitely beyond all burnt offerings <note>original: "Holocausts"</note> and religious sacrifices <note>original: "Oblations"</note>. They believed that a pious and devout mind was the fittest temple for God to dwell in, and that to do one's duty, to abstain from sin, and to be intent upon the duties and services of prayer and praise is the truest festival. Indeed, the whole life of a good man is nothing else but a holy and festive solemnity. This was the religion of Christians then, and it rendered their profession lovely <note>original: "amiable"</note> and worthy of respect to the world. It many times forced even its most violent opponents to fall down and say that *God was truly among them*. But less will be said on this argument here, as a full account of it has been given in a work specifically on this subject.

<margin>*Primitive Christianity, Part 2, Chapter 7* <note>original: "Prim. Christ. Part. II. Ch. 7."</note></margin>

XVII. FIFTHLY, the disciples of this holy and excellent religion gained innumerable converts <note>original: "Proselytes"</note> to their side by their patience and constancy under sufferings. They were unshakeably resolved to maintain their ground, despite all attempts to force them from it. They met the fiercest threats with a steady mind and fearlessly beheld the racks and instruments of torture <note>original: "engins"</note> prepared for them. They laughed at torments, welcomed the flames, and went out to meet death in its darkest form. They died rejoicing and triumphed in the midst of the greatest tortures. Because this happened almost every day for several centuries, it could not help but convince their enemies that they were in earnest, that they heartily believed their religion to be true, and that there must be a divine and supernatural power supporting them through it. **Justin Martyr** confesses that this was one main inducement for his conversion to Christianity. What particular methods of cruelty were used against the early <note>original: "Primitive"</note> Christians, and with what brave and noble patience—with what composure and tranquility of mind—they endured the heaviest and most painful torments, we have sufficiently described in another place. Therefore, we shall only take a short survey here of those ten famous persecutions that so notably tested the faith and patience of the early saints, and then summarize the force of the argument resulting from it. We do this primarily because it will provide us with the best view of the state of the Church in those early ages. As for the specific dates and periods of some of these persecutions, different accounts are given by **Sulpicius Severus**, **Eusebius**, **Orosius**, **Jerome** <note>original: "Hierom"</note>, and others; we shall follow the account that appears to be most likely and probable.

<margin>a) Tertullian's *Apology*, Chapter 5, page 6. <note>original: "Apol. cap. 5. p. 6."</note></margin>

XVIII. THE first who raised a general persecution against the Christians was **Nero**, as **Tertullian** <gloss>a</gloss> tells the pagans <note>original: "Gentiles"</note>; for the truth of it, he refers them to their own public archives and records. Nero was a prince of such a wild and ungovernable temper, and of such brutish and extravagant behavior, that their own writers did not hesitate to call him a beast in human shape and the very monster of mankind. He was guilty of unbounded pride and ambition, drunkenness, luxury, and all manner of debauchery, including various sexual vices <note>original: "Sodomy"</note> and incest, which he even attempted to commit with his own mother. But cruelty seemed to be the strongest of his vices. Besides countless others, he executed the greater part of the Senate and put to death his tutor **Seneca** <note>A famous Stoic philosopher and advisor to Nero</note> and his wife, as well as **Lucan** the poet. Furthermore, he violated all the laws of nature by attacking his own close relations: he was aware of, if not responsible for, the death of his stepfather <note>original: "Father"</note> **Claudius**; he killed his two wives, **Octavia** and **Poppaea**; and he murdered **Antonia** because she refused to marry him after their deaths. He also poisoned his brother **Britannicus**.

<meta>catchword: and</meta>

<page-num>xviii</page-num>
<header>The INTRODUCTION.</header>

---

...and to complete all his villainies, he next fell upon his own mother, *Agrippina*, whom he hated for her bold reproofs of his loose and extravagant lifestyle. After first stripping her of all public honors and causing her to be openly disgraced and ridiculed, he tried to poison her three times before finally sending an assassin to stab her. Tradition at the time suggested that, not content with doing this, he personally went to view her naked corpse, contemplating and handling its various parts—praising some and criticizing others. If he was so barbarous and inhuman toward his own family and subjects, we cannot imagine he would be favorable to Christians. According to *Eusebius* ᵃ, he earned the title of being the first Emperor to become an enemy to the Christian religion.

<margin>ᵃ Eusebius, *Ecclesiastical History*, Book 2, Chapter 25, p. 67.</margin>

He published laws and edicts to suppress it and prosecuted its followers with the utmost severity everywhere, using the following occasion as a pretext. Among countless other examples of his madness and folly, he decided to burn *Rome*, either because he was offended by the narrowness of the streets and the ugliness of the buildings, or because he was ambitious to build a more grand and magnificent city named after himself. Whatever the reason, he ordered it set on fire around the 19th of *July*, *64 AD*. The conquering flames quickly overwhelmed the city—a city that had so often triumphed over the rest of the world—and in six or seven days, they destroyed and reduced the vast majority of it (ten out of fourteen districts <term>Regions</term>) to ashes. Houses, temples, and all the ancient monuments and relics that had been preserved with care and reverence for ages were laid waste. Meanwhile, Nero watched the tragic spectacle with pleasure and delight from the Tower of *Maecenas*, dressed as a stage performer and singing about the destruction of *Troy*.

<margin>ᵇ Tacitus, *Annals*, Book 15, Chapter 44, p. 319.</margin>
<margin>ᶜ In Janus Gruter’s *Inscriptions*, cited above.</margin>

When the people tried to search the ruins of their own homes, he forbade them, refusing to let them salvage what the flames had spared. This act (as it rightly should) exposed him to all the hatred and loathing an injured and abused people could feel. He tried to deflect this by making large promises, giving great rewards, consulting the *Sibylline Books* <note>A collection of oracular prophecies consulted by the Roman state in times of crisis.</note>, and offering public prayers and sacrifices to the gods. Despite all this, *Tacitus* ᵇ tells us that the people still believed he was the author of the disaster. When this failed, he tried to clear himself by shifting the blame onto the Christians, whom he knew were already sufficiently hated by the public. He accused them of being the arsonists <term>incendiaries</term> and proceeded against them with the most agonizing <term>exquisite</term> tortures. 

After arresting some who were either forced or persuaded to confess, their information led to the betrayal of large numbers of others. *Tacitus* admits that it was not the burning of the city, but general public hatred that made them appear criminal. They were treated with every form of mockery and cruelty. Some were sewn into the skins of wild animals and torn apart by dogs; others were crucified; still others were burned alive. These were dressed in paper coats <note>original: "tunica molesta" – a shirt impregnated with flammable materials like pitch and wax.</note> dipped in pitch, wax, and other flammable materials so that, when daylight failed, they could serve as torches in the night. *Nero* put on these shows in his own gardens, yet the people viewed them with more pity than pleasure, knowing they were not done for the public good but merely to satisfy his own private rage and malice. Christians met with little better treatment in other parts of the Empire, as shown by the inscription ᶜ found at *Clunia* in *Spain*, dedicated

<meta>This page continues the account of the First Persecution under the Emperor Nero, listing specific martyrs before transitioning to the Second and Third Persecutions under the Emperors Domitian and Trajan.</meta>

<page-num>xix</page-num>
<header>The INTRODUCTION.</header>

...to *Nero* in memory of his having cleared the province of those that had introduced a new superstition among mankind <note>This refers to a contemporary Roman view of Christianity as a "superstition" because it deviated from traditional Roman paganism.</note>. Under this persecution suffered *Tecla*, *Torques*, *Torquatus*, *Marcellus*, and several others mentioned in the ancient martyrologies, especially the Apostles *Peter* and *Paul*; the one upon the cross, the other by the sword.

XIX. The troublesome changes and revolutions of affairs that happened under the succeeding Emperors, *Galba*, *Otho*, and *Vitellius*, and the mild and merciful disposition of *Vespasian* and *Titus*, gave some rest to the Christians. This peace lasted until *Domitian* succeeded to the throne and began a **SECOND PERSECUTION**. He was a man of a temperament vastly different from that of his father and his brother; for though at first he put on a plausible appearance <note>original: "carriage"</note>, he soon dropped the mask <note>original: "vizor"</note> and appeared as his true self: lazy and inactive, ill-natured and suspicious, greedy and covetous, proud and insolent. Indeed, he was so vainly ambitious as to affect divinity, assuming for himself in all public edicts—and requiring from others in all petitions and addresses—the titles of *Lord* and *God*. He never truly loved any man; and when he most pretended to do so, it was a sure sign of that man’s ruin. He exercised his cruelty first upon flies, thousands of which he killed every day; next he turned upon men, and those of all ranks and states. He put to death the most illustrious Senators and persons of the greatest honor and nobility upon the most trifling pretences, and many times for no cause at all. In the fierceness and brutality of his temper, 
<margin>In the place cited above <note>original: "Loc. super. citat."</note></margin>
he equaled *Nero*—"A portion of Nero in terms of cruelty" <note>original: "Portio Neronis de crudelitate"</note>, as *Tertullian* styles him; in fact, he exceeded him in this: *Nero* was content to command executions to be carried out from a distance, while *Domitian* took pleasure in beholding his cruelties exercised before his eyes—an argument for a temperament even more deeply stained in blood. 

But the Christians, alas, bore the heaviest load of his rage and malice, whom he everywhere persecuted either by death or banishment. Under him, St. *John* the *Evangelist* was sent for to *Rome*, and by his command thrown into a cauldron of boiling oil; in the midst of which, when Divine Providence had miraculously preserved him, the Emperor immediately banished him to the island of *Patmos*. He put to death his cousin-german <term>first cousin</term> *Flavius Clemens* (who was at that time *Consul*) for being a Christian, and banished his wife *Flavia Domitilla* (his own kinswoman also) upon the same account to the island of *Pandataria*. At length his brutish and bloody practices rendered him intolerable to his own friends and servants, who conspired against him (his own wife *Domitia* being part of the conspiracy) and slew him. His successor *Nerva* repealed his acts and recalled those whom he had outlawed and banished; among whom St. *John*, taking the benefit of that *Act of Revocation*, left *Patmos* and returned to *Ephesus*.

XX. The **THIRD PERSECUTION** commenced under *Trajan*, whom *Nerva* had adopted to be his successor. He was a Prince of excellent and incomparable virtues, whose justice and impartiality, gentleness and modesty, generosity and liberality, kindness and friendliness rendered him infinitely dear and acceptable to the people; the extravagances of his predecessors contributed not a little to making his government sweeter to them. He was mild and calm, familiar and courteous; he showed great reverence to the Senate, by whose advice he usually acted; and they, to reward him, gave him the title of *Optimus* <note>original: "Optimus," meaning "The Best."</note>, as they judged him the best of all their Princes. He conversed freely and innocently with all men, desiring rather to be beloved than either...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Roman Emperors and their relationship with the early Church, focusing specifically on the transition to the reign of Trajan and the legal basis for the "Third Persecution."</meta>

<page-num>XX</page-num>
<header>The INTRODUCTION.</header>
<sig>(c 2)</sig>

<margin>
a Law 1 and 3 in the *Digest*, concerning colleges and corporations, Book 47, Title 22. <note>original: "L. 1. & 3. ff. de colleg. & corp. Lib. 47. tit. 22."</note>
b Ulpian, *On the Duties of a Proconsul*, Book 6 and Book 2. <note>original: "Ulpian de off. procons. l. 6. ib. l. 2."</note>
c Book 10, Letters 42 and 43. <note>original: "Lib. 10. Epist. 42, & 43."</note>
</margin>
...than either feared or honored by the people. The glory of all these achievements is greatly stained in the records of the Church by his severe proceedings against the Christians. He looked upon the religion of the Empire as being daily undermined by this new way of worship, fearing that the number of Christians was becoming formidable and might possibly endanger the peace and tranquility of the Roman State. He believed there was no better way to secure the favor of the gods for himself, especially during his wars, than to defend <note>original: "vindicate"</note> their cause against the Christians. Accordingly, he issued orders to proceed against them as *illegal societies* <note>The Roman authorities were suspicious of any private group or "collegia" that met without official state permission, fearing they were fronts for political conspiracy.</note>, which were established and acting contrary to the laws. Under these laws, all colleges and corporations were considered illegal if they were not <gloss>a</gloss> settled either by the Emperor’s constitution or by a decree of the Senate; those who frequented such groups were judged guilty of high treason. <gloss>b</gloss> Indeed, the Emperors (as we have observed elsewhere) were incredibly suspicious of such meetings, believing they could easily turn into factions and treasonous conspiracies. This is why, when Pliny <gloss>c</gloss> interceded with *Trajan* on behalf of the city of *Nicomedia*—asking that, since the city was so prone to fires, the Emperor would establish a corporation of *blacksmiths* <note>original: "Smiths"</note>, even just a small number who could be easily kept in order and whom Pliny promised to watch closely—the Emperor answered: "By no means; for we ought to remember that this province, and especially those cities, are greatly disturbed by such kinds of factions. Whatever the title or the occasion may be, if they meet together, they will become *secret societies* <note>original: "Heteriæ," a Greek term for political clubs or unauthorized associations</note>, even if they are less numerous than others." It will appear from *Pliny’s* letter to him that the Romans looked upon Christian assemblies as being among these unlawful corporations, and that under this pretense, *Trajan* endeavored to suppress them. In the meantime, he commanded them either to offer sacrifice to the gods or to be punished as those who hold the gods in contempt. In several places, the common people also turned violently against them in popular riots. The most prominent of those who obtained the crown of martyrdom under him were *St. Clement*, Bishop of *Rome*; *St. Simeon*, Bishop of *Jerusalem*; and *St. Ignatius*, Bishop of *Antioch*, whom *Trajan* himself condemned and sent to *Rome* to be thrown to wild beasts.

XXI. THE persecution raged in other parts of the Empire, but especially in the provinces of *Pontus* and *Bithynia*, where *Pliny the Younger* (who had previously served as *Consul*) then governed as *Pro-Praetor* <note>A provincial governor acting with the authority of a judge</note> with *consular* power and dignity. Seeing vast multitudes of Christians indicted by others, and even more coming forward of their own accord to face execution, Pliny realized that proceeding severely against every person who came would essentially lay those provinces to waste. He thought it best to write to the Emperor about this matter to know his will in the case. We shall insert his letter here, because it acquaints us so exactly with the state of the Christians and the manner of proceeding against them, and provides such an eminent testimony to their innocence and integrity.

<summary>This page describes how Emperor Trajan justified the persecution of Christians by classifying their assemblies as "illegal societies" or "Heteriæ" under Roman law. It concludes by introducing Pliny the Younger's famous letter to Trajan regarding the overwhelming number of Christians in Pontus and Bithynia.</summary>
<keywords>Trajan, Pliny the Younger, illegal societies, Heteriae, Roman Law, St. Ignatius of Antioch, St. Clement of Rome, St. Simeon of Jerusalem, Nicomedia, religious persecution</keywords>

<meta>This page contains the text of the famous letter from Pliny the Younger, governor of Bithynia, to Emperor Trajan (c. 112 AD). It continues the discussion of how the Roman government legally treated the early Christian church as an "unlawful assembly."</meta>

<page-num>xxi</page-num>
<header>The INTRODUCTION.</header>

### C. PLINY THE YOUNGER to the Emperor TRAJAN.

It is my custom, Sir, to turn to you in all matters where I have doubts. For who is better able to guide my *indecision* or instruct my *ignorance*? I have never before been present at the examination and trial of Christians; therefore, I do not know what the crime is, to what extent it is usually punished, or how to proceed with these inquiries. 

I was also quite uncertain whether any consideration should be given to the age of the accused—whether the young and weak should be distinguished from the strong and elderly. I wondered whether repentance should be allowed, and if it benefits someone who was once a Christian to cease being one? Furthermore, should the mere name of "Christian" be punished even without other crimes, or only the crimes associated with the name? 

In the meantime, I have followed this procedure with those brought before me as Christians: I asked them whether they were Christians. If they confessed, I asked them a second and third time, threatening them with punishment. If they persisted, I ordered them to be executed. For I had no doubt that, whatever the nature of their confession, their stubbornness and inflexible obstinacy deserved to be punished. There were others afflicted with similar madness whom I ordered to be sent to Rome, because they were Roman citizens. 

As the proceedings continued, the error (as is common) began to spread, and more cases arose. An anonymous document <note>original: "Libel"</note> was presented containing the names of many people who denied that they were, or ever had been, Christians. I dismissed those who, following my lead, called upon the gods and offered wine and incense to your statue (which I had ordered to be brought out for this purpose along with the images of the gods) and who also cursed Christ. It is said that no true Christian can be compelled to do any of these things. 

Others named in the document confessed they were Christians but then immediately denied it, saying they had indeed been Christians once but had since renounced it—some three years ago, others many years ago, and one as long as twenty-five years ago. All of these worshipped your statue and the images of the gods, and they cursed Christ. They maintained that the full extent of their "guilt" or error was simply this: they were accustomed to meet on a fixed day before sunrise and sing responsively a hymn to Christ as to a god. They bound themselves by a solemn oath <note>The Latin word *sacramentum* used here originally referred to a military oath of allegiance.</note> not to commit any crime, but rather to refrain from theft, robbery, and adultery; to never break their word; and never to deny a trust when called upon to restore it. 

After this, it was their custom to depart and then meet again to eat food—ordinary <note>original: "promiscuous"</note> and harmless food. However, they stopped doing this after I issued an edict, following your orders, which forbade political clubs <note>original: "Heteriæ," referring to private associations or secret societies.</note> or unlawful assemblies. To find out the truth of the matter, I thought it necessary to torture <note>original: "examined upon the Wrack"</note> two female slaves whom they call deaconesses. But I discovered nothing more than a depraved and excessive superstition. 

Therefore, I have paused the proceedings to seek your advice. The matter seemed important enough to consult you, especially given the large number of people endangered. Many people of every age and rank, both men and women, are being—and will be—brought to trial. This contagious superstition has spread not only through the cities but also into the towns and country villages. Nevertheless, it seems possible to stop and cure it. It is very clear that the temples, which were almost completely abandoned, are beginning...

<summary>This page features Pliny the Younger's letter to Emperor Trajan, providing a rare Roman perspective on early Christian worship and Pliny's legal dilemma regarding their prosecution. It describes early Christian rituals, including sunrise hymns to Christ and ethical oaths, while noting the rapid spread of the faith.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the text of Pliny the Younger's letter to Emperor Trajan regarding the treatment of Christians in Bithynia, followed by the author's analysis of the Roman government's stance and Trajan's official reply.</meta>

<page-num>xxii</page-num>
<header>The INTRODUCTION.</header>

...to be frequented again, and that the holy rites and ceremonies, which had been neglected for a long time, are being restored. Sacrifices are being brought from all parts to be sold, though until now they found very few buyers. From this, it is easy to guess what a multitude of people might be reclaimed if they are given the opportunity to repent.

<margin>
<sup>a</sup> Tacitus, *Annals*, Book 15, Chapter 44, page 319.
<sup>b</sup> Suetonius, *Life of Nero*, Chapter 16, page 571.
</margin>
This letter was likely written around AD 107, in the ninth year of Trajan's reign. Trajan was staying at *Antioch* <note>Modern-day Antakya, Turkey, then a major capital of the Roman East</note> at the time to prepare for his wars in the East, and the persecution there was very intense. From this, it is evident what unreasonable and deep-seated <note>original: "inveterate"</note> prejudices even the more moderate and honorable members of the Gentile world held against the Christian religion. Although it was so innocent and blameless that it forced a positive character reference even from its greatest enemies and most malicious apostates—and though the rack and torture could force out nothing to its disadvantage—yet, rather than restrain their resentment (which was unbecoming for men of talent and education), they loaded it with insults and harsh words. *Pliny*, we see here, does not hesitate to label it not only an "error" but "madness," and a "wicked and immoderate superstition." He charged the constant profession of it as "stubbornness" and an incurable obstinacy, when in reality it was the result of the most brave and noble resolution. It found the very same "courtesy" from his two intimate friends, *Tacitus* and *Suetonius*; the former calls it a <sup>a</sup> *detestable* superstition, and the latter a <sup>b</sup> *novel and mischievous* superstition. By this account, we also see that although the severity of the persecution might tempt some to become renegades, Christianity had spread so extensively in those regions that this great man did not know how to deal with them. To guide him in this matter, the Emperor returned the following rescript <note>An official imperial response to a legal query</note>.

## TRAJAN to PLINY,
### *Greeting.*

> Regarding the manner of your procedure, my Secundus, in examining the cases of those who have been brought before you for being Christians, you have taken the course which you ought to take. No certain and general law can be framed that would provide for all particular cases. They should not be hunted down; but if they are accused and convicted, let them be punished. Yet, if anyone denies being a Christian and gives evidence of it by offering sacrifice to our gods, he should be pardoned upon his repentance, even if he has been suspected in the past. But as for anonymous accusations <note>original: "Libels"</note> published without the name of the authors, they must not be considered valid evidence; for that would be a bad precedent and is not the practice of our reign.

<margin>
<sup>c</sup> Tertullian, *Apology*, chapters 2, 6, and 3.
</margin>
Tertullian, <sup>c</sup> speaking of this imperial edict, calls it: 
> "A sentence confused by a strange necessity. It forbids them to be hunted down, as if they were innocent, and yet commands them to be punished as if they were guilty. It spares them and yet rages against them; it ignores them and yet punishes them. Why does he entangle himself in his own judgment? If he condemns them, why does he not track them down? If he thinks they should not be searched for, why does he not acquit them?" Where Tertullian seems—

<summary>This page concludes Pliny the Younger's letter describing the growth of Christianity and presents Emperor Trajan's official policy: Christians should not be sought out, but must be punished if they refuse to sacrifice to Roman gods. The author also notes the deep prejudices held by Roman historians like Tacitus and Suetonius, and includes Tertullian's famous critique of the logical inconsistency in Trajan's decree.</summary>

<page-num>xxiii</page-num>
<header>The INTRODUCTION.</header>

...to argue more like an orator than a logician. For **Trajan** might have been unwilling to have the Christians specifically hunted out, and yet he did not necessarily think them innocent. He could not find them guilty of any massive crime, but only of a strange and novel superstition. Therefore, while they remained hidden, he did not think it reasonable that they should be left to the malice and greed of busy subordinate officers who acted under the provincial presidents and governors—men who were mere sycophants and slanderers, <margin>a. In Eusebius, *Ecclesiastical History*, book 4, chapter 26, page 147.</margin> "impudent accusers and those who lust after other men's property," <note>original Greek: ἀναιδεῖς συκοφάνται & τῶν ἀλλοτρίων ἐρασταὶ</note> as **Melito** styles them in his *Apology* to **Marcus Antoninus**. Melito complains of them as impudent accusers and ravenous devourers of other men’s estates, who, under a pretense of the Imperial Edicts, openly spoil and plunder the harmless and the innocent day and night. **Trajan** might have thought it fit to restrain these officers; but where the facts were public knowledge—where Christians were duly summoned before the public tribunals and the charges were substantially proven—there they were to be left to the sentence of the law. But however it was, by this means the edge of their enemies' fury was taken off; and though popular rage might still continue in some particular places, the general force and rigor of the persecution did abate and cease.

XXII. **TRAJAN** dying at *Selinus* in *Cilicia*, **Hadrian** <note>original: "Adrian"</note> (whom he had adopted) succeeded him in the Empire. He was a prince of excellent talents and significant learning—a "king most devoted to the Muses" <note>original: "μουσικώτατος βασιλεύς" (mousikōtatos basileus)</note>, as <margin>b. *The Deipnosophists*, book 8, chapter 16, page 361.</margin> **Athenaeus** calls him. Yet he was one in whom it is hard to say whether vice or virtue had the upper hand; and what is more, he seemed to reconcile most vices with their contrary virtues. He highly honored the Senate, without whose authority he would never transact any affairs of importance, and on solemn days he would condescend to escort the **Consuls** to their houses. Yet he was proud, vainglorious, and ambitious of honor, which he greedily sought on every small occasion. He was magnificent in his public works and liberal in his gifts; but at the same time, he was envious, detracting from the glory of his predecessor and criticizing the most eminent artists in every field. He spoke familiarly with his friends, visited them in their illness many times (twice or thrice a day), and treated them with the freedom and kindness of companions; and yet he was fierce and cruel, as is evident by the many persons of nobility and renown whom he put to death. But we have noted enough of his character elsewhere, in the *Life of Saint Quadratus*. He was addicted to magic and was a great zealot for religion, especially the rites of **Greece**, but he despised and hated all other religions. For this reason, he was no friend to Christians. In his time, a **FOURTH PERSECUTION** was raised against them, as <margin>c. *Sacred History*, book 2, page 142.</margin> **Sulpitius Severus** positively calls it. I know **Eusebius** (followed by **Orosius** and some others) assigns the fourth persecution to the reign of **Marcus Aurelius**; but whoever impartially considers the state of things will see that it ought to be fixed here. It is true, we do not find any new laws which this Emperor made against the Christians, but the laws of his predecessors were still in force, and the people in most places were ready enough to take up this cause of their own accord, sacrificing the poor, innocent Christians to their own spite and malice. Therefore, when **Eusebius** speaks of the *Apologies* <note>Formal written defenses of the Christian faith</note> presented to this Emperor, he says <margin>d. *Ecclesiastical History*, book 4, chapter 3, page 116.</margin> it was because wicked and ill-minded men began to harass and disturb the Christians. And **Saint Jerome** <margin>e. *On Famous Men*, under "Quadratus".</margin> more particu-

->larly<-

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Emperor Hadrian's reign and his shifting policies toward Christians, leading into the reign of Marcus Aurelius and the Fifth Persecution.</meta>

<page-num>xxiv</page-num>
<header>The INTRODUCTION.</header>

<margin>a. St. Jerome, *Epistle to Magnus the Orator*, p. 327, Vol. 2.</margin>
<margin>b. Tertullian, *To Scapula*, chapter 4, p. 71.</margin>

St. Jerome particularly <note>The text begins mid-word from the previous page: "particularly."</note> tells us that the zeal the Emperor Hadrian showed in being initiated into the holy mysteries and the Rites of Greece gave the people the opportunity and encouragement (though without any specific warrant) to attack the Christians. He elsewhere <gloss>a</gloss> calls this a "most grievous persecution." And so indeed it was, as is evident not only from the *Apologies* which both Quadratus and Aristides presented to the Emperor on behalf of the Christians, but also from the conduct of Arrius Antoninus <gloss>b</gloss> (whom most suppose to be the same person who succeeded Hadrian). When Antoninus was Proconsul of Asia and severely prosecuted the Christians there, all the Christians of the city where he resided beset his tribunal as one man, openly confessing themselves to be Christians. Amazed at the multitude, he caused a few of them to be executed, telling the rest that if they had a mind to end their lives, they had enough cliffs and ropes <note>original: "Precipices and Halters"</note> at home and did not need to crowd his court for an execution.

Indeed, the hostility rose so high that Serenius Granianus, one of the following Proconsuls, was forced to write to Hadrian to ask for its mitigation. The Emperor accordingly commanded this by a Rescript <note>A "rescript" was an official written reply from an emperor to a legal question or petition.</note> directed to Minucius Fundanus, Granianus's successor in that province. He did the same for several others <gloss>c</gloss>, as Melito particularly tells us in his *Apology*. But though the fire seemed to be well quenched for the moment, it broke out again in the succeeding reign of Antoninus Pius, consuming many whose sufferings are recorded in the records of the martyrs <note>original: "Martyrologies"</note> of the Church. To stop this, Justin Martyr presented an *Apology* to this Emperor, which produced that excellent Letter of his to the Common Council of Asia in favor of the Christians, which we have included as an example in the *Life of Justin Martyr*.

<margin>c. Eutropius, *Roman History*, book 8, p. 1919.</margin>

### XXIII.

Marcus Aurelius Antoninus and his brother Lucius Verus succeeded Antoninus Pius. Marcus Aurelius was a person of whom his biographers deservedly speak great things. He was a good man and a great philosopher, whom the historian says is easier to admire than to commend. However, he was infinitely superstitious in his religion and was therefore easily incited <note>original: "blown up"</note> by the priests and philosophers around him into a prejudice against Christianity. He was persuaded to set on foot the **FIFTH PERSECUTION** against the Christians, whom he endeavored to curb and suppress by new laws and edicts, exposing them to all the malice and ferocity of their enemies.

The persecution began in the Eastern regions around the seventh year of his reign, where it continued for almost his entire rule. Not content to stay there, it spread into the West, especially France <note>Though the text says "France," this refers to the Roman province of Gaul, specifically the famous persecutions at Lyons and Vienne.</note>, where it raged with great severity. That the conflict was very sharp and fierce may be guessed by the crowd of *Apologies* that were presented to him by Justin Martyr, Melito, Athenagoras, and Apollinaris. In Asia, Saint Polycarp, Bishop of Smyrna, was first condemned to the fire and then run through with a sword, along with twelve others from Philadelphia who suffered with him; Germanicus, who shortly before was devoured by wild beasts, also died. At Rome, besides Ptolomy and Lucius, Justin the Martyr and his six companions—Charito, Charitina, Euelpistus, Hierax, Peon, and Valerianus—were beheaded. In the French persecution, Vettius Epagathus, a young man of incomparable piety and nobility <note>original: "magnanimity"</note>, suffered; as did Blandina, a lady of singular virtue, who after infinite and inexpressible torments was tied to a beam in the shape of a cross and thrown to wild beasts. Biblis also suffered; though through frailty she initially denied the faith, she recovered <meta>The catchword "her" indicates the sentence continues: "she recovered her faith."</meta>

<summary>This page describes the shift in Christian persecution from the reign of Hadrian to the intense "Fifth Persecution" under Marcus Aurelius, highlighting key martyrs like Polycarp and Blandina.</summary>
<keywords>Hadrian, Antoninus Pius, Marcus Aurelius, Fifth Persecution, Justin Martyr, Polycarp, Blandina, Roman Empire, Martyrdom</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the account of the Fifth Persecution under Marcus Aurelius, describing the brutal martyrdoms in Gaul and the subsequent relief brought by the "Thundering Legion" before transitioning to the reign of Septimius Severus and the start of the Sixth Persecution.</meta>

<page-num>XXV</page-num>
<header>The INTRODUCTION.</header>

her courage, and expired in the midst of the most acute tortures. **Pothinus**, Bishop of **Lyons**, who was over ninety years old, was beaten and stoned to death. **Sanctus**, a Deacon of **Vienne**, together with **Maturus**, were exposed in the Amphitheater, tormented, and imprisoned for several days together. They were presented to wild beasts, placed in a red-hot iron chair, and at last pierced through with a spear. **Attalus**, a **Roman** citizen, was disgracefully led up and down in triumph, roasted in an iron chair, and then beheaded; as was also **Alexander** the physician, a **Phrygian**, who readily professed himself a Christian. And **Ponticus**, a youth of fifteen years of age, entered into the Kingdom of Heaven after enduring all the methods of cruelty and torment that might have shaken even someone of a more mature age. A larger and more detailed account of all their martyrdoms is recorded in the letter written by the Churches of **Lyons** and **Vienne** in **France** to those of **Asia** and **Phrygia**, which is still preserved in the writings of **Eusebius** <note>Eusebius of Caesarea (c. 260–339 AD) was a historian often called the "Father of Church History."</note>. At length, the Emperor seems to have relaxed the persecution. He was inclined to do so, it is thought, by the remarkable victory which he gained in his **German wars** through the prayers of the Christian Legion. When the fortunes of the **Roman** Empire were at stake, the Christians so remarkably and immediately engaged heaven in its rescue and deliverance by supplying the army with rain and fighting against the enemy with lightning and thunder. Consequently, the Emperor is said to have written to the **Senate**, acknowledging the greatness of the blessing and commanding that all just favor and indulgence be shown to the Christians. The substance of the story is universally admitted by **Pagan** <note>original: "Gentile"</note> writers, though out of spite toward the Christians, they attribute it either to the power of magic or the effectiveness of the Emperor's own prayers.

<margin>
a. *Tertullian, Apology, chapter 5, page 6; see also To Scapula, chapter 4, page 71.*

</margin>
That such letters were written is plain, in that **Tertullian**^a, who lived only a little while after, cites them and appeals to them; though I confess little weight can be laid upon the version of the Epistle that exists today. There still exists^b a law of **Marcus Aurelius** and his brother **Verus** permitting those who follow the **"Jewish Superstition"**^c <note>original: "Jewish Superstition," a Roman legal term often applied to both Jews and Christians</note> to obtain public honors and granting them guards to defend them from wrong and injury. Very learned men understand this to refer to Christians, at least equally with the **Jews**; these two groups were commonly confused by the writers of those times, and "superstition" was the word by which they usually described Christianity. But however it was, this law was made before that **German victory**, as **Marcus Aurelius** was not engaged in that war until after the death of his brother **Verus**.

<margin>
d. *Spartianus, Life of Severus, chapter 14, page 349.*
</margin>
XXIV. THE Christian affairs were tolerably quiet and peaceful during the reigns of **Commodus**, **Pertinax**, and **Julian**, until **Severus** ascended the throne. He was a prince who was clever and learned, prudent and political, hardy and valiant, but also crafty and subtle, treacherous and unfaithful, bloody and passionate, and as the historian^d observes, of a nature truly answering to his name: *Truly persistent, truly severe* <note>original: "Vere Pertinax, vere Severus." A Latin pun on the names of his predecessors and his own name.</note>. Under him began the SIXTH PERSECUTION. For though at first he showed himself favorable to the Christians, he afterwards changed his mind and listened to those who slandered them as an impious and infamous generation—a people who intended nothing but treason and rebellion against the state. Consequently, he not only allowed his ministers and governors of provinces to treat them with all imaginable cruelty, but he himself issued edicts forbidding anyone, under the most terrible penalties, to profess either the **Jewish** or **Christian** religion. These were executed with such rigor and inhumanity that the Christians of those days truly believed that the times of **Antichrist** were then beginning to take...

It then introduces Emperor Septimius Severus, whose reign marked the beginning of the Sixth Persecution against Christians and Jews.</summary>

heaven. This took place. Notable martyrs whom this persecution sent to heaven were **Victor**, Bishop of **Rome**; **Leonidas**, **Origen’s** father (who was beheaded at **Alexandria**); **Serenus**, **Heraclides**, **Heron**, another **Serenus**, and **Herais**, a catechumen <term>catechumen: a person receiving basic instruction in the Christian faith before being baptized</term>—all of whom were **Origen’s** students. **Potamiana**, an illustrious virgin, and her mother **Marcella**, were committed to the flames after enduring various torments; **Basilides**, one of the officers who had led them to execution, also suffered. **Felicitas** and **Perpetua**, two noble ladies at **Tuburbis** in **Mauritania**, also died; one had just given birth the day before, and the other was still a nursing mother at that time. **Speratus** and his companions were beheaded at **Carthage** by the command of **Saturninus** the Proconsul. **Irenaeus**, Bishop of **Lyons**, and many thousands of his people were martyred with him; although their names and sufferings are unknown to us, they are honorably written in the *Book of Life*.

XXV. The next person to cause disturbance for the Christians was **Maximinus**, a **Thracian** by birth. He was a man of low and obscure origins, with a lowly and crude education. He had been first a shepherd, then a highwayman, and finally a soldier. He possessed strength and stature far beyond the ordinary size and standard. His manners were as robust and boisterous as his physique, and they completely reflected the coarseness of his upbringing. 

<margin>
ᵃ When Maximinus took power, he made a great change, using his authority very harshly and with much fear. From a poor and very mild reign, he tried to turn everything into the cruelty of a tyranny, being conscious of his own ill-omened start, because he was the first to rise from such extreme lowliness to such great fortune. By nature, his character was like his body—barbaric. Having inherited a murderous streak from his ancestors and his country, he planned to secure his rule through cruelty. <note>original: Ὁ δὲ Μαξιμῖνος... βεβαιῶσαι. Herodian, Book 7, in *Maximinus*, p. 253.</note>
² Julius Capitolinus, in *The Life of Maximinus*, chapter 9, p. 609.
</margin>

Never did a more cruel beast (says the historian ᵃ) walk the earth. Relying entirely on his strength, he considered himself almost immortal. He seized whatever came his way, plundering and destroying without any distinction and without any legal process or form of law. His strength was his only law of justice, and his will was the only measure of his actions. He spared no one, but he specifically killed everyone who knew anything about his humble descent, so that no one could reproach him for the obscurity of his birth. After murdering his master, **Alexander Mamaeus** <note>Also known as the Emperor Alexander Severus (reigned 222–235 AD); "Mamaeus" refers to his mother, Julia Mamaea.</note>, that excellent and incomparable prince, he usurped the government and managed it according to his own maxim: *that the Empire could only be maintained by cruelty*. 

The **SEVENTH PERSECUTION** was raised by him. Indeed, **Sulpitius Severus** includes this in his count and therefore lists only nine pagan persecutions,
<margin>
ᵇ *Ecclesiastical History*, Book 6, chapter 28, p. 228.
</margin>
reserving the *tenth* for the times of the **Antichrist**. But **Eusebius** ᵇ expressly states that **Maximinus** stirred up a persecution against the Christians. This was motivated by his hatred for his predecessor, in whose household many Christians had found shelter and patronage. However, this persecution was almost entirely aimed at the Bishops and ministers of religion, whom he saw as the primary authors and spreaders of Christianity. 

<margin>
ᶜ *Among the Letters of Cyprian*, p. 146.
</margin>

Regarding this, **Firmilian**, Bishop of **Cappadocia**, says in his letter to **St. Cyprian** ᶜ that it was not a general persecution but a local one that raged in particular places—especially in the province where he lived, as **Serenianus** the governor drove the Christians out of all those regions. He adds that many terrifying earthquakes happened in those parts, which overturned and swallowed up towns and cities, adding life and vigor to the persecution. It was common for the pagans to immediately attack the Christians whenever a famine, plague, earthquake, or flood occurred, 
<margin>
ᵈ *Homily 28 on Matthew*, folio 55, p. 2.
</margin>
concluding that they were the causes of all the evils and disasters that befell the world. And this is what **Origen** ᵈ meant when he tells us that he knew of some places destroyed by earthquakes, the cause of which the heathens...

<keywords>Septimius Severus, Maximinus Thrax, Seventh Persecution, Origen, Eusebius, Firmilian, Alexander Mamaeus, martyrdom, earthquakes, Roman Empire</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the persecutions under the Emperor Maximinus, specifically mentioning the work of Origen in response to the calamities blamed on Christians.</meta>

<page-num>XXVII</page-num>
<header>The INTRODUCTION.</header>

The heathens blamed the Christians for these calamities; consequently, their churches were persecuted and burned to the ground. Not only the common people, but even the wiser sort among them did not hesitate to openly claim that these disasters happened because of the Christians. In response, Origen wrote his book *On Martyrdom* <note>original: *De Martyrio*</note> for the comfort and support of those who suffered in this evil time.

XXVI. After **Maximinus**, **Pupienus** and **Balbinus** reigned; they were succeeded by **Gordian**, and he by **Philip**. During all this time, for at least ten years together, the Church enjoyed a reasonable calmness and tranquility. This lasted until **Decius** was essentially forced to take the Empire upon himself for his own defense. He was a man of great energy and determination—a strong commander and a wise, prudent governor. He was so universally respected for his modesty and excellent conduct that the Senate voted him no less than **Trajan**, awarding him the title of "Best" <note>original: *Optimus*</note>. However, he was a bitter and relentless enemy to Christians. He initiated the **EIGHTH PERSECUTION**, which proved to be the shortest, yet the most intense of all the persecutions that had afflicted and oppressed the Church until that time.

<margin>
* Eusebius, *Ecclesiastical History*, Book 6, Chapter 39, page 234. *Chronicle* for the year 252. Orosius, Book 7, Chapter 21, folio 310. Nicephorus, Book 5, Chapter 27, page 377.
</margin>

Church historians generally attribute this persecution to **Decius's** hatred of his predecessor, **Philip**, because Philip was a Christian. However, it is more accurately credited to his zeal for the cause of declining paganism. He saw that paganism was being fatally undermined by Christianity and believed there was no way to support the one without the ruin of the other. We have noted this more than once in the following *Lives*, and therefore we shall say less here. **Decius** reigned for a little over two years, during which time the storm was very dark and violent, and no place escaped its dreadful effects. Christians were everywhere driven from their houses, stripped of their estates, and tormented in their bodies. Whips and prisons, fires and wild beasts, scalding pitch and melted wax, sharp stakes and burning pincers were just some of the methods used against them; and when the old methods were exhausted, new ones were daily invented and contrived. The laws of nature and humanity were broken down; friends betrayed friends, and the nearest relative betrayed his own father or brother. Everyone was ambitious to enforce the Imperial Edicts and thought it meritorious to bring a Christian to the stake. This persecution swept away at **Alexandria**: **Julian**, **Chronion**, **Epimachus**, **Alexander**, **Ammon**, **Zeno**, **Ptolemy**, **Ammonaria**, **Mercuria**, **Isidore**, and many others mentioned by **Dionysius**, Bishop of that Church. At **Carthage**, it took **Mappalicus**, **Bassus**, **Fortunio**, **Paulus**, **Donatus**, **Martialis**, and others. It crowned <note>Meaning he received the "crown of martyrdom" through death.</note> **Babylas**, Bishop of **Antioch**; **Alexander** of **Jerusalem**; **Fabian**, Bishop of **Rome**; **Victoria**; **Anatolia**; **Parthenius**; **Marcellianus**; and thousands more.

<margin>
† Nicephorus, Book 5, Chapter 29, page 379.
</margin>

† **Nicephorus** stated that it would be easier to count the sands on the shore than to reckon up all the martyrs who suffered under this persecution. This is not even to mention those incredible numbers of "confessors" <term>confessor: a person who suffered and maintained their faith under torture or imprisonment but was not executed</term> who were beaten, imprisoned, and tormented; nor the far greater number of those who chose voluntary exile. They preferred to entrust themselves to the barrenness of rocks and mountains and the mercy of wild beasts rather than to those men who had abandoned all reason and humanity. Among these was **Paul of Thebais**, a youth of fifteen years of age, who withdrew into the **Egyptian** deserts. There, he found a large and convenient cave in a rock (which in earlier times had been a pri- <note>The text ends mid-word; historical accounts of Paul of Thebes usually describe this cave as a secret mint for counterfeit coins during the time of Antony and Cleopatra.</note>

<summary>This page describes the transition from a period of relative peace for the Church to the "Eighth Persecution" under Emperor Decius, detailing the extreme brutality and the numerous martyrs across the Roman Empire.</summary>
<keywords>Decius, Eighth Persecution, martyrs, Alexandria, Carthage, Paul of Thebais, Origen, Christian history</keywords>

<page-num>xxviii</page-num>
<header>The INTRODUCTION.</header>

(a private mint-house during the time of Antony and Cleopatra) he made his home and residence. There, he led a solitary and anchoretic <term>anchoretic: a lifestyle of extreme seclusion and religious devotion, typical of a hermit</term> way of life, becoming the father of hermits and those who later desired to withdraw from the world to devote themselves to solitude and a more strictly disciplined life. In this pious and devout retirement, he continued until he was 113 years old. In the final period of his life, he was visited by Antony <note>Saint Anthony the Great</note>, who had spent the better part of 90 years in those desert places, and who now performed the final service for him by burying his body in the earth.

### XXVII.

**Gallus** succeeded **Decius** both in his government and in his enmity toward Christians, continuing what the other had begun. But that trouble soon passed; for after he was killed, he was succeeded by **Valerian**, who began his reign with universal applause and high expectations. In the beginning of his reign, he was a great supporter of Christians, treating them with all kindness and humanity and even welcoming them into his own family. Indeed, his court seemed to be a small church for piety and a sanctuary for good men. But, alas, this pleasant scene was quickly over. Seduced by a chief magician of Egypt, who persuaded him that the only way to prosper in his affairs was to restore the pagan rituals <term>original: "Gentile Rites"</term> and suppress Christianity—which was claimed to be hateful to the gods—he began a **NINTH PERSECUTION**. In this, he prosecuted the Christians with all imaginable fury in every part of the Empire.

<margin>* Letter to Dionysius of Alexandria, quoted in Eusebius, *Ecclesiastical History*, Book 7, Chapter 11, page 250.</margin>

The fierceness with which it raged in Egypt is recorded at length by **Dionysius of Alexandria**, and we have noted much of it in his *Life*. "There is less need," he says, "to specifically list the Christians who suffered in this persecution. You may simply observe this: that both men and women, young and old, soldiers and country people—persons of all ranks and ages—were some of them scourged and whipped, others beheaded, and others, overcoming the violence of flames, received the crown of martyrdom."

**Cyprian** eloquently and passionately laments the miseries and sufferings that the martyrs underwent in his letter to **Nemesianus** and the others who were condemned to the mines. Nor did he himself escape, being beheaded at Carthage, just as **Sixtus** <note>original: "Xistus"</note> and **Quartus** had been before him. So too were the three hundred martyrs of the "White Mass" <note>original: *De Massa Candida*</note>, who, rather than offer sacrifices to idols, cheerfully leapt into a massive pit of burning lime kindled for that purpose and were immediately suffocated in the smoke and flames. In Spain, **Fructuosus**, Bishop of Tarragon, suffered together with his two deacons, **Augurius** and **Eulogius**. At Rome, **Sixtus** the Bishop and **Saint Lawrence**, his deacon and the treasurer of that church, were martyred. At Caesarea, **Priscus**, **Malchus**, and **Alexander**—ashamed to think that they remained idle and safe while so many others were contending for the crown—unanimously went to the judge, confessed they were Christians, received their sentence, and underwent their martyrdom.

But Divine Providence, which sometimes pleads the cause of oppressed innocence in this world, was resolved to punish the Emperor for his senseless cruelty toward those whose favor with heaven had secured his happiness while he remained kind to them. Therefore, God not only allowed the Northern Nations to invade his territory, but the Emperor himself was taken prisoner by **Sapor** <note>Shapur I</note>, King of Persia. Sapor treated him worse than the lowest slave, using him as a footstool to mount his horse. After several years of captivity, Sapor caused him to be flayed alive and rubbed with salt, thus ending

his miserable life. This was a fair warning to his son **Gallienus**, who, growing wiser because of the mischief and miscarriages of his father, stopped the persecution and restored peace and security to Christians.

<margin>But you, Valerian, etc. <note>original: Ἀλλὰ Κόγεσ, &c.</note></margin>
