### XXVIII.

A long period of peace and prosperity had somewhat corrupted the manners of Christians. Except for a little disturbance during the time of **Aurelian**, they met with no opposition through the reigns of **Gallienus**, **Claudius**, **Tacitus**, **Florianus**, **Probus**, **Carus**, and **Numerian**. Therefore, God was pleased to permit a **TENTH PERSECUTION** to come upon them to purge and winnow <term>winnow: to blow air through grain to separate the valuable seeds from the waste (chaff)</term> the rubbish and the chaff. The ulcer had begun to putrefy, and it was time to call for the knife and the caustic <term>caustic: a substance used to burn away infected or dead flesh</term>. 

This persecution began under **Diocletian** and his colleague **Maximian**. Diocletian was an active and diligent prince, crafty and subtle, and fierce by nature, though he knew how to cunningly disguise it. His zeal for the pagan religion engaged him with all possible earnestness to oppose Christianity. He carried this out with a heavy hand, as it was both the last and the fiercest persecution—like the final efforts of a dying enemy who summons all his strength to give the parting blow. 

While residing at **Nicomedia**, Diocletian published his edicts around the very time of the solemnity of our Savior’s Passion <note>Good Friday/Easter</note>. He commanded the Christian churches to be pulled down, their Bibles to be burnt, the higher-ranking Christians to be branded with infamy, and the common people to be made slaves. In subsequent orders, he commanded the bishops to be imprisoned everywhere and forced to offer sacrifice to pagan gods. But these were only a prelude <note>original: præludium</note> to what followed. Other proclamations were put forth, commanding that those who refused to offer sacrifice be exposed to all manner of torments. 

It would be endless to count the particular persons who suffered at this evil time. **Eusebius**, who lived during this very persecution, has recorded a vast number of them, along with the *Acts* of their *Martyrdom*; they are too many to account for here. It is enough to note from his records that they were scourged to death, had their flesh torn off with pincers or raked off with pieces of broken pottery, and were cast to lions, tigers, wild boars, and bears—animals which were provoked and enraged with fire to set upon them. They were burnt, beheaded, crucified, thrown into the sea, or torn in pieces by the distorted boughs of trees. Their legs were miserably stretched in the stocks; they were roasted over a gentle fire or had melted lead poured into their bowels through holes made for that purpose. 

It is impossible to conceive, much less to express, the cruelties of that time. Eusebius himself, who saw them, tells us that they were innumerable and exceeded all description. <margin>Book 8, chapter 12, page 307.</margin> He assures us that they endured all of this with the most admirable and undaunted patience. They thronged to the tribunals of their judges and freely told them who they were; they despised the threats and barbarity of their enemies and received the fatal and final sentence with a smile. When persuaded to be careful with their lives and to have compassion for their wives and children, they resisted the temptation with a manly and philosophical mind—or as Eusebius adds, "rather with a soul truly pious and devoted unto God" <gloss>rather with a pious and God-loving soul <note>original: μᾶλλον ἢ ἐὐσεβεῖ & ϕιλοθέῳ ψυχῆ</note></gloss>. Thus, neither fears nor charms could take hold of them. They gave undeniable evidence of both their own courage and fortitude, and of that divine and inconceivable power of our Lord that accompanied them.

<page-num>xxx</page-num>
<header>The INTRODUCTION.</header>

<margin>
a *Against Celsus, book 7, p. 357.* <note>original: "Contr. Cels. l. 7. P. 357."</note>
b *Preparation for the Gospel, book 1, chapter 4, p. 13.* <note>original: "Praepar. Evan. l. 1. c. 4. p. 13."</note>
c *In Gruter, page 280, numbers 3 & 4.* <note>original: "Ap. Gruter. pag. CCLXXX. num. 3. & 4."</note>
</margin>
them. The sharpest torments did not shake the firmness and stability of their minds, but they could, with as much indifference, lay down their lives (as Origen$^a$ tells Celsus) as easily as the best philosopher could take off his coat. They valued their innocence above their ease, or life itself, and sufficiently showed they believed in another state of being by an argument stronger than what any school of philosophy could provide. "The pagans," (as Eusebius$^b$ argues in this matter) "as much as they talk of immortality and the happiness of the future state, nevertheless showed that they looked upon it only as a childish and a trivial report. Whereas among us, even boys and girls—and in outward appearance, the most humble and uneducated persons—being assisted by the power and aid of our Blessed Saviour, do by their actions rather than their words demonstrate the truth of this great principle: the immortality of the soul." This persecution lasted in its full strength and vigor for ten years under **Diocletian** in the East and **Maximian** in the West. It seems they thought they had finished their work, and accordingly they told the world in some ancient inscriptions$^c$ that they had utterly erased the name and "superstition" of the Christians, and had restored and spread the worship of the gods. But they were miserably mistaken in the matter; and, as if weary of the work, they resigned their imperial authority <note>original: "laid down their purple," referring to the purple robes worn by Roman emperors</note> and retired to the solitude of a private life. And though **Galerius**, **Maximianus**, **Jovius Maximinus**, **Maxentius**, and **Licinius** did what they could to set the persecution in motion again, it was all in vain; both they and the persecution expired and dwindled into nothing within a very few years.

<margin>
d *Apology, final chapter, p. 40.* <note>original: "Apolog. c. ult. p. 40."</note>
e *On Justice, book 5, chapter 13, p. 494.* <note>original: "De Justit. l. 5. c. 13. p. 494."</note>
</margin>
XXIX. THUS we have seen the hardships and miseries, the torments and sufferings to which the Christians were exposed for several ages, and with what invincible patience they endured them. Let us now briefly review the argument and see what force and influence it had to convince the world of the truth of their religion and bring converts to the faith. Tertullian$^d$ tells the pagans, "That all your cruelty was to no purpose; it was only a stronger invitation to bring others over to our side. The more often you mowed us down, the faster we sprang up again; and the blood of Christians was a seed that grew into a more plentiful harvest. Several among the pagans have exhorted their listeners to patience under suffering, but they could never make as many converts <term>proselytes: people who have moved from one opinion or belief to another</term> with all their fine speeches as the Christians did by their actions. That very persistence <note>original: "obstinacy"</note> which was so often charged against them served as a teacher to instruct others. For who, when they beheld such things, could not be powerfully moved to inquire what was truly at the heart of it? Who, when he had once found it, would not embrace it? And having once embraced it, who would not be desirous to suffer for it, so that he may obtain the full grace of God and the pardon of his sins, assured by the shedding of his own blood?" Lactantius$^e$ manages this argument with incomparable eloquence and strength of reason; his discourse is somewhat long, but not unworthy of the reader's consideration. "Since our number," says he, "is always increased from among the followers of the heathen deities, and is never lessened—not even in the hottest persecution—who is so blind and stupid as not to see in which party true wisdom resides? But they, alas, are blinded with rage and malice, and think everyone to be fools who, when it is in their power to escape punishment, choose rather to be tortured and to die. Yet they might perceive by this that it can be no such folly, since so many thousands throughout the whole world unanimously agree in it. Suppose

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the fortitude of Christian martyrs during times of intense persecution, specifically contrasting their genuine endurance with the theoretical ideals of Roman stoicism and pagan heroism.</meta>

<page-num>xxxi</page-num>
<header>The INTRODUCTION.</header>

->### The INTRODUCTION.<-

<margin>Horace, *Odes*, Book 3, Ode 3, page 154. <note>original: "Horat. Carm. l. 3. Od. 3. p. 154."</note></margin>
"Women, through the weakness of their sex, may fail (and they <note>The pagan critics of Christianity.</note> are pleased sometimes to style this religion an *effeminate* and *old wives' superstition*); yet certainly men are wiser. If children and young men may be rash, yet at least those of a mature age and old men have a more stable judgment. If one city might play the fool, yet innumerable others cannot be supposed to be guilty of the same folly. If one province, or one nation, should lack care and foresight, yet all the rest cannot lack the understanding to judge what is right. 

But now, when the Divine Law is accepted from the rising of the sun to its setting, and every sex, age, nation, and country serves God with one heart and soul; when there is everywhere the same patience and contempt for death, they ought to consider that there is some reason for it. It is not without cause that it is maintained even unto death. There must be some fixed foundation when a religion is not only not shattered by injuries and persecutions, but always increased and rendered more firm and stable. 

When even the common people see men torn in pieces by various engines of torment, and yet maintain an unconquerable patience in the midst of their tired tormentors, they cannot help but realize the truth: that the agreement of so many, and their perseverance until death, cannot be in vain. Nor could that patience itself, without divine assistance, be able to overcome such exquisite tortures. Highwaymen and persons of the most robust constitutions are not able to bear such pulling apart; they roar, and groan, and sink under pain, because they are not furnished with a divine patience. 

But our very children (to say nothing of our men) and our tender women conquer their tormentors through silence; nor can the flames extort even one sigh from them. Let the *Romans* go now and boast of their *Mucius* and their *Regulus* <note>Mucius Scaevola and Marcus Atilius Regulus were legendary Roman heroes celebrated for their endurance of pain and loyalty to the state.</note>. One of them delivered himself up to be put to death by his enemies because he was ashamed to live as a prisoner; the other thrust his hand into the fire when he saw he could not escape death. 

Behold, among us the weaker sex and the more delicate age allow their whole bodies to be torn and burnt—not because they could not avoid it if they wished, but voluntarily, because they trust in God. This is true virtue, which philosophers only talk about in vain when they tell us that nothing is so suitable to the dignity and consistency of a wise man as not being driven from his sentiments and opinions by any terrors. They say it is virtuous, and great indeed, to be tortured and die rather than betray one's faith, or fail in one's duty, or do anything that is unjust or dishonest, even for fear of death or the sharpest torment. Unless, of course, they think their own poet was raving when he said:

> *The man who is just and firm in his purpose,* <note>original: "Justum & tenacem propositi virum,"</note>
> *Neither the heat of citizens commanding wrong things,* <note>original: "Non civium ardor prava jubentium,"</note>
> *Nor the face of a threatening tyrant,* <note>original: "Non vultus instantis tyranni"</note>
> *Shakes his solid mind.* <note>original: "Mente quatit solida."</note>

The just man who stands resolved,
Neither tyrants' frowns, nor fierce commands,
Nor all the people's rage combined,
Can shake the firmness of his mind.

"Nothing can be more truly said than this, if it is meant of those who refuse no tortures, nor death itself, so that they may preserve fidelity..."

<meta>This page completes the sentence and quote started on the previous page regarding the endurance of Christian martyrs. The previous sentence likely described people of robust constitution acting out of "fidelity and justice."</meta>

<page-num>xxxii</page-num>
<header>The INTRODUCTION.</header>

> "...fidelity and justice; who disregard the commands of tyrants and the swords of governors so that they may, with a steady mind, preserve real and solid liberty—the state in which true wisdom alone is maintained." So far goes the argument of that eloquent apologist <note>An 'apologist' is someone who writes a formal defense of a belief or cause, in this case, likely Lactantius.</note>. And certainly, the truth of his reasoning was fully confirmed by world history. Christians gained ground and overcame opposition through nothing more than their patience and steadfastness, until they had brought the Empire itself to acknowledge the truth. And once Constantine the Great had embraced Christianity, it moved forward with the wind and tide, sweeping away everything in its path. It would surely be a fascinating study to consider the true state of paganism under the first Christian emperors, and to see how—and by what degrees—that religion, which had governed the world for so many centuries, faded away into obscurity and silence. However, exploring that subject is beyond the scope of my current investigation.

1. <image-desc>Decorative initial 'T' with floral and fruit motifs</image-desc>The Christian religion, being designed by God for the reformation of mankind and the rooting out of that barbarism and idolatry with which the world was so overgrown, could not help but meet with opposition, as all corrupt interests conspired to give it a very unwelcome reception. Vice and error had too long usurped the throne to part with it by a tame and easy resignation; instead, they would rather summon all their forces against a doctrine that openly proclaimed the subversion and ruin of their empire. Hence, this *sect* was everywhere *spoken against*, equally opposed by both *Jew* and *Gentile*.

The *Gentiles* despised it for its recent appearance and novelty, as it had no antiquity to recommend it. Nor could they endure that their philosophy, which then everywhere ruled the academic chair, should be controlled by a plain, simple doctrine that pretended to no elaborate schemes, no persuasive strains of eloquence, no precise and subtle arts of reasoning, nor any abstruse and sublime speculations.

The *Jews* were vexed to see their expectations of a mighty prince—who should greatly exalt their state and redeem it from that oppression and slavery under which it groaned—frustrated by the coming of a *Messiah* who appeared under all the circumstances of meanness and disgrace. He was so far from rescuing them from the power of the *Roman yoke* that, for their obstinacy and unbelief, he threatened the final and irrevocable ruin of their country. Furthermore, by the doctrine he published, he plainly told them he intended to abolish those ancient *Mosaic institutions* <note>original: "Mosaick Institutions"</note> for which they had such dear regards and so solemn a veneration.

Accordingly, when he came among them, they treated him with every instance of cruelty and contempt, and whatever might expose him to the scorn and hatred <note>original: "odium"</note> of the people. They vilified and reproached his person as being merely the son of a carpenter, a glutton and a drunkard, a traitor and an *enemy unto Caesar*. They slighted his doctrine as the talk only of a rude and illiterate person, and traduced his miracles as tricks of imposture, and the...

# The Life of St. Stephen the Protomartyr.

...the effects of a dark conspiracy with demonic <note>original: "infernal"</note> powers. When all of this failed to work, they violently seized him and took his life. One would have thought their spite and fury would have cooled and died out after this; but malice and revenge are too fierce and hot to stop at the first attempt. They resolved to continue these bloody methods, showing the world that the disciples and followers must expect no better treatment than their Master. It was only a few months before they took the opportunity to renew their rage in St. Stephen's martyrdom. We now come to relate the history of his life and death and to make some brief remarks upon it.

II. The sacred story gives us no specific account of either the country or the family <note>original: "Kindred"</note> of this holy man. That he was a Jew is unquestionable; he sufficiently admits the relationship in his defense <note>original: "Apology"</note> to the people. However, whether he was originally descended from the stock of Abraham, or born of parents who were admitted through conversion <note>original: "Proselytism"</note>, or whether he was born at Jerusalem or among those dispersed in the non-Jewish <note>original: "Gentile"</note> provinces, is impossible to determine. Baronius <note>Cesare Baronius: A 16th-century Italian cardinal and historian known for his ecclesiastical history of the Church.</note> (basing his guess upon a letter from Lucian, which we will discuss later) suggests he was one of Gamaliel’s disciples and a fellow student of St. Paul, who later became his mortal enemy. But I must confess, I find no shadow of probability in that entire letter to support that guess. Ancient tradition suggests, quite possibly, that he was one of the seventy <note>original: "LXX"</note> disciples chosen by our Lord to assist the Apostles in the ministry of the Gospel. Indeed, his admirable knowledge of Christian doctrine and his singular ability to defend the cause of Christ's status as the Messiah against its most sharp-witted opponents plainly argue that he had been trained for a significant time under our Savior's immediate instruction. It is certain that he was a man of great zeal and piety, endowed with extraordinary measures of that divine Spirit that was recently poured upon the Church, and incomparably equipped with miraculous powers. These qualities uniquely qualified him for a place of honor and usefulness in the Church, to which he was promoted on this occasion.

III. Among the many examples of religious practice for which the primitive Church was famous and respected, it was most remarkable for its charity. The members lived and loved as brothers, *were of one heart and one soul, and remained together in one accord.* Love and charity were the common soul that animated the whole body of believers, providing warmth and life to every part. They prayed and worshipped God in the same place and ate together at the same table. No one could be in need, for *they held everything in common.* The rich sold their estates to serve the needs of the poor and placed the money into one common treasury. The care of this treasury was committed to the Apostles, who ensured distribution was made as everyone's case and needs required. But even in the most perfect harmony, there will be some conflicts and discord; only heaven is free from quarrels and the causes of offense. As the Church increased every day with vast numbers of converts to the faith, the Apostles could not precisely supervise the distribution of the Church’s funds and provide for every group. They were therefore likely forced to take in the help of others, sometimes more and sometimes fewer, to assist in this affair. Because of this, a proper equality and balance were not maintained, but either through favoritism and partiality, or the oversight of

--- Page 78 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the early Church's internal challenges, specifically the social tensions that arose during the distribution of charitable aid, which led to the appointment of the first deacons, including St. Stephen.</meta>

Among those who managed the distribution of aid, some received larger portions, while others received less relief than their actual <note>original: "just"</note> needs required. This caused some immediate tension <note>original: "heats"</note> and hostility in the first and purest Church that ever existed: <margin>Acts 6:1.</margin> *the Grecians murmuring against the Hebrews because their widows were neglected in the daily distribution.*

IV. It may be helpful to investigate who these *Grecians* or *Hellenists* were, who are contrasted here with the *Hebrews*, even though it is a matter of some difficulty and debate. The most generally accepted opinion is that they were originally *Jews* born and raised in *Grecian* or Heathen <margin>John 7:35.</margin> countries—part of the "dispersion among the Greeks" <note>original: "διασπορὰ τῶν Ἑλλήνων"</note> (the word *Greeks* <note>original: "Ἕλληνες"</note> in the style of the New Testament, as well as in the writings of the Church Fathers, being commonly used for the non-Jewish <note>original: "Gentile"</note> world). These people adapted to the Greek way of life and spoke the Greek language, though it was mixed with *Hebraisms* and *Jewish* forms of speech (called the *Hellenistic Language* <note>original: "Lingua Hellenistica"</note>), and they used no other Bible but the Greek translation of the *Septuagint* <note>The Septuagint is the earliest Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible, completed around the 2nd century BCE.</note>. 

However, the scholar *Salmasius* <margin>Salmasius, *Commentary on the Hellenists*, Questions 1, 2, 3, 4, 5; especially page 232, etc. See also, among others, Beza and Camerarius on this passage.</margin> has taken great pains to refute this. He shows that no people ever went by that name or description, and that the *Jews*, in whatever parts of the world they lived, were not considered a distinct nation from those who lived in *Palestine*. He argues there was never any such peculiar, distinct *Hellenistic* dialect mentioned by any ancient writer, and that the phrase is very improper for expressing such a mixed language. Rather, he suggests that "Hellenist" <note>original: "Ἑλληνιστὴς"</note> implies someone who expresses themselves in better *Greek* than usual, just as "Atticizer" <note>original: "Ἀττικιστὴς"</note> denotes someone who studies to speak pure *Attic Greek*. 

It is probable, therefore, that they were not of the *Hebrew* race, but were *Greek* or Gentile *proselytes* (converts). These people, or their ancestors, had abandoned pagan superstitions and joined the *Jewish* Church, accepting circumcision and the observance of the rites of the *Mosaic* laws. The *Jews* called such people *Proselytes of Righteousness* <note>original: "גרי הצדק," transliterated as *Gere Ha-Tzedek*</note>. Many of them had now converted to Christianity. 

It is evident that there were great numbers of these proselytes at *Jerusalem* at this time. It would be strange if, when they were so eager to have the Gospel preached to them at other times, none of them had been brought over to the faith. Even among the *seven* chosen to be *deacons*—most, if not all, of whom we may reasonably conclude were chosen from these *Grecians*—we find one explicitly said to have been a *proselyte of Antioch*. In all likelihood, some or all of the others might have been *proselytes of Jerusalem*. 

This interpretation holds whenever we meet the word *Hellenists* or *Grecians* in the history of <margin>Acts 9:29; 11:20.</margin> the *Acts of the Apostles* (where it appears in two other places). So, these *Hellenists* (who spoke Greek and used the translation of the *Septuagint*) were *Jews* by religion but Gentiles by descent. They shared the same origin as the *Greeks* <note>original: "Ἕλληνες"</note> but the same faith as the *Jews*. Therefore, they are not contrasted here with *Jews* (a term that could apply to anyone embracing *Judaism* and the rites of *Moses*, even if not born of *Jewish* ancestors), but with the *Hebrews*, who were *Jews* by both religion and nationality. 

This may provide a probable explanation for why the widows of these *Hellenists* were not as well cared for as those of the *Hebrews*. The people whom the Apostles largely entrusted with the distribution were naturally more helpful to those of their own nation, their neighbors, and perhaps their own relatives.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the early Christian Church's internal challenges. It explains why the Apostles decided to appoint deacons to manage the distribution of resources, ensuring that the needs of both native Jews and Hellenistic Jews were met fairly.</meta>

# The Life of St. STEPHEN the Protomartyr.

...then to those who only agreed with them in the profession of the same religion, and who indeed were not generally as capable of contributing to the Church's stock as the native *Jews*, who had lands and possessions, which they *sold and laid at the Apostles' feet*.

V. The peace and quiet of the Church being by this means a little ruffled and unsettled, the Apostles—who well understood how much order and unity contributed to the goals of religion—immediately called the Church together. They told them that disposing of the common stock and daily providing for the necessities of the poor, however convenient and necessary, was a matter of too much trouble and distraction to be consistent with a faithful discharge of the other parts and duties of their office. They did not judge it fit and reasonable to neglect the one to attend to the other. Therefore, they suggested that the people should choose from among themselves some who were duly qualified and present them to the Apostles. These men would be set apart specifically to supervise this affair, so that the Apostles themselves, being freed from these burdens, might more freely and without interruption devote themselves to prayer and preaching of the Gospel. 

It was not that the Apostles thought caring for the poor was an office too far below them, but rather that this work might be performed by other hands, allowing them to better attend to things of higher importance—ministries more immediately serviceable to the souls of men. This was the first origin of *Deacons* <note>The word "Deacon" comes from the Greek *diakonos*, meaning a servant or minister.</note> in the Christian Church. They were to *serve tables*—that is, to attend to the necessities of the poor, to make daily provisions for their public feasts, to keep the Church’s treasure, and to distribute to everyone according to their need. And this perfectly agrees with one common meaning of the word *Diakonos* <note>original: "Διάκονος"</note> in ancient non-Christian writers, where it is used for that specific servant who waited at feasts, whose office it was to distribute the portions to every guest, either according to the command of the *Architriklinos* <note>original: "Ἀρχιτρίκλινος," meaning the steward or master of the feast</note> or according to the rule of equality, giving everyone an equal share.

<margin>"A portion of meat, and equal to all; let the deacons do nothing for favor to anyone. Let them not place a large portion before me and a very small one before another, but equality for all." <note>original: "Μοῖρα κρεῶν, καὶ ἴσον ἁπᾶσιν' οἱ Διάκονοι πρὸς χάριν μηδενὶ μηδέν. Μὴ τῷ μὲ μεγάλα, τῷ δ' κομιδῇ μικρὰ παρατιθέτω, ἀλλ' ἰσότης ἐπὶ πᾶσιν."</note> Lucian, *Chronosolon, or on the Saturnalian Laws*, Volume 2, page 823.</margin>

But though it is true this was a main part of the Deacon's office, it was not the whole of it. For had this been all, the Apostles would not have needed to be so exact and careful in their choice of persons—since men of an ordinary rank and very average ability might have served that purpose—nor would they have used such solemn rites of consecration <term>consecration: the formal act of dedicating someone to a sacred office; ordination</term> to ordain them to it. No question, therefore, but their *serving tables* also implied their attendance at the table of the Lord’s Supper. 

<margin>"And it is necessary that those who are deacons of the mysteries of Jesus Christ should in every way please everyone; for they are not deacons of food and drink (meaning, not only that), but servants of the Church of God. It is necessary, therefore, that they avoid accusations as they would fire." <note>original: "Δεῖ δὲ καὶ τοὺς Διακόνους ὄντας μυστηρίων Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ κατὰ πάντα τρόπον πᾶσιν ἀρέσκειν' οὐ γὰρ βρωμάτων καὶ ποτῶν (h. e. non solum) εἰσὶν Διάκονοι, ἀλλ' ἐκκλησίας Θεοῦ ὑπηρέται' δέον οὖν αὐτοὺς φυλάσσεσθαι τὰ ἐγκλήματα ὡς πῦρ."</note> Ignatius, *Epistle to the Trallians*, Ussher’s Appendix, page 17.</margin>

For in those days their *Agapae* <note>The *Agapae* were "love-feasts," communal meals shared by early Christians to signify their fellowship.</note> or common love-feasts (at which both rich and poor sat down together) were held at the same time as the Holy Eucharist, and both were administered every day, so that their ministry respected both the one and the other.

<margin>Justin Martyr, *Apology II*, page 97.</margin>
And we find this was the practice of the Church: for *Justin Martyr* tells us it was so in his time, that when the President of the assembly had consecrated the Eucharist, the Deacons distributed the bread and the wine to all who were present, and afterward carried them to those who were necessarily absent from the congregation. Nor were they restricted to this one particular service, but were in some cases allowed to preach, baptize, and absolve penitents <term>absolve penitents: to formally declare the forgiveness of sins for those who have repented</term>, especially where they had the specific warrant and autho-

...under the authority of the Bishop to support them; nor do we need to look far beyond the present story to find St. Philip, one of the Deacons elected here, both preaching the Gospel and baptizing converts with great success.

VI. In order that this excellent office might be properly managed, the Apostles directed and commanded the Church to nominate people who were suitable for it: pious and good men of known honesty and integrity, of approved and untainted reputations, furnished and endowed with the extraordinary gifts of the Holy Spirit—wise and prudent men who would discreetly fulfill the trust given to them. The number of these people was limited to *seven*, probably for no other reason than because the Apostles thought this was enough for the business; unless we also suppose the entire body of believers was divided into seven different sections for a more orderly and convenient management of their common feasts and distributions to the poor, and that a Deacon was appointed to supervise and direct each of these groups; <margin>* See Baronius, Year 112, no. 7, Volume 2.</margin> without further intending any specific mystery, which *some would like to find in it. Nevertheless, the Church thought it right for a long time to follow this original institution, so much so that the Fathers of the <margin>† Council of Neo-Caesarea, canon 15, Council Volume 1, Column 1484.</margin> † Council of Neo-Caesarea ordained that in no city, however great, should there be more than *seven Deacons*—a rule <note>original: "Canon"</note> which they based <margin>∴ Ecclesiastical History, book 7, chapter 19, p. 734.</margin> ∴ upon this passage. And ∴ *Sozomen* tells us that in his time, though many other Churches did not stick to a specific number, the Church of *Rome*—in compliance with this apostolic example—admitted no more than seven Deacons. The people were very pleased with the order and decision the Apostles had made in this matter and accordingly chose seven men, whom they presented to the Apostles. The Apostles (as the solemnity of the event required) first made their appeal to Heaven through prayer for a divine blessing on the undertaking and then laid their hands upon them—an ancient symbolic rite of investment <note>original: "Inveštiture"</note> and consecration to any extraordinary office. The result of all this was that the Christian religion gained ground and prospered. Converts came flocking to the faith—even many of the *priests* themselves and those of their tribe and family. These men, who were usually the most zealous and stubborn <note>original: "pertinacious"</note> defenders of the *Mosaic* laws <note>original: "Constitutions"</note>, the bitterest enemies of Christian doctrine, and the cleverest defenders of their own religion, laid aside their prejudices and embraced the Gospel. So unstoppable is the power of divine truth that it often leads its greatest enemies in triumph behind it.

<margin>* Epiphanius, Heresy 20, p. 27.</margin> VII. The first and chief of the people elected here (who were all chosen from the seventy disciples <note>The "seventy disciples" or "LXX" refers to the larger group of followers commissioned by Jesus in the Gospel of Luke.</note>, as * *Epiphanius* informs us) was our St. *Stephen*. The ancients frequently call him the *Archdeacon* because he held the <language>Greek</language>primacy <note>original: "πρωτεῖα"</note></language> (as <margin>† Chrysostom, Homily 15 on Acts, p. 555.</margin> † *Chrysostom* says)—the leadership and precedence among these newly elected officers. He is identified by the author of <margin>∴ Letter to Hero in the Library of the Greek and Latin Church Fathers, p. 37.</margin> the letter to ∴ *Hero* (written under the name of *Ignatius*), as well as the editor <note>original: "Interpolator"</note> of the letter to the <margin>‥ Letter to the Trallians, p. 6, in the same place.</margin> ‥ *Trallians*, as having been in a more specific way the Deacon to St. *James* in his role as Bishop of *Jerusalem*. He is not only placed first in the list but specifically recommended with this description: *a man full of faith and of the Holy Spirit*. He was expertly skilled in all parts of Christian doctrine and equipped with great eloquence and public speaking skills <note>original: "elocution"</note> to declare and publish it. He was enriched with many miraculous gifts and powers, and a spirit of courage and resolution to face the most powerful opposition. He preached and pleaded the cause

<meta>This page continues the biography of St. Stephen, moving from his appointment as a Deacon to his active defense of the Christian faith against its first major opponents in Jerusalem.</meta>

<header>The Life of St. STEPHEN the Protomartyr.</header>

He defended the cause of Christianity with a firm and fearless mind; and so that nothing would be lacking to make it effective, he confirmed his doctrine with many public and unquestionable miracles—clear evidence and proof of the truth and divinity of the religion he taught. But truth and innocence, though a better cause, are the usual targets of spiteful and hateful people. The zeal and diligence of his ministry, and the extraordinary success that followed it, quickly awakened the malice of the *Jews*, and there was no shortage of those who were ready to oppose and contradict him. It is natural for error to rise up against the truth, just as light and darkness mutually resist and expel each other.

<margin>*Oration on St. Stephen, Volume 6, p. 276.*</margin>
VIII. In *Jerusalem*, besides the Temple where sacrifices and the more solemn parts of their religion were performed, there were vast numbers of synagogues for prayer and the expounding of the Law; the *Jews* themselves tell us there were no fewer than 480 in that city. In these, or at least in some apartments adjoining them, there were *schools* or *colleges* for the instruction and education of scholars in their laws. Many of these were built at the expense of *Jews* who lived in foreign countries and were named after them. To these schools, they used to send their youth to be trained in the knowledge of the Law and the mysterious rites of their religion. Five of these synagogues combined together to send some of their members to confront and oppose St. *Stephen*. It was an unequal match! "A five-citied union of most wicked men," <note>original: "ανδρῶν ασεβεστάτων Πεντάπολις"</note> as *Chrysostom* calls it—a whole army of wicked adversaries, the leaders of five different synagogues, were brought out against one man, and him a young man <note>original: "stripling"</note> at that. It was as if they intended to overwhelm him by the number of attackers rather than to overcome him by the strength of their arguments.

<margin>*Junius on this passage and on Genesis 8:4.*</margin>
<margin>*Suidas on the word "Libertines."*</margin>
<margin>*Jewish Antiquities, book 17, chapter 12, p. 610.*</margin>
<margin>*Philo, On the Embassy to Gaius, p. 785.*</margin>
IX. THE first of them were those of the *Synagogue* of the *Libertines*; but who these "Libertines" were is a matter of various debate. We can set aside the notion held by *Junius* that *Labra* signified in the *Egyptian* language the entire district under one synagogue, from which *Labratenu*—or, as he says, the corrupted form *Libertini*—must refer to those belonging to the *Synagogue* of the *Alexandrians*. We omit this as altogether absurd and fanciful, especially since the *Synagogue* of the *Alexandrians* is mentioned separately later. *Suidas* tells us it was the name of a nation, but he leaves us entirely in the dark as to what part of the world this people or country was in. It is most probable, therefore, that the name refers to the *Jews* who were emancipated and set free. To understand this, we must know that when *Pompey* had subdued *Judea* and brought it under *Roman* government, he carried great numbers of *Jews* captive to *Rome*. The generals who succeeded him did the same, and in such multitudes that when the *Jewish* state sent an embassy to *Augustus*, *Josephus* tells us there were about eight thousand *Jews* then living at *Rome* who joined the ambassadors upon their arrival. Here they continued as slaves until, by degrees, they were granted their freedom <note>original: "manumitted"</note>. This was generally done in the time of *Tiberius*, who (as *Philo* informs us) allowed the *Jews* to inhabit the *Transtiberine* region <note>The area across the Tiber River in Rome.</note>. Most of these were *Libertines* <note>In Roman law, a "Libertinus" was a freedman—a person who had been legally freed from slavery.</note>—those who, having been made captives by the fortune of war, had been set free by their masters and permitted to live according to the traditions of their ancestors. They had their *houses of prayer* <note>original: "Proseucha's"</note> or oratories where they assembled and performed their devotions according to the religion of their country; every

<margin>* Tacitus, *Annals*, book 2, chapter 85, page 88.</margin>
<margin>† Suetonius, *Life of Tiberius*, chapter 36, page 334.</margin>
<margin>∴ Josephus, *Antiquities*, book 18, chapter 5, page 623.</margin>
In that year, they sent a contribution instead of first-fruits to Jerusalem and appointed certain people to offer sacrifices for them at the Temple. Indeed, later (as we find in \* Tacitus and † Suetonius), by an order of the Senate, the Emperor Tiberius caused four thousand of the *class of freedmen* <note>original: "Libertini generis"; these were Jews who had been enslaved and later granted their freedom</note>—specifically those Libertine Jews who were young and vigorous—to be transported to Sardinia to clear that island of bandits (the reason for this is related by ∴ Josephus). He ordered the rest, both Jews and converts, to be banished from the city. Tacitus adds that they were banished from Italy itself. I have no doubt that many of these Libertine Jews took this opportunity to return home to their own country and build this synagogue in Jerusalem for themselves and for the use of their fellow countrymen who visited from Rome. They named it after themselves, the *Synagogue of the Libertines*. This is undoubtedly what St. Luke means when, among the various nations present at Jerusalem on the day of Pentecost, he mentions *visitors from Rome*, including both Jews and converts.

<margin>From Josephus, *Antiquities of the Jews*, book 16, chapter 10, page 561.</margin>
<margin>Acts 2:10.</margin>
<margin>Acts 13:1; 11:19, 20.</margin>
X. THE next opponents were from the *Synagogue of the Cyrenians*. These were Jews who lived in Cyrene, a famous city of Libya, where (as seen in an official decree from Augustus) large numbers of them resided. They were accustomed to sending their sacred funds or traditional offerings to Jerusalem annually, where they also (as we see) had their own specific synagogue. Accordingly, we find among the various nations at Jerusalem "those who lived in the parts of Libya around Cyrene." Thus, we read of Simon of Cyrene, whom the Jews forced to carry our Savior's cross; of Lucius of Cyrene, a famous teacher in the Church of Antioch; and of men from Cyrene who, during the persecution following St. Stephen’s death, were scattered from Jerusalem and preached as far as Phoenicia, Cyprus, and Antioch. The third group were those from the *Synagogue of the Alexandrians*. There was a massive amount of interaction between the Jews in Jerusalem and those in Alexandria, where vast numbers of them lived; the great privileges they enjoyed there are too well known to require further explanation. The fourth group were those from Cilicia, a well-known province of Asia Minor. Its capital was Tarsus, which was well-populated with Jews. This was St. Paul’s birthplace, and we cannot doubt that he played a leading role among these attackers, as we later find him so active and involved in St. Stephen's death. The last were those from the *Synagogue of Asia*. By "Asia," we should probably understand only that part of Asia properly so-called (as that was only a portion of Asia Minor)—namely, the area near Ephesus—as it is clear that this is how "Asia" is meant in the New Testament. The history in the Acts of the Apostles sufficiently informs us of the infinite number of Jews in these parts, especially at Ephesus.

<margin>Location cited above.</margin>
XI. These were the various parties that took the field—people from very different countries, men skilled in the complexities of their religion—who all rose up at once to argue with Stephen. We do not find the specific subject of the debate recorded, but along with St. Chrysostom, we may imagine them challenging him in this way:

> "Tell us, young man, what has come into your mind to reproach the Deity so rashly? Why do you study such cleverly crafted speeches to entice and persuade the people, and use deceitful miracles to ruin the nation? Here lies the core of the controversy. Is it likely that he should be God, who was born of Mary? Or that the Maker of the world should be the *son of a carpenter*? Was not Bethlehem the place of his birth, and Nazareth the place of his upbringing? Can you imagine—"

It concludes with an imagined speech from St. Chrysostom representing the theological challenges they leveled against Stephen's preaching.</summary>

"Can you imagine him to be God, who was born upon Earth? Who was so poor
"that he was wrapped in swaddling clothes and thrown into a manger?
"Who was forced to flee from the rage of *Herod*, and to wash away his
"impurity by being baptized in the *Jordan*? Who was subject to hunger
"and thirst, to sleep and weariness? Who, being bound, was not able to

"he was hanged, could not come down from the Cross, but underwent
"a cursed and shameful death? Will you make us believe that he is
"in Heaven, whom we know to have been buried in his grave? That
"he should be the life of the dead, when he is so closely linked to mortality
"himself? Is it likely that God should suffer such things as these? Would
"he not rather with an angry breath have struck his adversaries dead at
"the first encounter, and set them beyond the reach of making attempts
"upon his own person? Either stop deluding the people
"with these deceptions, or prepare yourself to undergo the same fate."

XII. IN answer to which, we may imagine St. *Stephen* thus to have re-
plied to them. "And why, sirs, should these things seem so incre-
"dible? Do you not have with you the writings of the prophets? Do you
"not read the books of *Moses*, and profess yourselves to be his disci-
"ples? Did not *Moses* say, *The Lord your God shall raise up for you a prophet
"from among your brothers, like me; you shall listen to him?* Have not the pro-
"phets long since foretold that he should be born at *Bethlehem*, and
"conceived in the womb of a virgin? That he should flee into *Egypt*, that
"he should *bear our griefs and carry our sorrows?* That they should pierce
"his hands and his feet, and hang him on a tree <note>original: "tree"; referring here to the wooden cross</note>? That he should be bu-
"ried, rise again, and ascend up to Heaven with a shout? Either now
"show me someone else in whom all these prophecies were accomplished,
"or learn with me to adore our crucified Savior as God. Blind and
"ignorant as you are of the predictions of *Moses*, you thought you
"crucified a mere man; but had you known him, you would not have
"crucified the Lord of Glory. You denied the Holy and Righteous One,
"and requested a murderer to be released to you, but you put to death the
"Prince of Life."

<margin>

Codex Bezae Manuscript & 2 Manuscripts of Henri Estienne.
</margin>
XIII. THIS is the summary of what that ingenious and eloquent Fa-
ther <note>referring to an early church writer, likely Gregory of Nyssa, who composed famous orations on Stephen</note> conceives St. *Stephen* did, or might have returned in answer to their inquiries.
Whatever it was, it was delivered with such life and zeal, such evi-
dence and strength of reason, and such freedom and majesty of speech,
that his antagonists did not have one word to say against it; *they were not
able to resist the wisdom and the spirit by which he spoke.* So particularly did
our Lord make good what he had promised to his disciples: *Settle it in
your hearts not to meditate beforehand what you shall answer, for I will give you
a mouth and wisdom, which all your adversaries shall not be able to contradict
nor resist.* At this, the men immediately began to retreat and left the
arena of debate, equally divided between shame and grief. They were ashamed
to be so openly defeated by a single adversary, and they were vexed and troubled
that they had not won the day, and that the religion which they
opposed had thereby received such remarkable credit and confirmation. And
now, being no longer able <term>to look the truth in the eye</term> <note>original Greek: ἀντοφθαλμεῖν τῇ ἀληθείᾳ (antophthalmein tē alētheia)</note> (as the addition in
some ancient manuscript copies elegantly expresses it) or to
openly resist the truth, they turned to secret <note>original: "clancular"</note> arts and to
sly and sinister designs, hoping to accomplish by craft and subtlety what
they could not achieve by fairness and the force of reason.

# The Life of St. STEPHEN the Protomartyr.

XIV. To this end, they manipulated men with corrupt and immoral consciences to undermine him with false accusations. Their goal was for him to fall as a sacrifice to their spite and malice, executed by the hand of public justice. <margin>As mentioned above, page 278.</margin> St. *Chrysostom* <note>John Chrysostom (c. 347–407 AD), an influential Archbishop of Constantinople known for his preaching.</note> portrays them using smooth and plausible persuasion to encourage the men in this wicked attempt:

> "Come on, worthy and honorable friends, help our failing cause, and let your
> tongues serve our plans and schemes. Look—a new patron and advocate for the GALILEAN <note>A common derogatory term used by early critics to refer to Jesus or his followers.</note> has appeared: one who worships a God who was buried and preaches about a Creator shut in a tomb. He thinks that the one whom the soldiers despised and mocked on Earth is now conversing with the host of angels in Heaven. He promises that he, who was not able to defend or right himself, shall come to judge the world. His own disciples denied him, as if they thought him a fraud; yet this man claims that every tongue shall confess and do him homage. He was not able to come down from the Cross, yet this man talks of his second coming from Heaven. The vilest villains insulted him at his death, saying he could not save either himself or them, yet this man boldly proclaims him to be the Savior of the world. Did you ever see such boldness and impudence? Or have you ever heard words of such madness and blasphemy? Therefore, take up this cause and find some plausible excuse and pretense for it, and by doing so, earn glory and renown for yourselves from this generation."

XV. These wretches were easily persuaded to the task and to swear to whatever their teachers directed them. Now the case was ready for action; the matter was made public, and the *Elders* and *Scribes* were approached (and very little rhetoric was needed to convince them). The people were filled with horror at the alleged crime, the *Sanhedrin* <note>The supreme judicial and administrative council of the Jewish people in Jerusalem.</note> was summoned, the *accused* was hauled to the *dock*, the *witnesses* were produced, and the charge was delivered. *They bribed <term>original: "suborned"</term> men who said, "We have heard him speak blasphemous words against Moses and against God." The false witnesses said, "This man does not stop speaking blasphemous words against this holy place and the Law; for we have heard him say that this Jesus of Nazareth shall destroy this place and shall change the customs which Moses delivered to us."* That is to say (to continue with that excellent man’s explanation of the various parts of the charge):

> "He has dared to speak against our wise and great Lawgiver and blasphemed
> that *Moses*, for whom our whole nation has such a just reverence. That
> *Moses* who had the whole of creation at his command, who freed our 
> ancestors from the house of bondage, and with his staff turned the waters 
> into walls, and by his prayer drowned the *Egyptian* army at the bottom 
> of the sea. He kindled a pillar of fire for light by night; and without 
> plowing or sowing, he fed them with *Manna* and *bread from heaven*. With 
> his staff, he struck the rock and gave them drink. But why do we even 
> speak of *Moses*, when he has sharpened his tongue and stretched it out 
> against God himself, setting up one who is dead as a rival god to the 
> great Creator of the world? He has not hesitated to insult the Temple, 
> that holy place where the divine oracles are read and the writings of 
> the prophets are displayed—the resting place of the Showbread <note>Twelve loaves of bread placed weekly on a golden table in the Sanctuary as an offering.</note> and the 
> heavenly Manna, the Ark of the Covenant, and the staff of *Aaron*. 
> There, the gray-haired and respected heads of the High Priests, the 
> dignity of the Elders, and the honor..."

# The Life of St. STEPHEN the Protomartyr.

> "...of the Scribes is seen. This is the place which he has reviled and set at naught; and not this only, but the Law itself, which he boldly declares to be but a shadow, and the ancient Rites but types and figures <note>In this context, "types and figures" refers to the idea that Old Testament laws were merely symbolic precursors to the reality of Christ.</note>. He affirms the *Galilean* <note>original: "Galilean"; a derogatory reference to Jesus.</note> to be greater than *Moses*, and the Son of *Mary* stronger than our Law-giver. He has not honored the dignity of the Elders, nor shown any reverence to the assembly of the Scribes. He threatens us with a dead Master; the young man is surely dreaming when he talks of *Jesus* of *Nazareth* rising again and destroying this holy place. He little considers with how much wisdom it was designed, with what infinite expense it was erected, and how long it took before it was brought to its perfection. And yet, forsooth, this *Jesus* of *Nazareth* must destroy it and change the customs which *Moses* delivered to us: our most holy Sabbath must be turned out of doors, circumcision abolished, the *New Moon* festivals rejected, and the Feast of Tabernacles laid aside. Our sacrifices must no longer be accepted by God; our ceremonial sprinklings and solemn purifications must be done away with—as if we did not know this *Nazarene’s* end, and as if one who is dead could avenge himself upon those who are living. How many of the ancient Prophets and holy men have been cruelly murdered, whose deaths no one ever yet undertook to avenge? And yet this man must needs appear in the cause of this crucified *Nazarene*, and tell us of a dead man who shall judge us. Foolish imposter! To frighten us with a Judge who is himself imprisoned in his own grave."

XVI. This then is the summary of the charge: that he should threaten the ruin of the Temple and the abolition of the *Mosaic* Rites, and blasphemously affirm that *Jesus* of *Nazareth* should take away that Religion which had been established by *Moses* and by God himself. Indeed, the *Jews* had an immeasurable reverence and veneration for the *Mosaic* Institutions and could not with any patience endure to hear of them being laid aside; they accounted it a kind of blasphemy even to mention their dissolution. They little thought in how short a time these things, which they now so highly valued, should be taken away, and their Temple itself razed to the ground. This came to pass a few years later by the *Roman* Army under the command of *Titus Vespasian*, the *Roman* General, when the City was sacked and the Temple burnt to the ground. And so final and irrevocable was the sentence by which it was doomed to ruin that it could never afterward be repaired, as Heaven itself immediately declared against it. 

Insomuch that when *Julian* the Emperor <note>Julian the Apostate (reigned 361–363 AD), who attempted to revive Roman paganism and undermine Christianity.</note>, out of spite and opposition to the Christians, was resolved to give all possible encouragement to the *Jews*, he not only permitted but commanded them to rebuild the Temple, furnishing them with all the funds and materials necessary for the work (hoping that by this he would prove our Savior a false prophet). No sooner had they begun to clear the debris and lay the foundation, but a terrible earthquake shattered the foundation, killed the workers <note>original: "Undertakers"; meaning those who undertook the project.</note>, and shook down all the buildings that were around it. And when they attempted it again the next day, great balls of fire suddenly breaking out from under the foundations consumed the workmen and those who were near it, forcing them to give up the attempt.

Sozomen, *Ecclesiastical History*, Book 5, Chapter 22, page 631.
</margin>

This was a remarkable instance of Heaven’s displeasure toward a place which God had fatally devoted to destruction. This is related not only by Christian writers, but, in its substance, also by Pagan <note>original: "Heathen"</note>...

historian himself. And the same curse has ever since pursued and followed them, as they have been destitute of a Temple and Sacrifice for sixteen hundred years together. “Were that bloody Sanhedrim <term>Sanhedrim: the supreme judicial and religious council of the Jews in ancient Jerusalem</term> now in existence, and here present,” (says one of the † Ancients speaking of this accusation) “I would ask them about those things for which they were here so concerned: what has now become of your once famous and renowned Temple? Where are those vast stones and incredible piles of building? Where is that gold that once equaled all the other materials of the Temple? What has become of your legal sacrifices? Your rams and calves, your lambs and heifers, pigeons, turtledoves, and scapegoats? If they therefore condemned *Stephen* to die so that none of these miseries might befall them, let them show which of them they avoided by putting him to death; but if they escaped none of them, why then did they soak <note>original: "imbrue"</note> their hands in his innocent blood?”

XVII. The Court being thus seated, and the charge brought in and opened, they gave him liberty to defend himself, so that nothing might be lacking to carry on their mock scene of justice. In preparation for this, while the judges of the *Sanhedrim* looked earnestly upon him, they discovered the appearance of an extraordinary splendor and brightness upon his face; the innocence of his cause and the clarity of his conscience manifested themselves in the brightness and cheerfulness of his countenance. The High Priest having asked him whether he was guilty or not, he pleaded his own cause in a lengthy discourse to this effect: “That whatever apprehensions you may have regarding the stateliness and magnificence of your Temple, the glory and grandeur of its services and ministrations, and those venerable customs and usages among you—as if you looked upon them as indispensably necessary and thought it blasphemy to think God might be acceptably served without them—yet if you look back to the first origins of your nation, you will find that God chose *Abraham* to be the father and founder of it, not when he lived in a *Jerusalem* and worshipped God with the pompous services of a Temple, but when he dwelt among the idolatrous nations. It was then that God called him from the impieties of his father’s house and admitted him to a familiar acquaintance and interaction with Himself. He continued in this state for many years without any of those external and visible rites which you place so much stress upon. And when at last God entered into a covenant <term>covenant: a formal, solemn, and binding agreement between God and His people</term> with him to give his posterity the Land of *Canaan*, and promised that *in his seed all the nations of the Earth should be blessed*, He bound it upon him with no other ceremony but that of circumcision, as the badge and seal of that federal compact <term>federal compact: a binding agreement or treaty between two parties</term> that was between them. Without any other fixed rite but this, the succeeding Patriarchs worshipped God for several ages until the time of *Moses*—a wise, learned, and prudent person to whom God particularly revealed Himself and appointed as ruler over His people to lead them out of the house of bondage. He was a great and famous Prophet who was continually instilling this lesson to your ancestors: *A Prophet shall the Lord your God raise up unto you of your brethren like unto me; him shall you hear*. That is, that in the latter days God would send among them a mighty Prophet who should do as *Moses* had done—introduce new rites and set up more excellent institutions and ways of worship—to whom they should yield all diligent attention and ready obedience...

<meta>This page continues Stephen’s defense before the Sanhedrim. He argues that the physical Temple is not the only place where God can be worshipped, then transitions into a sharp rebuke of the council for their historical rejection of God's prophets and their recent execution of Jesus. # The Life of St. STEPHEN the Protomartyr.

"That when their forefathers had frequently lapsed into idolatry, God commanded *Moses* to set up a *Tabernacle* as a place of public and solemn worship, where he would manifest himself and receive the appeals <note>original: "addresses"</note> and adorations of his people. Yet, this was only a transient and temporary ministry. Although it was erected by the immediate order of God himself, after some years it was to give way to a permanent Temple designed by *David* but built by *Solomon*. It was magnificent and majestic, yet not absolutely necessary. This is because the infinite Being who made the world, who has heaven for his throne and the earth for his footstool, cannot be confined within a material temple or tied to any single way of worship. Therefore, there was no such absolute or indispensable necessity for those Mosaic <note>original: "Mosaical"</note> rites and ceremonies as they claimed—especially when God was resolved to introduce a new and better state of things. 

"But it was the disposition <note>original: "humour"</note> of this loose and unruly, this stubborn <note>original: "refractory"</note> and uncontrollable generation (as it had always been of their ancestors) *to resist the Holy Spirit* <note>original: "Holy Ghost"</note> and oppose him in all those methods by which he sought to reform and reclaim them. There were few of the prophets whom their forefathers had not persecuted; they had even killed those who foretold the *Messiah's* coming—the *just and holy Jesus*. Now, they, as the unhappy descendants of those people, had actually betrayed and murdered him, without any due reverence or regard for that *Law* which had been solemnly delivered to them by the mediation <note>original: "ministry"</note> of angels, and which he came to fulfill and perfect."

XVIII. The holy man was continuing with this application of his argument when the patience of his listeners, which had held out until then, began to fail. That fire which gently warms at a distance scorches when it comes too near; their consciences being deeply stung by the close approach of the truths he delivered, they began to rage and fume, showing every sign of anger and fury. But Stephen, regardless of what was happening below, had his eyes and thoughts directed toward a higher and nobler object. Looking up, he *saw the heavens opened* and some bright, perceptible appearances of the divine majesty. He saw the holy *Jesus* clothed in the robes of our glorified nature, not *sitting* (as he is usually described in Scripture) but *standing* at the *right hand of God*—as if ready to protect and help, and to crown and reward his suffering servant. 

Heaven can so easily delight and comfort us when we lack all earthly comforts; divine consolations are often nearest to us when human assistance is farthest away. The good man was infinitely Ravished by the vision, and it inspired his soul with fresh zeal and courage. It made him long to arrive at that happy place. Concerned very little with how his enemies would react, he could not help but share his happiness; his heart was full, and it naturally overflowed. He told his adversaries what he himself beheld: "*Behold, I see the heavens opened, and the Son of Man standing on the right hand of God*."

XIX. The heavenly vision had very different effects: it encouraged *Stephen*, but it enraged the *Jews*. Now taking it as a self-evident fact <note>original: "pro confesso"</note> that he was a blasphemer, they resolved upon his death without any further legal process. How furious and impatient is misguided zeal! They did not wait to procure a warrant from the *Roman* Governor (without whose leave they had no power to put any man to death), nor did they have the patience to wait for a judicial sentence from the *Sanhedrim* <note>original: "Sankedrim"</note>, but—

<summary>Stephen concludes his defense by asserting that God is not restricted to a physical temple and rebukes the council for murdering the Messiah. After Stephen describes a vision of Jesus in heaven, the enraged council bypasses legal procedures to execute him for blasphemy.</summary>
<keywords>St. Stephen, Moses, Tabernacle, Solomon's Temple, Mosaic Rites, Holy Spirit, Messiah, Sanhedrim, Blasphemy, Martyrdom</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the account of St. Stephen’s martyrdom following his speech to the Sanhedrin. After his bold rebuke of the council, the scene shifts from a legal hearing to a violent execution by a mob.</meta>

<page-num>14</page-num>
# The Life of St. STEPHEN the Protomartyr.

<margin>See Paul Fagius <note>original: "Vid. P. Fag." - Paul Fagius was a 16th-century Hebrew scholar.</note> on Exodus 21:16.</margin>
They acted as *Zealots* <note>original: "Zealots"—members of a Jewish sect known for their fanatical and often violent enforcement of religious law</note>, who were accustomed to execute vengeance upon capital offenders without waiting for the ordinary formalities of justice. Raising a great noise and clamor, and *stopping their ears* so they might hear no further blasphemies and remain deaf to all cries for mercy, they unanimously rushed upon him. 

But religious zeal is superstitious even in its maddest fury: they would not execute him within the city walls, lest they should pollute the Holy City with his blood. Instead, they hurried him *outside the city* and there fell upon him with a shower of stones. Stoning was one of the four capital punishments among the *Jews*, inflicted for the most enormous crimes—especially blasphemy, idolatry, and the worship of false gods. 

The *Jews* describe many specific customs used in this sort of punishment. The criminal was to be led out of the courtroom <note>original: "Consistory"</note>, at the door of which a person was to stand with a napkin in his hand, and a man on horseback at some distance from him. If anyone came forward to say he had something to offer for the deliverance of the prisoner, the man with the napkin would wave it, and the horseman would give notice to bring the offender back. Two grave persons walked with the condemned man to exhort him to confession along the way. A crier went before him, proclaiming who he was, what his crime was, and who the witnesses were. Having arrived near the place of execution (which was two cubits <note>about three feet</note> from the ground), he was first stripped, then stoned, and afterwards hanged. He was to remain there until sunset, at which time he and his gallows <note>original: "Gibbet"</note> were both buried together.

<margin>Deuteronomy 17:7.</margin>
XX. Such were their customs in ordinary cases, but alas, their greed for *St. Stephen’s* blood would not allow for these tedious proceedings. We find them using only one formality required by the Law: that *the hands of the witnesses should be first upon him to put him to death, and afterward the hands of all the people*. This Law was surely contrived with great wisdom and prudence, so that the witness, if he had committed perjury, might take the guilt of the blood upon himself while the rest remained free; *so you shall put the evil away from among you*. 

Accordingly, the witnesses here took off their *upper garments*—which made them less nimble and quick <note>original: "expedite"</note>, being loose and long according to the fashion of those *Eastern countries*. They laid them down at the feet of *Saul*, a zealous youth who was at that time a student under *Gamaliel*. The fiery zeal and activity of Saul’s temperament undoubtedly made him busy in this act, just as we find he was in the persecution that followed. <margin>Acts 22:20.</margin> This was an action that later cost him tears and penitent reflections, as he eventually preferred the indictment against himself, saying: *When the blood of your martyr Stephen was shed, I also was standing by, and consenting to his death, and kept the clothes of those who killed him*. 

Thus prepared, they began the tragedy, and their example was soon followed by the multitude. All this time, the innocent and holy man was upon his knees, sending up his prayers to heaven faster than they could rain down stones upon him. He piously commended his own soul to God and charitably interceded for his murderers, praying that God would not charge this guilt against them, nor severely hold them to account for it. And then he gave up his spirit, or as the sacred historian elegantly expresses it, *fell asleep*. Death is such a soft pillow to a good man; he leaves the world as quietly as a weary laborer goes to bed at night. Whatever storms or tempests may follow him while he lives, his sun sets serene and calm despite all the malice and cruelty of his enemies: *Mark the perfect man, and behold the upright, for the end of that man is peace.*

<meta>This page concludes the account of St. Stephen's death and character, then examines the conflicting historical traditions regarding the exact date and location of his martyrdom.</meta>

# The Life of St. STEPHEN the Protomartyr.

<margin>Eusebius, Church History, Book 2, Chapter 1, p. 38.</margin>
XXI. Thus died St. **Stephen**, the first martyr <note>original: "Protomartyr"</note> of the Christian Faith, obtaining—as Eusebius says—a reward truly answering to his name, a **Crown** <note>In Greek, the name "Stephanos" means "crown." Original Greek: αὐτῷ φερώνυμον Στέφανον</note>. He was a man in whom the virtues of a divine life were very eminent and illustrious; a man *full of Faith, and of the Holy Spirit*. Admirable was his zeal for God and for religion, for the spreading of which he refused no labor and declined no troubles or difficulties. His courage was not shaken either by the angry frowns or the fierce threats of his enemies; nor did his spirit sink, though he stood alone and had neither friend nor kinsman to assist and comfort him. His constancy was firm and unshaken, despite temptations on one hand and the dangers that assaulted him on the other. In all the opposition that he met with, and under all the torments and sufferings that he underwent, he displayed nothing but the meek and innocent temper of a lamb, never uttering a single passionate or revengeful word, but calmly resigning his soul to God. He possessed a charity large enough to forgive the highest insults and the greatest wrongs and injuries inflicted upon him; and accordingly, following the example of his Master, he prayed for the pardon of his murderers, even while they were spilling his blood. And the *effectual, fervent prayer of the righteous man availed much*;

<margin>
"I am Jesus of Nazareth, whom you persecute. What is there between you and me? Why do you set yourself against me, after all the evils you have committed against me? Long ago I should have destroyed you, but my Stephen prayed for you. O Saul, you ravenous wolf, you have eaten; wait a little, and you will digest. I will say clearly: the son of perdition is cast down. For if Saint Stephen had not prayed in this way, the Church would not have Paul. But Paul was raised up because Stephen, bowed down on the earth, was heard. What the persecutor did, the preacher now suffers." — Augustine, Sermon 1 on St. Stephen, Volume 10, column 1168.
</margin>

Heaven was not deaf to his petition, as appeared in the speedy conversion of St. **Paul**, whose admirable change we may reasonably suppose to have been the birth of the good man's dying groans, the fruit of his prayer and influence in Heaven. To top all these excellencies, he was not puffed up with lofty and arrogant conceits, nor did he think more highly of himself than he ought to think; he held a humble opinion of himself and preferred others before himself. Therefore, the author of the *Apostolic* <margin>Book 8, chapter 46. Councils, Vol. 1, col. 509.</margin> *Constitutions* <note>A 4th-century collection of early Christian ecclesiastical laws and instructions.</note> depicts the Apostles commending St. **Stephen** for his humility; though he was such a great person and honored with such singular and extraordinary visions and revelations, he never attempted anything above his station. He did not consecrate the Eucharist, nor confer Holy Orders <note>original: "Orders"</note> upon anyone; but—as became a martyr of Christ to preserve order and decency <note>original Greek: εὐταξίαν ἀποσώζειν</note>—he contented himself with the position of a **Deacon**, in which he persevered to the last minute of his life.

XXII. His martyrdom happened (some say) three years after our Savior's Passion. Euodius, the Bishop of Antioch—if that letter cited by <margin>Church History, Book 2, Chapter 3, p. 134.</margin> Nicephorus was actually his, which is likely enough not the case—extends this to as many as seven years. This is undoubtedly a very large mistake. I am certain that <margin>In the place cited above.</margin> Eusebius affirms it was some few days less than eight months after his ordination to the office of Deacon; the author of the *Chronological Excerpts* published by <margin>At the end of Eusebius's Chronicle, p. 82.</margin> Scaliger specifies more particularly that it was a few days less than eight months after our Lord's Ascension. He <margin>Oration on St. Stephen, as cited above.</margin> is generally supposed to have been young at the time of his martyrdom; and Chrysostom makes no scruple of styling him "young man" at every turn, though for what reason, I confess I have yet to learn. He was martyred outside the walls, near the gate on the North side that leads to Cedar (as <margin>Letter on the Discovery of St. Stephen, in Surius for August 3rd; Bede, On the Holy Places, ch. 1, p. 363, vol. 3; Brocardus, Description of the Holy Land, p. 328; Cotovicus, Travels, book 2, ch. 11, p. 259.</margin> Lucian tells us), which was afterwards called **St. Stephen's Gate**. Anciently, some say it was called the *Gate of Ephraim*, or as others say, the *Valley Gate* or the *Fish Gate*, which stood on the East side of the city. There, we are told, the place is still shown where St. **Paul** sat when he kept the...

# The Life of St. Stephen the Protomartyr.

...the clothes of those who killed him. Over this place (wherever it was) <margin>Evagrius, *Church History*, Book 1, Chapter 22, p. 280.</margin> the Empress **Eudocia**, wife of **Theodosius**, when she repaired the walls of **Jerusalem**, built a beautiful and stately church in honor of St. **Stephen**, where she herself was later buried. The great stone <margin>Bede, *ibid.*, Chapter 3, p. 364.</margin> upon which he stood while he suffered martyrdom is said to have been later moved into the church built in honor of the Apostles on Mount **Sion**, and kept there with great care and reverence. Indeed, one of the stones with which he was killed, having been preserved by some Christian, <margin>Baronius, *Notes on the Roman Martyrology*, for August 3, p. 475, from the *Martyrology of St. Cyriac*.</margin> was later (as we are told) carried into **Italy** and kept as a precious treasure at **Ancona**, where a church was built in memory of the martyr.

XXIII. The Church suffered a great wound by the death of this pious and good man, and could not help but express deep sorrow for it: *Devout men* (probably converts <note>original: "Proselytes"</note>) *carried Stephen to his burial, and made great lamentation for him.* They "carried," or as the Greek word *synekomisan* <note>original: συνεκόμισαν</note> properly signifies, they "prepared" or "gathered up" the dead body for burial. For we cannot reasonably suppose—given that the **Jews** were so enraged against him at the time—that the **Apostles** would have thought it wise to further provoke their angry mood by holding a solemn <margin>Lucian, *Epistle on the Discovery of St. Stephen*, cited above and in Baronius at the year 415, p. 371; see also Nicephorus, Book 14, Chapter 9, Volume 2, p. 454.</margin> and pompous funeral.

His burial (if we are to believe one of the ancient writers who claims it was revealed to him in a vision by **Gamaliel**, whom many ancients believe was a Christian convert) happened in this way: The **Jewish Sanhedrin** <note>The supreme judicial and religious council of the Jews</note> had ordered that his corpse should remain at the site of his martyrdom to be eaten by wild beasts; it lay there for some time, day and night, untouched by any beast or bird of prey. Eventually, **Gamaliel**, feeling compassion for the holy martyr, persuaded some religious Christian converts who lived in **Jerusalem**—and provided them with everything necessary—to go in total secrecy and retrieve his body. They took it away in his own carriage and conveyed it to a place called **Caphargamala**. (This is likely a corruption of *Caphargamaliel*; otherwise, the Hebrew *Kephar Gamla* <note>original: כפר גμלא</note> properly means the "Town of Camels.") This was the "Village of Gamaliel," twenty miles away from **Jerusalem**, where a period of solemn mourning was kept for him for seventy days at **Gamaliel's** expense. He also had him buried on the east side of his own monument, where he was later buried himself. 

<margin>The divine first martyr, having found rest from his struggle, fell into a sweet sleep. His sacred body was gathered up by devout men in a coffin made of Persea wood (this is a type of plant). *Greek Menaion*, for December 26, under the letter S. III. <note>original: Ὁ θεῖος πρωτομάρτυς ἀντίπαλον κατα- λαβὼν, ἢ γλυκὺν ὕπνον ἀνεπαύσατο· Συγκο- μίζεται τὸ ἱερὸν σκήνωμα ὑπὸ ἀνδρῶν εὐλαβῶν ἐν γλωσσοκόμῳ Πρσίας· εἶδος δὲ τοῦτο φυτοῦ. Menæon Græcor. τῇ κϛ' τοῦ Δε- κεμβρ. sub lit. Σ. III.</note></margin>

The Greek *Menaion* <note>A liturgical book of the Eastern Orthodox Church</note> adds that his body was placed in a coffin made from the wood of a tree called the **Persea**. (This was a large, beautiful Egyptian tree, as **Theophrastus** tells us <margin>Theophrastus, *History of Plants*, Book 4, Chapter 2, p. 286.</margin>, from which statues, beds, tables, and other items were commonly made). How they obtained such specific information (since it is not mentioned in **Gamaliel's** "revelation") I cannot imagine.

<margin>Johannes Phocas, *Description of the Holy Places*, etc., Chapter 14, p. 19, Allatius edition.</margin> **Johannes Phocas**, a Greek writer from the middle age of the Church, agrees with the account of his burial by **Gamaliel**, but adds that he was first buried on Mount **Sion** in the house where the **Apostles** were gathered when our Lord came to them—*the doors being shut*—after his resurrection. <margin>*Ibid.*, Chapter 15, p. 25.</margin> He was later moved by **Gamaliel** to another place which, Phocas says, was on the left side of the city facing toward **Samaria**, where a famous monastery was later built.
