...for then, as ª *Sozomen* informs us, it was discovered at the same time as the bones of the Prophet *Zachary*. He promises to give an account of both, but having spoken of the Prophet, he abruptly ends his history there. However, what is missing in his work is fully supplied by others, especially the previously mentioned ᵇ *Lucian*, a priest <note>original: "Presbyter"</note> of the town of *Caphargamala* in the Diocese of *Jerusalem*. He provides a very large and precise <note>original: "punctual"</note> account, the summary of which (so far as it concerns the present case and is important to relate) is this:

While sleeping one night in the **Baptistery** <note>original: "Baptisterium"</note> of his church (this was in the year 415, during the twenty-first year of the reign of Emperor *Honorius*), a grave and venerable old man appeared to him. The man told him he was *Gamaliel* <note>The famous Pharisee and teacher of St. Paul.</note> and ordered him to go to *John*, the Bishop of *Jerusalem*, and ask him to remove his remains and those of some others (of whom *St. Stephen* was the most important) from the place where they lay. The vision appeared to him three separate times before he was fully satisfied of its truth. He then informed the Bishop, who commanded him to search for the burial site. After some attempts, he found the place of their repository and notified the Bishop, who came bringing two other bishops, *Eleutherius* of *Sebaste* and *Eleutherius* of *Jericho*, with him.

When the monument was opened, they found an inscription on *St. Stephen’s* tombstone in deep letters: **CELIEL**, which signifies (according to my author) the *Servant of God*. At the opening of the coffin there was an earthquake, and a very pleasant and delightful fragrance came from it, and several miraculous cures were performed by it. The remains were closed up again (except for a few bones and a little of the dust that was taken out and given to *Lucian*) and were conveyed with great triumph and rejoicing to the church that stood upon **Mount Sion**, the place where he himself, while alive, had performed the duties of a deacon. I will add nothing more to this, except that this story is mentioned not only by ᶜ *Photius*, and before him by ᵈ *Marcellinus Comes*—who was once the Chancellor or Secretary to *Justinian* (who later became Emperor)—but also by others. *Marcellinus* records the event as happening in the very same year and under the same Consuls as reported in *Lucian’s* letter. Even earlier, it was mentioned by ᵉ *Gennadius*, a priest of *Marseilles* who lived around the year 490, not long after the time of *Lucian* himself. *Gennadius* also adds that *Lucian* wrote an account of it in *Greek* to all the churches, which *Avitus*, a *Spanish* priest, translated into *Latin*. *Avitus* added a letter to the front of the work, in which he gives an account of the matter to *Balchonius*, Bishop of *Braga*, and sent it into *Spain* by way of *Orosius*.

<margin>f. Nicephorus, *Church History*, Book 14, Chapter 9, p. 454, Vol. 2. The same account is in the *Greek Menaion* <note>A liturgical book of the Orthodox Church.</note> for August 2, section 11. <note>original: "Αὐγυς. τῇ β' sub. lit. β'. 11."</note></margin>

XXV. These remains (whether this happened before or after, the reader must judge by the rest of the story, though I doubt they will have enough faith to believe all the details) were moved to *Constantinople* on the following occasion. ᶠ *Alexander*, a nobleman of senatorial rank who had a particular devotion to the *Protomartyr* <note>The "first martyr," a title given to St. Stephen.</note>, had built an oratory <note>A small chapel for private prayer.</note> to him in *Palestine*. He requested that when he died, he should be placed in a coffin like that of *St. Stephen* and buried beside him. Eight years later, his wife (whose name, some say, was *Juliana*) was moving to *Constantinople* and decided to take her husband’s body with her. However, in her haste, she accidentally mistook *St. Stephen’s* coffin for that of her husband and set out on her journey. The mistake soon revealed itself by an extraordinary odor and some miraculous effects. The news of this spread ahead to *Constantinople*, preparing the people to conduct the coffin with great joy and ceremony into the Imperial Palace.

This, however, could not be accomplished: for the strong mules that carried the treasure, having come as far as the *Baths of Constantine*, would not move a single step further. When they were unreasonably whipped and prodded, they spoke aloud and told those leading them that the Martyr was to be rested and buried in that place. This was done accordingly, and a beautiful church was built there. Certainly, those who first added this passage to the story would have been at a great loss for imagination <note>original: "invention"</note> had the story of *Balaam's Donkey* <note>A biblical story from Numbers 22, where a donkey miraculously speaks to its master.</note> not been recorded in Scripture. I 
<margin>Baronius, *Annals* for the year 439, Volume 5, page 681. <note>original: "Bar. ad Ann. 439. Tom. 5. p. 681."</note></margin> 
confess that *Baronius* <note>Cesare Baronius (1538–1607), a cardinal and influential church historian.</note> does not seem very eager to believe this account—not because of its trivial and absurd improbabilities, but only because he could not easily reconcile it with the time the remains were first found by *Lucian*. Indeed, my authors tell us that this happened in the time of *Constantine*, while *Metrophanes* was Bishop of *Constantinople*, and that it was only some part of his remains (buried again by some devout Christians) that was revealed in a vision to *Lucian*. They also say that the Empress *Pulcheria*, with the help of her brother *Theodosius*, obtained the Martyr's right hand from the Bishop of *Jerusalem*. When it arrived at *Constantinople*, it was brought into the palace with extraordinary reverence and rejoicing; a grand and magnificent church was built for it, decorated with all sorts of rich and costly ornaments.

<margin>
a. Marcellinus, *Chronicle*, Indiction 7, page 24; Theodorus Lector, Book 2, page 558.

XXVI. Authors mention another relocation <margin>Year 439 AD</margin> (and I will let those curious and inquisitive about these matters try to reconcile the different accounts) of his remains to *Constantinople* by the Empress *Eudocia*, wife of *Theodosius*. Having been in *Jerusalem* for certain religious and charitable purposes, she carried back the remains of St. *Stephen* to the Imperial City, where she carefully placed them in the Church of St. *Lawrence*. 
<margin>b. *Roman Martyrology* for May 7th, page 284. <note>original: "Ad VII. Maii p. 284."</note></margin> 
The *Roman* <gloss>b</gloss> Martyrology says that in the time of Pope *Pelagius*, they were moved from *Constantinople* to *Rome* and placed in the tomb of St. *Lawrence* the Martyr in the *Verano field* <note>original: "agro Verano," a historic area in Rome where a major cemetery now stands.</note>, where they are honored with great piety and devotion. 
<margin>

</margin>
But I find no author from near those times who mentions their transfer into any of these *Western* regions, except for the small portion that <gloss>c</gloss> *Orosius* brought from *Jerusalem*. (Orosius had been sent there by St. *Augustine* to learn St. *Jerome's* opinion on the question of the origin of the soul.) He received this relic from *Avitus*, who had obtained it from *Lucian*, and he brought it with him into the *West*—that is, into *Africa*. Whether it went any further, I cannot find.

XXVII. As for the miracles reported to have been performed by the 
<margin>
d. *On the Glory of the Martyrs*, Book 1, Chapter 33, page 42, etc. <note>original: "De glor. Martyr."</note>
</margin>
remains of this Martyr, <gloss>d</gloss> *Gregory*, Bishop of *Tours*, and writers of later ages have provided the world with abundant examples. I do not dwell on these, as superstition was the particular spirit <note>original: "genius"</note> and mood of those Middle Ages of the Church, and the Christian world was miserably overwhelmed by an excessive and extreme reverence for the relics of departed saints. However, I can risk the reader’s annoyance by relating one, especially since it is so solemnly 
<margin>e. Annotations on the *Roman Martyrology* for August 3rd, page 474.</margin> 
asserted by <gloss>e</gloss> *Baronius* himself. St. *Gaudiosus*, an African Bishop fleeing from the *Vandal* persecution <note>The persecution of Catholics by the Arian Vandal kings in North Africa during the 5th and 6th centuries.</note>, brought a glass vial of St. *Stephen's* blood with him to *Naples* in *Italy*. It was famous there especially for one miraculous effect: when placed upon the altar during *Mass*, it was accustomed to annually melt and bubble on the *third of August* (the day St. *Stephen's* body was first discovered) as if it were newly shed.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of miracles associated with St. Stephen's relics, transitioning from a skeptical look at "modern" miracles to the historical accounts provided by St. Augustine.</meta>

<page-num>19</page-num>

# The Life of St. Stephen the Protomartyr.

But the miracle of the miracle lay in this: when Pope *Gregory* XIII <note>Pope Gregory XIII (1502–1585) introduced the Gregorian calendar in 1582 to correct the drift of the Julian calendar.</note> reformed the *Roman Calendar* and created a difference of no less than ten days from the former system, the blood in the vial ceased to bubble on the third of *August* according to the old calculation, and instead bubbled on the day it fell according to the new reform. I admit (as *Baronius* <note>Cesare Baronius (1538–1607), a cardinal and historian who defended the new calendar.</note> well observes) that this is a great justification of the divine authority of the *Gregorian Calendar* and the Pope's decrees; yet it was ill-advised to set the calendars at odds when both had been equally justified by the miracle. However, it is not difficult to imagine how easily the world was deceived by such tricks, especially in these later ages, when the trickery of the priests had reached a kind of perfection in these matters.

<margin>The location previously cited. <note>original: "Loc. ante citat."</note></margin>
XXVIII. Let us then look to the earlier ages, when greed and worldly interests had not yet generally driven men toward the arts of craft and subtlety. We are told by both *Lucian* and *Photius* that at the first discovery of the Martyr's body, many strange miraculous cures were performed: seventy-three people were healed merely by smelling the fragrance of the body; in some, demons <note>original: "Dæmons"</note> were cast out, while others were cured of hemorrhaging <note>original: "Issues of Bloud"</note>, tumors, agues, fevers, and countless other diseases that afflicted them. But what carries the most weight with me is what *St. Augustine* <note>Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), one of the most influential Latin Church Fathers.</note> reports of these matters. He seems to have been inquisitive about the facts, as the argument he was making required.
<margin>*The City of God*, Book 22, Chapter 8, column 1346, etc., Volume 5. <note>original: "De Civ. Dei. lib. 22. cap. 8. col. 1346. &c. Tom. 5."</note></margin>
For, intending to demonstrate to the pagans <note>original: "Gentiles"</note> that miracles had not altogether ceased in the Christian Church, he produces several instances of cures miraculously performed by the remains of *St. Stephen*. These remains had been brought there (as we noted before) by *Orosius* from *Jerusalem*. All these miracles occurred in that vicinity, and some in the very place where Augustine himself lived. He tells us that they recorded these events in books, which were solemnly published and read to the people. At the time of his writing, there were no fewer than seventy accounts written of the cures performed at *Hippo* (the place where he lived), even though it had not been two full years since the memorial of *St. Stephen's* martyrdom had begun to be celebrated in that place—not to mention many other cures for which no written account had been given. 

To record them all would tire the reader's patience beyond recovery; a few examples shall suffice as a specimen. At the *Baths of Tibilis* <note>original: "Aquæ Tibilitanæ"</note>, a bishop named *Projectus* was bringing the remains of the Martyr through a vast multitude of people. A blind woman asked to be brought to the Bishop, and some flowers she had brought were laid upon the relics and then applied to her eyes; to the wonder of all, she instantly received her sight. *Lucillus*, the Bishop of *Synica* near *Hippo*, while carrying the same remains accompanied by all the people, was suddenly freed from a desperate disease under which he had suffered for a long time, and for which he was even then expecting the surgeon's <note>original: "Chirurgeons"</note> knife. *Eucharius*, a Spanish priest <note>original: "Presbyter"</note> then living at *Calama* (where *Possidius*, who wrote *St. Augustine's* life, was Bishop), was cured by the same means of "the stone" <note>A painful condition caused by kidney or bladder stones.</note>, which he had suffered from for a long time. He later recovered from another illness after he had been given up for dead. *Martialis*, an elderly gentleman of great note and rank in that place, but a pagan and highly prejudiced against the Christian faith, had often been unsuccessfully urged by his daughter and her husband (both Christians) to convert. This was especially true during his sickness, but he always reacted to the suggestion with indignation. His son-in-law went to the place dedicated to *St. Stephen's* Martyr—

<meta>This page continues the accounts of miracles attributed to St. Stephen’s relics, specifically those recorded by St. Augustine in Africa. It completes a story of a father's conversion and begins a theological reflection on the purpose of miracles.</meta>

<page-num>20</page-num>

# The Life of St. Stephen the First Martyr <note>original: "Protomartyr"</note>

...martyrdom, and there with prayers and tears passionately begged God for his conversion. Upon departing, he took some flowers from that place with him, which he put under his father's head at night. His father slept well, and in the morning he called for the Bishop. Because the Bishop was away at that time (staying with St. Augustine at Hippo), the priests <note>original: "Presbyters"</note> were sent for instead. When they arrived, he declared himself a Christian and, to the joy and wonder of everyone, was immediately baptized. As long as he lived, he often repeated these words, and they were the last words he spoke (for he died not long after): "O Christ, receive my spirit." He was completely unaware that these were the dying words of the First Martyr.

XXIX. He relates many similar events that took place at his own city of Hippo, including this one: Ten children of a prominent family <note>original: "eminency"</note> at Caesarea in Cappadocia (all the children of one man) had been cursed by their mother for a notorious offense after their father’s death. As a result, they were all seized with a constant trembling and shaking in every part of their bodies. Two of these children, Paulus and Palladia, traveled over to Africa and lived at Hippo, where they were well known to the entire city. They arrived fifteen days before Easter and frequented the church every day—especially the area dedicated to the martyrdom of St. Stephen—praying that God would forgive them and restore their health. 

On Easter Day, while the young man was praying as he usually did in the accustomed place, he suddenly dropped down and lay as if asleep, but without any trembling. When he woke up, he found himself perfectly restored to health. With the joyful cheers of the people, he was brought to St. Augustine, who received him kindly. After the public devotions were over, Augustine hosted him for dinner, where he heard the full account of the misery that had befallen him. The next day, when the narrative of his cure was to be read to the people, his sister was also healed in the same manner and at the same place. St. Augustine relates the specific circumstances of both healings at great length.

<margin>See Augustine in another place, at the beginning of the chapter. <note>original: "Vid. Aug. loc. alt. initio cap."</note></margin>

XXX. I do not know what the thoughtful and fair-minded reader will think of these and other similar instances reported by this good Father [Augustine]. Perhaps the reader will think it reasonable to believe that God might allow these strange and miraculous cures to be performed in a place where many people still persisted in their paganism <note>original: "Gentilism"</note> and lack of faith. These people made one great objection against the Christian faith: they argued that although miracles might have been claimed in the past to confirm the Christian religion, they had now ceased, even though they were still necessary to lead the world to believe in Christianity. 

It is certain that nothing was done here that did not align perfectly with the wisdom and goodness of God. He is never known to be wasteful in multiplying the effects of His all-powerful strength beyond what is truly necessary, yet He is never failing to provide all the necessary evidence and methods for conviction. Therefore, so that the unbelieving world (who used this as their great excuse for their lack of faith) might see that His arm was not grown exhausted <note>original: "effœte"</note> and weak, and that He had not left the Christian religion entirely destitute of immediate and miraculous proof, He was pleased to exercise these extraordinary powers. He did this to defeat their unbelief and silence their objections against the divine nature of the Christian faith. For this reason, God never totally withdrew the power of working miracles from the Church until the world was, in a sense, entirely won over to the faith of Christ. And then He left it

<meta>catchword: to</meta>

<summary>This page describes miraculous healings at St. Stephen's shrine in Hippo, including the conversion of a dying father and the curing of two cursed siblings. It concludes with St. Augustine's argument that God continued miracles in the early Church specifically to overcome the skepticism of the pagan world.</summary>

<keywords>St. Stephen, St. Augustine, Hippo, miracles, healing, conversion, paganism, Caesarea, Cappadocia, early Church history</keywords>

--- Page 95 ---
<meta>This page concludes the discussion of the miracles attributed to St. Stephen's relics as recorded by St. Augustine, transitioning into a critique of contemporary Roman Catholic claims regarding miracles and the proper focus of Christian worship.</meta>

<page-num>21</page-num>

<header>The Life of St. Stephen the First Martyr.</header>

...to be led by more gentle <note>original: "humane"</note> and regular means, and to preserve its authority over the minds of people through those permanent and innate signs of divinity which He has impressed upon it. It is true that the Church of *Rome* still claims <note>original: "pretends to"</note> this power, which it attempts to justify by appealing to these and similar instances. 

But it does so in vain and to no purpose. The alleged miracles of that Church are generally trivial and ridiculous—far beneath the dignity and seriousness that should influence a wise and thoughtful mind. The way they are performed is obscure and ambiguous, their frequency is excessive and immoderate, and the reasons for them are light and frivolous. After all, the events themselves are mostly false, and the reports are very often so outrageous and extreme that they would overwhelm any sober or rational belief. In fact, a person would have to be a miracle themselves to believe them. 

I will only note that in all these cases recorded by *St. Augustine*, we never find that the people called upon or prayed to the Martyr, nor did they beg to be healed by his merits or intercession. Instead, they immediately directed their prayers to God Himself.

---

---

<summary>The author concludes that while God once used miracles to establish the Church, He now uses natural reason. He critiques the Roman Catholic Church's claims of modern miracles as frivolous and notes that in St. Augustine's time, believers prayed to God rather than to the martyrs themselves.</summary>

## THE LIFE OF
# SAINT PHILIP

---

<margin>
Letter, Book 1, Letter 449 to Antiochus, page 95. <note>original Latin: "Epist. l. I. Ep. 449. ad Antioch. p. 95."</note>
</margin>

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'S' featuring acanthus leaf scrollwork; a smaller "S." is positioned within the woodcut frame to the right of the main letter.</image-desc>

I. **SAINT PHILIP** was born (as *Isidore of Pelusium* <note>A 5th-century ascetic and prolific letter writer</note> clearly suggests) at *Caesarea*, a famous port town between *Joppa* and *Ptolemais* in the province of *Samaria*. Whether he had any other authority for this besides his own guess, I do not know; however, there are some circumstances that make it probable. He has been carelessly confused with *Saint Philip the Apostle* by some writers, both in the past and more recently, due to a lack of proper attention to the differences between the two men. This mistake is very old and seems to have been accepted by some of the earliest writers of the Church. 

But whoever considers that the one was an *Apostle* and one of the *Twelve*, while the other was only a *Deacon* and one of the *Seven* chosen from among the people and set apart by the *Apostles*—so that they themselves might attend to the more immediate ministries of their office—will see a clear reason to acknowledge a vast difference between them. Furthermore, the one was traveling throughout the *country* while the other remained with the *Apostolic College* in *Jerusalem*. Additionally, while the Deacon was commissioned to preach and to baptize, he could not bestow the *Holy Spirit* (the unique privilege of the Apostolic office). 

Our *Saint Philip* was one of the *Seventy Disciples* and was the next colleague to *Saint Stephen* in the *Deacon's Office*. This office was established for the convenience of the poor and to assist the Apostles in subordinate services and ministrations. This shows him to have been a person of great esteem and reputation in the *Church*, endowed with miraculous powers, and *full of wisdom and of the Holy Spirit*—which were the qualifications required by the Apostles for those appointed to this role. He continued in the discharge of this ministry at *Jerusalem* for some months after his election, until the Church was scattered, forcing him to leave his post. And is it any wonder that the stewards are dismissed when the *household* is broken up?

II. THE *first martyr* <term>Protomartyr: Saint Stephen, the first person to be killed for the Christian faith</term> had recently been sacrificed to the rage and fury of his enemies; but the bloody cloud did not simply pass by. Instead, it grew into an even darker storm. Cruelty and revenge never say "it is enough"—much like the nature of the Devil, whose malice is insatiable and eternal. *Stephen's* death would not suffice; the whole Church was now targeted, and the enemies of the faith resolved (if possible) to wipe out the religion itself. The primary architect of this persecution was *Saul*, whose active and fiery nature and passionate concern for the traditions of the ancestors made him pursue the plan with the spirit of a *zealot* and the rage of a madman. Having obtained a commission from the *Sanhedrin* <note>The supreme judicial and religious council of the Jews in Jerusalem</note>, he quickly put it into effect, breaking open houses and seizing whomever he met.

<vocab>Philip the Deacon, Simon Magus, Samaria, Ethiopia, Eunuch, Candace, Casarea, Apostle, Stephen, Saul, Sanhedrim, Protomartyr</vocab>

<summary>This page clarifies the identity of Saint Philip the Deacon, distinguishing him from the Apostle Philip, and describes his early ministry in the context of the intense persecution following Saint Stephen's martyrdom.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Philip the Deacon, Caesarea, Isidore of Pelusium, Seven Deacons, Seventy Disciples, Saint Stephen, Saul of Tarsus, persecution, early Church hierarchy</keywords>

--- Page 99 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of the persecution of the early Church in Jerusalem and describes Philip's subsequent mission to the province of Samaria.</meta>

...anyone who looked even remotely like a disciple of the crucified **Jesus**. Without any regard for gender or age, he beat them and hauled them off to prison, snatching husbands from the arms of their wives and mothers from the embraces of their children. He blasphemed God, persecuted people, and committed many injuries, breathing out nothing but slaughter and threats wherever he went. Because of this, <margin>Eusebius, *Ecclesiastical History*, Book 2, Chapter 1, p. 39.</margin> **Eusebius** calls this the first and most terrible persecution of the Church. The Church was forced to go into hiding, with only the Apostles remaining privately in **Jerusalem** so they could better supervise and guide the Church’s affairs. Meanwhile, the rest were scattered throughout the neighboring countries, announcing the good news of the Gospel and explaining its nature and purpose everywhere they went. Thus, what their enemies intended as a way to ruin them—by breaking the bond of their fellowship and society—actually became an effective means to expand the boundaries of Christianity. In the same way, few people benefit from excellent perfumes while they are kept closed in a box; however, once they are spilled on the ground, whether by accident or out of malice, their fragrant scent immediately fills every corner of the house.

III. Among those who were scattered was our **Evangelist**—a title given to him not because he wrote a Gospel, but because he preached it. He traveled toward the province of **Samaria** and arrived at "a city of Samaria" (as the text may be read), which was probably **Gitton**, the birthplace of **Simon Magus**; though it is safest to assume it was the city of **Samaria** itself. This was the capital <note>original: "Metropolis"</note> of the province and had been the royal seat of the kings of **Israel** for several centuries. However, after being utterly destroyed by **Hyrcanus** <note>John Hyrcanus, a Hasmonean leader and Jewish High Priest who destroyed the Samaritan temple on Mount Gerizim around 110 BCE</note>, it had recently been rebuilt by **Herod the Great** and renamed **Sebaste** in honor of **Augustus** (<term>Sebastos <note>original Greek: Σεβαστός; meaning "venerable" or "majestic," the Greek equivalent of the Latin "Augustus"</note></term>). The **Samaritans** were a mix of **Jews** and **Gentiles**, made up of the remnants of the Ten Tribes who were carried away into captivity and the pagan colonies that the King of **Babylon** brought in to replace them. 

Accordingly, their religion was nothing but **Judaism** blended with **pagan rites**, though they valued it so highly that they did not hesitate to argue about its status or to compete with the worship at the Temple in **Jerusalem**. For this reason, there was an ancient and deep-seated grudge and conflict between the **Jews** and the Samaritans, so much so that they completely refused any interaction with one another. <margin>John 4:9</margin> This is why the **Samaritan woman** wondered that our Lord, being a **Jew**, would ask for a drink from her, a woman of Samaria; "for the Jews have no dealings with the Samaritans." They despised them as much as heathens, cursed them with the most solemn excommunications, and did not allow them to become converts <note>original: "Proselytes"</note> or to have any share in the "Resurrection of the Righteous." They would not allow an **Israelite** to eat with them, nor even to say "Amen" to their blessing. They did not think they could give our Savior a more insulting title than to say he was a "Samaritan" and "had a demon." 

But God does not care about human prejudices, nor does He always withhold His kindness from those whom we are ready to exclude from our love and friendship. It is true that when the Apostles were first sent out, they were commanded <margin>Matthew 10:5</margin> not to go "into the way of the Gentiles" nor to "enter any city of the Samaritans." But once Christ, through His death, had <margin>Ephesians 2:14-15 and following.</margin> "broken down the wall of partition and abolished in His flesh the hostility—even the law of commandments contained in ordinances"—then the **Gospel** came and "preached peace to those who were far off as well as to those who were near." **Philip**, therefore, freely preached...

<sig>E</sig>

<summary>This page details the intense persecution of the early Church in Jerusalem and how the resulting dispersion of believers led Philip the Evangelist to preach in Samaria. It explains the historical and religious animosity between the Jews and Samaritans, highlighting how the Gospel eventually bridged this divide.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the account of Philip's mission in Samaria, following his departure from Jerusalem during the first great persecution of the Church.</meta>

# The Life of St. Philip the Deacon, etc.

...preached the Gospel to these **Samaritans**, who were so odious and distasteful to the **Jews**. He effectively prepared his way with many great and undeniable <note>original: "uncontrollable"</note> miracles. These miracles, being arguments suited to the understanding and senses of even the simplest people <note>original: "the meanest"</note>, most easily convey the truth into the minds of men. Consequently, the mission was successful; the people generally embraced **Christian Doctrine** as they saw him curing all kinds of diseases and powerfully casting out **demons**. These spirits were forced to leave their hosts with great horror and regret, to the shared joy and wonder of the city.

<margin>
a Book 1, chapter 20, p. 115.
b Apology 2, p. 69. See Tertullian, *The Prescription Against Heretics*, chapter 46, p. 219.
The first God <note>original Greek: πρῶτον Θεόν</note>
</margin>

IV. In this city lived a man named **Simon**, born in a nearby town, who through **sorcery** and **magic arts** had strangely gained the reverence and veneration of the people. He was a crafty, ambitious, daring, and insolent man whose diabolical deceptions and tricks had long amazed the common people <note>original: "the Vulgar"</note>, so much so that they actually thought him to be the supreme Divinity (a claim he undoubtedly made for himself). He likely promoted himself as that divine power expected to visit the **Jews** as the **Messiah** or the Son of God. Among the **Samaritans**, he presented himself as the Father (as ᵃ **Irenaeus** assures us), while his countryman ᵇ **Justin Martyr** tells us the people worshipped him as the first and greatest deity. Later, among the **Gentiles**, he styled himself as the **Holy Spirit**. It is no wonder that the people were tempted and seduced into admiring and adoring him by this series of tricks. This was the situation when **St. Philip** arrived, whose greater and more undeniable miracles quickly turned the tide. Deception <note>original: "Imposture"</note> cannot withstand the close approach of truth but flees before it, just as darkness vanishes in the presence of the sun. Recognizing their error, the people flocked to **St. Philip’s** sermons; convinced by the effectiveness of his teaching and the power of his miracles, they became converts and were initiated into the **Christian** faith through **baptism**. Even the magician himself, astonished by the mighty deeds he saw **Philip** perform, declared himself a follower and disciple and was baptized. He was either truly persuaded by the convincing evidence of the truth or was craftily hiding his true motives and faking his belief in Christianity for some sinister purpose.

<margin>
c *Ecclesiastical History*, Book 2, chapter 1, p. 39.
</margin>

**Eusebius** tells us that ᶜ Simon's disciples and followers still practiced this kind of deception in his own time. Imitating their "father," they would creep into Christian communities like a plague or leprosy so they could more easily poison and infect others. Many of these people, once discovered, were shamefully expelled from the Church.

<margin>
d Epiphanius, *Against Heresies*, 21, p. 29.
e Chrysostom, *Homily 18 on Acts*, p. 580.
</margin>

V. News of **St. Philip’s** success in **Samaria** quickly reached **Jerusalem**, where the Apostles immediately arranged to send some of their own members to confirm these new converts in the faith. **Peter** and **John** were sent on this mission. Upon arrival, they prayed for the converts and laid their hands on them—likely ordaining some as governors of the church and ministers of religion. As soon as this was done, the miraculous gifts of the **Holy Spirit** fell upon them. This was clear evidence of apostolic power. **Philip** had converted and baptized them, but because he was only a deacon (as ᵈ **Epiphanius** and ᵉ **Chrysostom** correctly observe), he could not bestow the **Holy Spirit**, as this was a gift granted only to the Apostles. When **Simon the Magician** saw that the power to work miracles was conveyed by the laying on of the

<meta>catchword: the</meta>

<summary>Philip’s preaching and miracles in Samaria lead to mass conversions and the apparent conversion of the sorcerer Simon Magus. The Apostles Peter and John arrive from Jerusalem to bestow the Holy Spirit, a power Philip lacked as a deacon.</summary>
<keywords>St. Philip, Samaria, Simon Magus, miracles, baptism, Holy Spirit, Apostles, Peter, John, deacons, sorcery, Eusebius, Irenaeus</keywords>

ᵃ Antiquities Appendix, Life of St. Peter, Section 8, number 1; Section 9, number 4.
</margin>
By the laying on of the Apostles' hands, Simon Magus hoped to obtain this power to recover his reputation and standing with the people. To this end, he sought to use the methods he thought most likely to succeed—attempting to bribe the Apostles with a sum of money to confer this power upon him. Peter resented the proposal with a sharpness and severity that was entirely appropriate. He told the wretched man about the wickedness of his offer and the evil state he was in, advising him to repent and make his peace with God <note>original: "Heaven"</note> so that he might, if possible, prevent the miserable fate that would otherwise befall him. What passed between Peter and this magician, both here and in their famous encounter at Rome (which is discussed so often by ancient writers), we have related in greater detail in another place ᵃ.

<margin>
ᵇ Homily 19 on the Acts of the Apostles, page 585.
"Do you see how the angels assist in the preaching? And yet they do not preach themselves, but call these [ministers to do it]? The wonder of it is shown from this." <note>original Greek: Εἶδες ἀγγέλους συναπιλαμβανομένους τῷ κηρύγματι· κ᾿ αὐτοὺς μὲν οὐ κηρύττοντας, τούτους δὲ καλοῦντας; τὸ δὲ θαυμαστὸν κ᾿ ἐντεῦθεν δείκνυται.</note> Chrysostom, ibid. page 586.
ᶜ Geography, book 16, page 759. Zechariah 2:4. Jeremiah 47:5.
</margin>
VI. Whether St. Philip returned with the Apostles to Jerusalem or, as ᵇ Chrysostom thinks, stayed in Samaria and the surrounding areas, we have no surviving records. But wherever he was, an angel was sent to him with a message from God to go and instruct a stranger in the faith. One might have thought the angel would have been the most likely to manage this business successfully himself. However, the wise God maintains method and order and will not allow an angel to take over the work he has placed in the hands of his ministers. The purpose of his commission was to go toward the south, to the road that goes down from Jerusalem to Gaza, which is a desert. It is difficult to decide whether this description refers to the road or the city, as it was likely true of both. Gaza was a city anciently famous for the amazing feats of Samson’s strength, his captivity, his death, and the burial of himself and his enemies in the same ruins. It was afterward sacked and laid waste by Alexander the Great and, as ᶜ Strabo notes, remained waste and a desert in his time. The prophetic curse was truly fulfilled there: *Gaza shall be forsaken*. This was a fate the prophet Jeremiah had foretold as being as certain as if he had already seen it happen: *baldness has come upon Gaza*. Thus, divine threats certainly capture and take hold of a proud and unrepentant people. God easily opens the gates for ruin to enter the strongest and best-fortified cities once sin has undermined them and stripped them of divine protection.

<margin>
ᵃ Dorotheus, Synopsis, page 148.
ᵇ Sophronius in Jerome, *On Illustrious Men* <note>original: "de Scriptoribus Ecclesiasticis"</note> in the chapter on Crescentius.
</margin>
VII. As soon as St. Philip received his orders, even though he did not yet know the purpose of his journey, he set out immediately: *he arose and went*. He did not wonder if he might be mistaken or if it was a false and deceptive vision sending him on such an inexplicable mission into a desert and a wilderness where he was more likely to find trees, rocks, and wild beasts than people to preach to. He went anyway, knowing well that God never sends anyone on a vain or foolish mission. This is an excellent example of obedience, just as it is recorded to Abraham's eternal honor and praise that when God issued his command, *he obeyed and went out, not knowing where he was going*. As Philip was on his journey, he saw a man of Ethiopia coming toward him—a eunuch <note>A high-ranking court official.</note> of great authority under Candace, Queen of the Ethiopians. He was in charge of all her treasury and had come to Jerusalem to worship. There has been some dispute about where this country was located (as the word "Ethiopia" is used in various ways in Scripture). ᵃ Dorotheus and ᵇ Sophronius in ancient times, and some later writers, place it in Arabia Felix <note>original: "Arabia the Happy"</note>, not far from the Persian Gulf; but...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of St. Philip’s mission to baptize the Ethiopian official, specifically examining the geography of Ethiopia and the identity of Queen Candace.</meta>

# The Life of St. Philip the Deacon, etc.

<margin>
ᵃ Jerome, *To Paulinus*, volume 3, page 7.
</margin>
It is most generally believed that this refers to African Ethiopia, located within or near the Torrid Zone <term>the equatorial region between the Tropics of Cancer and Capricorn</term>. Its people are described by Homer as "the remotest of men" <note>original Greek: ἔσχατοι ἀνδρῶν</note>; accordingly, St. Jerome says of this eunuch that he came from Ethiopia—that is, "from the farthest ends of the world" <note>original Latin: ab extremis mundi finibus</note>. The country is sometimes called Cush, probably due to a mixture with the Arabians. Living on the other side of the Red Sea, they may have sent colonies here who, upon settling in these parts, gave them the names of Cush and Sheba <note>original: "Sabaea"</note>. The customs of the people were very crude and uncivilized, and the people themselves—especially to the Jews—were
<margin>
Amos 9:7.
</margin>
so despised as to be a proverb: *Are you not like the children of the Ethiopians to me, O children of Israel? says the Lord.* In fact, even encountering an Ethiopian was considered a bad omen and an unlucky sign. But no country is a barrier to Heaven; the grace of God that brings salvation tears down the barriers <note>original: "plucks up the enclosures"</note> and appears to everyone; so that *in every nation, he who fears God and works righteousness is accepted by him.*

<margin>
ᵇ Josephus, *Antiquities of the Jews*, book 2, chapter 5, page 58.
ᶜ Pliny, *Natural History*, book 6, chapter 29, page 105.
</margin>
VIII. However, we cannot reasonably suppose that this refers to Ethiopia at large—especially as it is compared today with the Abyssinian Empire—but rather to that part of the country whose capital was called Meroe and Saba (as it is called both by Josephus and the Abyssinians themselves today). It was located on a large island surrounded by the Nile and the rivers Astapus and Atbarah <note>original: "Astoborra"</note>, as Josephus informs us. For it was in these parts (as Pliny tells us) that queens had long governed under the title of **Candace**. This custom (as we find in Strabo) first began in the time of Augustus, when a queen of that name was so dear to the people for her incomparable virtues that her successors took the title of **Candace** in her honor. This was used in the same way that Ptolemy was the common name of the Kings of Egypt, Artaxerxes of the Kings of Persia, and Caesar of the Roman Emperors. Indeed,

<margin>
"It should be known that the Ethiopians call every mother of the king 'Candace,' since the Ethiopians do not record the fathers, but pass down that they are sons of the sun, and they call each mother the lady 'Candace.'" <note>original Greek: Ἰστέον δὲ ὅτι Κανδάκην Αἰθίοπες πᾶσαν τὴν τοῦ βασιλέως μητέρα καλοῦσι, ἐπειδὴ πατέρας Αἰθίοπες οὐκ ἀναφέρουσιν, ἀλλ' ὡς ὄντας υἱοὺς ἡλίου παραδιδόασιν, ἑκάστην δὲ τὴν μητέρα κυρίαν Κανδάκην.</note> Ecumenius, *Commentary on Acts*, VIII, page 82.
</margin>
Ecumenius was of the opinion that Candace was only the common name of the queen-mothers of Ethiopia, because that nation did not give the title of "father" to their kings; instead, they acknowledged the Sun as their only common father and their princes as the sons of that parent. But in this, I think he stands alone and contradicts the general consensus of the ancients, who affirm that this nation was subject to
<margin>
ᵈ Eusebius, *Ecclesiastical History*, book 2, chapter 1, page 40.
</margin>
women. Certainly, Eusebius explicitly states it was the custom of this country to be governed by queens even in his own time. The name of the current queen (it is said) was Lacasa, daughter of King Baazena, and she survived the death of our Savior by four years.

IX. Among the high officials of her court, she had at least one eunuch, likely to avoid any suspicion of impropriety. It was the fashion of those Eastern countries (as it still is today) to employ eunuchs in positions of great trust and honor, especially those involving close access to and attendance upon
<margin>
"Among the barbarians, eunuchs are more valued than those who are not, because of their complete trustworthiness." <note>original Greek: Παρὰ τοῖσι βαρβάροισι τιμιώτεροί εἰσι οἱ εὐνοῦχοι, πίστεος εἵνεκα τῆς πάσης, τῶν ἐνορχέων.</note> Herodotus, book 8.
"The leader was Sinnaces, a man of noble family and equal wealth, and next to him was Abdus, though emasculated—not a defect among barbarians, but rather a source of power." <note>original Latin: Auctor Sinnacis, insigni familiâ ac perinde opibus, & proximè huic Abdus, ademptâ virilitatis, non defectum id apud barbaros, ultròque potentiam habet.</note> Tacitus, *Annals*, book 6, chapter 31, page 182.
ᵉ Exists in Bzovius, *Ecclesiastical Annals*, year 1524, number 32, page 543.
</margin>
queens. For although among us the very name sounds vile and contemptible, it is different in those countries. "Among the barbarians" (says Herodotus)—meaning the Eastern people—"eunuchs are persons of the greatest esteem and value." Our eunuch's name (as we find it in the confession made by Zaga Zabo, ambassador from the Ethiopian...

# The Life of St. Philip the Deacon, etc.

<margin>
a Pontius the Deacon in *The Life of Cyprian*, page 11.
</margin>
The Ethiopian official's name (according to the historian of the Ethiopian Emperor) was *Indich*. He was a powerful courtier <gloss>potentate; original Greek: dynastes</gloss>, an officer of state of the highest rank and quality, being no less than the High Treasurer to the Queen. Nor do we find that *Philip*, at the time of his conversion or baptism, found fault with him because of his position or greatness. Certainly, holding public office is in no way inconsistent with Christianity; the Church and the State may well agree, and *Moses* and *Aaron* may go hand in hand. *Peter* baptized *Cornelius*, and *St. Paul* baptized *Sergius*, the *Proconsul* of *Cyprus*, into the Christian faith; yet neither of them found any more fault with them for their positions of authority and power than *Philip* did here with the Lord Treasurer of the *Ethiopian* Queen. 

As for his religion, if he was not a *proselyte of justice* <term>a full convert to Judaism who was circumcised and bound to the whole Law</term> (as some think), he was at least a *proselyte of the gate* <term>a Gentile who followed the basic moral laws of Noah and worshiped the God of Israel without full conversion</term>. It is in this respect that one of the ancient writers calls him a "Jew." He had already entered into the knowledge of the true God and had now come to *Jerusalem* (probably for the solemnity of the *Passover* or the *Feast of Pentecost*) to give public and solemn evidence of his devotion. Though he was an *Ethiopian* and lived many thousands of miles away—and though he was a great statesman necessarily overwhelmed by a crowd of business—yet *he came to Jerusalem to worship*. No road is so long, so rugged, or so difficult, and no expense or interest is so precious, that it should hinder a good person from attending to the concerns of religion. No flimsy or trifling excuses, and no minor or ordinary occasions, should excuse our attendance at places of public worship. Consider here a man who did not think it too much to take a journey of over four thousand miles so that he might appear before God in the solemn place of divine adoration—the *place which God had chosen above all other parts of the world to establish His name*.

<margin>
b John Chrysostom, *Homily 19 on Acts*, page 585.
"So great a lover of the divine Law and of knowledge was he, that he read the sacred scriptures even in his carriage." Jerome, *Letter to Paulinus*, volume 3, page 7. <note>original Latin: Tantus amator Legis divinae, scientiae fuit, ut etiam in vehiculo sacras literas legeret.</note>
</margin>
X. HAVING performed his homage and worship at the Temple, he was now returning to his own country. He had not left his religion behind him at the church, nor did he think it enough simply to have been there; instead, he improved himself while traveling on the way. Even while he sat in his chariot (as <gloss>b</gloss> *Chrysostom* observes), he read the Scriptures. A good person is unwilling to lose even common minutes, but seeks to reclaim whatever time is possible for holy uses. Whether sitting, walking, or journeying, our thoughts should be at work and our affections traveling toward Heaven. 

While the *eunuch* was thus employed, a messenger was sent to him from God; the best way to receive divine communications is to be diligent in our duty. By a voice from Heaven, or some immediate inspiration, *Philip* was commanded to *go near the chariot* and speak to him. He did so and found him reading a section or paragraph of the prophet *Isaiah* concerning the death and sufferings of the *Messiah*—specifically his meek and innocent conduct under the bloody and barbarous violence of his enemies, who treated him with total cruelty and injustice. Since the *eunuch* did not fully understand this, and did not know for certain whether the prophet meant it of himself or another, he asked *St. Philip* to explain it. Philip, being courteously invited up into his chariot, showed him that all this was meant of, and had been fulfilled in, the Holy *Jesus*. He took the opportunity to speak to him about Jesus's birth, his actions and miracles, his sufferings and resurrection from the dead, and his ascension into Heaven, explaining to him the entire system of the Christian faith. His discourse did not fail to achieve its desired

# The Life of St. Philip the Deacon, etc.

It is most generally understood to refer to *African Ethiopia*, lying under or near the Torrid Zone. Its people are described by *Homer* as the "farthest of men" <note>original Greek: *eschatoi andron*</note>, the remotest part of mankind.
<margin>
a *Jerome to Paulinus*, Volume 3, page 7.
</margin>
Accordingly, <sup>a</sup> *St. Jerome* says of this *Eunuch* that he came from *Ethiopia*, that is, "from the farthest corners of the world" <note>original Latin: *ab extremis mundi finibus*</note>. The country is sometimes styled *Cush*, probably from a mixture of *Arabians* who, living on the other side of the *Red Sea*, might have sent colonies here; settling in these parts, they passed on the names of *Cush* and *Saba* to them. The manners of the people were very rude and uncivilized, and the people themselves—especially to the *Jews*—were considered contemptible, even to the point of becoming a proverb:
<margin>
Amos 9:7.
</margin>
> *Are you not like the children of the Ethiopians to me, O children of Israel? says the Lord.*

Indeed, even meeting an *Ethiopian* was accounted a bad omen and an unlucky prediction. But no country is a barrier to *Heaven*; the *grace of God that brings salvation* breaks down the enclosures and *appears to all*; so that *in every nation, he who fears God and works righteousness is accepted by him.*

VIII. But we cannot reasonably suppose that it refers to *Ethiopia* as a whole (especially as it is compared today with the *Abyssinian* Empire), but rather to that part of the country whose capital
<margin>
b *Jewish Antiquities*, Book 2, chapter 5, page 58.
</margin>
was called *Meroe* and *Saba*. It is called both by <sup>b</sup> *Josephus* and the *Abyssinians* themselves today, and is situated on a large island encompassed by the *Nile* and the rivers *Astapus* and *Astoborra*, as *Josephus*
<margin>
c *Natural History*, Book 6, chapter 29, page 105.
</margin>
informs us. For it was around these parts (as <sup>c</sup> *Pliny* tells us) that Queens had governed for a long time under the title of *Candace*. As we find in *Strabo*, this practice first began in the time of *Augustus*, when a Queen of that name, having been dear to the people for her incomparable virtues, led her successors to take the title of *Candace* in her honor. This was used in the same sense that *Ptolemy* was the common name of the Kings of *Egypt*, *Artaxerxes* of the Kings of *Persia*, and *Caesar* of the *Roman* Emperors. 
<margin>
"It should be known that the Ethiopians call all the queen mothers 'Candace,' since the Ethiopians do not refer to a father, but assign their sons to the Sun, and to each mother they give the title Candace." <note>original Greek: *Oecumenius, Commentary on Acts VIII*, p. 82.</note>
</margin>
Indeed, *Oecumenius* was of the opinion that *Candace* was only the common name of the *Queen-mothers* of *Ethiopia*, as that nation did not give the name of "Father" to their kings, acknowledging only the Sun as their common father, and their princes as the sons of that common parent. But in this I think he stands alone and contradicts the general consensus and testimony of the ancients, which affirms this nation to have been subject to women. 
<margin>
d *Ecclesiastical History*, Book 2, chapter 1, page 40.
</margin>
Certainly, <sup>d</sup> *Eusebius* expressly says it was the custom of this country to be governed by Queens even in his own time. The name of the current Queen (it is said) was *Lacasa*, daughter of King *Baazena*, and she survived the death of our Savior by four years.

IX. Among the great officers of her court, she had one (if not more) *Eunuch*, probably to avoid any suspicion of scandal. It was the fashion of those Eastern countries (as it still is today) to employ eunuchs in places of great trust and honor, especially for close access to and attendance upon Queens. For however the very name
<margin>
"Among the barbarians, eunuchs are more valued than those who are uncastrated because of their absolute loyalty." <note>original Greek: *Herodotus, Book 8*</note>
</margin>
sounds vile and contemptible to us, in those countries
<margin>
"Sinnaces was the leader, a man of noble family and wealth, and next to him was Abdus, who had been castrated; this is not looked down upon among the barbarians, but actually brings power." <note>original Latin: *Tacitus, Annals, Book 6*, p. 182.</note> 
e *Exists in Brown's Ecclesiastical Annals for the year 1524*, number 32, page 543.
</margin>
it is otherwise. Among the barbarians (says *Herodotus*)—meaning the Eastern people—eunuchs are persons of the greatest esteem and value. Our eunuch's name (as we find it in the confession made by <sup>e</sup> *Zaga Zabo*, Ambassador from the Ethio-

# The Life of St. Philip the Deacon, etc.

(The Ethiopian Emperor) was *Indich*; a powerful official <note>original Greek: δυνάστης, *dynastes*</note>, a potent courtier, and an officer of state of the highest rank and quality, being no less than High Treasurer to the Queen. We do not find that *Philip*, at either the official's conversion or his baptism, found fault with him for his high position or greatness. Certainly, holding public office is in no way inconsistent with Christianity; the Church and the State may well agree, and *Moses* and *Aaron* <note>Moses represents the law/state, and Aaron represents the priesthood/church</note> may go hand in hand. *Peter* baptized *Cornelius*, and *St. Paul* baptized *Sergius* (the Proconsul of *Cyprus*) into the Christian faith, yet neither of them found any more fault with them for their positions of authority and power than *Philip* did here with the Lord Treasurer of the *Ethiopian* Queen. 

As for his religion, he was—if not a <term>Proselyte of Justice</term> <note>A full convert to Judaism who observed all Mosaic rites, including circumcision</note> as some think—at least a <term>Proselyte of the Gate</term> <note>A Gentile who worshipped the God of Israel and followed basic moral laws without fully converting to Judaism</note>. It is in this respect that one of the ancient writers calls him a "Jew," <margin>a Pontius the Deacon in the *Life of Cyprian*, page 11.</margin> having already entered into the knowledge of the true God. He had now come to *Jerusalem* (probably for the solemnity of the *Passover* or the *Feast of Pentecost*) to give public and solemn evidence of his devotion. Though he was an *Ethiopian* and lived many thousand miles away, and though he was a great statesman necessarily overwhelmed by a crowd of business, yet *he came to Jerusalem to worship*. No journey is so long, so rugged, or so difficult, and no duty or interest is so dear or great, that it should hinder a good person from attending to the concerns of religion. No flimsy or trifling excuses, and no small or common occasions, should justify our absence from places of public worship. Behold here a man who did not think it too much to take a journey of over four thousand miles so that he might appear before God in the solemn place of divine adoration—the *place which God had chosen above all other parts of the world to establish His name*.

X. Having performed his homage and worship at the Temple, he was now returning to his own country. He had not left his religion behind at the temple, nor did he think it was enough simply to have been there; instead, he improved himself while traveling on the road. Even while <margin>b *Chrysostom*, Homily 29 on Acts, page 585. "He was such a lover of divine law and knowledge that he read the sacred scriptures even in his vehicle." Jerome, Letter to Paulinus, Volume 3, page 7.</margin> he sat in his chariot (as <sup>b</sup> *Chrysostom* observes), he read the Scriptures. A good person is unwilling to waste even common minutes, but seeks to redeem whatever time is possible for holy uses; whether sitting, walking, or traveling, our thoughts should be at work and our hearts traveling toward Heaven. 

While the Eunuch was thus employed, a messenger was sent to him from God; the best way to receive divine communication is to be busy with our duty. By a voice from Heaven, or some immediate inspiration, *Philip* was commanded to *go near the chariot* and speak to him. He did so and found him reading a section or paragraph from the Prophet *Isaiah* concerning the death and sufferings of the *Messiah*—His meek and innocent behavior under the bloody and barbaric violence of His enemies, who treated Him with total cruelty and injustice. Since the Eunuch did not fully understand this, and did not know for certain whether the Prophet meant it of himself or another, he asked *St. Philip* to explain it. Being politely invited up into the chariot, *Philip* showed him that all of this was meant for and had been fulfilled in the Holy *Jesus*. He took that opportunity to speak to him about His birth, His actions and miracles, His sufferings and resurrection from the dead, and His ascension into Heaven, explaining to him the entire framework of the Christian faith. His discourse did not fail to achieve its desired...

<meta>This page continues the account of the Ethiopian official’s conversion and baptism by St. Philip. It focuses on the specific location of the baptism, the miraculous circumstances of Philip's departure, and the Eunuch's legacy as the "Apostle of the Ethiopians."</meta>

# The Life of St. Philip the Deacon, &c.

The desired effect was achieved; the *Eunuch* was fully satisfied regarding the *Messiahship* and Divine Authority of our Saviour. He now lacked nothing but the solemn Rite of Initiation to make him a Christian <term>Proselyte</term> <note>A person who has converted from one religion or belief to another; here, a convert to Christianity.</note>. Having come to a place where there was a convenient source of water, he requested to be baptized. After professing his faith in the Son of God and his sincere acceptance of the Christian religion, they both went down into the water. There *Philip* baptized him and, in a spiritual sense, "washed this *Ethiopian* white" <note>This is a rhetorical play on Jeremiah 13:23, "Can the Ethiopian change his skin?" implying that baptism performs a spiritual miracle that nature cannot.</note>.

<margin>
a Annotations on Acts 8:36.
b *Description of the Holy Land*, page 330.
c Eusebius, *On Hebrew Places*, under the entry "Beth-sor," page 66.
d Jerome, *On Hebrew Places*, under the entry "Besur."
e Ado the Martyrologist, June 8th <note>original: "VIII. Idus Jun."</note>.
f Cotovicus, *Travels*, book 2, chapter 9, page 247.
Verse 39. "The Holy Spirit fell upon the Eunuch, and an angel of the Lord snatched away Philip." <note>original Greek: Πνεῦμα ἅγιον ἐπέπεσεν ἐπὶ τὸν Εὐνοῦχον, ἄγγελος δὲ Κυρίου ἥρπασε τὸν Φίλιππον.</note> Codex Alexandrinus in the Royal English Library and several other manuscript codices.
</margin>

XI. Regarding the place where this *Eunuch* was baptized, <sup>a</sup> *Beza* <note>Theodore Beza (1519–1605), a major Protestant theologian.</note> makes a very significant error by identifying it as the River *Eleutherus*, which ran near the foot of Mount *Lebanon* in the northernmost borders of *Palestine*—quite at the other end of the country. <sup>b</sup> *Brochardus* places it near *Nehel Escol*, or the "Torrent of the Grape" (the place from which the biblical spies fetched the giant cluster of grapes). He claims that on the left side of this valley, about a mile and a half <note>original: "half a League"</note> away, a brook runs not far from *Sicelech* <note>Likely Ziklag.</note>, in which this *Eunuch* was baptized. However, <sup>c</sup> *Eusebius* and <sup>d</sup> *St. Jerome* (followed in this by <sup>e</sup> *Ado* the *Martyrologist*) more probably place it near *Beth-soron* <note>Now known as Beth-zur.</note>. We are told <sup>f</sup> the site is still visible today, located in a village 20 miles from *Jerusalem* on the road toward *Hebron*, near which there was a spring bubbling up at the foot of a hill. *St. Jerome* adds that the spring was immediately swallowed up by the same ground that produced it, and that it was here that *Philip* baptized the *Ethiopian*. No sooner was this done than Heaven set an extraordinary seal upon his conversion and admission into the Christian faith—especially if it is true, as some very ancient *Manuscripts* add to the passage, that upon being baptized, *the Holy Ghost fell upon him*, furnishing him with miraculous gifts and powers, and that *Philip* was immediately snatched away from him.

<margin>
g Commentary on Isaiah 53, Volume 5, page 195.
h Eusebius, *Ecclesiastical History*, book 2, chapter 1, page 40; Cyril of Jerusalem, *Catechetical Lectures*, 17, page 457; Psalm 68:31.
i Quoted in Bzovius as cited above; see also Godignus, *On Abyssinian Matters*, book 1, chapter 18, page 113.
k *Synopsis* as cited above; see also Sophronius, quoted in Jerome.
l Quoted in Godignus at the location cited, page 117.
</margin>

XII. Though the *Eunuch* had lost his tutor, he rejoiced that he had found such a great treasure: the knowledge of Christ and of the true way to Heaven. He continued his journey with infinite peace and tranquility of mind, satisfied with the happiness that had befallen him. Upon returning to his country, he preached and spread the Christian faith, sharing the glad tidings of a Saviour. In this respect, <sup>g</sup> *St. Jerome* titles him the *Apostle of the Ethiopians*. The <sup>h</sup> ancient writers generally believe that the prediction of *David* was fulfilled in him: "*Ethiopia shall stretch out her hands unto God*" <note>Psalm 68:31</note>. Consequently, the *Ethiopians* are known to boast (as appears in the <sup>i</sup> confession made by the *Abyssinian* Ambassador) that through this *Eunuch*, they received baptism nearly earlier than any other Christians in the world. Indeed, they have a constant tradition that for many ages they had knowledge of the true God of *Israel*, dating back to the time of the *Queen of Sheba*. Since "*Seba*" was the name of this country, as we noted before, it is probable she ruled here. They tell us her name was *Maqueda* <note>Makeda</note>; having learned the *Jewish* Law from *Solomon* and received the books of their religion, she taught them to her subjects and sent her son *Meilech* <note>Menelik I</note> to *Solomon* to be educated by him. The story of this may be read at length in that confession. I add nothing more concerning the *Eunuch* than what <sup>k</sup> *Dorotheus* and others relate: that he is reported to have suffered martyrdom and to have been honorably buried, and that diseases were cured and other miracles performed at his tomb even in his own time. <sup>l</sup> The traditions of the country specifically tell us that after the *Eunuch* returned home, he first converted his mistress, Queen *Candace*, to the—

<meta>This page continues the account of the Ethiopian Eunuch's return home and begins the narrative of St. Philip’s later ministry and life in Caesarea.</meta>

# The Life of St. Philip the Deacon, &c.

Christian Faith, and afterwards by her leave propagated it throughout *Ethiopia*, until meeting with *St. Matthew* the Apostle, by their joint efforts they expelled idolatry out of all those parts. When this was done, he crossed the Red Sea and preached the Christian religion in *Arabia*, *Persia*, *India*, and many other of those Eastern nations, until at length in the island of *Taprobana* <note>The ancient name for Sri Lanka.</note>, since called *Ceylon*, he sealed his doctrine with his blood <note>He was martyred.</note>.

XIII. GOD, who always provides what is sufficient, is not accustomed to multiply means further than is necessary. *Philip*, having completed the errand upon which he was sent, was immediately caught up and carried away—no doubt by the ministry of an angel—and landed at *Azotus*. This city was known in ancient times as *Ashdod*, a *Philistine* city on the borders of the territory of the Tribe of *Dan*. It was famous of old for the temple and residence of the idol *Dagon*, and for the captivity of the *Ark of the Covenant* which was kept for some time in this place. Now, it was enlightened by *St. Philip’s* preaching; he went up and down publishing the Gospel in all the surrounding parts until he arrived at *Caesarea*. 

This city was previously called *Turris Stratonis* <note>Strato's Tower</note>, and was afterwards rebuilt and enlarged by *Herod* the Great. In honor of *Augustus Caesar*, to whom he was greatly indebted, Herod named it *Caesarea*. For Caesar's sake, he also erected a stately palace of marble there called *Herod’s Judgment Hall* <note>The Praetorium; this was the site of the trial of many early Christians.</note>, where his nephew—ambitious for greater honors and applause than were appropriate for him—had that fatal execution served upon him <note>Referring to Herod Agrippa I, who was struck down by an angel as described in Acts 12:21–23.</note>. It was a place remarkable for many devout and pious men; here lived *Cornelius*, who (along with his family) was baptized by *Peter* and was, in that respect, the "first fruits" of the Gentile world. To this city came *Agabus* the prophet, who foretold *St. Paul’s* imprisonment and martyrdom. Here *St. Paul* himself was kept prisoner and made those brave and noble defenses of himself, first before *Felix*, and later before *Festus* and *Agrippa*. 

Here also our *St. Philip* had his house and family, to which he probably now retired and where he spent the remainder of his life. For many years later, we find *St. Paul* and his company,
<margin>Acts 21:8, 9.</margin>
coming from *Ptolemais* on their journey to *Jerusalem*, entering into the *house of* Philip *the Evangelist, who was one of the seven <term>deacons</term>, and staying with him; and this same man had four daughters, virgins, who did prophesy.* These virgin-prophetesses were endowed with the gift of foretelling future events. Although "prophecy" in those times also implied a faculty for explaining the more obscure and difficult parts of Christian doctrine, a peculiar ability to demonstrate Christ’s Messiahship from the predictions of *Moses* and the Prophets, and the ability to speak spontaneously upon any difficult and emerging occasion, we cannot suppose these virgins had this specific part of the prophetic faculty—or at least, they did not publicly exercise it in the congregation. This gift, therefore, unquestionably concerned things to come. It was an instance of God accomplishing an ancient promise that in the times of the *Messiah*, he would
<margin>Acts 2:17, 18.</margin>
*pour out his Spirit upon all flesh; on their sons and daughters, servants and handmaidens, and they should prophesy.* The names of two of these daughters, the Greek *Menaion* <note>A liturgical book of the Eastern Orthodox Church containing the lives of saints.</note> tells us, were *Hermione* and *Eutychis*, who came into *Asia* after *St. John’s* death; the first of them died and was buried at *Ephesus*.

not certainly known. *Dorotheus* <note>Dorotheus of Tyre, a 4th-century bishop and writer.</note>, I do not know upon what grounds, will have

# The Life of St. Barnabas the Apostle.

*His commission to confirm the Church of Antioch. His taking St. Paul as his assistant. Their being sent with contributions to the Church at Jerusalem. Their unique <note>original: "peculiar"</note> separation for the ministry to the Gentiles. The laying on of hands <note>original: "Impoſition of hands"</note> as the usual Rite of Ordination. Their travels through several countries. Their success in Cyprus. Barnabas at Lystra mistaken for Jupiter, and why. Their return to Antioch. Their embassy to Jerusalem regarding the controversy concerning the legal rites <note>the requirements of the Jewish Law</note>. Barnabas misled <note>original: "seduced"</note> by Peter’s hypocrisy <note>original: "diſſimulation"</note> at Antioch. The disagreement <note>original: "diſſenſion"</note> between him and St. Paul. Barnabas’s journey to Cyprus. His voyage to Rome and preaching the Christian Faith there. His martyrdom by the Jews in Cyprus. His burial. When his body was first discovered. St. Matthew's Hebrew Gospel found with it. The great privileges then conferred upon the Bishop's seat <note>original: "See"</note> of Salamis. A description of his person and character <note>original: "temper"</note>. The Epistle published in ancient times under his name. The purpose of it. The practical part of it excellently presented under the "two ways" of Light and Darkness.*

---

I. <image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'T' within a square frame with foliate patterns.</image-desc> <margin>"And it seems to me that he received the name from his virtue, as being capable and fit for this purpose." <note>original Greek: Καὶ δοκεῖ μοι ἀπὸ τῆς ἀρετῆς εἰληφέναι τὸ ὄνομα, ὡς πρὸς τοῦτο ἱκανὸς ὤν, καὶ ἐπιτηδής.</note> Chrysostom, Homily 11 on the Acts of the Apostles, page 529.</margin> The proper and (if I may call it so) original name of this *Apostle* (for with that title *St. Luke*, and after him the ancient writers, constantly honor him) was *Joses*—a softer ending familiar to the Greeks for *Joseph*, and so the Kings' and several other manuscript copies read it. It was the name given to him at his circumcision, no doubt in honor of *Joseph*, one of the great Patriarchs of their nation. After his embracing Christianity, the Apostles added the name *Barnabas* to this. *Joses*, who was surnamed *Barnabas* by the Apostles, was so called either to imply he was a *Son of Prophecy*, eminent for his prophetic gifts and endowments, or to denote him (which was a specific part of the Prophet’s office) a *Son of Consolation*, for his admirable skill <note>original: "dexterity"</note> in lifting up <note>original: "erecting"</note> troubled minds and leading them on by the most mild and gentle methods of persuasion. <margin>See Notker’s Martyrology at the 3rd Ides of June [June 11] in Canisius’s Ancient Readings, Volume 6.</margin> However, I rather believe he was so titled for his generous charity in refreshing the souls <note>original: "bowels"</note> of the Saints, especially since the name seems to have been given to him on that occasion. He was born in *Cyprus*, a well-known island in the Mediterranean Sea, lying between *Cilicia*, *Syria*, and *Egypt*. It is a large and fertile country, the setting in ancient times of no fewer than nine different kingdoms. It was so fruitful and richly furnished with everything that can serve either the necessity or pleasure of human life that it was of old called *Macaria*, or *The Happy*. <margin>L. Florus, book 3, chapter 9, page 67.</margin> The historian reports that *Portius Cato*, having conquered this island, brought more treasures from there into the treasury <note>original: "Exchequer"</note> at *Rome* than had been done in any other triumph. But in nothing was it more happy, or on any account more memorable in the records of the Church, than that it was the birthplace of our Apostle. His ancestors, during the troublesome times of *Antiochus Epiphanes*, or during the conquest of *Judea* by *Pompey* and the *Roman* army, had fled here as a place best secured from violence and invasion, and settled here.

II. He was descended from the Tribe of *Levi* and the line of the Priesthood, which rendered his conversion to Christianity even more remarkable. All his personal interests would have naturally biased <note>original: "leaven him with mighty prejudices"</note> him against the

# The Life of St. Barnabas the Apostle.

...the Christian Faith. But God's grace often delights in asserting itself against the strongest opposition, and loves to triumph where there is the least probability of success. His parents were wealthy and pious, and seeing that he was a handsome and promising youth (says my author, who draws his information from Clement of Alexandria and other ancient writers), they sent or brought him to Jerusalem to be educated in the knowledge of the Law. To that end, they committed him to the tutelage of Gamaliel, the great Doctor of the Law and the most famous teacher in Israel at that time. At his feet, he was raised alongside St. Paul; if this is true, it may have laid the early foundation for the close friendship that later existed between them. Here he improved in learning and piety, visiting the Temple and devotedly practicing fasting and prayer. 

We are further told that, being a frequent spectator of our Savior’s miracles—including the healing of the paralytic at the Pool of Bethesda—he was soon convinced of Christ’s divinity and persuaded to commit himself to his teachings and discipline. Since the nature of true goodness is always communicative, he immediately went and informed his sister Mary about the Messiah. She hurried to see him and urged him to come to her house, where our Lord later used to gather with his disciples (as the Church continued to do after his death). It is said that her son Mark was the "young man" carrying the pitcher of water whom our Lord commanded the two disciples to follow home to prepare for the celebration of the Passover.

III. But however that was, he undoubtedly remained with our Lord until the end. After the Ascension, he was a strong candidate to be chosen as one of the twelve, if it is true (as is generally assumed, though I believe without reason—John Chrysostom certainly disagrees) that he is the same person as Joseph called Barsabas, who was a candidate alongside Matthias for the Apostleship <note>original: "Apostolate"</note> to replace Judas. Regardless, Clement of Alexandria expressly affirms that he was one of the seventy disciples, as others do after him. When the daily increasing needs of the Church required more than ordinary resources, he—following the free and noble spirit of those times—sold lands of great value and laid the money at the Apostles' feet. If one asks how a Levite <term>A member of the Hebrew tribe of Levi, who traditionally served as assistants to the priests in the Temple and were not typically allotted land in Israel</term> came to own lands and possessions, when the Mosaic Law allowed them no specific portions of land except what was provided by the public, the answer is simply that this estate was his family inheritance in Cyprus, where Jewish land laws did not apply. It was surely an estate of very significant value, and parting with it was an act of extraordinary charity; otherwise, the sacred historian would not have made such a specific point of mentioning it.

IV. After the Church was scattered following St. Stephen’s martyrdom, we have no certain account of what happened to him. In all probability, he stayed with the Apostles in Jerusalem, where we find him not long after St. Paul’s conversion. For that fierce and active zealot, having been miraculously stopped in the height of his rage and now adopting the innocent and harmless nature of a lamb, came to Jerusalem after a short time and addressed the Church. But the believers, not convinced that his change was real and fearing it might be a clever trick to betray them, avoided his company entirely. And what wonder is it if the harmless sheep fled at the sight of the wolf that had...
