# The Life of St. Barnabas the Apostle.

...had made such havoc of the flock—until *Barnabas*, likely relying on their former acquaintance <note>likely referring to their shared education under Gamaliel</note>, entered into a more familiar conversation with him. He introduced him to the Apostles and testified to them about the manner of his conversion and the clear evidence he had given of it at *Damascus* through his bold and resolute debates with the *Jews*.

<margin>Acts 11:20.</margin>
V. *There is one who scatters, and yet increases* <note>A reference to Proverbs 11:24</note>: the scattering of the Church by *Saul’s* persecution proved to be the means of a more plentiful harvest, as the Christian religion was thereby carried in all directions to both *Jews* and *Gentiles*. Among the rest, some *Cypriot* and *Cyrenian* converts went to *Antioch*, where they preached the Gospel with mighty success. Great numbers of both *Jews* and *proselytes* <term>Gentile converts to Judaism</term> (with which that city abounded) heartily embraced the Christian faith. When news of this reached the Apostles at *Jerusalem*, they sent *Barnabas* down to investigate and to settle this new plantation <note>meaning a newly established church community</note>. Having arrived, he rejoiced to see that Christianity had made such fair progress in that great city. He earnestly urged them to persevere cordially and constantly in the excellent religion they had embraced. He himself, like a pious and good man, underwent many labors and difficulties; God was pleased to crown these with corresponding success, leading to the addition of multitudes of new converts to the faith. But the work was too great to be managed by one person alone. Therefore, to provide himself with suitable assistance, he went to *Tarsus* to look for *St. Paul*, who had lately arrived there. He brought Paul back with him to *Antioch*, where both of them continued industriously ministering to the growth and establishment of the church for a whole year. It was then and there that the disciples of the Holy *Jesus* first had the honorable name of *Christians* solemnly given to them.

VI. It happened about this time, or not long after, that a severe famine (predicted by *Agabus*, a *Christian* prophet who came down to *Antioch*) pressed upon the provinces of the *Roman Empire*, and especially *Judea*. Because of this, the Christians, whose resources were exhausted by their continual contributions for the maintenance of the poor, were reduced to great extremities. The Church of *Antioch*, feeling compassion for their miserable condition, agreed to provide a liberal and charitable supply for their relief. They entrusted this to *Barnabas* and *Paul*, whom they sent with the funds to the governors of the churches so they might distribute them as necessity required.

<margin>Greek Rituals for the promotion of the High Steward, page 281. <note>original Latin: "Ritual. Graecor. in promot. Oeconom. p. 281."</note></margin>
The *Greek Rituals* no doubt refer to this charitable mission when, in the liturgy for the promotion of the *Magnus Oeconomus* <note>original Latin: "High Steward"</note>, or High Steward of the Church (whose role it was to manage and distribute the church’s revenues), they make particular mention of the holy and most famous *Barnabas* the Apostle and generous martyr.

<margin>Acts 12:25.</margin>
Having discharged their trust, they returned from *Jerusalem* to *Antioch*, bringing with them *John*, surnamed *Mark*, the son of *Mary*, sister to *Barnabas*. Her house was a sanctuary where the Church found both shelter for their people and a convenient place for their solemn worship services.

VII. The Church of *Antioch* being now sufficiently provided with spiritual guides, our two Apostles could be better spared for the conversion of the Gentile world. As they were engaged in the duties of fasting and prayer, and other public exercises of their religion, the Spirit of God—by some prophetic *afflatus* <term>divine breath or inspiration</term> or revelation made to some of the prophets present there—commanded that *Barnabas* and...

--- Page 113 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of St. Barnabas, focusing on the specific ceremony of his ordination alongside St. Paul and the beginning of their first missionary journey to Cyprus.</meta>

# The Life of St. Barnabas the Apostle.

Saul should be set apart for that specific ministry for which God had designated them. Accordingly, after they had fasted and prayed, *hands* were solemnly *laid* upon them to signify their particular appointment to that service. The laying on of hands had been a ceremony of ancient origin. Even among the *Gentiles*, it was customary to appoint people to public functions and offices by lifting up or stretching out the hand, by which they gave their votes and support for those positions. But in this, although they did *stretch forth their hands* <note>original: *χειροτονεῖν* (cheirotonia)</note>, they did not lay on their hands, which was the proper ceremony used and of much older standing in the *Jewish* Church. When *Moses* chose the *seventy elders* to be his assistants in the government, the *Jews* say it was by laying his hands on them; and when he appointed *Joshua* to be his successor, he *laid his hands on him and gave him his charge in front of the whole congregation.* This custom they constantly maintained when appointing both civil and church officials, not only while their Temple and government stood, but long after the fall of their church and state. <margin>a *Benjamin of Tudela, Travels, p. 73.*</margin> For so *Benjamin* the *Jew* <note>The famous 12th-century traveler Benjamin of Tudela</note> tells us that in his time, all the *Israelites* of the *East*, when they needed a Rabbi or teacher in their synagogues, would bring him to the "Head of the Exile" <note>original: *Resh Galuta* (ראש הגולה) or *Aichmalotarches* (Αἰχμαλωτάρχης)</note> residing at *Babylon* (at that time *Rabbi Daniel*, the son of *Hasdai*). This was so he might receive the authority and power <note>original: *ha-semikhah ve-rashut* (הסמיכה ורשות)</note> by *the laying on of hands* to become a preacher to them. From the *Jews*, this practice—along with some other rites—was transferred into the Christian Church for ordaining guides and ministers of religion, and it has been used through all ages and periods to this day. Though the *laying on of hands* <note>original: *cheirothesia* (χειροθεσία)</note> and *ordination* <note>original: *cheirotonia* (χειροτονία)</note> are not used in exactly the same way in the writings and practice of the Church; the first implies the simple rite of laying on hands, while the other refers to the ordination itself and the entire ceremony. This is why the <margin>b *Apostolical Constitutions, Book 8, Chapter 28, Column 494.*</margin> *author of the Apostolical Constitutions*, speaking of the involvement of elders <term>presbyters</term> in this matter, says "he lays on his hands, but he does not ordain" <note>original: *cheirothetei ou cheirotonei* (χειροθετεῖ οὐ χειροτονεῖ)</note>. He was referring to the custom then, and ever since, of elders laying their hands on the candidate along with the Bishop in that solemn act.

VIII. BARNABAS and Paul, having thus received a divine commission for their apostleship to the *Gentiles*, and taking *Mark* along with them as their assistant and attendant, immediately entered upon their territory. First, they went to *Seleucia*, a neighboring city located where the River *Orontes* flows into the *Mediterranean* Sea. From there, they set sail for *Cyprus*, *Barnabas's* native country, and arrived at *Salamis*. This was once a city of great importance, the ruins of which are two miles from modern *Famagusta*. There, they undauntedly preached in the *Jewish* synagogues. From *Salamis*, they traveled across the island to *Paphos*, a city famous in ancient times for the worship of *Venus*, "the powerful goddess of Cyprus" <note>original: *Diva potens Cypri*</note>, the protective goddess of the island. She was worshipped there with the most immoral and immodest rites and had a famous temple dedicated to her for that purpose. Regarding this, the inhabitants have a <margin>c *Cotovicus, Travels, book 1, chapter 16, p. 100.*</margin> tradition that at St. *Barnabas's* prayers, the temple fell flat to the ground. The ruins of an ancient church are still shown to travelers, and beneath it is an arch where *Paul* and *Barnabas* were shut up in prison. In this place was the court or residence of the *Praetor*, or governor of the island. He was not strictly a "Proconsul" <note>original: *Anthypatos* (Ἀνθύπατος)</note>, for *Cyprus* was not a proconsular but a praetorian province <note>meaning it was governed by a praetor rather than a former consul</note>. This governor, being entirely influenced by the advice and sorceries of *Bar-Jesus*, a prominent magician, held back...

This quickly convinced the Governor [Sergius Paulus] and brought him over as a convert to that religion; as it made the best offers, he could not help but see it had the strongest evidence to support it.

<margin>Acts 13:13</margin>

IX. LEAVING *Cyprus*, they sailed over to *Perga* in *Pamphylia*, a city famous for a temple dedicated to *Diana*. Here, *John Mark* apparently became weary of this wandering life and the unavoidable dangers that went with it; he took his leave and returned to *Jerusalem*. This departure laid the foundation for an unhappy disagreement that later broke out between these two Apostles. The next place they visited was *Antioch* in *Pisidia*. In the *Jewish* synagogue there, *St. Paul* converted great numbers of both *Jews* and *proselytes* <note>original: "Proselytes"; non-Jews who had converted to Judaism</note> with an elegant speech. However, when others raised a persecution against them, they were forced to leave the city. From there, they passed to *Iconium*, a noted city of *Lycaonia*, where they preached in the synagogues for a long time with good success. Eventually, a conspiracy was formed against them, and they withdrew to *Lystra*. After *St. Paul* performed a miraculous cure there, the inhabitants treated them as gods who had come down from Heaven in human form. Because *St. Paul* was the primary speaker, they called him *Mercury*, the interpreter of the gods. They looked upon *Barnabas* as *Jupiter*, their supreme deity—either because of his age or, as *St. Chrysostom* thinks, because he was "stately in appearance" <note>original: *ἀπὸ τ᾿ ὄψεως ἀξιοπρεπὴς* (apo t’ opseōs axioprepēs)</note>. He was known for the gravity and handsomeness of his person, being (as ancient accounts describe him) a very handsome man with a venerable appearance. In this regard, he had a significant advantage over *St. Paul*, who was described as having a very plain and unimpressive presence. Yet the malice of the *Jews* followed them even here, and they persuaded the people to stone *St. Paul*. After he quickly recovered, he and *Barnabas* went to *Derbe*. Once they had converted many there to the faith, they returned back through *Lystra*, *Iconium*, and *Antioch*, and then through *Pisidia* to *Pamphylia*. From *Perga*, they went to *Attalia*, strengthening the churches they had planted during their first journey. At *Attalia*, they took a ship and sailed to *Antioch* in *Syria*, the place where they had first started. There, they gave the church an account of the entire success of their travels and how the way had been opened for the spread of Christianity in the *Gentile world*.

<margin>Acts 15:1</margin>
X. THE restless enemy of all goodness [Satan] was annoyed to see such fair and smooth progress for the *Gospel*. Therefore, he resolved to attack it using the old, subtle tactics of internal divisions and animosities. What the *envious man* [the devil] could not suppress through open violence, he sought to stifle by *sowing tares* <note>A reference to Jesus’s parable in Matthew 13:24–30 about weeds (tares) planted among wheat</note>. Some zealous converts coming down from *Jerusalem* to *Antioch* introduced a specific idea, which they defended with all possible passion and stubbornness: that unless people joined the observance of *Mosaic rites* <note>original: "Mosaic Rites"; the laws and ceremonies of the Old Testament, such as circumcision</note> to their Christian religion, there could be no hope of salvation for them. *Paul* and *Barnabas* opposed this incorrect opinion with all their vigor and wit. Unable to settle the matter, they were sent by the church to consult with the Apostles and brothers in *Jerusalem*. Upon their arrival, they were kindly and courteously received. The *right hand of fellowship* was given to them by the three great Apostles—*Peter*, *James*, and *John*—and an agreement was made between them: wherever they went, those three would focus on the *Jews*, while *Paul* and *Barnabas* would dedicate themselves to the *Gentiles*. And it was likely here

<meta>This page continues the biography of St. Barnabas, detailing the resolution of the Council of Jerusalem, a subsequent theological disagreement in Antioch involving St. Peter, and the eventual missionary separation of Paul and Barnabas.</meta>

<margin><sup>a</sup> Alexander the Monk, as cited above, number 15.</margin>
...that *Mark* reconciled himself to his uncle *Barnabas*. According to one author <sup>a</sup>, he did this with tears and great persistence, earnestly begging him to forgive his weakness and cowardice, and promising a firmer constancy and more undaunted resolution for the future. But they were especially careful to attend to the great mission they were sent for, and accordingly presented the case in a public Council convened for that purpose. After *Peter* had first given his judgment that the *Gentile converts* were under no such obligation to follow Jewish law, *Paul* and *Barnabas* informed the *Synod* <note>a council of church leaders</note> what great things *God* had performed through their ministry for the conversion of the *Gentiles*. This was plain evidence that they were accepted by *God* without the *Mosaic rites and ceremonies* <note>the traditional laws and rituals of the Old Testament</note>. Once the matter was decided by the Council, the determination was drawn up in the form of a *Synodical Epistle* <note>a formal letter from a church council</note>, which was delivered to *Barnabas* and *Paul*. The Council gave them this *tribute* <note>original: "elogium"</note> and character: that they were men who had risked their lives for the name of the *Lord Jesus Christ*. The Council also joined two of their own men with them so they might carry the letter to the *Churches*. Having arrived at *Antioch*, they delivered the *decrees* of the Council; the *Church* was abundantly satisfied with these, and the controversy was put to rest for the time being.

<margin>Galatians 2:11</margin>
<margin><sup>b</sup> Commentary on Isaiah 11, page 55, Volume 2.</margin>
XI. IT was not long after this that *St. Peter* came down to *Antioch*. Reluctant to offend the *zealous Jews*, he withdrew from all conversation and meals with the *Gentile converts*, contrary to his former practice and his recent vote and support in the *Synod* at *Jerusalem*. The minds of the *Gentiles* were greatly disturbed by this, and the *converted Jews*, tempted by his example, also abstained from all communion with the *Gentiles*. Indeed, so strong was this temptation that *St. Barnabas* himself was carried away by the current; he began to doubt whether it was lawful to hold communion with the *Gentiles*, with whom he had previously conversed so familiarly and had been so remarkably instrumental in converting to *Christianity*. The example of a great or good man has such a powerful influence in determining whether others do good or bad. How careful we should be in the course we take, lest we seduce and compel others to walk in our crooked paths and load ourselves with the guilt of those who follow after us! Shortly after, *St. Paul* proposed to *Barnabas* that they should again visit the *Churches* where they had recently planted the *Christian faith*. Barnabas liked the proposal but desired that his cousin *Mark* might go with them again. *St. Paul* would by no means consent to this, having found by Mark’s cowardly desertion of them at *Pamphylia* how unfit he was for such a troublesome and dangerous service. This led to a sharp contest and ripened into an almost irreconcilable difference between these two holy men. While this shows that even the best people are subject to the same passions and infirmities as others—liable to be swayed by partiality and carried off by the heat of irregular passion—it also shows us how great a matter a little fire kindles. It reveals how a seemingly small occasion can lead to strife and division, risking the breach of the firmest charity and friendship. The result was that the sacred pair <note>original Greek: "ἡ ξυνωρὶς ἡ ἱερὰ" (hē xynōris hē hiera); Theodoret, as cited in margin b, uses this phrase for the two Apostles</note>, who had until then equally and unanimously shared the yoke of the *Gospel*, now pulled in different directions and parted from each other in some discontent. *St. Paul* took *Silas* and went to the *Churches* of *Syria* and *Cilicia*, while *Barnabas*, accompanied by his cousin *Mark*, set sail for *Cyprus*, his own country.

XII. THUS far the sacred historian [Luke] has primarily guided us,

ᵇ *Recognitions*, Book 1, chapter 7, page 400, Paris edition, 1672. *Clementine Homilies*, 1, chapter 7, page 549. *Epitome of the Acts of the Blessed Peter*, chapter 7, page 752.
ᶜ Baronius, for the year 51 AD, numbers 52, 54. Notes on the *Roman Martyrology*, page 359.
</margin>
...us, who ends his accounts concerning him here. What happened to him afterward is a matter of great uncertainty. ᵃ Dorotheus and the ᵇ author of the *Recognitions*, along with some other writings attributed to St. Clement, claim that he went to Rome and was one of the first to preach the Christian faith in that city. For this, ᶜ Baronius <note>Cesare Baronius (1538–1607) was a prominent Italian Cardinal and ecclesiastical historian</note> attacks them, as he is unwilling to believe that anyone was thought to have been there before St. Peter. However, Baronius is not unwilling to grant that Barnabas was there *after* Peter—out of good manners, he insists on letting Peter go first. Setting aside this difference regarding the timing, let us see what the records say about him there. Upon his first arrival in Rome around autumn, he is said to have publicly addressed the people in this way: "Men of Rome, listen" <note>original Greek: Ἄνδρες Ῥωμαῖοι ἀκούσατε</note>.

> "O Romans, listen. The Son of God has appeared in the land of Judea, promising eternal life to all who are willing to accept it and to live their lives according to the will of the Father who sent him. Therefore, change your way of life and turn from a worse state to a better one, from temporary things to those that are eternal. Recognize that there is only one God, who is in Heaven, and whose world you unjustly occupy in his righteous presence. But if you reform and live according to his laws, you shall be brought into another world, where you shall become immortal and enjoy the indescribable glories and happiness of that state. However, if you persist in your lack of faith, your souls, after the death of these bodies, shall be thrown into a place of fire where they shall be eternally tormented by the pain of a useless and late repentance. For the present life is the only time and opportunity given to everyone for repentance."

<margin>
ᵈ Clement, in the same place, chapters 8, 9, 10.
ᵉ *Epitome*, chapter 8 and following.
</margin>
This was spoken with great plainness and simplicity, without any elaborate rhetorical tricks. Accordingly, it appealed to the attentive common people. Meanwhile, the philosophers and more inquisitive intellectuals treated the discourse with scorn and laughter. The ᵈ author of the *Clementina* <note>original: τὰ Κλημέντια; a collection of early Christian literature attributed to Pope Clement I</note> and the ᵉ *Epitome* refer to this happening while he was at Alexandria, rather than Rome, which differs somewhat from the *Recognitions*. These critics attacked him with tricky questions, logical arguments, and sophisticated reasoning. But Barnabas, taking no notice of their irrelevant questions, continued his plain discourse. He concluded by saying that he had presented these things to them plainly, and that it was up to them whether they would reject or accept them. He stated that, for his part, he could not remain silent without harming his own soul, nor could they disbelieve without infinite danger to themselves.

<margin>
ᶠ Clement and the *Epitome*, in the same place. Alexander the Monk, cited above, numbers 13, 14.
ᵍ Baronius, for the year 51 AD, number 54. Sanctius, *On the Preaching of St. James*, Treatise 3, chapter 1, number 9.
ʰ *Synopsis* in the *Library of the Church Fathers*, page 148, Volume 3.
ⁱ Alexander the Monk, in the same place, number 18 and following.
ᵏ For the year 485 AD, number 4, page 428.
</margin>
XIII. DEPARTING from Rome, different writers claim ᶠ he took different paths. The Greeks say he went to Alexandria and then to Judea. The ᵍ writers of the Roman Church (with whom ʰ Dorotheus agrees on this matter) say that he preached the Gospel in Liguria and founded a church at Milan, where he became the first bishop, spreading Christianity throughout those parts. However that may be, it is probable that in the final years of his life he returned to Cyprus. My ⁱ source tells us that he converted many there, until some Jews from Syria came to Salamis, where he was then staying. Enraged with fury, they attacked him as he was debating in the synagogue. They locked him in a corner of the building until nightfall, when they brought him out and, after extensive torture, stoned him to death. The author adds (and the truth of this must rest on the credibility of the storyteller, who ᵏ Baronius tells us lived at the time when Barnabas's body was first discovered) that they threw

<meta>This page concludes the account of St. Barnabas's death and burial, the discovery of his remains during the reign of Emperor Zeno, and the jurisdictional independence granted to the Church of Cyprus as a result. # *The Life of St. BARNABAS the Apostle.*

<margin>
ᵃ Theodore Lector, *Ecclesiastical History*, book 2, page 557. Alexander the Monk, in the location cited above, number 31.
ᵇ Nicephorus, *Ecclesiastical History*, book 16, chapter 37, page 716, Volume 2.
ᶜ Alexander, as above, numbers 29, 30.
</margin>

They threw his body into the fire with the intent to consume it, but the flames had not the least power over it. His kinsman Mark privately buried him in a cave not far from the city, while his friends mourned the loss with solemn lamentation. I will omit the miracles reported to have occurred at his tomb. The remains of his body were discovered in the year 485 AD, during the reign of Emperor Zeno (though Nicephorus mistakenly dates this to the twelfth year of Anastasius). He was dug up under a carob tree <note>original: "Bean or Carob Tree"</note>, and upon his breast was found a copy of St. Matthew’s Gospel written in Barnabas’s own hand. Bishop Anthemius took the Gospel with him to Constantinople, where the Emperor received it with great reverence and laid it away with great care and diligence.

As a testimony of his joy, the Emperor honored the Episcopal See of Salamis with a special prerogative: it was to be an "autocephalous" see <note>original Greek: sedes αὐτοκέφαλος</note>, meaning it was independent of any foreign jurisdiction. This privilege was later ratified by Emperor Justinian, whose wife, Theodora, was a Cypriot. The Emperor also greatly enriched the Bishop upon his return, commanding him to build a church dedicated to St. Barnabas over the place of his burial, which was accordingly erected with more than ordinary stateliness and magnificence. 

It is added to the story that these remains were discovered because St. Barnabas himself appeared to Anthemius three separate times. However, I view this as a mere addition to the story, designed only to serve a contemporary purpose. At that time, Peter (nicknamed "the Fuller"), the Patriarch of Antioch, claimed jurisdiction over the Cypriot churches as if they were subject to his see. Anthemius would not agree to this and stubbornly asserted his own rights. How easy it would have been to take the occasion of finding St. Barnabas's body and add the detail of the visions to gain credit for the cause and advance it with the Emperor. Accordingly, it had its intended effect. Whoever reads the whole story and the circumstances of the appearances, as related by my author, will see that they seem plainly calculated for such a purpose.

<margin>
ᵈ Ibid. number 18.
ᵉ *On Modesty*, chapter 20, page 582. See also Philastrius, *On Heresies*, chapter 60.
ᶠ *Against Celsus*, book 1, page 49.
ᵍ *Ecclesiastical History*, book 3, chapter 25, page 97.
</margin>

XIV. As for his outward form and shape, he is described by the ancients in this way: He was a man of a handsome countenance and a grave, venerable appearance. His eyebrows were short and his eyes were cheerful and pleasant, expressing a sense of majesty without any bitterness or harshness. His speech was sweet and obliging. His clothing was simple <note>original: "mean"</note>, as became a man of a disciplined and self-denying life; his gait <note>original: "gate"</note> was composed, natural, grave, and decent. 

This elegant physical structure was but the lodging of a more noble tenant: a soul richly furnished with divine graces and virtues. He possessed profound humility, overflowing charity, firm faith, an immovable constancy, and an unconquerable patience. He had a mighty zeal and unwearied diligence in propagating Christianity and working for the good of souls. He so entirely devoted himself to a traveling life, constantly moving from place to place, that he could find little or no time to leave any writings behind for the benefit of the Church—at least none that have certainly reached us. Indeed, in ancient times, there were some—Tertullian in particular—who supposed him to be the author of the Epistle to the Hebrews, an opinion that is now generally rejected. There is also an epistle still in existence under his name, of great antiquity, which is frequently cited by Clement of Alexandria and his student Origen (to name a few). Origen even styles it the "Catholic Epistle of Barnabas," but it was placed by Eusebius among the "spurious" <note>original Greek: νόθα; meaning books of disputed or illegitimate apostolic origin</note> works...

# The Life of S. BARNABAS the Apostle.

...writings that were not considered genuine. The structure and composition of it are intricate and obscure, consisting of unusual allegories and forced, improbable interpretations of Scripture, though its main purpose is to show that the Christian religion has replaced the rites and customs of the **Mosaic Law**. The latter part of it contains a useful and excellent exhortation framed around the concept of **two ways**: one of **light** and the other of **darkness**. The first is under the guidance of the **Angels of God** (illuminating ministers <note>original Greek: φωταγωγοὶ ἄγγελοι</note>, as he calls them), while the other is under the direction of the **Angels of Satan**, the prince of the iniquity of this age. In describing the way of **light**, he encourages most of the specific duties and examples of the **Christian** and **spiritual** life, which are listed there with admirable **accuracy** and **conciseness**. In the way of **darkness**, he represents those specific sins and vices we are to avoid. I am confident that the **pious reader** will not think it a waste of time, nor regret the effort to read through such an ancient and useful discourse. He expresses himself in this way:

<margin>Epistle of Barnabas, page 248. Vossius Edition.</margin>

XV. THE **way of life** is this: **Whoever travels toward the appointed place will hasten by their works to reach it. The knowledge given to us on how to walk in this way is this: You shall love your Creator. You shall glorify him who redeemed you from death. You shall be simple in heart and, being rich in spirit, you shall not join yourself with those who walk in the way of death. You shall hate to do anything displeasing to God. You shall hate all kinds of hypocrisy. You shall not forsake the commandments of the Lord. Do not exalt yourself, but be of an humble mind. You shall not take glory for yourself, nor shall you take evil counsel against your neighbor. You shall not add boldness to your soul. You shall not commit fornication, nor be guilty of adultery or sexual perversion <note>original: "buggery"—a term historically used for various sexual acts then considered "unnatural" or criminal</note>.**

**You shall not neglect God’s command in correcting the impurity of others, nor shall you show favoritism when you rebuke someone for their faults. You shall be meek and silent, and stand in awe of the words you hear. You shall not hold a grudge against your brother. You shall not be of a double and unstable mind, doubting whether this or that is true.**

<margin>You shall love your neighbor above your own life <note>original Greek: Ἀγαπήσεις πλησίον σου ὑπὲρ τὴν ψυχήν σου</note>.</margin>

**You shall not take the name of the Lord in vain. You shall love your neighbor above your own life. You shall not destroy a child by abortion, nor kill it once it is born. You shall not withhold your hand from your son or daughter, but shall teach them the fear of the Lord from their youth. Do not desire your neighbor's goods, nor be covetous. Neither shall you join yourself wholeheartedly with the proud, but shall be numbered among the just and the humble. Accept trials and temptations when they happen to you as instruments for good. You shall not be double-minded or have a deceitful tongue, for a double tongue is the snare of death. You shall be subject to the Lord and to masters as God’s representatives, in reverence and fear. You shall not command your maid or manservant with bitterness and severity—especially those who hope in God—lest you yourself prove to be one who does not fear him who is over both. For he came not to call people based on outward appearance, but those whom his Spirit has prepared. You shall share all things with your neighbor and shall not call what you have your own. For if you mutually share in incorruptible things <note>spiritual or eternal matters</note>, how much more should you share in things that are corruptible <note>material or worldly goods</note>? Do not be rash with your tongue, for the mouth is a snare of death. Keep your soul as pure as you can; do not stretch out your hands to take while closing them when you should give. Love all those who speak the word of the Lord to you as the apple of your eye. Remember the day of judgment day and night. Seek out the company of holy people daily, and searching by the Word, go forth to**

exhort...

...exhort, and through it study how to save a soul. And with your hands you shall labor for the redemption of your sins. Do not delay in giving, nor begrudge <note>original: "begrutch"</note> when you are charitable. Give to everyone who asks you; and you shall know who is the good rewarder <note>original: "recompenser"</note> of the prize. You shall keep the things which you have received, neither adding to them, nor taking from them. You shall always hate a wicked person. Judge righteously. Make no schism <note>A formal split or division within a religious body.</note>. Make peace between those who are at odds, reconciling them to each other. Confess your sins, and do not come to prayer with an evil conscience. This is the way of light.

XVI. BUT now the *way of darkness is crooked* and full of curses. For it is the way of eternal death accompanied by punishment; in which there are things destructive to souls: *idolatry, audacity, height of domination <note>Arrogant exercise of power.</note>, hypocrisy, double-heartedness, adultery, murder, robbery <note>original: "rapine"</note>, pride, transgression, deceit, malice, arrogance, witchcraft, magic, greed <note>original: "covetousness"</note>, and a lack of the fear of God. Persecutors of good men, haters of the truth, men who love but do not know the wages of righteousness. People who do not cling to what is good, nor who, through righteous judgment, consider the plight of the widow and the orphan; watchful not for the fear of God, but for what is evil: total strangers to meekness and patience. Lovers of vanity, greedy for revenge, who have no compassion for the poor, nor strive to relieve the oppressed; prone to slander <note>original: "detraction"</note>, not knowing their Maker, murderers of children, defacers of God’s handiwork <note>Referring to the destruction of human life created in God's image, often interpreted in this context as abortion or infanticide.</note>, those who turn away from the needy, add affliction to the afflicted, plead for the rich, and unjustly judge the poor—sinners in every way.*

<margin>Acts 11:24.</margin>
And having thus described these two different ways, he concludes his discourse with a hearty and passionate exhortation: that since the time of rewards and punishments was drawing near, they should keep these things in mind as those who were taught by God, searching for what God required of them and dedicating themselves to the practice of it, so that they might be saved at the day of judgment. I have nothing more to say regarding this excellent person than to add the description given of him by an inspired author <note>Referring to St. Luke, the author of the Book of Acts.</note> who could not err: *“He was a good man, full of faith, and of the Holy Spirit.”*

## THE LIFE OF
# ST. TIMOTHY
### THE
## APOSTLE and EVANGELIST.

<header>The Life of ST. TIMOTHY.</header>

*The reasons why this did not contradict St. Paul’s doctrine concerning circumcision. His travels with St. Paul for the propagation of the Faith. His return from Thessalonica, and St. Paul’s two Epistles to that Church. St. Timothy consecrated Bishop of Ephesus. The consensus of antiquity on this matter. Ordination in those times was usually done by prophetic designation, and the reason for it. An inquiry into Timothy’s age. The importance of the Greek words "neos" <note>original: νέος; young</note> and "neotes" <note>original: νεότης; youth</note> in the phrase "let no man despise your youth"; these words are shown to be used by the best writers to describe a person of considerable age. St. Paul’s first and second Epistles to him, and their importance. The manners of the Ephesians noted. Their festival called the Katagogion <note>original Greek: Καταγώγιον; a festival involving the carrying of images through the streets</note>. St. Timothy’s martyrdom. The time of his death, the place of his burial, and the moving <term>original: "translation"</term> of his body. His weak and infirm constitution. His great abstinence and admirable zeal. St. Paul’s singular affection for him. How he differs from the Timotheus mentioned by St. Dionysius the Areopagite. Another Timothy, a disciple of St. Paul, martyred under Emperor Antoninus.*

---

I. <image-desc>A large decorative initial 'S' set within a square frame, embellished with intricate floral and foliate scrollwork.</image-desc>ST. TIMOTHY was, as we may probably assume, a *Lycaonian*, born at *Lystra*, a noted city of that province. He was a person in whom the *Jew*, the *Gentile*, and the *Christian* all met together. His father was by birth a *Greek* and by religion a *Gentile*—or if he was a convert, he was at most only a "sojourning proselyte" <note>original Hebrew: גר תושב, *Ger Toshav*</note> or *Proselyte of the Gate*. These were converts who did not obligate themselves to circumcision and the rites of *Moses*, but only to the observance of the seven laws of the sons of *Noah*. His mother, *Eunice*, daughter to the devout and pious *Lois*, was a *Jewess*, who nevertheless did not hesitate to marry this *Greek*.

<margin>"Do you see how the Law was gradually set aside when such intermarriages began to occur?" <note>original Greek: Ὁρᾶς πῶς ἤρετο ὁ νόμος καταλυθείς, τῆς ἐπιμίξεων οὕτως γινομένων. Chrysostom, Homily 1 on 2 Timothy, p. 1627.</note></margin>
This is an argument that the "partition wall" was now tottering and ready to fall, seeing that *Jew* and *Gentile* began to marry in this way. His mother and grandmother were women of eminent virtue and holiness, and they seem to have been among the first converted to the Christian faith. It was no small instance of their piety that they took great care in his education, instructing him in the knowledge of divine things and seasoning his tender years with virtuous and sober principles,

<margin>2 Timothy 3:15.</margin>
so that *from a child he was acquainted with the Holy Scriptures*. By this, he was admirably prepared to receive Christianity and equipped to lead a strictly pious life.

<margin>"The source and root of goodness is to receive a proper education." <note>original Greek: Πηγὴ καὶ ῥίζα καλοκαγαθίας, τὸ νομίμως τυχεῖν παιδείας. Plutarch, On the Education of Children, p. 4.</note></margin>
And indeed, religion never thrives more naturally than when it is planted early and its foundations are laid in youthful piety. For the mind being then soft and tender, it easily receives the best impressions. These degrees of influence gradually work their way into the soul and imperceptibly reconcile it to the difficulties of a holy life. Thus, what must necessarily feel harsh and severe to a man who tries to rescue himself from a lifelong habit of sin, the youth is unacquainted with; he proceeds smoothly in a way that has become pleasant and delightful. None start with greater advantages, nor usually persevere with more vigorous constancy, than those who *remember their Creator in the days of their youth*. They sacrifice the first fruits of their time to God and to religion before corrupt affections have placed a bias upon their inclinations, and before a series of vices has depraved and largely put to sleep the natural instincts of good and evil.

II. PREPARED by such excellent cultivation in the *Jewish* religion, God was pleased to transplant him into a better soil. *St. Paul*, in pursuing his commission to preach the Gospel to the *Gentiles*, had come as far as *Antioch*...

<summary>This page discusses the heritage and early upbringing of Saint Timothy, highlighting his mixed Jewish and Greek parentage and the importance of his early education in the Scriptures by his mother and grandmother.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Timothy, Lystra, Eunice, Lois, Proselyte of the Gate, religious education, intermarriage, childhood piety, St. Paul</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the biography of St. Timothy, detailing his recruitment by St. Paul at Lystra, the strategic reasons for his circumcision, and their subsequent missionary travels through Macedonia and Greece.</meta>

<page-num>47</page-num>
<header>The Life of ST. TIMOTHY.</header>

*Antioch* in *Pisidia*, then to *Iconium*, and so to *Lystra*, where the miraculous cure of a paralyzed cripple made way for the acceptance of the Christian doctrine.
<margin>a St. Simeon Metaphrastes, Life of St. Timothy, in Laurentius Surius, Jan. 24, section 11, page 411.</margin>
Among others converted there, we are $^a$ told, were *St. Timothy’s* parents, who courteously treated and entertained the Apostle at their house, wholly resigning their son to his care and guidance. About two years later, during his review of these recent church plantings, Paul came again to *Lystra*, 
<margin>Acts 16:1, 2, 3.</margin>
where he chose *Timothy*—who was recommended to him by the universal testimony of the Christians in that area—to be an *Evangelist* <note>original: "Evangelist"; here meaning a traveling missionary assistant to the Apostles</note>, serving as his assistant and traveling companion. Paul wanted someone always with him whom he could entrust with important matters and dispatch for any extraordinary business or urgent need of the Church. 

Indeed, *Timothy* was not circumcised, for since this was a part of paternal authority, it did not lie within his mother's power to perform. This was well known to all the *Jews*, and *St. Paul* knew this would be a significant prejudice against Timothy’s ministry wherever he went. Because the *Jews* were extremely zealous for circumcision, they would not patiently endure any man preaching to them—or even speaking with them—who was himself uncircumcised. To remove that obstacle, Paul had him circumcised, becoming in lawful matters *all things to all men, that he might gain the more*. 

<margin>b Homily 34 on the Acts of the Apostles, page 684.</margin>
*St. Chrysostom* says $^b$ the wisdom and prudence of *St. Paul* was admirable in this design: "He circumcised him, so that he might take away circumcision" <note>original Greek: Περιέτεμεν, ἵνα περιτομὴν καθέλῃ</note>. By this, he meant that Timothy would be more acceptable to the *Jews* and, by that means, better able to correct their mistaken opinion regarding the necessity of those legal rites. At other times, we find Paul sharply arguing against circumcision when it was treated as a justification for following *Mosaic* institutions, which he saw as effectively undermining the great goals of Christianity. 

In this instance, he did not contradict his own doctrine or practice an unauthorized conformity with the *Jews*. Rather, it was
<margin>c Stromata (Miscellanies), book 7, page 730.</margin>
only (as $^c$ *Clement* of *Alexandria* observes regarding this passage) a prudent concession to the current mood of the *Jews*. Paul was unwilling to offend them or cause them to completely reject the faith by too suddenly and violently tearing them away from the circumcision of the flesh, as he hoped to bring them over to the "circumcision of the heart." Therefore, someone who accommodates himself for the salvation of another cannot be charged with dissimulation or hypocrisy. He does this purely for the benefit of others—something he would not do for any other reason or because of the rites themselves. This is "the part of a wise and kind instructor, who is a true lover of God and the souls of men" <note>original Greek: τοῦ φιλανθρώπου & φιλοθέου παιδαγωγοῦ</note>.

III. Once *ST. PAUL* was equipped with such a suitable companion, they set forward in their evangelical progress. Having passed through *Phrygia* and *Galatia*, they came down to *Troas*. From there they set sail for *Samothrace*, then to *Neapolis*, and then to *Philippi*, the capital of that part of *Macedonia*. After being mistreated by the magistrates and people there, they departed for *Thessalonica*, where the fury and malice of the *Jews* forced them to flee to *Berea*. Here they met people of a more generous and noble character who were ready to embrace the Christian doctrine, but only after they had first compared it with the predictions the Prophets had made concerning the *Messiah*. Yet even here they could not escape the relentless spirit of the *Jews*, so the Christians were forced to privately escort *St. Paul* to *Athens*, while *Silas* and *Timothy* 

<meta>The text cuts off mid-sentence; the catchword "not" suggests the next page begins with a phrase like "not long after" or "stayed behind."</meta>

<margin>
1 Thessalonians 3:1, 2, 3.

</margin>
...who were not so much the immediate objects of their spite and cruelty, stayed behind to instruct and confirm the converts of that place. Whether they came to him during his stay at *Athens* is uncertain; *St. Luke* takes no further notice of them until they came to him at *Corinth*, his next stop. Upon their arrival there (if it was not earlier at *Athens*), *St. Paul* dispatched *Timothy* to *Thessalonica* to inquire into the state of Christianity in that city and to confirm them in the belief and profession of the Gospel. Paul seems to have had a particular kindness for that church; since his last visit there, he had resolved more than once to return to them, but the great Enemy of Souls <note>meaning Satan</note> had repeatedly thrown some hindrance <note>original: "rub"</note> in his way.

<margin>
1 Thessalonians 3:6, 7, and following.
</margin>
IV. FROM *Thessalonica*, *Timothy* returned with the welcome news of their firmness and constancy despite the persecutions they endured, their mutual charity to each other, and their particular affection for *St. Paul*. This news pleased the good man infinitely, as certainly nothing can provide greater joy and satisfaction to a faithful guide of souls than to behold the welfare and prosperity of his people. Nor did his care for them end here; he immediately wrote his first Epistle to them to encourage them in their sufferings. He urged them not to desert the Christian religion just because the Cross <note>symbolizing suffering and persecution</note> accompanied it, but rather to adorn their Christian profession by living a life consistent with its holy designs and precepts. In the opening of this Epistle, he included not only his own name but also those of *Silas* and *Timothy*, partly to give greater honor to his fellow-workers, and partly so that their united authority and agreement might have a stronger influence and force upon the readers. He did the same in a *second* Epistle, which he sent to them not long after to make up for his lack of personal presence—something he had given them hope for in his previous letter and which he himself seemed to desire so passionately. They remained at *Corinth* for at least eighteen months when *St. Paul* resolved to travel to *Jerusalem*. He did not stay there long but went to *Antioch*. After traveling through the regions of *Galatia* and *Phrygia* to establish the Christianity recently planted in those parts, he came to *Ephesus*. Although he met with great opposition there, he preached with even greater success. He became so completely occupied with the concerns of that city that, although he had intended to go into *Macedonia* himself, he was forced to send *Timothy* and *Erastus* in his place. Having completed their mission, they returned to *Ephesus* to assist him in promoting the affairs of religion in that city.

<margin>
1 Timothy 1:3.
<sup>a</sup> Eusebius, *Ecclesiastical History*, book 3, chapter 4, page 73.
<sup>b</sup> *Martyrdom of Timothy* in Photius, Code 254, column 1401.
<sup>c</sup> Council of Chalcedon, Act 11, Council Tome 4, column 609.
<sup>d</sup> *Apostolical Constitutions*, book 7, chapter 47, column 451.
</margin>
V. AFTER ST. PAUL had resided at *Ephesus* and the surrounding areas for three years, he determined to take his leave and depart for *Macedonia*. It was at this time (as he himself plainly suggests, and the ancient writers generally believe) that he appointed *Timothy* as Bishop and Governor of that church. He was the *first Bishop* (says <sup>a</sup> *Eusebius*) of the province or diocese of *Ephesus*. He was the "first to act as Bishop of Ephesus" <note>original Greek: πρῶτος Ἐφέσου Ἐπισκοπῆσαι</note>, says the <sup>b</sup> author in *Photius*. Furthermore, in the Council of <sup>c</sup> *Chalcedon*, twenty-seven Bishops are said to have sat successively in that chair, of whom *St. Timothy* was the first. In the <sup>d</sup> *Apostolical Constitutions*, he is explicitly said to have been ordained Bishop of Ephesus by *St. Paul*. As the author in *Photius* expresses it—using the terminology of his own time—*he was ordained and enthroned* (*or installed*) *Bishop of the Metropolis of the Ephesians by the great St. Paul*. *Ephesus* was a great and populous city, and the civil government of the *Proconsul* who resided there extended over the whole *Lydian* or *Pro-consular* [province]...

In a similar way, the ancient writers describe the ecclesiastical jurisdiction <note>original: "Ecclesiastical Jurisdiction"</note> of that church. ᵃ *St. Chrysostom* states that it is plain and evident that *Timothy* had the *Church*, or rather the *whole nation of Asia*, entrusted to him. To him (says ᵇ *Theodoret*), the divine *St. Paul* committed <margin>the care and the charge of Asia <note>original: τῆς Ἀσίας ἐπιμέλειαν</note></margin> the care and the charge of *Asia*; because of this, a little later ᶜ he calls him the *Apostle of the Asians*. Regarding the manner of his ordination—or rather his appointment <note>original: "designation"</note> to the ministries of religion—it was by a specific and extraordinary appointment. God immediately testified that it was his will and pleasure. Therefore, it is said to have been done <margin>according to some preceding predictions concerning him <note>original: κατὰ τὰς προαγούσας προφητείας</note></margin> according to some *preceding predictions concerning him*, and he received it not only by the *laying on of hands, but by prophecy*. That is, as ᵈ *Chrysostom* truly explains, it was by the Holy Spirit <note>original: "Holy Ghost"</note>. It was part of the prophetic office (he adds, and especially at that time) not only to foretell future events but to declare things present, with God extraordinarily revealing whom he would have set apart for that important <note>original: "weighty"</note> office. Thus, *Paul* and *Barnabas* were separated by the special command of the Holy Spirit. Regarding the governors of the *Ephesian* <note>original: "Ephesine"</note> churches who met at *Miletus*, it is said that the Holy Spirit had made them bishops, or overseers of the *Church*. This method of election by prophetic revelation continued in use at least during the Apostolic Age. ᵉ *Clement* <note>Clement of Rome</note>, in his Epistle to the *Corinthians*, tells us that as the Apostles preached throughout various cities and countries, they appointed their first converts <note>original: "first-fruits"</note> to be the bishops and deacons of those who would believe, <margin>testing them by the spirit <note>original: δοκιμάσαντες τῷ πνεύματι</note></margin>, testing them by the Spirit. And another ᶠ *Clement* <note>Clement of Alexandria</note> reports that *St. John*, while visiting the neighboring churches around *Ephesus*, ordained bishops and those who were *indicated* <note>original: "signified"</note> or pointed out to him by the Spirit.

1 Timothy 4:12.

ʰ Attic Nights, Book 15, Chapter 28, page 383.

VI. **THIS** extraordinary and miraculous way of choosing bishops and ecclesiastical officers, besides other advantages, created <note>original: "begat"</note> a great reverence and respect <note>original: "veneration"</note> for the governors of the church. They were looked upon as God’s choice, bearing the immediate mark <note>original: "character"</note> of Heaven upon them. This method seemed especially necessary for *St. Timothy* more than others, to protect him from the contempt that his youth might otherwise have invited. That he was quite young at that time is evident from *St. Paul’s* counsel to him to conduct <note>original: "demean"</note> himself so that *no man might despise his youth*. The governors of the church in those days were <margin>Elders <note>original: Πρεσβύτεροι</note></margin>, in terms of their age as well as their office. Indeed, they were called *Elders* because those admitted into the orders of the church were usually persons of a significant age. *Timothy* had not yet reached this age. And yet the word <margin>youth <note>original: νεότης</note></margin>, *youth*, allows for a broader meaning than we usually give it in ordinary speech. ᵍ *Cicero* tells us of himself that he was <term>adolescentulus</term> <note>original Latin: a very young man or youth</note>, a mere youth, when he pleaded *Roscius*'s cause; yet ʰ *Aulus Gellius* proves him to have been no less than 27 years old at that time. *Alexander*, the son of *Aristobulus*, is called ⁱ <margin>a youth <note>original: νεανίσκος</note></margin>, *a youth*, at the time of his death, even though he was over thirty. *Hiero* in ᵏ *Polybius* is styled <margin>a very young man <note>original: κομιδῆ νέος</note></margin>, *a very young man*, whom ᵏ *Casaubon* proves to have been 35 years of age. The same historian, speaking of *Titus Flamininus* <note>original: "T. Flaminius"</note> making war upon *Philip* of *Macedon*, says he was <margin>a very young man <note>original: νέος κομιδῆ</note></margin>, *a very young man*, for he was not over thirty years old. As *Casaubon* observes, it was the custom of both *Greek* and *Latin* writers to extend the *juventus*, or youthful age, from the thirtieth until the fortieth year of a man's life.

<header>The Life of St. TIMOTHY.</header>

<margin>Annotations on the passage. <note>original: "Annot. in loc."</note></margin>life. To which we may add what *Grotius* <note>Hugo Grotius (1583–1645), a famous Dutch legal scholar and theologian.</note> observes: that the Greek word for youth <note>original: "νεότης"</note>, answering to the Hebrew word for a young man <note>original: "בחור" (Bachur)</note>, denotes the military age—that is, all the active and mature part of a man’s life that is opposed to old age. So, *Timothy’s* youth, without any force or violence to the word, might very well be consistent with his being at least thirty or thirty-five years of age. He was styled "young" only comparatively, in respect to that important role <note>original: "weighty Function"</note> which was usually conferred upon none but grave and elderly men. But enough of this.

<margin>Acts 20:2, 3, etc.</margin>VII. St. *TIMOTHY*, having been thus settled at *Ephesus*, did nonetheless accompany St. *Paul* for some part of his journey into *Greece*. At the very least, he went to him there regarding some urgent affairs of the Church and then returned to his charge. Not long after this, St. *Paul* wrote his *First Epistle* to him to encourage him in his duty and to direct him how to behave himself in that eminent position in which he had placed him. Because the success of the ministry depends in large measure upon the people employed in it, Paul gives him more particular rules on how to proceed in this matter, and how those people ought to be qualified whom he admitted to that honorable and important office. As *Nicephorus* <note>Nicephorus Callistus Xanthopoulos, a 14th-century church historian.</note> speaks, <margin>* Ecclesiastical History, book 2, chapter 34, page 189.</margin>"vividly detailing, as in a model, the life and teaching of a church leader" <note>original: "ὡς ἐν τύπῳ ⛬ ἱεραρχικὸν βίον & λόγον ἀναζωγύρως διεξιὼν"</note>. In that Epistle, he excellently represents, as if in a brief sketch, the life and conduct of the sacred governors of the Church, describing the tempers and manners of those who are appointed to be the guides and ministers of religion. He also knew well that crafty teachers and false apostles were creeping into the Church; he remarks upon their principles and practices, warning Timothy to beware of them and to stand continually on his guard against them. The holy man followed his instructions and was, no doubt, faithful to his trust, which he managed with all care and diligence. About six years later, St. *Paul*, being then a prisoner at *Rome*, wrote a *Second Epistle* to him <margin>Apostolic Antiquities, Life of St. Paul, Section 7, number 5; 2 Timothy 4:9.</margin>(for we have shown elsewhere that this Epistle was written during his first arrival in *Rome*) to excite him to great care and fidelity in his business, and in undermining the false and subtle insinuations of deceivers. In it, he orders *Timothy* to come to him with all speed to *Rome*. Timothy accordingly came and joined with Paul in several Epistles written from there to the *Philippians*, *Colossians*, and to *Philemon*, as his name at the beginning of those Epistles clearly declares. During his stay at *Rome*, he was for some reason cast into prison, and then released <margin>Hebrews 13:23, 24.</margin>and set at liberty about the time of St. *Paul’s* enlargement <note>original: "enlargement"; meaning release from confinement.</note>, as Paul clearly suggests at the close of his Epistle to the *Hebrews*. After this, he came back to *Ephesus*, nor is it probable that he ever moved from there again until his translation into Heaven. And here it was that he became <margin>In the Bollandist collection, January 24. <note>original: "Ap. Bolland. Januar. XXIV"</note></margin>acquainted with St. *John*, whose apostolic province mainly lay in *Asia* and the parts around *Ephesus*. The *Acts* attributed to *Polycrates* <note>Polycrates of Ephesus (c. 130–196), an early bishop.</note>—which are undoubtedly of great antiquity, being those mentioned and used by *Photius* <note>Patriarch Photius of Constantinople (9th century).</note>—report that he conversed with and was a student of St. *John the Divine*, who leaned against the chest of our Lord.

<margin>Strabo, *Geography*, book 14.</margin>VIII. THE *Ephesians* were a people of great immorality and godlessness; their manners were indulgent and self-indulgent, profane and wasteful. They banished *Hermodorus* only because he was more sober and frugal than the rest, enacting a decree: *Let none of ours be frugal.* They were strangely bewitched with the study of *magic* and the arts of sorcery and divination, and were miserably overrun with idolatry, especially the...

# The Life of St. Timothy.

Temple and worship of *Diana*, for which they were famous throughout the whole world. Among their many idolatrous festivals, they had one called *Katagogion* <note>original: "ΚΑΤΑΓΩΓΙΟΝ"; a festival involving the "bringing down" of images or a homecoming celebration.</note>, which was celebrated in this manner: dressing themselves in grotesque <note>original: "antic"</note> costumes and covering their faces with ugly masks <note>original: "Vizors"</note> so that they might not be recognized, they carried clubs in their hands and moved idols in a wild and frantic manner through the most prominent places of the city, singing certain songs and verses to them. Without any compassion or respect for age or sex, they set upon all persons they met and beat out their brains, glorying in it as a brave achievement and a great honor to their gods.

<margin>
a. On the Martyrdom: *Timothy the Apostle according to Photius, Code 254, columns 1401, 1404. Commentary on St. Timothy by Symeon Metaphrastes in Surius, January 24, numbers 9, 10. Fragments of the life of St. Timothy in Greek by P. Halloix in the Life of Polycarp, page 558. Perhaps from the Acts of St. Timothy written by Polycrates (as they say), which contain the same accounts, in the Bollandists, January 24, page 566.*
</margin>

This cursed and detestable custom gave just offense to all pious and good men, especially *St. Timothy*, whose spirit was grieved to see God so openly dishonored. He saw human nature sunk into such a deep degeneracy and so arbitrarily driven to the most savage barbarities by the great murderer of souls <note>A reference to the Devil.</note>. The good man often endeavored to reclaim them by gentle and mild entreaties; but alas, gentle medicine <note>original: "Physic"</note> works little upon a stubborn constitution. When that would not do, he came out to them in the middle of the street during one of these fatal ceremonies and reproved them with necessary sharpness and severity. 

But cruelty and licentiousness are too headstrong to tolerate opposition. Impatient at being controlled in their wild extravagances, they fell upon him with their clubs, beat and dragged him up and down, and then left him for dead. Some Christians, finding him still breathing, took him up and lodged him outside the gate of the city, where he expired three days later. He suffered martyrdom on the 30th day of the fourth month according to the *Asian* computation, or on the 22nd of *January* by the *Roman* account (the date the *Greek* Church celebrates his memory), or the 24th according to the *Latin* Church. This happened (as some believe) in the time of the Emperor *Nerva*, while others more probably refer it to the reign of *Domitian*, as it occurred before *St. John’s* return from his banishment on *Patmos*, which was about the beginning of *Nerva's* reign. 

After he died, the Christians of *Ephesus* took his body and decently buried it in a place called *Pion*—or *Piron* according to *Isidore*, who adds that it was a mountain. There it rested securely for several ages until *Constantine* the Great (or as others say, his son *Constantius*) caused it to be translated <note>The formal ritual of moving a saint's relics from one location to another.</note> to *Constantinople*. It was laid to rest together with the remains of *St. Andrew* and *St. Luke* in the great Church of the Holy Apostles, erected by *Constantine*.

<margin>
b. *Isidore of Seville, On the Life and Death of the Saints, chapter 86, page 542. Jerome against Vigilantius, page 122, Volume 2. Nicephorus, Ecclesiastical History, book 2, chapter 43, page 210. Metaphrastes, as cited above, number 10.*
</margin>

IX. He was a man of a weak and sickly constitution, frequently assaulted by diseases in addition to the constant infirmities that burdened him. *St. Chrysostom* <note>John Chrysostom (c. 347–407), an influential Archbishop of Constantinople known for his preaching.</note> believes these were largely due to his extraordinary temperance and too frequent fastings. This was an effective way to subdue those *youthful lusts* which *St. Paul* cautioned him to avoid, there being no better way to extinguish a fire than to withdraw the fuel. He allowed himself no delicious meats or fine wines; bread and water was his usual bill of fare, until by excessive abstinence and the meanness and coarseness of his diet, he had weakened his appetite and rendered his stomach unfit to serve the

<margin>
"What could be equal to the virtue of Timothy? He looked down on luxury and chose not the feasts of a costly table, but intense fasting. For he was not like this by nature, but by fasting and water-drinking he broke the strength of his stomach." <note>original Greek: "Τί δ' ἂν ἀρετῆς ἴσον τῷ Τιμοθέῳ; ὅπως ὑπεριδὼν τρυφῆς, κ' ἐκλελεγμένα πολυτελοῦς τραπέζης εὐωχίας, καὶ ἐπιτεταμένης νηστείας. οὐ γὰρ ἐκ φύσει τοιοῦτος ἦν, ἀλλ' ὑπὸ νηστείας καὶ ὑδροποσίας τὸ τοῦ στομάχου κατέβαλε τ' ἰσχύν."</note>
*Chrysostom, Homily 1 to the People of Antioch, Volume 1, page 5.*
</margin>

<meta>This page continues the description of Timothy’s character, focusing on how his physical frailty was overcome by his spiritual vigor, and the deep, paternal affection Saint Paul held for him.</meta>

# The Life of St. Timothy.

<margin>1 Timothy 5:23.</margin>
...ends of nature. So much so that *St. Paul* was forced to impose it as a kind of law upon him, that he *should no longer drink only water, but use a little wine for the sake of his stomach and his frequent illnesses.* And yet, in the midst of this weak, tottering body <note>original: "carcase"</note>, there lived a vigorous and lively mind, a soul driven by a mighty zeal and inspired with a true love for God. He thought no difficulties too great and no dangers too formidable to be serviceable to the purposes of religion and the interests of souls. He flew from place to place with a quicker speed and a more unwearied resolution than could have been expected from a stronger and healthier person—now to *Ephesus*, then to *Corinth*, often into *Macedonia*, and then to *Italy*—crossing sea and land and surmounting a thousand
<margin>a. In the place cited <note>original: "Loc. citat."</note>, page 7.</margin>
hazards and oppositions. In all of this (as *Chrysostom’s* words are), the weakness of his body did not hinder the divine wisdom <note>original: "Philosophy"</note> of his mind; so strangely active and powerful is zeal for God, and so nimbly does it wing the soul with the swiftest flight. And certainly (as he adds), just as a great and robust body is little better for its health if it has nothing but a dull and heavy soul to inform it, so bodily weakness is no great impediment where there is a quick and generous mind to animate and enliven it.

<margin>
1 Thessalonians 3:2.
2 Timothy 1:2.
Philippians 2:19, 20, etc.
</margin>
X. These excellent virtues infinitely endeared him to *St. Paul*, who seems to have had a very passionate kindness for him, never mentioning him without great tenderness and titles of reverence and respect. Sometimes he styles him his *son*, his *brother*, or his *fellow-laborer*: *Timothy our brother, and Minister of God, and our fellow-laborer in the Gospel of Christ*. Sometimes he adds expressions of particular affection and honorable regard: *Timothy, my dearly beloved son*; and *Timothy, who is my beloved son, and faithful in the Lord*. To the Church at *Philippi* he wrote even more expressly: *I trust to send Timothy shortly to you, for I have no man like-minded* (<term>isopsychon</term> <note>original: "ἰσόψυχον"; meaning "of equal soul" or "of the same mind"</note>, equally dear to me as myself) *who will naturally care for your state. For all seek their own, not the things that are Jesus Christ's; but you know the proof of him, that as a son with the father, he has served with me in the Gospel.* And because he knew that Timothy was a young man and of a temperament
<margin>1 Corinthians 16:10, 11.</margin>
easily affected by harsh and unkind impressions, Paul entered a particular caution on his behalf with the Church of *Corinth*: *If Timothy comes, see that he may be with you without fear, for he works the work of the Lord, as I also do. Let no man therefore despise him, but conduct him forth in peace, that he may come unto me.* These are instances of great care and tenderness, which plainly suggest that *Timothy* was an extraordinary person.
<margin>b. Homily 1 on 2 Timothy, page 1626.</margin>
*Chrysostom* thinks that Paul merely calling him his "dearly beloved son" is a sufficient argument for Timothy’s virtue. For such affection, not being based on biological nature, can flow from nothing but virtue and goodness—the lovely and essential ornaments of a divine and holy soul. We love our children not only because they are witty, handsome, kind, or dutiful, but because they are ours, and very often for no other reason; nor can we do otherwise as long as we are subject to the impressions and laws of nature. However, true goodness and virtue have no other arts but their own naked worth and beauty to recommend them, nor can they challenge regard and veneration from us by any other argument.

XI. Some dispute has arisen among the writers of the Church of *Rome* as to whether our *St. Timothy* was the same person to whom *Dionysius the Areopagite* dedicates the books said to be written by him; and troops of arguments are mustered on either side. But the foundation...

<meta>This page concludes the biography of St. Timothy by addressing the spurious writings attributed to Dionysius the Areopagite and distinguishing between the Timothy of Ephesus and another St. Timothy associated with the early Christian conversion of Britain.</meta>

<header>The Life of ST. TIMOTHY.</header>

<margin>
a *Pietro de' Natalibus, History of the Saints, book 1, chapter 24. Nauclerus, Chronicle, vol. 2, generation 6. Compare Ado’s Martyrology at June 20 <note>original: "ad XII. Kal. Jul."</note>. See Ussher, On the Beginnings of the British Churches, chapter 3, page 31.*

...the foundation of the controversy is completely removed for us, as we are sufficiently assured that those books were written several hundred years after **St. Denis’s** <note>original: "S. Denys"</note> head was laid in the dust. However, it may not be out of place to mention that, besides our Bishop of **Ephesus**, we are <sup>a</sup> told of another **St. Timothy**, also a disciple of **St. Paul** and the son of **Pudens** and **Priscilla**. He is said to have lived to a great age, until the times of the Emperor **Antoninus** and **Pius**, Bishop of Rome, and it is said that he came over into **Britain** and converted and baptized **Lucius**, King of this island—the first king who ever embraced the Christian faith. **Pius**, Bishop of Rome, in a <sup>b</sup> letter to **Justus**, Bishop of **Vienne** <note>original: "Vienna"</note> (which, though suspected as a forgery by most, is yet accepted as genuine by <sup>c</sup> **Baronius**), includes him among the elders <note>original: "Presbyters"</note> who had been educated by the Apostles and had come to Rome, and tells us that he had suffered martyrdom. Accordingly, the <sup>d</sup> ***Roman Martyrology*** informs us that he obtained the crown of martyrdom under the Emperor **Antoninus**. This is a story which, as I cannot disprove it, so I am not overly eager to believe; nor is it important enough for my purpose to investigate it more particularly.

# THE LIFE OF ST. TITUS.

*His accompanying St. Paul in his travels. Their arrival in Crete. Titus appointed by him as Bishop of that island. The testimonies of the ancient writers to that effect. The hints of it in St. Paul’s Epistle to him. St. Paul’s criticism of the people of Crete, justified by the accounts that non-Christian <note>original: "Gentile"</note> writers give of their bad behavior. A short overview of the Epistle itself. The directions concerning church officials <note>original: "Ecclesiastick persons"</note>. His duty to encourage and convince those who argue against him. Crete’s abundance of heretical teachers. What Jewish fables and genealogies were, and where they came from. The Aeons <note>original: "Æones"</note> and pairings <note>original: "συζυγίαι"</note> of the ancient Gnostics borrowed from the "Generations of the Gods" <note>original: "Θεογονίαι"</note> of the pagan <note>original: "Heathen"</note> poets. This is shown by specific examples. Titus commanded to meet St. Paul at Nicopolis. His arrival to meet him in Macedonia. His following St. Paul to Rome and his departure for Dalmatia. The story of Pliny the Younger being converted by him in Crete, criticized. His age and death. The church built in his memory.*

I. <image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'T' within a square frame featuring floral and leaf patterns.</image-desc><margin>\* Homily 1 on Titus, page 1693.</margin> The ancient writers of the church make little mention of this holy man; who he was and where he came from is not known, except by uncertain probabilities. <margin>Acts 18:7.</margin> St. Chrysostom conjectures <note>original: "conjectures": guesses based on incomplete information</note> him to have been born at Corinth, for no other reason than because in some ancient copies (as is still found in several manuscripts today) mention is made of St. Paul going into the house of one [Titus] <margin>Florus, Roman History, book 3, chapter 7, page 65.</margin> named Justus, a worshiper of God, while at Corinth. Writers of later ages generally claim he was born in Crete—better known by the modern name of Candia. It is a noble island (as the historian <note>referring to Lucius Annaeus Florus</note> calls it, adding that the only reason the Romans went to war there was their desire to conquer such a fine country) in the Aegean Sea. In ancient times, it was no more famous for being the birthplace of Jupiter (the sovereign of the pagan gods) and for the Labyrinth of Daedalus <note>original: "Dædalean Labyrinth"</note> said to be located there, than it has been in recent times for being the site of long wars between the Ottoman <note>original: "Turkish"</note> Emperor and the Republic <note>original: "State"</note> of Venice. History <note>original: "Antiquity"</note> has not certainly passed down to us any specific details about his parents. However, if we believe the account given by some, he was of no common origin <note>original: "extract"</note>, but <margin>"The blessed Titus, descending from Minos [the text notes that 'Myron' is likely a scribal error for 'Minos'], the King of Crete, as stated by Zonas the lawyer, who wrote his life, and whom the holy Apostle Paul mentions." <note>original Greek: "Τίτος ὁ μακάριος ἐκ Μυρῶν... Menæon Græc. Αὐγούστ. τῆ κε'." from the Greek Menæon (a liturgical book) for August 25th</note></margin> of royal blood. His lineage was said to be derived from none other than Minos, King of Crete. The poets claim Minos was the son of Jupiter and, because of the fairness of his laws and the impartial justice of his government, they promoted him to be one of the three great judges in the underworld <note>original: "infernal Regions"</note>, whose role is to determine the future and eternal state of souls. Historians, however, more accurately state that he was the son of Xanthus, King of that island, and that he succeeded his father on the throne. But I will move past that.

II. But whatever his parentage was, we are sure that he was a <margin>as cited above</margin> Greek, probably both by nationality and religion. In their <margin>"Titus, you must depart from here and save your soul, for this education will not benefit you." <note>original Greek: "Τίτε, δεῖ σε ἐντεῦθεν ἐκδημῆσαι..." from the Menæon</note></margin> public services <note>original: "Offices"</note>, the Greek Church gives this account of his younger years and his conversion to Christianity: that being born to noble parents, his youth was dedicated to learning and a high-quality <note>original: "generous"</note> education. At twenty years old, he heard a voice telling him that he must leave his home so that he might save his soul, for all his other learning would be of little use to him. Not satisfied with just this warning, he desired...

# The Life of ST. TITUS.

---

He was inspired again to hear the voice. A year later, he was again commanded in a vision to study the volume of the Jewish Law. He opened the book and cast his eye upon the words of the prophet:
<margin>Isaiah 41:1</margin>
> *Keep silence before me, O Islands, and let the people renew their strength: let them come near, let them speak: let us come near together to judgment, etc.*

Whereupon his uncle, who was at that time the Proconsul <note>A provincial governor in the Roman Republic and Empire.</note> of Crete, having heard the fame of our Lord’s miracles in Judea, sent him to Jerusalem. He stayed there until Christ’s ascension, when he was converted by that famous sermon of St. Peter, through which he gained three thousand souls at once. I cannot guarantee the truth of this story, though it is claimed to be derived from the *Acts* said to have been written by Zenas the Lawyer (mentioned by St. Paul). I confess that without better evidence, I dare not encourage the reader to place too much trust in it. Let us, therefore, move on to something more certain and unquestionable.

III. Having arrived in Judea or the surrounding areas, and being convinced of the truth and divinity of the Christian faith, he became St. Paul’s convert and disciple, though we do not find when or where he was converted. It is likely either that he followed St. Paul as a companion and assistant, or that he joined the Church of Antioch. It was there that the famous controversy arose concerning circumcision and the Mosaic laws <note>The religious laws and rituals established by Moses.</note>, and whether they were as necessary to observe as the belief and practice of Christianity.
<margin>Acts 15:1, 2, etc.</margin>
The church determined that *Paul and Barnabas, and certain others of them, should go up to Jerusalem to the Apostles and Elders about this question.*
<margin>a Codex Bezae MS on Acts 15:2. <note>original: "Cod. Bezæ MS. ad Act. 15. 2."</note></margin>
In fact, a very ancient manuscript <gloss>a</gloss> adds that when Paul earnestly persuaded them to remain in the doctrine they had been taught, those very Jewish zealots who had come down to Antioch and first caused the doubt were the ones who requested that Paul, Barnabas, and some others go and consult with the Apostles and Elders at Jerusalem and abide by their judgment and determination of the case. Among those sent on this Gospel embassy was our St. Titus,
<margin>Galatians 1:2, etc.</margin>
whom St. Paul (encouraged to take this journey by a particular revelation) was willing to take along with him. No sooner had they come to Jerusalem than spies were at hand. Some zealous Jews, pretending to be Christian converts, worked their way into St. Paul’s company and acquaintance, narrowly observing what liberties he took regarding legal rituals so they might find an accusation against him. They charged him with preaching to and associating with the Gentiles <note>Non-Jewish people.</note>, noting that at this very time, Titus—an uncircumcised Greek—was his close companion. They claimed this was a scandal that could only be avoided by circumcising him, so that it might appear Paul had no intention of undermining the rites and customs of the Law. St. Paul (who knew when to give ground and when to maintain his position) would by no means consent to this. Although at another time he was willing to circumcise Timothy (a Jew on his mother's side) to please the Jews for their own education and to have a better chance to win them over, he refused here to circumcise Titus, a Gentile. He did this so he would not seem to betray the freedoms of the Gospel, harden the Jews in their unreasonable and deep-seated <note>original: "inveterate"</note> prejudices against the non-Christians <note>original: "Heathens"</note>, or give the Gentiles a just reason to feel offended or discouraged and drive them further away from Christianity. Accordingly, he resisted their demands with unbreakable resolution, and his practice in this matter was immediately justified by the formal decision <note>original: "decretory Sentence"</note> of the Council called to settle this matter.

*His attendance of St. Paul in his travels. Their arrival in Crete. Titus appointed by him as Bishop of that island. The testimonies of the Ancients for that purpose.
