I. Ancient Church writers mention little of this holy man; his identity and origins are known only through uncertain probabilities. <sup>a</sup> St. Chrysostom <note>John Chrysostom (c. 347–407 AD), an influential Archbishop of Constantinople and Church Father.</note> suggests that he was born in Corinth, for no other reason than that in some ancient copies of the Bible (as is still seen in several manuscripts today), mention is made of St. Paul visiting the house of one [Titus] named Justus, a worshiper of God, while in Corinth. Later writers generally state he was born in Crete, now better known by <margin>b *Florus, Roman History, book 3, chapter 7, page 65.*</margin> the modern name Candia—a noble island (as the <sup>b</sup> historian calls it, adding that the only reason the Romans went to war there was their desire to conquer such a magnificent country) in the Aegean Sea. In ancient times, it was famous for being the birthplace of Jupiter, the sovereign of the pagan gods, and for the Labyrinth of Daedalus; more recently, it has been known as a major site of war between the Ottoman [Turkish] Emperor and the Republic of Venice. History has not preserved any certain records of his parents, though if we believe some accounts, he was of no common lineage, but of royal blood. <margin>The blessed Titus was of the lineage of Minos [the text likely means Minos], the King of Crete, being a descendant, as says Zonaras the legal scholar who wrote his life, though the holy Apostle Paul does not mention it. *Greek Menaion*, August 25th, under the letter mu III. <note>original: Τιτος ὁ μακάριος εκ γενους [Legend. fine dubio Μινωος] του βασιλεως Κρητης...</note></margin> These accounts trace his lineage back to none other than Minos, King of Crete, whom the poets describe as the son of Jupiter. Because of the fairness of his laws and his impartial justice, the poets elevated Minos to be one of the three great judges in the underworld, whose role is to decide the eternal fate of men. Historians, however, more accurately state that he was the son of Xanthus, King of that island, and that he succeeded his father on the throne. But I will move past that.

<margin>
*As cited above.*
</margin>
II. Regardless of his parentage, we are certain that he was a Greek, likely both by nationality and religion. The Greek Church, in its liturgy, gives us this account of his early years and conversion to Christianity: having been born to noble parents, his youth was dedicated to scholarship and a classical education. At twenty years old, he heard a voice telling him that he must leave his home and save his soul, for all his learning would otherwise be of little use to him. <margin>Titus, you must depart from here and save your soul, for this education will not benefit you. *Menaion*, as above. <note>original: Τιτε, δει σε εντευθεν εκδημησαι...</note></margin> Not satisfied with the warning, he in-

# The Life of ST. TITUS.

He was eager once more to hear the voice. A year later, he was again commanded in a vision to study the scrolls of the Jewish Law. He opened the book and his eyes fell upon the words of the Prophet:

<margin>Isaiah 41:1</margin>
> "Listen in silence before me, you islands, and let the people renew their strength; let them come near, and let them speak; let us come together for judgment." 

Whereupon his uncle, who was the Proconsul <note>original: Proconsul; a governor of a Roman province.</note> of Crete at the time, having heard the reports of our Lord’s miracles in Judea, sent him to Jerusalem. He remained there until Christ’s ascension, when he was converted by the famous sermon of St. Peter, which won three thousand souls at once. I cannot guarantee the truth of this story, though it is claimed to be derived from the *Acts* said to have been written by *Zenas the Lawyer*, mentioned by St. Paul. This is an authority that, I confess, I dare not encourage the reader to rely on too heavily without better evidence. Let us therefore turn to something more certain and unquestionable.

<margin>
Acts 15:1, 2.
ª Manuscript of the Codex Bezae <note>original: Cod. Beze MS; an important 5th-century manuscript of the New Testament containing a unique version of the text of Acts.</note> regarding Acts 15:2.
Galatians 1, 2, etc.
</margin>
III. Having arrived in Judea or the surrounding areas and become convinced of the truth and divinity of the Christian faith, he became St. Paul’s convert and disciple, though we do not know exactly when or where he was converted. It is likely either that he followed St. Paul as a companion and assistant, or that he joined the Church at Antioch. It was there that the famous controversy arose concerning circumcision and the Mosaic Institutions <note>The religious laws and rituals established by Moses.</note>, which some argued were just as necessary to observe as the belief and practice of Christianity. They decided that *Paul, Barnabas, and certain others of them should go up to Jerusalem to the Apostles and Elders about this question.* Furthermore, a very ancient manuscript adds that when Paul earnestly persuaded them to remain in the doctrine they had been taught, those very Jewish zealots who had come down to Antioch and first started the argument *themselves requested Paul, Barnabas, and some others to go and consult with the Apostles and Elders at Jerusalem, and to abide by their judgment and decision on the case.*

In the group sent on this mission for the Gospel was our St. Titus, whom St. Paul (encouraged to take this journey by a specific revelation) was willing to take along with him. No sooner had they arrived in Jerusalem than spies were at hand—certain zealous Jews pretending to be Christian converts—who worked their way into St. Paul's company and acquaintance. They closely watched how much liberty he took regarding the religious laws so that they might find grounds for an accusation against him. They charged him with preaching to and associating with the Gentiles, and pointed out that at that very time Titus, an uncircumcised Greek, was his close companion. This was a scandal that could only be avoided by circumcising him, which would show that Paul had no intention of undermining the rites and customs of the Law. 

St. Paul (who knew when to yield and when to stand his ground) would by no means consent to this. Although at another time he was willing to circumcise Timothy—who was Jewish on his mother’s side—to please the Jews for their own spiritual growth and to better win them over, he refused to circumcise Titus, a Gentile. He did this so that he would not seem to betray the freedom of the Gospel, harden the Jews in their unreasonable and deep-seated prejudices against the Heathens <note>Non-Jewish people.</note>, or give the Gentiles a just reason for offense and discouragement, which might cause them to turn away from Christianity. Accordingly, he resisted their pressure with unshakable resolution, and his actions were immediately justified by the final ruling of the Council summoned to decide this matter.

# The Life of ST. TITUS.

<margin>
a Apostolic History at the year of Christ 46. <note>original: Histor. Apost. ad ann. Christ. 46.</note>
b At the year 57, number 212. <note>original: Ad ann. 57. n. 212.</note>
c In the Argument of the Epistle to Titus; Acts 27:7. <note>original: In Argum. Epist. ad Tit. Act. 27. 7.</note>
</margin>
III. The business for which they were sent being settled in the Synod <note>The Council of Jerusalem.</note>, he undoubtedly returned with St. *Paul* to *Antioch*. From there, he accompanied him in his travels until, after visiting the Churches of *Syria* and *Cilicia*, they set sail for *Crete*. I believe, along with ^a^ *Capellus*, that this period is the most likely time for their journey to that island. I find this more probable than the dates suggested by ^b^ *Baronius* and others, who place it during St. *Paul's* journey from *Macedonia* into *Greece* (which Baronius assumes was a sea voyage through the *Cyclades* islands in the *Aegean* Sea). I also disagree with ^c^ *Grotius*, who refers it to Paul's later voyage to *Rome*. Grotius bases his theory on a double mistake: first, that St. *Paul* and his company stopped and stayed at *Crete* on that trip, when the text only says they sailed *under it* and passed *by it*; and second, that *Titus* was even in the company at that time, of which there is no evidence or mention in the history. 

Sailing, therefore, from some port in *Cilicia*, they arrived at *Crete*. There, St. *Paul* diligently set himself to preach and spread the Christian Faith. He delighted, as much as possible, in being the first messenger of the joyful news of the Gospel in every place he visited, refusing to plant in *another man’s territory* or build upon *foundations already laid by others*. However, because the care of other Churches called for his attention, he could not stay long enough to see Christianity reach full maturity and perfection there. Consequently, he appointed *Titus* as Bishop of that island, so that he might nurture that infant Church, oversee its growth and prosperity, and manage its government and administration. 

The ancient writers declare this with one voice. He <margin>
d Ecclesiastical History, book 3, chapter 4, page 73. <note>original: H. Eccl. l. 3. c. 4. p. 73.</note>
e Preface to Titus, page 419, volume 5. <note>original: Præf. in Tit. p. 419. T. 5.</note>
f Dorotheus's Synopsis, page 148. <note>original: Doroth. Synops. p. 148.</note>
g Cited in Jerome, On Illustrious Men, regarding Titus. <note>original: Ap. Hier. de Script. in Tit.</note>
h Homily 1 on Titus, page 1692. <note>original: Homil. 1. in Tit. p. 1692.</note>
i Argument of the Epistle to Titus, volume 3. <note>original: Argum. Epiſt. ad Tit. Tom. 3.</note>
</margin> was the *first Bishop* (says ^d^ *Eusebius*) *of the Churches in Crete*. The Apostle *consecrated him Bishop of it*, according to ^e^ St. *Ambrose*, ^f^ *Dorotheus*, and ^g^ *Sophronius*. He was (says ^h^ *Chrysostom*) an esteemed person, to whom <term>the whole island</term> <note>original Greek: ἡ νῆσος ὁλόκληρος</note> was entirely committed, so that he might exercise power and jurisdiction over many bishops. St. *Paul* ordained him Bishop of *Crete*, despite it being a very large island, so that he might ordain bishops under him, as ^i^ *Theodoret* expressly states. To this could be added the testimonies of *Theophylact*, *Oecumenius*, and others, as well as the subscription at the end of the Epistle to *Titus* (which, though not written by Paul himself, is nonetheless ancient), where it is said that he was *ordained the first Bishop of the Church of the Cretans*. <margin>
k Argument on 1 Timothy, page 1519. <note>original: Argum. in 1 ad Tim. p. 1519.</note>
</margin> And ^k^ St. *Chrysostom* gives this as the reason why St. *Paul* wrote Epistles to *Titus* and *Timothy*, but not to *Silas* or *Luke*: he had committed the care and government of Churches to the former, while keeping the others as attendants and ministers to travel with him.

<margin>
Titus 1:5.
l Homily 2 on Titus, page 1700. See also Theophylact and Oecumenius on this passage. <note>original: Homil. 2. in Tit. p. 1700. vid. etiam Theoph. & Oecumen. in loc.</note>
</margin>
IV. This is not merely an interpretation made up by ancient writers; it seems clearly based on St. *Paul's* own words. He tells *Titus*, *For this reason I left you in Crete, that you should set in order the things that are lacking, and ordain elders in every city, as I had commanded you*. This means: "I appointed you governor of that Church, so that you might organize and manage its affairs according to the rules and directions I gave you." *Ordain Elders*] he means Bishops (says ^l^ *Chrysostom*), as I have often explained elsewhere. *Elders in every city*] Paul was not willing (as Chrysostom adds) for the entire administration of such a large island to be managed by one person. Instead, he wanted every city to have its own governor to oversee and care for it. In this way, the burden would be lighter by being shared among many, and the people would be served with greater diligence. Indeed, *Crete* was famous for having more cities than any other island in the world; for this reason, it was called <term>Hecatompolis</term> <note>From the Greek 'hekaton' (hundred) and 'polis' (city), meaning "The Island of a Hundred Cities."</note> in ancient times. In short, it is plain that *Titus* had

# The Life of ST. TITUS.

...had power of jurisdiction, ordination, and ecclesiastical censures <note>Formal punishments or reprimands within the church.</note> conferred upon him, placing him above all other pastors or ministers in that church.

V. For several years, *St. Titus* continued his duties in *Crete* until he received a summons from *St. Paul*, who was then preparing to leave *Ephesus*. The Apostle had asked *Apollos* to accompany *Timothy* and others whom he had sent to *Corinth*. However, *Apollos* chose instead to go to *Crete*. By him and *Zenas*, Paul wrote an Epistle <note>A formal letter; specifically the biblical Book of Titus.</note> to *Titus* to encourage him to be active and vigilant, and to teach him how to conduct himself in the position where Paul had placed him. Indeed, he needed all the advice *St. Paul* could provide, as he had a very unruly and difficult group of people to manage. The country itself was no more fruitful and abundant than the people's behavior was corrupt and immoral. <margin>Titus 1:12.</margin> *St. Paul* reminded *Titus* of the bad reputation one of their own poets (who certainly knew them best) had given them:

->The Cretans are always liars, evil beasts, lazy gluttons. <note>original Greek: Κρῆτες ἀεὶ ψεῦσται, κακὰ θηρία, γαστέρες ἀργαί.</note><-

<margin>
a Homily 3 on Titus, page 1707.
</margin>
This verse *St. Chrysostom* <note>An influential early Church Father and preacher (c. 347–407 AD).</note> believes the Apostle took from *Callimachus*, who indeed uses the first part of it. *Callimachus* accused the *Cretans* of being notorious liars for claiming that *Jupiter* <note>The Roman name for Zeus, the king of the gods.</note> was not only born but also died among them, and that they had his tomb with this inscription: "Here lies *Jupiter*" <note>original Greek: ΕΝΤΑΥΘΑ ΖΑΝ ΚΕΙΤΑΙ</note>. Since the deity is immortal, this claim made them liars. The "good Father" <note>Chrysostom</note> worries himself with many unnecessary difficulties trying to reconcile this. In reality, *St. Paul* did not borrow it from *Callimachus* but from *Epimenides*, a native of *Crete* who was famous among the ancients for his trances and prophetic divinations. <margin>b In the Life of Solon, page 84.</margin> He was "beloved by the gods and wise in divine matters, possessing an inspired and ritualistic wisdom," <note>original Greek: Θεοφιλὴς & Σοφὸς περὶ τὰ θεῖα, τὴν ἐνθουσιαστικὴν & τελεστικὴν Σοφίαν</note> as *Plutarch* says of him. From him, *Callimachus* cites part of <margin> "The Cretans are always liars; for even your tomb, O King, the Cretans built; but you did not die; for you exist forever." <note>original: Callim. Hymn. to Zeus. p. 1.</note> To "act like a Cretan" <note>original: κρητίζειν</note> is a proverb meaning to lie. <note>original: Vet. Schol. ibi.</note> "To act like a Cretan" means to lie. Another proverb is "to act like a Cretan against a Cretan," because they are liars and deceivers. <note>original: Suid. in voc. Κρητίζειν. Also cited by Michael Apostolius and Psellus.</note> </margin> the verse and applies it to his own specific purpose, while *St. Paul* quotes it in its entirety from the original author. *This witness (he says) is true.* Indeed, he did not lie about them; we have the supporting evidence of most pagan writers who make the same accusations. They were so famous for lying that "to act like a Cretan" <note>original: Κρητίζειν</note> and "to act like a Cretan against a Cretan" <note>original: Κρητίζειν πρὸς Κρῆτα</note> became proverbs meaning "to lie like a Cretan" and "to out-cheat a cheater." Nothing was more common than the phrase "lying Crete" <note>original: mendax Creta</note>. <margin>c History, book 6, page 681 and book 4, page 386.</margin> *Polybius* <note>A famous Greek historian.</note> tells us that nowhere else could one find more cunning and deceitful minds, or more wicked and harmful schemes. He notes their manners were so greedy and stingy that, of all the people in the world, only the *Cretans* thought nothing was shameful if it brought profit or advantage. Furthermore, they were lazy and hated work, being gluttonous and lacking self-control. They were unwilling to exert any effort except to *make provision for the flesh* <note>To satisfy physical desires or lusts.</note>. As a natural result of ease, idleness, and abundance, they were sexually immoral and prone to the most disgraceful types of lust. They were "outrageously obsessed with young boys" <note>original Greek: περὶ τὰ παιδικὰ δαιμονίως ἐπτόηνται</note> (as <margin>d The Deipnosophists, book 13, page 601.</margin> *Athenaeus* informs us), being passionately driven toward that sin that is named after *Sodom*. Given this situation, it is no wonder *St. Paul* tells *Titus* to re-

# The Life of ST. TITUS.

...reprove <note>original: "prove"</note> them sharply, seeing that their corrupt and depraved manners would require the sharpest scalpels <note>original: "lancets"</note> and the most stinging medicines <note>original: "corrosives"</note> he could apply to them.

## VI.

In the Epistle itself, the main body consists of rules and directions for the various ranks and relationships of men. Because spiritual and church affairs are more significant than all others, he first instructs Titus on the qualifications of those he should appoint to be Bishops and Guides of Souls. They must be holy and harmless, innocent and inoffensive—men who had not divorced their first wife to marry a second <note>This refers to the biblical requirement that a bishop be the "husband of one wife."</note>, whose children were sober, orderly, and raised in the Christian faith. They must be approachable <note>original: "easie and treatable"</note>, mild-mannered, and not prone to anger. They must be free from a love of wine and any desire for riches through dishonest <note>original: "sordid"</note> and greedy designs. They should be kind and hospitable, lovers of goodness and good men, modest and prudent, just and honest, strict and temperate. They must be firm and constant in owning and defending the Christian doctrines that have been delivered to them, so that they may be able both to persuade and comfort others, and mightily convince those who resist and oppose the truth.

It was certainly not without great reason that the Apostle required the guides and governors of the Church to be thus able to refute opponents <note>original: "gainsayers"</note>. For whatever authors report of *Crete*—that it bred no serpents or venomous creatures—it is certain that the poison of error and heresy had crept in there along with the acceptance of

<margin>
Titus 1:10.
Verse 14.
Titus 3:9.
</margin>

Christianity. There were many unruly and vain talkers, especially those of the "Circumcision" <note>Jewish converts to Christianity who maintained that followers of Jesus must still follow Jewish Law.</note>, who endeavored to corrupt the doctrine of the Gospel with Jewish fables, groundless and unauthorized traditions, mystical and Cabalistic <note>Referring to the Kabbalah, a tradition of Jewish mysticism.</note> explanations, and foolish questions and genealogies. For the Jews, borrowing their notions from the schools of *Plato*, had fallen into a habit of deriving things from an imaginary generation: first *Binah* or "Understanding," then *Achmoth* or *Cochmah* "Wisdom," and so on until they came to *Milcah* "the Kingdom," and *Shekinah* or "the Divine Presence." This was much the same way the poets of old traced the family trees <note>original: "pedigrees"</note> of their gods. They first had their several conjunctions <note>original Greek: Συζυγίαι—the coupling and mixing of things together</note>, and from there proceeded their genealogies <note>original Greek: γενεαλογίαι—their generations</note>. Out of Chaos came *Erebus* <note>A personification of darkness.</note> and the dark Night; their union produced the *Ether* and the Day. From there, <sup>a</sup> *Hesiod* proceeds to explain the whole pagan theology concerning the origin of their gods.

<margin>
<sup>a</sup> Hesiod, *Theogony*, page 466.
</margin>

## VII.

In imitation of all this, and from a mixture of it all, the *Valentinians*, *Basilidians*, and the rest of the *Gnostic* <note>Early Christian heretics who claimed to possess secret, "higher" knowledge.</note> crowd formed the senseless and unintelligible systems of their "Fullness" <note>original Greek: Πλήρωμα (Pleroma)</note> and thirty *Aeons* <note>Divine beings or "ages" in Gnostic mythology.</note>, divided into three classes of conjunctions. In the first were four couples: *Profundity* and *Silence*, *Mind* and *Truth*, the *Word* and *Life*, and *Man* and the *Church*. In the second were five, namely: *Profound* and *Mixture*, *Unaging* <note>original: "Ageratus"</note> and *Union*, and so forth. In the third were six: the *Advocate* <note>original: "Paraclete"</note> and *Faith*, *Paternal* <note>original: "Patricos"</note> and *Hope*, and so forth. Of all this, if anyone desires to know more, they may find enough in the works of *Irenaeus*, *Tertullian*, and *Epiphanius* to this purpose—if they can understand it.

<margin>
<sup>b</sup> *Heresies* 31, page 76; see also Tertullian, *The Prescription Against Heretics*, chapter 7, page 204.
</margin>

The <sup>b</sup> last of these writers not only affirms expressly that *Valentinus* and his party introduced "pagan-mythical poetry" <note>original Greek: ἐθνόμυθον ποίησιν</note>—the fabulous and poetic fancies of the heathens—but he also draws a particular parallel between *Hesiod’s Theogony* and their thirty Aeons or Ages, consisting of

All of this was nothing but a trivial and imaginative <note>original: "fantastical"</note> imitation of Hesiod’s <note>Hesiod (8th century BC) was a Greek poet whose "Theogony" described the origins and genealogies of the gods.</note> lineage and generation of the gods, which, being joined in pairs, succeeded in this order: *Chaos and Night; Erebus and Earth; Ether and Day;* etc. There is (as the author observes) no difference between the one scheme and the other, except for the change and alteration of the names. This may suffice as a sample <note>original: "Specimen"</note> to show where this idle generation borrowed their extravagant ideas, though there

---

And this is the tragedy of the thirty Eons <note>Eons; in Gnosticism, these were divine beings or emanations from the primary Godhead</note> they have fabricated, and the harmony of the pairs within their supposedly spiritual "Fullness" <note>original: "Pleroma"</note>. If one compares this to the works of Hesiod, Stesichorus <note>An early Greek lyric poet</note>, and other Greek poets, he would find them to be exactly the same, and nothing else. These masters of heresy have crafted nothing wonderful or mysterious, as they claim; they have merely echoed the fictional style of the Greek poets to spread their errors and teachings, changing nothing except the invented, barbaric names they use. <note>original: Καὶ αὐτὴ μὲν ἡ τῶν τριάκοντα αἰώνων παρ' αὐτοῖς μυθοποιουμένη τραγῳδία... Id. ibid. (Epiphanius, *Against Heresies*)</note>

---

were those who had established much the same thing before the time of Valentinus. By such obscure and erratic notions and principles, the false apostles, both in Crete and elsewhere, sought to undermine the Christian doctrine. They also mixed it with principles of great moral laxity and false freedom <note>original: "looseness and liberty"</note>, so that they might more easily win over the affections of men.

<margin>Titus 1:11</margin>
By these means, they brought over numerous converts to their party, whom they exploited for profit <note>original: "made merchandise, gaining sufficient advantage to themselves"</note>. Therefore, it was absolutely necessary that these men’s mouths should be stopped. They should not have been allowed to continue under the guise of such lofty and sublime speculations, nor under a pretense of Christian liberty, to lead people away from the Christian religion and the plainness and simplicity of the Gospel.

Having finished with church leaders <note>original: "Ecclesiastics"</note>, the Apostle proceeds to give directions for people of all ages and roles, whether old or young, men or women, children or servants. He then addresses matters of more public concern—rulers and the people—and indeed, how to conduct ourselves in the general course of our lives.

At the close of the Epistle, he asks Titus to provide Zenas and Apollos—the two apostolic messengers by whom this letter was delivered to him—with everything they need for their return. He also commands that Titus himself should meet him with all convenient speed at Nicopolis, where Paul had resolved to spend his winter. It is not certain where that city was located: it may have been Nicopolis in Epirus (named after Augustus Caesar's victory there over Mark Antony and Cleopatra), or perhaps Nicopolis in Thrace, on the River Nesus, near the borders of Macedonia where Saint Paul was then traveling, or one of the many other cities of that name in those regions. And that by with-

VIII. After St. Paul departed from Ephesus, he came to Troas. Although he had a good opportunity to preach the Gospel offered to him there, yet (as he himself tells us) he had no rest in his spirit because he did not find his brother Titus. Paul had been impatiently expecting Titus to bring him an account of the state of the Church at Corinth. Whether Titus had already been with Paul and was sent on this errand, or had been commanded by him to stop at Corinth on his way from Crete, is not known. Not meeting him there, Paul went on to Macedonia, where at length Titus arrived. He comforted Paul in the midst of all his other sorrows and difficulties with the joyful news of the happy condition of the Church at Corinth. He reported how readily they had reformed those failings <note>original: "miscarriages"</note> which Paul had charged them with in his first Epistle. This fully proved the great character Paul had given of them to Titus, and they provided significant evidence of it in the kind and welcoming entertainment Titus found among them. Soon after, St. Paul—having received the collections from the Macedonian Churches for the poor <note>original: "indigent"</note> Christians at Jerusalem—sent Titus and St. Luke back to Corinth. He sent them to encourage their charity and prepare their contributions before his own arrival there, and through them, he wrote his second Epistle to that Church.

<margin>
<gloss>a</gloss> Peter de Natalibus, *History of the Saints* <note>original: "Hist. SS."</note>, book 7, chapter 108.
2 Timothy 4:10.
<gloss>b</gloss> Peter de Natalibus, as cited above; from the *Acts of St. Titus* written (as it is said) by Zenas. Pseudo-Dexter, *Chronicle* at the year 220.
</margin>

IX. TITUS faithfully carried out his mission to the Church of Corinth and, having finished the services for which he was sent, we may suppose he returned back to Crete. We do not hear any further news of him until St. Paul's imprisonment at Rome. Titus went there (if my <gloss>a</gloss> author speaks truly) about two years after Paul and stayed with him until his martyrdom. He was reportedly present at the execution and, together with St. Luke, committed Paul to his grave. This is an account which I confess I am less inclined to believe, because I am assured by St. Paul himself that before his death, Titus had left him and gone into Dalmatia—a province of Illyricum—to plant the "Gospel of Peace" among that fierce and warlike nation, probably visiting there on his way back to Crete. This is the last notice we find of him in the Holy Scriptures, and the records of the Church from that point on do not furnish us with any certain records or remarks <note>original: "Memoirs or Remarques"</note> concerning him.

Indeed, if the story some people tell were true, one thing alone would be enough to make him memorable to posterity: I mean his converting Pliny the Younger, that learned and eloquent man who was Proconsul of Bithynia and an intimate close advisor <note>original: "Privy Counsellor"</note> to the Emperor Trajan. For they tell us <gloss>b</gloss> that while returning from his province in Bithynia, Pliny landed in Crete, where the Emperor had commanded him to build a temple to Jupiter. This was done, but no sooner was it finished than St. Titus cursed it, and it immediately tumbled to the ground. The man, as you may guess, was strangely troubled and came with tears to the holy man to request his counsel. Titus advised him to begin the building again in the name of the God of the Christians, and it would not fail to prosper. He did so, and having finished it, he was baptized along with his son. Furthermore, some—to make the story "perfect"—add that he suffered martyrdom for the Faith at Novocomum, a city of Insubria in Italy, where he was born. I presume the reader will not expect me to take the pains to disprove this story, which is sufficiently improbable in itself, and which I view as being made of the same material <note>original: "Metal"</note> and coined in the same...

X. St. Titus lived, as the ancient writers tell us, to a great age, dying about the ninety-fourth year of his life. He died in peace (say Sophronius and Isidore) and lies buried in Crete. The Roman Martyrology adds that he was buried in that very church where St. Paul ordained him Bishop of that island. I understand this to mean a church that was built there later, as it is not likely there would be any at that time. At Candia <note>Modern-day Heraklion</note>, the capital <note>original: "Metropolis"</note> of the island, there is, or lately was, an ancient and beautiful <margin>ᵉ Cotovicus, *Travels*, Book 1, chapter 12, page 60. <note>original: "Cotovic. Itin. lib. 1. c. 12. p. 60."</note></margin> church dedicated to St. Titus. In that church, his remains are said to be honorably entombed under the high altar and are held in great veneration by both the Greeks and the Latins. Although what has become of them since that famous city lately fell into the hands of the Turks <note>The Siege of Candia ended in 1669 with the city falling to the Ottoman Empire</note>—that great scourge of Christendom—is unknown to me. His festival is celebrated in the Western Church on the 4th day of January; in the Greek Church on August 25th; and among the Christians in Egypt (as appears by the <margin>ᶠ *On the Sanhedrin*, Volume 3, chapter 15, page 396. <note>original: "De Synedr. Tom. 3. c. 15. p. 396."</note></margin> Arabic Calendar published by Mr. Selden), the 22nd of the month Barmahyth—corresponding to our March 18th—is consecrated to his memory.

# The Life of ST. DIONYSIUS.

The miraculous eclipse at our Savior’s Passion. *Dionysius’s remarks upon it.* His return to Athens, *and being made one of the Judges of the Areopagus.* The nature of this court: *the number and quality of its judges.* *St. Paul arraigned before it: his discourse, and its success.* Dionysius’s *conversion.* His further *instruction by Hierotheus.* Hierotheus, *who he was.* *Dionysius appointed Bishop of Athens.* A brief account of his story *according to those who confuse him with Dionysius, Bishop of Paris.* *These shown to be distinct people.* The origin and process of the mistake investigated. A probable account given of it. Dionysius’s *martyrdom at Athens, and the date of it.* A legendary <note>original: "fabulous"</note> miracle reported of his skull. The description of his person, and the exaggerated <note>original: "hyperbolical"</note> praise which the Greeks give him. The books attributed to him. *These were not his.* *Apollinaris (probably) shown to be their author.* Several passages from ancient authors noted for that purpose. Why books were often published under other men's names. These books were the *source of enthusiasm <note>In this context, "enthusiasm" refers to the belief in direct divine inspiration or religious ecstasy.</note> and mystical theology.* An example passage from them given for that purpose.

I.
<image-desc>Decorative initial 'S' depicting a landscape with hills, plants, and small buildings, possibly a stylized monastery or hermitage.</image-desc>
DIONYSIUS was born at Athens, the "Eye of Greece," and the fountain of learning and culture <note>original: "Humanity"</note>. It was the only place that, without competition, had for so many ages maintained an undisputed reputation for arts and sciences. There was a universal gathering of people from all parts of the world to Athens to perfect themselves in the more refined and useful studies. Though we find nothing particularly concerning his parents, we may safely conclude them to have been people of noble status, or at least of a higher rank than common, seeing that none were admitted to be judges of the Areopagus <margin>a *Isocrates, Orat. Areopag. p. 147.* *See also Maximus, Prologue to the Works of St. Dionysius, Preface, p. 34.*</margin> (as one who knew very well informs us) <term>unless they were nobly born, and had gained a good reputation for great virtue and moderation in their lives</term> <note>original: "Πλὺν οἱ καλῶς γεγόνοτες, ὃ πολλῶ ἀρετῶ ἐ ῷωφροσύνω ὃν τῇ βίω ἐυδοκίμησι"</note>. Being born in the very midst of arts and civility, his education could not have been anything but learned and honorable, especially considering the advantages of his birth and fortune. Accordingly, he was <gloss>b</gloss> <margin>b *Suidas, under the word "Stoud," p. 744.*</margin> instructed in all the learned sciences of Greece, wherein he made such vast improvements that he easily surpassed any of his time. There was scarcely any sect or school of philosophy then in fashion which he had not considered and tested. It does not indeed appear to which of them he particularly devoted and applied himself; those who suppose him to have joined the school of *Plato* do so, I believe, for no other reason than because the doctrine contained in the books that bear his name seems so closely related to the principles of that noble sect.

II. BUT it was not a domestic education, nor all the advantages which *Athens* could afford, that could satisfy the vast potential of his mind. He therefore resolved to refine and improve himself through foreign travel. Being in the prime and vigor of his youth, about the age of twenty-five <margin>c *Suidas, as above; Maximus, Pachymeres, Syncellus, and many others.* d *Eulogy of St. Dionysius, p. 349. Vol. 1.*</margin> years, he took with him one *Apollophanes, a rhetorician*, his fellow-student, and (if <gloss>d</gloss> *Syncellus* says truly) his kinsman. This Apollophanes was afterwards at *Smyrna*, the teacher of *Polemon the Laodicean*, just as Polemon was the teacher of *Aristides* <note>original: "Zerfides"</note>, the famous philosopher and apologist for the Christians. Thus furnished with a suitable companion, he is said to have gone to *Egypt* to associate with

<header>The life of St. Dionysius, 67</header>

<margin>
a *The Miscellanies* <note>original: *Stromat.*</note> Book 1, p. 302.
b *Geography*, Book 17, p. 806.
c Alexander Polyhistor, *History of the Jews* in Eusebius, *Preparation for the Gospel* <note>original: *Praep. Evang.*</note>, Book 9, Chapter 17, p. 419.
</margin>
He studied with their philosophers and wise men so that he might perfect himself in the study of **mathematics** and the more mysterious and obscure parts of learning. Egypt had in all ages been looked upon as the primary school not only of astrology, but of the more deep and uncommon speculations of theology. The great masters of wisdom and divinity among the non-Christians <note>original: "Gentiles"</note> never thought they had learned enough until they had crowned their studies by conversing with the Egyptian sages. For this reason, it was frequented by Orpheus, Homer, Solon, and Thales, as well as by Pythagoras and Plato—and who not? Indeed, regarding Pythagoras, <margin>a</margin> Clement of Alexandria reports that he even allowed himself to be circumcised so that he might be admitted into the secret shrines <note>original: "eis ta adyta"</note>—to the concealed rites and notions of their religion—and become acquainted with their secret and mystical philosophy. The place Dionysius settled in was Heliopolis, a city between Coptos and Alexandria, where the Egyptian priests mostly resided. It was a place excellently suited for the contemplation of the heavenly bodies and the study of philosophy and astronomy. There, <margin>b</margin> Strabo (who lived around this same time) tells us he was shown the dwellings of the priests and the apartments of Plato and Eudoxus, who lived there for thirteen years. Furthermore, a very ancient <margin>c</margin> historian assures us that Abraham himself lived there and taught the Egyptian priests astronomy and other branches of learning.

<margin>
d *Chronicle*, Book 13, in Eusebius, *Chronicle* at the Year of Christ 32. See Greek text p. 202. See Origen *Against Celsus*, Book 2, p. 80. And the *Alexandrian Chronicle* at the 18th year of Tiberius, 4th Indiction, 202nd Olympiad, 4, p. 520.
e *Prologue to the works of St. Dionysius*, p. 36.
</margin>
III. Dionysius no doubt applied himself to his studies in this place, during which stay one memorable event is reported. The Son of God about this time was delivered up at Jerusalem to a painful and shameful death by the hands of violence and injustice. At that moment the Sun, as if ashamed to behold such great wickedness, hid its head and put on mourning to attend the funeral of its Maker. This eclipse was contrary to all the known rules and laws of nature, happening during a Full Moon, when the Moon is at its greatest distance from the Sun and consequently cannot be in conjunction with it. The Moon moved itself under the Sun from its eastern <note>original: "Oriental"</note> to its western <note>original: "Occidental"</note> point, and then back again by a reverse <note>original: "retrograde"</note> motion, causing a strange loss of light for three hours together. That there was such a wonderful and supernatural darkness over all the Earth for three hours at the time of our Savior's suffering, by which the Sun was darkened, is unanimously attested by the Gospel historians. It is mentioned not only by them, but <margin>d</margin> Phlegon of Tralles (who was once a servant to the Emperor Trajan) speaks of an eclipse of the Sun that happened about that time, "the greatest of any that had been ever known" <note>original: "Megistē tōn egnōsmenōn proteron"</note>, by which the day was turned into night and the stars appeared at noon. An earthquake also accompanied it, by which many houses at Nicaea in Bithynia were overturned. Apollophanes, beholding this strange eclipse, cried out to Dionysius that these were changes and revolutions of some great importance, to whom the other replied, **"Either God is suffering, or at least he feels sympathy and shares the suffering of the one who is."** I confess these passages are not to be found in the most ancient writers of the Church, but that should not be a fair objection when we consider how little care was then taken to record things in writing, and how a great many of those few ancient records that were written were quickly lost, as Eusebius frequently complains. This is not to mention that a great many writings might have—and did—escape his notice. And <margin>e</margin> Maximus, I remember, in answering the objection that the books attributed to St. Dionysius <note>original: "S. Denys"</note> are not mentioned by Eusebius, tells us that him-

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the miraculous darkness at the crucifixion of Jesus Christ, addressing scholarly debates about its reach before detailing Dionysius's return to Athens and his appointment to the high court of the Areopagus.</meta>

# The Life of St. DIONYSIUS.

He himself had met with several writings of the ancient authors, of which there is no trace in *Eusebius* <note>original: "Eusebius of Caesarea, a 4th-century historian known as the 'Father of Church History'."</note>. But however that may be, it does not argue against the facts. Many things, though never formally recorded, are preserved in their substance by constant tradition and report. I do not deny that the various authors who report this event might have derived it directly from the epistles said to be written to St. *Polycarp* and *Apollophanes*. However, I cannot suppose that the author of these epistles simply invented the facts out of his own head, but rather delivered what tradition had passed down to his time. Indeed, what would more strongly shake the foundation of the story, if true, is what <margin>a Tract. 35 on Matthew, folio 89, column 1.</margin> <sup>a</sup> *Origen* supposes: that this darkness which was over all the earth, and the earthquake that attended our Lord's Passion, extended no farther than *Judea*, just as some of the other omens extended no farther than *Jerusalem*. But as to what degree of truth or probability that opinion may hold, I leave to others to inquire.

IV. DIONYSIUS, having finished his studies at *Heliopolis*, returned to *Athens*, incomparably fitted to serve his country. Accordingly, he was promoted to be one of the judges of the *Areopagus*, a position of great honor and renown. The *Areopagus* was a famous senate-house built upon a hill in *Athens*, where their great court of justice assembled. <margin>b Aristid. Volume 1, page 331.</margin> <sup>b</sup> One author calls it "the most honored and most sacred of the courts among the Greeks" <note>original: "τῆς ἐν τοῖς Ἕλλησι δικαςηρίων τιμιώτατον & ἁγιώτατον"</note>. Under their jurisdiction came all the greater and more capital cases, and especially matters of religion, blasphemy against the gods, and contempt of the holy mysteries. Therefore, St. *Paul* was brought before this court as a proclaimer of strange gods when he preached to them concerning *Jesus* and *Anastasis* <note>Greek: "Anastasis" means Resurrection</note>. No one could be a member of this council except persons of high birth and quality, wise and prudent men of very strict and severe manners. This solemn and grave assembly struck such great awe and reverence into those who sat in it that <sup>c</sup> *Isocrates* tells us that in his time—even when they had somewhat degenerated from their ancient virtue and men were otherwise irregular and excessive—once chosen into this senate, they immediately ceased their vicious inclinations. They chose instead to conform to the laws and manners of that court <margin>c In the place cited above.</margin> "rather than to persist in shameful vice" <note>original: "ἢ τῇ αἰσχρῇ κακίᾳ ἐμμένειν"</note>. They were exactly upright and impartial in their proceedings and heard cases at night or in the dark, so that the person of the plaintiff or the lawyer <note>original: "Pleader"</note> might have no undue influence upon them. Their sentence was final and decisive, and from their determination there was no appeal. Their number was uncertain; some say it was limited to nine, others say it was thirty-one, others fifty-one, and some say even more. Indeed, the "Nine Men" <note>original: "Novemviri"</note>—who were the King <note>original: "Basileus"</note>, the Archon, the General <note>original: "Polemarchus"</note>, and the six Lawgivers <note>original: "Thesmothetæ"</note>—were the constant source and nursery of this great assembly. Having discharged their various offices, they annually passed into the *Areopagus*. <margin>d Diogenes Laertius, Book 2, in the Life of Socrates, page 115.</margin> <sup>d</sup> Therefore, when *Socrates* was condemned by this court, we find no fewer than two hundred and eighty-one people giving their votes against him, besides those whose "white stones" <note>In ancient Greek courts, a white stone represented a vote for acquittal, while a black stone was for conviction.</note> were for his acquittal. In an ancient inscription upon a column in the *Acropolis* at *Athens*, erected to the memory of *Rufus Festus*, Proconsul of *Greece* and one of these judges, mention is made of the *Areopagite* Senate of three hundred.

<margin>
"The Council of Three Hundred of the Areopagus, and the People of Athens" <note>original: "Η ΕΞ ΑΡΕΟΠΑΓΟΥ ΒΟΥΛΗ ΤΩΝ ΤΡΙΑΚΟΣΙΩΝ, ΚΑΙ Ο ΔΗΜΟΣ Ο ΑΘΗΝΑΙΩΝ"</note> — See the rest in Raffaele Volaterrano, *Urban Commentaries*, Book 8, column 318.
</margin>

<meta>This page continues the biography of St. Dionysius, moving from his early life and education to his pivotal encounter with the Apostle Paul in Athens and his subsequent conversion to Christianity.</meta>

# The Life of St. DIONYSIUS.

<margin>
a *On the Priesthood, book 4, chapter 7, page 67, volume 4; Ambrose, Letter 82, page 198, volume 3.*
b *Hilduin in The Passion of Saint Dionysius, numbers 6, 7, 8, in Surius, October 9th, page 122.*
c *Location cited above.*
</margin>

V. Our **St. Dionysius** <note>original: "S. Denys"</note> sat in this grave and respected court when **St. Paul**, around the year 49 or 50 AD, came to **Athens**. There, Paul resolutely defended the cause of **Christianity** against the attempts of the **Stoic** and **Epicurean** philosophers, who were the main voices against it. The **Athenians**, who were deeply curious and religious <note>original: "superstitious" - in this context, it refers to an intense or excessive concern with religious matters</note> concerning matters of faith, did not know what to make of this new and strange doctrine he taught. They soon brought him before the **Senate of the Areopagus**, which had the proper authority over such cases. 

Here, in a concise and eloquent speech delivered with great freedom of mind and strength of reason, Paul plainly demonstrated the folly and absurdity of the many worthless gods they blindly worshipped. He explained to them that infinite Being who made and governed the world, and described the binding obligations He had placed upon all mankind to worship and adore Him. Paul also showed how God had strengthened all former calls to gratitude, obedience, repentance, and reformation through this final and greatest era—by sending His Son to reveal such an excellent religion to the world. 

While some treated his speech with scorn and laughter, and others politely dismissed it, the discourse still had a successful influence on many. It convinced them of the reasonableness and divinity of the Christian faith. Among these was our **Dionysius**, one of the judges who sat in judgment of him, and **Damaris** his wife (as <sup>a</sup> **St. Chrysostom** and others identify her), and likely his entire household. 

One <sup>b</sup> author (though I confess I do not know by what authority) tells of a specific debate between **Dionysius** and **St. Paul** concerning the **Unknown God**, who, as both God and man, was to appear in later ages to reform the world. The Apostle showed this to be the Holy **Jesus**, who had recently come down from heaven. This so satisfied **St. Dionysius** that he prayed Paul to intercede with heaven so that he might be fully confirmed in this belief. The next day, after **St. Paul** had restored sight to a man born blind, he told the man to go to **Dionysius** and, by that sign, claim his promise to become a convert. Dionysius, amazed by this sight, readily renounced his idolatry and was baptized into the faith of Christ along with his household. 

However, I know the reputation of this author too well to place much weight on this story, especially since I find that **Baronius** <note>Cesare Baronius (1538–1607) was an influential Catholic historian.</note> himself is unwilling to trust it. To this I might add <sup>c</sup> **St. Chrysostom's** observation that the **Areopagite** was converted <term>solely by public speech</term> <note>original: ἀπὸ δημηγορίας μόνης (apo dēmēgorias monēs)</note>, as there was no miracle we know of that might have prompted it.

<margin>
d *St. Metaphrastes in Surius, same location; Maximus the Confessor, as above; Pseudo-Dionysius, On the Divine Names, chapter 2, page 175, volume 1.*
e *On the 3rd of February.*
f *Chronicle of Pseudo-Dexter, for the Year of Christ 71.*
</margin>

VI. Once baptized, we are <sup>d</sup> told he was placed under the care and instruction of **St. Hierotheus** to be further taught in the faith. Hierotheus is a person not even mentioned by any of the ancient writers, which creates in me a strong suspicion that it is only a fictional name and that no such person ever actually existed. Indeed, the *Greek Menaion* <sup>e</sup> <note>A liturgical book of the Eastern Orthodox Church containing services for the saints.</note> claims he was one of the nine senators of the **Areopagus** and was then appointed as tutor to **St. Dionysius**. <sup>f</sup> Others claim he was a **Spaniard** by birth, the first Bishop of **Athens**, and that he later traveled to his own country to be the Bishop of **Segovia** in **Spain**. I believe both accounts are equally true—which is to say, not at all. Such a person would likely never have been thought of if there had not been some mentions of such a teacher in **Dionysius’s** own works, which were later confirmed by the <term>scholiasts</term> <note>Ancient commentators or grammarians who wrote explanatory notes on classical texts.</note> who wrote about him, and eventually developed by others into a formal legend. As for **St. Dionysius**, he is said to have traveled with **St. Paul** for three years after his conversion, and then to have been...

# The Life of St. DIONYSIUS.

<margin>
a *In Eusebius's Ecclesiastical History, book 3, chapter 4, page 74, and book 4, chapter 23, page 144.*
b *Nicephorus's Ecclesiastical History, book 2, chapter 20, page 167.*
</margin>
...having been appointed by him as Bishop of **Athens**. It seems it was necessary to pack **Hierotheus** off to **Spain** so that room might be made for him. Indeed, that **Dionysius** was the first Bishop of **Athens**—and this is no insult to **St. Hierotheus**—we are assured by an authority that cannot be doubted. **Dionysius**, the famous Bishop of **Corinth** (who lived not long after him), explicitly affirms this; and **Nicephorus** adds, which is likely enough, that he was ordained by **St. Paul’s** own hands. I shall only briefly mention his journey to **Jerusalem** to meet the Apostles, who are said to have come from all parts of the world to be present at the last hours of the **Blessed Virgin**, and his several visitations of the churches in **Phrygia** and **Achaia** to plant or confirm the Faith.

VII. **ALL** of this, even if we suppose it were true, is where we must take our leave. For now, the writers of his life generally have him prepare for a much longer journey. Having settled his affairs at **Athens** and appointed a successor to his See, he is said to have gone to **Rome**. (A brief account of these things will suffice, as no truth lies at the bottom of them.) At **Rome**, he was supposedly dispatched by **St. Clement** into **France**, where he planted the Faith and founded an episcopal See at **Paris**. From there, after many years and at about ninety years of age, he is said to have returned to the **East** to converse with **St. John** at **Ephesus**, and then back again to **Paris**. It was there that he suffered martyrdom. Among the infinite other miracles reported of him, he is said to have picked up his head after it had been cut off by the executioners and to have carried it in his hands for two miles—with an angel going before him and a heavenly choir attending him all the way—until he came to the place of his burial. There, he gently laid down his head and himself and was honorably entombed. This is the summary of a very tedious story. It is a tale so improbable in itself and so directly contrary to 
<margin>
c *Sacred History, book 2, page 143.*
</margin>
what **Severus Sulpitius** affirms—that no one was martyred for the Faith in **France** until the fifth persecution under the reign of **Marcus Aurelius Antoninus**—that I shall not spend much time refuting it. This is especially true since the matter has been unanswerably settled by many learned and brilliant men in the **Church of Rome**, and by none more effectively than **Sirmond** and **Launoy**, who have cleared it up beyond any possibility of just objection.

<margin>
d *Usuard's Martyrology, October Calendar, and the 7th day before the Ides of October; Bede's Martyrology, the 7th day before the Ides of October.*
e *Gregory of Tours's History of the Franks, book 1, chapter 28, page 265, Du Chesne edition.*
f *See his letter, and Hilduin's reply in Surius, at the location cited above.*
</margin>
VIII. **INDEED**, we find in several very ancient martyrologies <note>A martyrology is a catalog or list of Christian martyrs and saints arranged in the order of their feast days.</note>, as well as in the writings of **Gregory**, Bishop of **Tours**—who reports it from the *Acts* of the martyr **Saturninus**—that a different **Dionysius**, along with some others, was sent by the Bishop of **Rome** into **France** during the time of the Emperor **Decius** (around 250 AD). There, he preached the Christian Faith and became the Bishop of **Paris**. After great torments and suffering, he was beheaded for his resolute and constant profession of religion. Accordingly, his martyrdom is recorded in the most ancient martyrologies on a day distinct from that of the Athenian **Dionysius**, though the same miracles are often attributed to him as to the other. I suppose no wise man will doubt that this was the first and true foundation of the story. Nor, indeed, is the least mention made of any such thing—certainly not by any writer of name and note—until the times of **Charlemagne** <note>original: "Charles the Great"</note>. At that time, the Emperor and King of **France**, **Louis the Pious** <note>original: "Ludovicus"</note>, wrote to **Hilduin**, the Abbot of **St. Denis**, asking him to pick up whatever memoirs he could find concerning him, whether in the books of the **Greeks** or **Latins** or such records as they had at home, and to digest and compile them into orderly tracts. He did so and produced a very large and detailed...

This particular account was quickly improved and defended by **Hincmar**, Bishop of **Reims** (a student of **Hilduin**), and **Anastasius**, the Librarian of **Rome**; the **Greek** writers of that and the following ages readily gave their approval. A more recent author<sup>a</sup> <margin>a *Jean Mabillon, notes to the Letter of Hincmar, among the Old Miscellany, page 63.* <note>original: "Analect. Veter."</note></margin> hasn't much improved the case for its antiquity; he tells us that in a council of Bishops held in **France** in AD 825—ten years before **Hilduin** wrote his *Areopagitics* <note>Writings concerning Dionysius the Areopagite</note>—mention is made of **St. Dionysius** being sent into **France** by **Clement**, the successor of **St. Peter**. We can easily grant that there might have been some vague and obscure tradition at that time, though the fragment of the Synod he produces does not say a single word about this **Dionysius** being the **Areopagite**, or having any connection to **Athens**. In short, the case seems plainly to be this:

<margin>
b *Rescript to the Emperor Louis, 10. in the same place.* <note>original: "Rescript. ad Ludov. Imperat."</note>
c *Discussion of the response, chapter 9, page 120.* <note>original: "Respons. discuss."</note>
d *Letter to Emperor Charles the Bald, in Surius, in the same place, page 132.* <note>original: "Epist. ad Carol. Calv. Imp."</note>
e *Found in Surius, as above, and Mabillon in the place cited.* <note>original: "Extat apud. Sur."</note>
</margin>
IX. **HILDUIN**, encouraged by his powerful patron, partly to exalt the honor of **France** and partly to advance the reputation of his own monastery, found an obscure **Dionysius** who had been Bishop of **Paris**. He moved him back in time by an age or two and claimed he was the same person as the **Dionysius** of **Athens**, a person of much greater honor and veneration. Using a mix of records and the traditions current among his own people, he drew up a formal account of him from start to finish—likely adding whatever he thought appropriate of his own invention to complete the story. We may suppose these commentaries of his were quickly carried to **Rome**. There, they were found by the **Greeks**, who frequently came on embassies to the Roman See during that time, and were taken to **Constantinople**. From these, **Methodius** (who had himself been an <term>Apocrisiarius</term> <note>A high-ranking ecclesiastical representative or ambassador</note> or ambassador from **Nicephorus**, the **Greek Patriarch**, to **Pope Paschal** at **Rome**, and who was later promoted to the Patriarchate of **Constantinople** after infinite troubles) gathered the materials to write the life of **Dionysius**. It is clear he did not get these details from the records of his own Church; when **Hilduin** began composing his *Areopagitics*, he explicitly says<sup>b</sup> that the **Greeks** had written nothing concerning the martyrdom of **St. Denis**, because they could not obtain the details from such a great distance. Therefore, **Methodius** must have derived his information from **Hilduin**, or at least from some reports of that time. It is most probable he used **Hilduin's** work, because there is such an exact agreement between their accounts—not only in specific passages, but often in the very same words—as **Monsieur Launoy** has demonstrated by a detailed comparison.<sup>c</sup> This work by **Methodius** was quickly brought from **Constantinople** back to **Rome** by **Greek** ambassadors, where<sup>d</sup> **Anastasius** confesses he found it. He translated it into **Latin** and then sent it to **France**, where it was read, owned, and published by<sup>e</sup> **Hincmar**, as appears by his letter to **Charles the Bald**, the Emperor. In that letter, he plainly states that as soon as he read this *Life* written by **Methodius**, he found it to agree admirably with what he had read in his youth (meaning, I have no doubt, the writings of **Hilduin**). He explains by whom and how the *Acts* of **St. Denis** and his companions came to the knowledge of the **Romans**, and from there to the notice of the **Greeks**. This is the most likely origin and progression of the story that I can think of. From this point, how easy was it for later writers in both the **Western** and **Eastern** Church to swallow a story so plausibly tailored to their tastes? Nor did the **Greeks** have any reason to over-critically examine or reject something that brought so much honor to their Church and nation, and which seemed to put not only **France** but the entire **Western** Church under an obligation to them for furnish-

# The Life of St. **Dionysius**.

...ing them with so great and excellent a person. But to return to our **Dionysius**.

<margin>
a *At Usuard and Ado the Martyr, 5th Day before the Nones of October.* <note>original: "Apud Usuard. & Adon. Mart. V. Non. Octobr."</note>
</margin>
<margin>
b *See the authorities in P. Halloix, notes on the life of Dionysius, page 241.* <note>original: "Vid. Authoritat. ap. P. Halloix. not. ad vit. Dionys. p. 241."</note>
</margin>
X. Although we cannot doubt that he conducted himself with all diligence and faithfulness in the discharge of his office, yet because the ancient writers have passed down no specific details to us, we shall not rely on reports that are false or, at best, of doubtful credibility. Nothing certain can be recovered about him beyond what **Aristides**, the Christian philosopher (who lived and was likely born at **Athens** not long after **Dionysius**), relates in the <margin>a</margin> **Apology** <note>A formal defense of the faith</note> which he published for the Christian religion: that after a most resolute and prominent confession of the faith, and after enduring several of the severest kinds of torture, he gave the final and greatest testimony to it by laying down his life. This most likely occurred during the reign of **Domitian**, as is admitted (perhaps prompted by a secret instinct of truth) by Abbot **Hilduin**, **Methodius**, and their followers. Others, however, extend his life to the times of **Trajan**, and some even to the reign of **Hadrian**, who began his rule of the Empire in AD 117. They do this partly to allow enough time for the accounts they give of him, and partly to preserve the authority of his writings, in which a passage is quoted from the letters of **Ignatius**, written just before his martyrdom in AD 107.

I hope the reader will not expect from me an account of the miracles said to have been performed by him either before or after his death, or of the fierce disputes between various places in the Roman Church regarding his relics <note>Physical remains or personal effects of a saint</note>. One story, however, I shall not omit. In a village in **Luxembourg**, not far from **Trier** <note>original: "Treves"</note>, there is a church dedicated to **St. Denis**, where his skull (or at least a piece of it) is kept. On the crown of the skull there is a white cross, while the other parts of the bone are black. According to local tradition, supported by <margin>b</margin> some authors, this mark was made when **St. Paul** laid his hands upon him during his consecration <note>The formal act of ordaining someone as a bishop</note>. If this is true, I have nothing more to say except that "Holy Orders" (which the Roman Church considers a sacrament) did here, in a literal sense, confer an "indelible character" <note>A theological term for a permanent spiritual mark left on the soul by certain sacraments</note> and mark upon him.

<margin>
c *On the 3rd of October.* <note>original: "Τῇ γ'. τοῦ Ὀκτωβρ."</note>
</margin>
<margin>
*In the same place.* <note>original: "Ibid."</note>
</margin>
XI. His bodily appearance <note>original: "τυπῶς Σωματικὸς"</note> is described by the <margin>c</margin> **Greek Menaion** <note>A liturgical book of the Eastern Orthodox Church containing the lives of saints</note> in this way: he was of middle height, slender, and fair-skinned but pale. His nose was gracefully curved, his eyes deep-set with short eyebrows, his ears large, his hair thick and white, and his beard was moderately long but very thin. As for the character of his mind expressed in his speeches and the excellent conduct of his life, the Greeks—according to their tendency to exaggerate in both attitude and language—bestow the most extravagant praises and commendations upon him. They call him the "Sacred Interpreter" <note>original: "ἱεροφάντορα"</note> and "contemplator of hidden and unspeakable mysteries" <note>original: "τῶν ἀποῤῥήτων θεωρὸν"</note>, and an unsearchable depth of heavenly knowledge. They call him the "Theologian of the Trinity" <note>original: "τριαδικὸν θεόλογον"</note> and the "God-bearing instrument of those life-giving graces that are beyond all understanding" <note>original: "τῶν ὑπὲρ ἔννοιαν ζωοποιῶν χαρισμάτων θεοφόρον ὄργανον"</note>. They say of him that his life was wonderful, and his speech even more so; his tongue was full of light, and his mouth breathed holy fire; but his mind was most perfectly god-like <note>original: "ἀκριβῶς θεοείδεστατον"</note>. They include many more such descriptions throughout their liturgical services. And certainly, if the ideas he gave us about the celestial hierarchy, the orders of angels, and the nature of that otherworldly state were as clear and certain as some would have us believe, he might deserve that title which others...

# The Life of St. **Dionysius**.

<margin>
a See Anastasius the Librarian, Letter at Surius, in the place cited, page 132. Chrysostom on the False Prophets, page 401, Volume 6. <note>original: "Vid. Anastas. Biblioth. Epist. ap. Sur. loc. cit. p. 132. Chrysost. de Pseudo-Proph. p. 401. Tom. 6."</note>
</margin>
<sup>a</sup> Others give him the title *the Wing* or the *Bird of Heaven* <note>original Greek: πτερύγιον ἢ πετηνὸν τοῦ οὐρανοῦ</note>.

<margin>
b Suidas under the entry Dionysius, page 745. Nicephorus, Ecclesiastical History, book 2, chapter 20, page 167.
c Letter to Charles the Bald, King of the Franks, in Ussher's Irish Letters, page 59. <note>original: "Epist. ad Carol. Calv. Franc. Reg. ap. Usser. Epist. Hibern. p. 59."</note>
</margin>
XII. We are told<sup>b</sup> that the great and clear evidence of his wisdom and eloquence are the works he left behind. The ideas and language in which they are expressed are so lofty and sublime that they seem barely possible for a mere mortal to have produced. These books are infinitely intricate and complex—as our countryman <sup>c</sup> **Johannes Scotus** <note>Johannes Scotus Eriugena, a 9th-century scholar who translated these works from Greek into Latin</note>, who first translated them into Latin, tells us. They are far beyond the reach of modern understanding, and few are able to penetrate them, both because of their antiquity and the sublimity of those heavenly mysteries they discuss. It is a work so pleasing to all speculative seekers into the nature of things and the more hidden and secret parts of learning, that (if **Suidas** <note>The author of a massive 10th-century Greek encyclopedia</note> speaks truly) some of the Heathen philosophers—and particularly **Proclus**—often borrowed not only his ideas, but his very words and phrases. Because of this, Suidas suspects that some of the philosophers at **Athens** stole those books of his mentioned in the dedicatory letter to **St. Timothy** (which are now lost) and published them under their own names. 

But if I were to make a guess, I would rather suspect that this **Pseudo-Dionysius** <note>The name given by scholars to the anonymous 5th or 6th-century author who wrote under the name of Dionysius the Areopagite</note> fetched his speculations and a good part of his expressions from **Plotinus**, **Iamblichus**, and the rest of the later **Platonists**. For certainly, one egg is no more like another than this man's divinity is like the theology of that school, especially as explained by the philosophers who lived in the early ages of Christianity. I will not bother to prove here that our **Dionysius** was not the actual author of the books currently attributed to him. For although many people argue for his authorship with all possible zeal and stubbornness, there are many others—even notable men within the Roman Church—who clearly disown and deny it. Among the Reformed <note>Protestant</note> churches, it has been extensively disproved by many, and by none with greater learning and industry than **Monsieur Daillé** <note>Jean Daillé (1594–1670), a French Huguenot minister</note>, who has said everything necessary (if not more than enough) on this subject. However, regarding the date of their birth and first appearance, when he dates them as late as the sixth century, he takes away somewhat from the antiquity that might probably be allowed to them.

<margin>
d Annotations on the Acts of the Apostles, chapter 17.
e Socrates, Ecclesiastical History, book 2, chapter 46, page 160.
f Sozomen, Ecclesiastical History, book 5, chapter 18, page 623. Socrates, in the place cited.
g Letter 74, page 125, Volume 2.
h Leontius on Sects, Act 4, page 446.
</margin>
XIII. Identifying the specific author of these books is not easy to determine. Among the various guesses about this matter, none seems more worthy of consideration than what <sup>d</sup> **Lorenzo Valla** <note>A famous 15th-century Italian humanist</note> tells us some learned Greeks of his time believed: that it was **Apollinaris**. Whether it was the father or the son does not matter much, as both were men of great talent and shared the same style and character. "Both were masters of Greek learning" <note>original Greek: ἀμφότεροι ἑλληνικῶν λόγων διδάσκαλοι</note><sup>e</sup>, though of the two, the son was the most likely candidate. It is certain that **Apollinaris** was "trained in all sorts of knowledge and skilled in the styles and forms of speech," as <sup>f</sup> **Sozomen** describes him. And <sup>g</sup> **St. Basil** says of him that, being gifted with a facility for writing on any subject, joined with a great readiness and fluency of language, he filled the world with his books. Even in his theological tracts, he did not seek to establish them by proof from Scripture, but from human arguments and ways of reasoning; "he established his doctrine not from any specific text, but from his own intellect," as <sup>h</sup> another also says of him. He was born and raised at **Alexan-**

<margin>

[Alexan]dria (a place more famous than any other for schools of human learning, especially the profession of the **Platonic philosophy**) and afterwards lived at **Laodicea**. There, he was so intimately familiar with the pagan philosophers <note>original: "Gentile-Philosophers"</note> that **Theodotus**, the Bishop of that place, forbade him (though in vain) to keep company with them any longer, fearing he might be perverted to paganism. Later, **George**, his successor, excommunicated him for his insolent refusal to obey. This is said to have been the first occasion that led him to stray from the orthodox doctrines of the Church. Resenting this as a major insult, and <margin>a Socrates, page 161.</margin> "relying on the abundance of his sophistical eloquence" <note>original Greek: τῇ εὐφορίᾳ τοῦ Σοφιστικοῦ λόγου θαῤῥῶν</note>, he was prompted by a bold confidence in his own sophistical wit and subtle ways of arguing. He began to introduce new ideas in matters of doctrine and established a sect named after himself. 

Certainly, anyone who thoroughly considers the principles of **Apollinaris**, as they are represented by <sup>b</sup> **Socrates**, <sup>c</sup> **Sozomen**, <sup>d</sup> **Theodoret**, <sup>e</sup> **Basil**, and <sup>f</sup> **Epiphanius**, will find that many of them have a close affinity with Platonic ideas. Some are even similar to those found in the books attributed to **Dionysius**. Regarding the doctrine of the **Trinity**, they were generally correct; <sup>g</sup> **Socrates** particularly tells us the Apollinarians confessed the Trinity to be "consubstantial" <term>consubstantial: sharing the same essence or substance; a key term in the Nicene Creed</term>. To this, I add what a learned <sup>h</sup> man of our own nation has observed: that Apollinaris and his followers were guilty of forging church writings, which they falsely attributed to **Gregory Thaumaturgus**, **Athanasius**, and **Pope Julius**, as <sup>i</sup> **Leontius** proves in great detail. Thus, it is quite probable that these works were forged in the school of Apollinaris, either by himself or by some of his disciples.

<margin>
k See Conference of Catholics with the Severians, Councils, Volume 4, column 767.
l Theodoret, Ecclesiastical History, book 5, chapters 9 and 10, page 212.
m Socrates, book 3, chapter 16, page 187.
n Sozomen, book 6, chapter 25, page 672.
o Sozomen, book 5, chapter 18, page 623.

XIV. The theory seems even more likely when we consider that there was a certain <sup>k</sup> **Dionysius**, who was probably a friend of **Apollinaris**. Apollinaris is said to have written a famous letter to him that was circulated under the name of **Pope Julius**. Furthermore, among his own students, he had a man named **Timothy** (who was condemned along with his master by <sup>l</sup> **Pope Damasus** and the Synod at **Rome**). This gave them an easy opportunity to publish their own ideas under the more venerable names of those ancient and apostolic figures. 

Or, more likely, Apollinaris himself—being so well-versed in the art of counterfeiting—took the opportunity to compose and publish these works under the name of the ancient Dionysius. He could hardly have chosen a name more suited to his purpose: a man born in the very center of learning and eloquence <note>Athens</note>, who could easily be imagined to have been educated in all the schools of philosophy and to be specifically familiar with the writings and theories of **Plato** and his followers, which were so famous and widely accepted in that place. 

There is even more reason to believe this when we consider that <sup>m</sup> Apollinaris rewrote the Gospels and the writings of the Apostles into the form of dialogues, imitating Plato’s style among the Greeks. As for the style of the Dionysian works, which is very high-flown and affected, we previously noted how uniquely qualified Apollinaris was, possessing a talent for inventing new words and a complex way of speaking. The <sup>n</sup> historian observes that the primary tool he used to spread his heresy, and in which he had a singular talent, was his "art of words" <note>original Greek: τέχνη λόγων</note>—meaning artificial word structures and subtle ways of expressing himself. Besides this, he was an incomparable poet (both the father and the son). He was deeply devoted to the study of poetry and wrote poems in imitation of—and to the envy of—the best among the...

# The Life of Saint Dionysius.

<margin>
Sozomen, *Ecclesiastical History*, Book 6, Chapter 25, Page 671.
</margin>
...the pagans <note>original: "Heathens"</note>. In imitation of **Homer**, he wrote *Epic Poems* <note>original: "Heroic Poems"</note> based on the history of the Old Testament up until the reign of **Saul**. He also wrote *Comedies* in the style of **Menander**, *Tragedies* imitating **Euripides**, and *Odes* following **Pindar**. He composed *Divine Hymns* that were publicly sung in the churches of his breakaway sect, as well as *Songs* that men sang at feasts and during their work—even women sang them while spinning wool <note>original: "at the Distaff"</note>. Through these exercises, he was perfectly prepared for lofty and poetic styles of writing. He might easily have been tempted—especially since the subject matter allowed it—to indulge in an unrestrained and excessive imagination <note>original: "wanton and luxuriant fansie"</note> in his choice, composition, and use of words. Certainly, there was never a stranger collection—a "multitude of words" <note>original Greek: *λέξεων πολυκληθίαν*, Maximus himself calls it</note>—of sublime, affected, inflated, and poetic phrases than what is found in these books attributed to **St. Denis** <note>Saint Dionysius</note>.

<margin>
Concerning the Writings of Dionysius, Chapter 39, Page 228.
</margin>
XV. If one were to ask why a man, after so much effort, would choose to publish his works under another person’s name rather than his own, there is a simple answer: this has been an old practice. Some men have adopted it either because it was their whim to credit their own work to others, or to avoid the criticism that their ideas might provoke. Most often, however, it was done to gain greater respect and value for their writing by sending it out under the name of someone for whom the world already had a just regard and veneration. 

As for the theory of **Monsieur Daillé** <note>Jean Daillé (1594–1670), a French Protestant scholar known for his work on the early Church Fathers</note>: he suggests that the reason several learned volumes were written and falsely attributed to the Fathers of the ancient Church was to defend them from a common accusation made by the pagans <note>original: "Gentiles"</note>. The pagans used to charge Christians with being an unrefined and uneducated generation whose books were filled with nothing but plain, simple doctrines and who were strangers to all types of learning and eloquence. To counter this objection, several writers took it upon themselves to compose books full of learning and philosophy, which they published under the names of the first preachers and spreaders of the Christian faith. Daillé argues that this was specifically the case for the *Recognitions* attributed to **Clement**, and the writings attributed to **Dionysius**. 

I grant that the first case is very likely and rational. The *Recognitions* were probably written around the second century, when this objection was most common (as seen in **Celsus’s** book against the Christians) and when few learned discourses had been published by them. However, I can by no means agree with this regarding the second case. Dionysius’s works were written long after the learning and eloquence of Christians had sufficiently proven itself to the world—to the shame, conviction, envy, and admiration of its greatest enemies. There was far less need for them for this purpose, especially if it is true (as Daillé himself so confidently asserts and argues) that they were not written until the beginning of the sixth century, around the year 520. By that time, there were few learned pagans left to make such an objection, as paganism had been almost entirely banished from the civilized world.

XVI. But whoever the true author was, or for whatever reason he wrote them, it is clear that he laid the foundation for a mystical and incomprehensible theology <note>original: "Divinity"</note> among Christians. From this source came all those wild **Rosicrucean** notions <note>A reference to the Rosicrucians, a secret society claiming esoteric wisdom; the author views these mystical ideas as non-traditional</note> that some people are so fond of, and whose life and practice they praise as the very soul and perfection of the Christian state. The fact that this author directly serves this purpose can be judged by the reader from a single instance, and I assure

<meta>catchword: him</meta>
