# The Life of Saint Dionysius.

...him, it is not one of the most obscure and difficult passages in these books. I have set it down in its original language as well as our own, not being confident in my own translation (though I have expressed it word for word); for I claim no great skill in translating what I do not understand. Thus, he discusses the knowledge of God as follows:

<margin>Dionysius, *On Divine Names*, Chapter 7, page 238.</margin>

Therefore, God is known in all things and apart from all things; God is known through knowledge and through ignorance. There is of him thought, and word, and knowledge <note>original: "science"</note>, and touch, and sense, and opinion, and imagination, and name, and all other things; and yet he is not thought of, nor spoken of, nor named. He is not any of the things that exist, nor is he known in any of the things that exist. He is all things in all, and nothing in nothing; he is known to all from all things, and to no one from nothing. For we rightly say these things concerning God. And again, the most divine knowledge of God is that which is known through ignorance, according to the union that is beyond the mind; when the mind, having turned away from all existing things and then even letting go of itself, is united to the exceptionally radiant <note>original: "super-illustrious"</note> rays, being enlightened from there and therein by the unsearchable depth of Wisdom. <note>original Greek: Διὸ καὶ ἐν πᾶσιν... Σοφίας καταλαμπόμενος.</note>

<column-break/>

*God (he says) is known in all things, and without all things: he is known by knowledge, and by ignorance: there is both a thought <note>original: "cogitation"</note> of him, and a word, and a knowledge <note>original: "science"</note>, and a touch, and a sense, and an opinion, and an imagination, and a name, and all other things; and yet he is neither thought, nor spoken of, nor named. He is not any thing of those things that are, nor is he known in any of the things that are; he is both all things in all, and nothing in nothing; out of all things he is known to all, and out of nothing to nothing. These are the things which we rightly discourse concerning God. And this again is the most divine knowledge of God, that which is known*

*by ignorance, according to the union that is above understanding; when the mind getting at a distance from all things that are, and having dismissed itself, is united to those exceptionally radiant <note>original: "super-illustrious"</note> beams, from where and in which it is enlightened in the unfathomable depth of wisdom.* 

More of this and similar material <note>original: "stuff"</note> is plentifully scattered throughout these books. And if this is not mystical and profound enough, I do not know what is; certainly, anyone but one well-versed in this sort of theology would look upon it as a strange jargon of nonsense and contradiction. And yet this is the height of devotion and piety which some men earnestly strive for and in which they glory. It is as if a man could not truly understand the mysteries of religion until he had resigned his reason, nor be a Christian without first becoming an enthusiast <note>original: "Enthusiast" — in the 17th century, this referred to someone claiming direct, often irrational, divine inspiration</note>, nor be able to speak sense unless in a language which no one can understand.

# The Life of Saint Clement.

*Rome. The great confusion regarding the first Bishops of that See. A probable account attempted concerning the order of Saint Clement’s succession and reconciling it with the timelines of the other Bishops. The account given of him in the ancient Epistle to Saint James. Clement’s appointment of Notaries to record the **Acts of the Martyrs**, and his dispatching of messengers to spread the Gospel. The Schism in the Church of **Corinth**, and **Clement’s Epistle** to that Church. An inquiry into when that **Epistle** was written. The persecution under **Trajan**. His proceedings against the **Fellowships** <note>original Latin: "Heteriæ"</note>. A short account of **Saint Clement's** troubles from the writings of **Simeon Metaphrastes**. His banishment to **Cherson**. **Condemnation to the mines** <note>original Latin: "Damnatio ad Metalla"</note>, and what that entailed. The great success of his **Ministry** in his place of exile. **Saint Clement’s Martyrdom**, and the manner of it. The anniversary miracle reported on his feast day. The date of his **Martyrdom**. His genuine Writings. His **Epistle** to the **Corinthians**: the praise given to it by the **Ancient Church fathers**. Its **Style and Character**. The great modesty and humility that appears in it. The fragment of his second **Epistle**. **Spurious Writings** <note>original: "Supposititious Writings"</note>. The **Recognitions**; their various titles and different editions. Their **Antiquity**. A theory concerning their **Author**. The opinions of the **Ancient Church fathers** regarding the corruption of these texts. The **Epistle to Saint James**.*

---

I. <image-desc>Decorative initial T with mermaids and foliage</image-desc> It adds significantly to the honor of this venerable **Apostolic Man** (for all antiquity understands the reference to be to him) that he was a **fellow laborer** with Saint **Paul**, and one of those whose names were written in the **Book of Life**. He was born in **Rome**, on the **Caelian Hill** <note>original: "Mount Cœlius"</note>, as we are informed by the **Pontifical** <note>The *Liber Pontificalis* or "Book of the Popes," a record of early bishops of Rome</note> attributed to the name of **Damasus**, among other sources. His father's name was **Faustinus**, but who he was, and what his profession or way of life might have been, is not recorded. Indeed, in the **Book of Recognitions** and the **Clementine Homilies** <note>original Greek: "мі πλάσια" (Homiliae)</note> (mentioned by the ancients and lately published), we find more specific accounts of him. Although these books are falsely attributed to **Saint Clement** and are in some cases subject to valid criticism, they are of great antiquity in the Church—written not long after the **Apostolic Age** (as we shall show hereafter). Therefore, we shall draw a few details from them for our purpose, though we cannot absolutely guarantee their historical certainty. In those texts, **Saint Clement** is depicted as giving this account of himself:

<margin>a. Life of Clement, Council Records, Vol. 10, col. 74.</margin>
<margin>b. *Recognitions*, Book 7, no. 8, p. 476. *Clementine Homilies*, vol. 2, m. 8, p. 678. *Epitome*, no. 75, p. 781. Paris edition.</margin>

II. He was descended from a noble line, born of the family of the **Caesars**. His father, **Faustinian** (or **Faustas**), was a close relative of the **Emperor** (likely **Tiberius**) and was educated alongside him. Through the Emperor's arrangement, he was married to **Mattidia**, a woman from a prominent Roman family. Clement was the youngest of three sons; his two elder brothers were **Faustinus and Faustus**, who later changed their names to **Nicetas and Aquila**. His mother, who was apparently a woman of exquisite beauty, was relentlessly pressured by her husband’s own brother to engage in unchaste behavior. To avoid these troublesome advances, yet reluctant to reveal the truth to her husband for fear it would cause a scandal or dishonor their family, she devised a plan. She pretended to her husband that she and her two eldest sons had been warned in a dream to leave **Rome** for a period of time. Accordingly, he sent them to reside at Athens,

# The Life of Saint CLEMENT.

Athens, for the greater convenience of their education. But hearing nothing of them, even though he sent messengers specifically for that purpose every year, he resolved at last to go himself in pursuit of them. He did this, leaving his youngest son, then twelve years of age, at home under the care of tutors and guardians.

<margin>
a *Recognitions*, book 1, page 399. compare *Homilies* 1, page 546. *Epistles*, page 949.
</margin>
Saint Clement grew up engaged in all manly studies and virtuous actions, until he fell into a state of great mental dissatisfaction concerning the immortality of the soul and the state of the afterlife. He applied himself to search more narrowly into the nature and the truth of things. After having frustrated all his own ideas, he turned to the schools of the philosophers, where he met with nothing but fierce contentions, endless disputes, and deceptive and uncertain methods of reasoning. From there, he resolved to consult the Egyptian **Hierophants** <note>original: "Hierophantæ"; high-ranking priests who interpreted sacred mysteries</note> and to see if he could meet with anyone who, by the arts of magic, was able to summon back someone who had departed to the invisible world. He hoped the very sight of such a person might satisfy his curious inquiries about this matter. While he was in this state of suspense, he heard of the Son of God appearing in the world and the excellent doctrines he had published in Judea. He was further instructed in these by the ministry of Saint Barnabas, who came to Rome. He followed Barnabas first to Alexandria, and from there, after a little time, to Judea. Arriving at Caesarea, he met with Saint Peter, by whom he was instructed and baptized; he continued as Peter’s companion and disciple for a great part of his life.

<margin>
b *Ecclesiastical History*, book 2, chapter 35, page 191; book 3, chapter 2 & 18, page 247.
c Eucherius of Lyon to Valerian, *On Contempt of the World*. Anonymous, *On the Lives of Peter and Paul*, in Patrick Young's notes on Clement’s *Epistle to the Corinthians*.
d *Primitive Christianity*, part 1, chapter 3.
e *Synopsis of the Lives of the Apostles* in the *Library of the Holy Fathers*, volume 3, page 150, column 1.
f *Alexandrian Chronicle* <note>original: "Chronicon Alexandrinum"</note>, page 508.
</margin>
III. This is the summary of what I thought good to borrow from those ancient writings. As for his relatives—the various misfortunes his father, mother, and two brothers separately encountered, and the strange accidents by which they all afterwards met, were converted, and were baptized into the Christian faith—I omit those details. I do so partly because they are less relevant to my purpose, and partly because the story looks more like a dramatic scene of fiction than a true and real history. Regarding the account of his being related to the Imperial family: although it is more than once confidently asserted by Nicephorus (who transcribes a good part of the story) and by others before him, I cannot help but see it as an evident mistake. This error likely arises from no other source than the story of **Flavius Clemens** the **Consul** <note>A Roman aristocrat and relative of Emperor Domitian who was executed, possibly for Christian sympathies</note>, who was a first cousin to the Emperor Domitian, and whose wife, Flavia Domitilla, was also a near relative of the Emperor. We have elsewhere given an account of their conversion to and martyrdom for the faith of Christ, based on the writers of those times. It is probable that Saint Clement mostly followed Saint Peter’s travels and came with him to Rome, where he eventually had the government of that Church committed to him. Dorotheus tells us that he was the first of the **Gentiles** <note>Non-Jewish people</note> who embraced the Christian faith, and that he was first made Bishop of Sardica (a city in Thrace, afterwards called Triaditza) and then of Rome. But in this claim, I think he stands alone; I am sure he has none of the ancient writers to join him, unless he refers to another Clement, whom the *Alexandrian Chronicle* also identifies as one of the **Seventy Disciples** <note>original: "LXX Disciples"</note> but seems to confuse with our Saint Clement. That he was Bishop of Rome is a point of unanimous and unquestionable agreement among all ancient writers, though they strangely vary regarding the time and order of his appointment. The writers of the Roman Church, however grandly they speak of the constant and uninterrupted succession of Saint Peter’s Chair, are nonetheless involved in an inextricable labyrinth concerning the succession of the first four Bishops of that See,

# The Life of Saint CLEMENT.

...hardly two of them of any significance provide the same account. I shall not attempt to resolve the differences between the various schemes that have been proposed, but only offer what I believe to be the most likely and probable explanation.

<margin>
a *Against Heresies, book 3, chapter 3, page 232.*
b *Epiphanius, Heresies 27, page 51. See Hammond, Dissertation 5, chapter 1, page 256.*
c *Caius against Proclus and*
d *Dionysius, Letter to the Romans, cited in Eusebius, book 2, chapter 25, page 68.*
</margin>
IV. It is clear both from <sup>a</sup>Irenaeus and <sup>b</sup>Epiphanius, as well as before them from <sup>c</sup>Caius, an ancient writer, and from <sup>d</sup>Dionysius, Bishop of Corinth, that Peter and Paul jointly laid the foundations of the Church of Rome. Therefore, they are equally styled "Bishops" of it: one acting as the Apostle of the Gentiles (as we may probably suppose), taking care of the Gentile Christians, while the other, as the <term>Apostle of the Circumcision</term> <note>A title for Saint Peter, referring to his primary mission to the Jewish people.</note>, applied himself to the Jewish converts at Rome. We cannot imagine that—given such deep-seated and long-standing prejudices between Jews and Gentiles, especially in matters of religion—they would suddenly lay these aside and both join together in one public society. We know that in the Church of Jerusalem, until the destruction of the Temple, no one was admitted except Jewish converts. It might have been the same at first in Rome, where vast numbers of Jews resided at the time; they might have kept themselves for some time in distinct assemblies, the one under Saint Paul, the other under Peter.

There seems to be some foundation for such a conjecture even in Apostolic history. Saint Luke tells us that Saint Paul, upon his <margin>*Acts 28: 23, 24, 25, 28, 30, 31.*</margin> first coming to Rome, was rejected by the Jews and turned to the Gentiles, declaring to them the "salvation of God." The Gentiles gladly heard and accepted it, and Paul continued preaching the "Kingdom of God" and receiving all who came to him for two years together. I look upon this as the first settled foundation of a Gentile Church at Rome. The further care and presidency of this church Saint Paul might have passed down to Linus (whom the <term>interpolated Ignatius</term> <note>Referring to versions of the letters of Ignatius of Antioch that were later expanded or altered by other writers.</note> identifies as his deacon or minister). Meanwhile, Saint Peter, having established a church of Jewish converts, might have turned it over to Saint Clement. <sup>e</sup>Tertullian <margin>e *On the Prescription of Heretics, chapter 32, page 213.* f *Apostolic Constitutions, book 7, chapter 47, folio 451, 601.*</margin> expressly says that Peter ordained Clement as Bishop of Rome. Accordingly, the compiler of the <sup>f</sup>*Apostolic Constitutions* records that Linus was ordained Bishop of Rome by Saint Paul, and Clement by Saint Peter.

He says, indeed, that Linus was the first, and that may very well be true, seeing as Saint Paul was in Rome for a considerable time before Saint Peter arrived (regardless of what modern writers of that Church say to the contrary). When Linus died, he was probably succeeded in his distinct role by Cletus or Anacletus (for the Greeks—likely most correctly—generally consider them to be the same person). At that time, Clement, whom Saint Peter had ordained to be his successor, continued to act as the president over the church of Jewish converts. Things remained this way until the death of Cletus, at which point the difference between Jew and Gentile had quite worn off. The entire presidency and government of the whole Church of Rome then devolved upon Saint Clement as the survivor. From this period of time, the years of his episcopacy (according to the common calculation) are said to begin.

By this account, not only the records of <sup>g</sup>Optatus <margin>g *On the Schism of the Donatists, book 2, page 38.* h *Bucherian Catalogue; edited commentary on Victorinus’s Paschal Canon, chapter 15, page 269.*</margin> and the <sup>h</sup>*Bucherian Catalogue* may be true—which place Clement after Linus—but also the accounts of Baronius and many of the ancients, who place both Linus and Cletus before him. We can accept that they did indeed precede him as bishops and pastors of the Gentile Church. As for a more distinct and particular account of the dates, I calculate them as follows: Peter and Paul suffered martyrdom in the Neronian persecution (as we have likely shown elsewhere) in **AD 65**. After this, Linus held office for twelve years, four months, and twelve days. Cletus followed for twelve years, one (or as Baronius says, seven)...

...seven months, and eleven days. Together, these accounts make twenty-five years and extend to AD 90. If we then add the nine years, eleven months, and twelve days during which Clement sat as the sole Bishop over that entire Church, the dates fall exactly within the third year of the Emperor Trajan’s reign. This is the time assigned for his martyrdom by <term>Eusebius</term><note>A 4th-century Greek historian often called the "Father of Church History."</note>, Jerome, Damasus, and many others. Alternatively, if we follow Petavius, Ricciolus, and others in assigning the martyrdom of Peter and Paul to AD 67—two years later—the calculation runs even more smoothly. This provides enough time for the extra months and days recorded in different accounts to make the years of their <term>pontificate</term><note>The period of a pope's or bishop's time in office.</note> complete and full. Considering the great complexity and confusion of these records, I cannot think of any other way that offers such a clear solution to this matter. For if we grant that Clement was ordained by Saint Peter as his successor (as several ancient writers expressly affirm), yet recognize the evident fact that he did not die until the third year of Trajan (AD 100), it is very difficult to find any other way to reconcile these dates. <margin>* Against Carpocrates, Heresy 27, p. 51. See Clement’s Letter to the Corinthians, p. 69.</margin> As for the theory of Epiphanius—that Clement might have received the <term>imposition of hands</term><note>The ritual of placing hands on a person to ordain them into a religious office.</note> from Peter but refused to actually exercise the Episcopal office as long as Linus and Cletus were alive—he only proposes this as a conjecture. It was based merely on a misunderstood passage from Clement’s own *Letter to the Corinthians*, and Epiphanius confesses he dare not be certain whether it was so or not.

<margin>b This exists in Greek and Latin among the *Apostolic Fathers*, edited by Cotelerius.</margin>
V. If the ancient <sup>b</sup> Letter written to Saint James, the brother of our Lord, under the name of Saint Clement, could be admitted as reliable evidence, we would find there not only that Clement was appointed Bishop by Saint Peter, but also the specific ceremony by which the whole affair was carried out. It tells us that the Apostle Peter, sensible of his approaching death, presented Clement before the Church as a fit person to be his successor. The good man declined the honor with all possible modesty, but Saint Peter urged it upon him in a long discourse. Peter set out at length the particular duties of both the ministers in their respective orders and the people; once finished, he laid his hands upon Clement and compelled him to take his seat. Ecclesiastical records give us very little account of how he administered this great but difficult province. <margin>c *Book of Pontiffs*, in the Life of Clement, Council Volume 1, column 74.</margin> The author of the *Pontifical* (the book of popes) that is attributed to Pope Damasus tells us that he divided Rome into seven regions. In each of these, he appointed a <term>notary</term><note>In the early church, a scribe or official responsible for documenting legal and ecclesiastical proceedings.</note> who was to diligently inquire after all the martyrs who suffered within his division and faithfully record the acts of their martyrdom. I confess the credibility of this author is not good enough to rely solely on his testimony regarding matters from so long ago. However, we are otherwise sufficiently assured that the custom of notaries recording the speeches, deeds, and sufferings of the martyrs did exist in the early ages of the Church. Besides this, we are told by others that he sent out several people to preach and spread the Christian religion in those countries where the sound of the Gospel had not yet arrived. He did not only concern himself with spreading Christianity where it was missing, but also with preserving the peace of those churches where it was already established. <margin>* Hegesippus, cited in Eusebius, book 3, chapter 16, page 88.</margin> For instance, when an unhappy <term>schism</term><note>A formal split or division within a religious body.</note> broke out in the Church of Corinth, they sent to Rome to request his advice and assistance. In the name of the Church he governed, he wrote back an incomparable Letter to them to settle and quell *that impious and abominable sedition* <note>original Greek: μιαρὰν & ἀνόσιον στάσιν (*miaran & anosion stasin*)</note>, as <margin>d *Letter to the Corinthians*, page 2.</margin> <sup>d</sup> he calls it...

...shameful rebellion that had arisen among them. Indeed, there seems to have been a more intimate and friendly communication between these two Churches in those times than between any others mentioned in the writings of the Church. The exact time this Epistle was written is not known, as its date cannot be certainly determined by any ancient records or any hints within the Epistle itself. The theory that has been accepted by some of the most notable and learned scholars is that it was written before the destruction of Jerusalem, while the Temple and the <term>Levitical</term><note>Relating to the priests of the tribe of Levi and the ancient Jewish system of worship described in the Old Testament.</note> services were still active. They gather this, I suppose, from a passage where he speaks of them in the present tense. But whoever impartially considers the text will find no necessary foundation for such a conclusion; Saint Clement’s goal was only to illustrate his argument and show the reasonableness of observing those particular duties and ministries which God has appointed for us by alluding to the ordinances of the <term>Mosaic Institution</term><note>The religious and social system established by the Law of Moses.</note>. To me, it seems most probable that it was written shortly after the persecution under Domitian, and likely not long before Clement’s own exile. To excuse himself for not answering the letters of the Church of Corinth sooner, he tells them it was "on account of the calamities and misfortunes happening to us" <note>original: "διὰ γινομένας ἡμῖν Συμφορὰς κ̀ περιπτώσις"</note>. Now, it is clear that no persecution had been raised against the Christians, especially at Rome, from the time of Nero until Domitian. As for Mr. Young’s theory from this passage—that it was written during Clement's banishment—he forgot to consider that the Epistle was written not in Clement’s own name, but in the name of the Church of Rome. This circumstance makes the passage incapable of being applied specifically to him.

<margin>c Eutropius, Roman History, book 8, near the beginning.</margin>
<margin>d Toward the year 100, number 8, volume 2.</margin>

VI. With steady patience and prudent care, he weathered the stormy and troublesome times of Domitian and the short but peaceable reign of Nerva. But alas, the clouds returned after the rain, and a blacker storm began to gather during the time of Trajan. He was indeed an excellent Prince, with so sweet and agreeable a character and so mild and inoffensive a manner that it was forever after part of the solemn cheers at the election of a new Emperor to shout: "Better than Trajan!" <note>original: "MELIOR TRAJANO"</note>. Nonetheless, he was zealous for his religion, and for that reason, he was a severe enemy to Christians. Among several laws enacted at the beginning of his reign, he published one whereby he forbade the <term>Heteriae</term><note>From the Greek *hetaireia*, meaning associations or political clubs; the Roman state feared these groups could become centers of conspiracy.</note> (if Baronius <note>Cesare Baronius, a 16th-century cardinal and historian.</note>—whose dating I strongly doubt—conjectures the time correctly, for Pliny's Epistle <note>A famous letter from Pliny the Younger to Emperor Trajan asking for advice on how to prosecute Christians.</note> upon which he seems to base this was likely written at least nine or ten years later). These were the societies or colleges established throughout the Roman Empire where men used to meet and feast liberally under the pretense of handling business more conveniently and maintaining mutual love and friendship; yet the Roman State viewed them with a jealous eye as potential nurseries for treason and rebellion. Under the label of these unlawful combinations, Christian assemblies were viewed by their enemies. Finding them united under one common leader and constantly meeting at their solemn <term>Love-feasts</term><note>A communal meal shared by early Christians, also known as the Agape feast.</note>, and especially because they followed a way of worship different from the religion of the Empire, the authorities thought they could legally proceed against them as illegal societies and despisers of the Imperial law. In this, Saint Clement, as head of the society at Rome, was sure to suffer the most. Indeed, it was no more than he himself had long expected, as appears from...

VII. Simeon the Metaphrast <note>A 10th-century Byzantine author known for compiling the lives of saints.</note>, in the account of Clement's martyrdom <margin>See note B.</margin> (which is much the same as the *Life of Saint Clement* said to be written by an uncertain author and published long ago by Lazius at the end of the work by Abdias of Babylon), describes the beginning of his troubles as follows. Saint Clement, having converted Theodora, a noble lady, and afterwards her husband Sisinnius—a kinsman and favorite of the late Emperor Nerva—found that winning over such a prominent man quickly influenced others of high rank and status to embrace the Faith. Such is the power of religious example among the Great to sway people toward piety and virtue. But envy naturally resents the good of others and hates the person who brings it about. This success brought upon Clement the particular hatred <note>original: "odium"</note> of Torcutianus, a man of great power and authority in Rome at that time. Torcutianus used the city's lower magistrates to incite the people to riot against the holy man, charging him with magic and sorcery, and for being an enemy and blasphemer of the gods. They cried out that he must either offer sacrifice to the gods or pay for his impiety with his blood. Mamertinus, the Prefect of the City, a moderate and prudent man, wishing to quiet the uproar, sent for Saint Clement and mildly persuaded him to comply. But finding Clement’s resolution unshakable, he sent word to the Emperor to inform him of the case. The Emperor returned a short <term>rescript</term><note>An official written reply or edict from a Roman emperor.</note> stating that Clement should either sacrifice to the gods or be banished to Cherson <note>A city in the Crimea.</note>, a desolate city beyond the Black Sea <note>original: "Pontic Sea"</note>. Mamertinus, having received the imperial command, unwillingly complied and ordered that all things be made ready for the voyage. Accordingly, Clement was transported there to dig in the marble quarries and labor in the mines. "Condemnation to the mines" <note>original: "Damnatio ad Metalla"</note> is a punishment frequently mentioned in Roman laws, where it is described as a punishment next to death <margin>See note C.</margin> <note>original: "proxima morti pœna"</note>. Indeed, the treatment under this sentence was extremely harsh and rigorous. Besides the severest labor and most intolerable hardship, the condemned person was treated with every kind of inhumanity: whipped and beaten, chained and fettered, and stripped of his estate <margin>See note D.</margin>, which was forfeited to the imperial treasury. The person himself was permanently degraded to the status of a slave and consequently rendered incapable of making a will. Not only this, but they were further subjected to the most public marks of infamy and dishonor <margin>See note E.</margin>: their heads were half-shaved, their right eyes gouged out, their left legs disabled, and their foreheads branded with a mark of shame. This particular disgrace was first used in these cases by Caligula <margin>See note F.</margin> (the historian notes it as an instance of his cruel temper) and continued from his time until the reign of Constantine, who abolished it by law in the year of Christ 315 <margin>See note G.</margin>. This is to say nothing of the hunger and thirst, the cold and nakedness, and the filth and squalor they were forced to endure in those miserable places.

VIII. Arriving at the place of his harsh exile, he found vast numbers of Christians condemned to the same miserable fate. Their spirits were greatly uplifted <note>original: "erected"</note> amid all their hardships at the sight of such a good man. By his constant preaching and the frequent miracles he performed, their enemies were converted to a better opinion...

Having spoken there of the torments and sufferings which the Holy Apostles had undergone, he tells them <margin>a as cited above, page 9.</margin> that he looked upon himself and his people as being in the same arena <note>original: "ἐν αὐτῷ τῷ σκάμματι"</note>, set to run the same race, and that "the same struggle and conflict lies before us" <note>original: "& ὁ αὐτὸς ἡμῖν ἀγὼν ἐπίκειται"</note>.

<margin>b The complete Greek and Latin text is found in Cotelerius, in the place cited, page 826.</margin> VII. Simeon the *Metaphrast* <note>A 10th-century Byzantine author known for compiling and rewriting the lives of saints.</note>, in his account of Clement's martyrdom—which is much the same as the *Life of Saint Clement* said to be written by an unknown author and published long ago by Lazius at the end of the works of *Abdias of Babylon*—sets down the beginning of his troubles as follows. *Saint Clement* had converted *Theodora*, a noble lady, and later her husband *Sisinnius*, a kinsman and favorite of the late Emperor *Nerva*. Gaining such a prominent man quickly drew others of high rank and quality to embrace the Faith. Such is the power of the example of religious greatness in swaying people toward piety and virtue. 

But envy naturally resents the good of others and hates the person who achieves it. This success brought upon him the particular hatred <note>original: "odium"</note> of *Torcutianus*, a <margin>c Same source, page 832.</margin> man of great power and authority at that time in *Rome*. By influencing the city's lower magistrates, he incited the people to riot against the holy man, charging him with magic and sorcery, and with being an enemy and blasphemer of the gods. They cried out that he should either offer sacrifices to the gods or pay for his impiety with his blood. *Mamertinus*, the Prefect of the city—a moderate and prudent man—wishing to calm the uproar, sent for *Saint Clement* and mildly persuaded him to comply. But finding Clement's resolution inflexible, he sent word to the Emperor about the case. 

The Emperor returned this short imperial decree <note>original: "Rescript"</note>: that he should either sacrifice to the gods or be banished to *Cherson* <note>Modern Sevastopol in Crimea.</note>, a desolate city beyond the *Black Sea* <note>original: "Pontic Sea"</note>. *Mamertinus*, having received the imperial mandate, unwillingly complied with it and ordered everything to be prepared for the voyage. Accordingly, Clement was transported there to dig in the marble quarries and labor in the mines. 

"Condemnation to the mines" <note>original: "Damnatio ad Metalla"</note> is a punishment frequently mentioned in *Roman Law*, where it is described as "a punishment very close to death" <note>original: "proxima morti pœna"</note> <margin>d Law 28, Digest concerning punishments, Book 48, Title 19.</margin>. Indeed, the treatment under this sentence was extreme and rigorous. Besides the severest labor and most intolerable hardship, the condemned person was treated with every kind of inhumanity: whipped and beaten, chained and fettered, and deprived of his estate <margin>e Law 36, as cited above; Law 12, Digest on the rights of the treasury; Law 49, Title 14; Law 1, on the goods of the condemned; Law 8, Who can make a Will, Section 4. Cyprian, Epistle 77 to Nemesianus, page 155; Eusebius, Book 8, chapter 12, page 307.</margin>, which was forfeited to the government treasury. The person himself was permanently reduced to the status of a slave and consequently rendered unable to make a will. Not only this, but they were further exposed to the most public marks of infamy and dishonor: their heads were half-shaved, their right eyes bored out, their left legs disabled, and their foreheads branded with a shameful mark. This piece of disgrace was first used in these cases by <margin>f Suetonius in the Life of Caligula, chapter 27, page 428.</margin> *Caligula* (and the historian notes it as an instance of his cruel temper), and it continued from his time until the reign of *Constantine*, who abolished it by a <margin>g Law 2, Theodosian Code on punishments, Book 9, Title 40.</margin> law in AD 315. This is to say nothing of the hunger and thirst, the cold and nakedness, and the filth and squalor which they were forced to struggle with in those miserable places.

VIII. Arriving at the place of his uncomfortable exile, he found vast numbers of Christians condemned to the same miserable fate. Their spirits were greatly lifted under all their pressures at the sight of such a good man. Through his constant preaching and the frequent miracles he performed, their enemies were converted to a better opi-

# The Life of Saint Clement.

<margin>
<sup>a</sup> *Library, book 16.*
<sup>b</sup> *Suetonius's Life of Tiberius, chapter 62, page 366. Vulcacius Gallicanus's Life of Avidius Cassius, chapter 4, page 247. Mark 9:42.*
</margin>
...the opinion of them and their religion. The inhabitants of those countries daily flocked to the Faith, so that in a short time Christianity had driven Paganism out of the region, and all monuments of idolatry thereabouts were defaced and overturned. The news of this was quickly carried to the Emperor, who dispatched Aufidianus the President <note>original: "President"; a term for a Roman provincial governor or prefect.</note> to put a stop to this growing sect. He attacked them with methods of terror and cruelty, putting great numbers of them to death. But finding how readily and resolutely they stepped forward for execution, and that today's martyrs only prepared others for tomorrow's torments, he gave up contending with the multitude. Instead, he resolved to single out one person of note above the rest, whose exemplary punishment might strike dread and terror into the others. To this purpose Saint Clement was chosen. After all temptations to renounce his faith were tried on him in vain, the executioners were commanded to take him aboard a ship and throw him into the bottom of the sea, where the Christians would have no hope of finding him. This kind of death was called *katapontismos* <note>original: "καταποντισμὸς"; a Greek term for execution by drowning.</note>, and was used not only among the Greeks—as seen in the instance mentioned by <sup>a</sup> Diodorus Siculus—but also by the Romans, as we find several criminals condemned to be thrown into the sea by both <sup>b</sup> Tiberius and Avidius Cassius. To this our Lord refers when, in the case of willful scandal, he pronounces it *better for the man that a millstone were hanged about his neck, and he cast into the bottom of the sea.*

<margin>
<sup>c</sup> *Commentary on Matthew 18, page 53, volume 9.*
<sup>d</sup> *Sermon on the miracle involving the child performed by St. Clement, in Surius, November 23; and in Greek and Latin in Cotelerius, page 837.*
<sup>e</sup> *Ibid. page 841.*
<sup>f</sup> *On Miracles, book 1, chapters 35, 36, page 46.*
</margin>
Although <sup>c</sup> Saint Jerome tells us that this punishment was common among the ancient Jews in cases of more severe crimes, I do not remember that any such capital punishment ever actually prevailed among them. I shall not relate here what I find concerning the strange and miraculous discovery of Saint Clement’s body, nor the particular miracle of a little child preserved for an entire year in the church erected to him in the middle of the sea (though solemnly asserted by <sup>d</sup> Ephrem, Bishop of the place). I doubt people would ever have a belief wide enough to swallow such stories, or the infinite other miracles said to have occurred there. It shall only suffice to mention one: that upon the anniversary of his martyrdom, the sea retreats on each side into heaps and leaves a clear, dry passage for three miles to the martyr's tomb. This tomb was located within a church built (as it must be supposed, by angels) within the sea. Once the people’s devotions are ended, the sea returns to its place; "God by this means doing honor to the martyr," <note>original: "τιμῶντος τοῦ Θεοῦ κἀνταῦθα τὸν μάρτυρα"</note> says <sup>e</sup> one of my authors. I only add that these traditions were current before the time of Gregory, Bishop of Tours, <sup>f</sup> who speaks of them with great reverence and devotion.

<margin>
<sup>g</sup> *Book 3, chapter 34, page 106.*
<sup>h</sup> *On Ecclesiastical Writers, under Clement.*
<sup>i</sup> *Year 4 of the 220th Olympiad, Indiction 1, page 594, as cited above.*
<sup>k</sup> *Location cited above, page 269.*
</margin>
Saint Clement died (as both <sup>g</sup> Eusebius and <sup>h</sup> Saint Jerome testify—for I do not rely on the account of the <sup>i</sup> *Alexandrian Chronicle*, which places it four years later in the seventh year of Trajan, though the Consuls assigned there actually belong to the fourth year of that Emperor) in the third year of Trajan. This was a little more than two years after his banishment, after he had been the sole Bishop of Rome for nine years, six months, and several days, according to Baronius <note>Cesare Baronius, a 16th-century Cardinal and historian.</note> and others. However, the catalogue of Bucherius <note>Gilles Bucher, a Jesuit scholar who published ancient Roman chronologies.</note> is more reliable, as it was composed before the death of Pope Liberius in the year 354; it lists his reign as nine years, eleven months, and twelve days. His martyrdom happened on the 24th of November, according to Baronius and the ordinary Roman computation, but on the ninth of that month, says the small Martyrology published by <sup>k</sup> Bucherius, which was unquestionably one of the authentic calendars of the ancient...

# The Life of Saint Clement.

IX. THE writings which at this day bear the name of this apostolic man are of two sorts: genuine or supposititious <note>original: "supposititious"; meaning falsely attributed or forged.</note>. In the first class is that famous *Epistle to the Corinthians*, so much praised by the ancients. Irenaeus calls it "the most sufficient writing" <note>original: "ἱκανωτάτη γραφὴ"</note>; <sup>b</sup> Eusebius says it is a "truly great and wonderful" <note>original: "μεγάλη τε καὶ θαυμασία"</note> Epistle; and Saint Jerome <sup>c</sup> adds that it is "very useful." Photius <sup>d</sup> titles it "noteworthy" <note>original: "ἀξιόλογος"</note>, worthy of all esteem and veneration; and Eusebius <sup>e</sup> assures us it was "universally acknowledged" <note>original: "ἀνωμολογημένη ὡς καὶ πᾶσα"</note>, received by all, and indeed reverenced by them next to the Holy Scriptures. Therefore, it was publicly read in their churches for some ages, even until his time, and perhaps for a long time after. The style of it (as <sup>f</sup> Photius truly observes) is very plain and simple, imitating an ecclesiastical and unaffected way of writing, which breathes the true spirit and character of the Apostolic Age. 

It was written on the occasion of a great schism <note>original: "Schism"; a formal division or split within a church.</note> and rebellion in the Church of Corinth, started by two or three factious persons against the governors of the church. These individuals, envying either the spiritual gifts or the authority and esteem of their guides and teachers, had attempted to depose them and had drawn the greatest part of the church into the conspiracy. Clement, therefore, endeavors by soft words and strong arguments to reduce them back to peace and unity. 

His modesty and humility in this letter are particularly noticeable—not only in that he writes it entirely in the name of the Church of Rome without ever mentioning his own name, but also in that he treats the Corinthians with such gentle and mild persuasions. Nothing of sourness or an imperious "lording it over God’s inheritance" <note>A reference to 1 Peter 5:3, criticizing leaders who rule by force.</note> is to be seen in the whole Epistle. Had he known himself to be the infallible judge of controversies, to whose sentence the whole Christian world was bound to submit—invested with a supreme, unaccountable power from which there was no appeal—we might have expected to hear him argue in a very different manner. But these were the infringements and usurpations of later ages, when a spirit of greed and worldly ambition had stifled the modesty and simplicity of those first and best ages of religion. 

There is so great a similarity in many things, both in words and matter, between this and the *Epistle to the Hebrews*, that it tempted Eusebius, Saint Jerome, and some others before them to conclude that Saint Clement was at least the translator of that Epistle. This *Epistle to the Corinthians*, after it had been generally mourned as lost for many ages, was published here in England about forty years ago <note>This refers to the 1633 publication of the *Codex Alexandrinus* by Patrick Young, which contained the text of Clement's epistle.</note>—a treasure not sufficiently to be valued, serving to the benefit of the church in general and the honor of our own church in particular. 

Besides this first letter, there is a fragment of a second Epistle, or rather a homily <note>original: "Homily"; a sermon or religious discourse.</note>, containing a serious exhortation and direction to a godly life. It is ancient indeed, and many would persuade us that it is his and was written many years before the first, as it shows no signs of troublesome and unquiet times. However, Eusebius, Saint Jerome, and Photius assure us that it was rejected and never obtained the same level of approval among the ancients as the first. Therefore, though we do not strictly rule against it being his, we think it safer to agree with the judgment of the ancients than with a few recent writers on this matter.

X. AS for those writings that are undoubtedly spurious and falsely attributed...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of writings attributed to Saint Clement of Rome, moving from acknowledged works to those considered fictitious or disputed by early church historians, specifically focusing on the "Recognitions" and the potential authorship of Bardesanes the Syrian.</meta>

# The Life of St. Clement

<margin>
a Ibid. page 110.
Preface to *Primitive Christianity*.
b Codex 112, column 289.
c Preface to *Gaudentius*, page 397.
d *Church History*, book 3, chapter 18, page 248.
</margin>

...fictitious and disowned (as Eusebius says) because they did not "preserve the pure character of apostolic orthodoxy" <note>original: "καθαρὸν τὸν ἀποστολικῆς ὀρθοδοξίας ἀποσώζειν χαρακτῆρα"</note>. Although, the truth is, he says this only of the *Dialogues of Peter and Appion*, not even mentioning the *Decretal Epistles* <note>Letters of early Popes later found to be forgeries.</note> as being not worth noticing. There are four works extant today that are attributed to him: the *Apostolical Canons* and the *Constitutions* (said to be written by him, though dictated by the Apostles), the *Recognitions*, and the *Epistle to St. James*. Regarding the first two, the *Apostolic Canons and Constitutions*, I have expressed my opinion on them elsewhere, to which I shall add nothing here. The *Recognitions* follow, which were passed down to us under different titles by the ancients: sometimes styled *St. Clement's Acts, History,* or *Chronicle*; sometimes *St. Peter’s Acts, Itinerary, Periods,* or *Dialogues with Appion*. All of these are unquestionably just different titles (or perhaps portions) of the same book. It is true, as **b** Photius suspected and **c** Rufinus (who translated it) expressly tells us, that there were two different editions of this book, differing in some things but the same in most. It deserves to be considered whether "the Clementines" <note>original: "τὰ Κλημέντια"</note> mentioned by **d** Nicephorus—which he says the Church received, denying they were the ones meant by Eusebius—and those *Clementine Homilies* lately published under that very name, are not that other edition of the *Recognitions*, since they exactly match Rufinus’s description, differing in some things but mostly agreeing with them. There is yet a third edition, or rather an abstract of them all, styled *Clement’s [work] concerning the Acts, Travels, and Preachings of St. Peter* <note>original: "Κλημέντος περὶ τῶν πράξεων"</note>, agreeing with the former, though keeping closer to the *Homilies* than the other. I suspect this was compiled by Simeon the Metaphrast, for several reasons, but especially because the addition joined to it by the same hand concerning Clement’s martyrdom is identical, word-for-word, with that of Metaphrastes, except for the conclusion, which is taken out of St. Ephrem’s homily on the miracle performed at his tomb.

<margin>
e Pseudo-Isidore, page 28.
f *Philocalia*, chapter 23, pages 81, 82.
g *Recognitions*, book 10.
h Extant in Eusebius, *Preparation for the Gospel*, book 6, chapter 10, page 273 and following. See *Recognitions*, book 9, page 503, etc.
i Eusebius, *Church History*, book 4, chapter 30, page 151. Epiphanius, *Heresies* 56, page 207.
k Eusebius, *Preparation for the Gospel*, book 6, chapter 9, page 273.
</margin>

XI. The *Recognitions* themselves are undoubtedly of very great antiquity, written about the same time and by the same hand (as Blondel likely conjectures) as the *Constitutions*, around the year 180 AD or not long after. I am certain they are cited by **f** Origen as the work of Clement in his *Periods* (or Travels), and his large quotation is found word-for-word in them today. Furthermore, before him, we find a very long **h** fragment of Bardesanes the Syrian (who flourished around 180 AD) concerning *Fate*, which is word-for-word the same as what we find in the *Recognitions*; it seems just as reasonable to suppose that Bardesanes took it from there as it is to think the other author borrowed it from him. Indeed, what if Bardesanes himself was the author of these books? It is certain that he was a man of great talent and learning, quick and eloquent, "and a most skilled debater" <note>original: "καὶ διαλεκτικώτατος"</note>. He was heretically inclined, for he came out of the school of Valentinus, whose strange notions he had so deeply absorbed that even after his recantation, he could never fully clear himself of their remnants, as Eusebius informs us. However, Epiphanius tells us he was originally Orthodox and later fell into the errors of that sect, like a well-loaded ship that, after performing its voyage, is shipwrecked in the very sight of the harbor. He was a great mathematician and astrologer, "having reached the height of Chaldean knowledge" <note>original: "ἐπ’ ἄκρον τῆς Χαλδαϊκῆς ἐπιστήμης ἐληλακώς"</note>, meaning he was accurately versed in Chaldean learning, and wrote incomparable *Dialogues* concerning Fate...

<summary>This page analyzes the various ancient writings falsely or debatably attributed to Saint Clement, such as the Recognitions and the Homilies, and discusses the possibility that the philosopher Bardesanes the Syrian was their true author.</summary>
